The Government They Found

The American Presidency from Washington to Trump

Joshua Paladino

79
Chapters
129,518

NextIntroduction

Introduction

The American presidency was never intended to be a laboratory for continuous reinvention. From the first inauguration onward, the men who held the office largely accepted a common set of premises about the character and purpose of the national government. Those premises were not identical in every detail, nor were they immune to the pressures of war, debt, territorial expansion, or sectional conflict. Yet for most of the republic’s history they formed a recognizable framework: a constitutional system in which the federal government possessed energy sufficient for defense, credit, and the regulation of interstate and foreign commerce, while the ordinary regulation of property, local order, education, and domestic life remained with the states and the people; an economy grounded in private ownership and voluntary exchange; a foreign policy that sought commercial opportunity without permanent political alliances; and a preference, wherever possible, for legislative or gradual solutions to deep social problems rather than abrupt national rupture.

Differences among presidents were real. They argued over the proper level of a tariff, the constitutionality or wisdom of a national bank, the precise line between federal and state authority, and the degree of risk that could be accepted abroad. Those arguments sometimes grew bitter and produced lasting party coalitions. They did not, however, amount to successive rejections of the basic architecture. The major realignments that later reshaped American politics—shifts in regional strength, voter coalitions, and rhetorical emphasis—occurred inside this longer continuity. The visual record of those shifts, whether in side-by-side platform comparisons, simple matrices of presidential positions, or timelines of coalition movement, is best understood as movement within a shared tradition rather than as a sequence of mutually alien regimes.

This volume examines that continuity, and its limits, across a fixed set of sixteen issues: fiscal policy and the scope of government; trade and tariffs; monetary and banking arrangements; labor; immigration and naturalization; race and civil rights; federalism; foreign policy and the use of military force; national defense; religion and public morality; education; the management of land and natural resources; energy; crime and the administration of justice; relief of poverty; and the provision of medical care. Each presidential chapter states a position on all sixteen, explains how his party differed from the party of the president before him, and sets apart at least four matters that belonged to his years alone. Between some of those chapters, the book stops for a change that was not a president’s program: an amendment or a Supreme Court decision. Those chapters explain how the change happened, which justices sat, which presidents appointed them, and what the government could do afterward that it could not before. In the earliest decades, many of these categories existed only in embryonic form. The comparison remains useful because the underlying questions—how much power the national government should exercise, how it should finance itself, how it should relate to other nations, and how it should treat property and persons—were already present. The record runs from Washington through Joseph Biden’s presidency. The final chapter asks what remains of the Democratic Party once that record ends. The office those men held did not exist in the colonies, or in the Congress that carried on the war. The next chapter is the government that did exist.

The first two presidencies display the pattern with unusual clarity. Both men were practical Federalists. Both worked to establish the new government’s credit and authority, avoid entanglement in European wars, and keep the Union intact. Their differences were matters of emphasis and circumstance rather than of first principles.

PreviousCover NextThe Government They Left

Before 1789

The Government They Left

There was no American government, in the singular, before the Revolution. There were thirteen colonies, each with its own laws, and above them a king and a Parliament in which no American sat. A man in Virginia and a man in Massachusetts were subjects of the same crown. They were not citizens of the same republic, and no officer on the continent could tax them both.

The governments they actually met were local and colonial. In New England the town meeting and the selectmen did the ordinary work. Farther south the county court did it, staffed by justices of the peace who were often the same men who sat in the assembly. Churches kept their own discipline. Poor relief, where it existed, was a town or parish charge. Crime was tried in colonial courts under English forms, with a jury in the common-law cases and, for some offenses against the trade laws, in vice-admiralty courts that sat without one. Slavery was lawful in every colony by the time of the war. It was a colonial institution, heaviest in the South, present in the North, and protected by no national code because no nation had yet been made.

Over the town and the county stood the colony. The pattern was not identical in all thirteen. In a royal colony the governor was the king’s man. In Pennsylvania and Maryland a proprietor stood in the king’s place. Connecticut and Rhode Island elected their own governors under old charters and were left, for long stretches, to do so. The common piece was an elected assembly that controlled the money. It paid the governor’s salary or refused to, raised taxes, and claimed the right to originate money bills. A council, sometimes appointed and sometimes elected, sat as an upper house and often as a court. The governor commanded the militia, called and dissolved the assembly, and could refuse his assent to a law. London could afterwards disallow a colonial statute. The Board of Trade read the laws. The Privy Council heard appeals. None of this was a national legislature. It was thirteen small governments under one empire.

What the empire reserved to itself was trade, war, and the ultimate claim of sovereignty. The navigation acts required that certain colonial goods travel in English or colonial ships and pass through Britain. Customs officers collected the duties. Parliament, from London, could legislate for the colonies, and after the Stamp Act crisis it said so in the Declaratory Act of 1766: the king in Parliament had power to bind the colonies in all cases whatsoever. The assemblies answered that they would not be taxed by a body in which they were not represented. They did not, at first, deny the king. They denied that Parliament’s power over them was unlimited. Agents such as Franklin carried that argument in London. It was an argument inside the empire, conducted by colonial legislatures that already knew how to stop a government by refusing it money.

The break turned those legislatures, and conventions standing in for them, into something else. The First Continental Congress met in 1774 after the Coercive Acts had closed the port of Boston and altered the charter government of Massachusetts. The Congress was a meeting of colonies, not a state. It had no power to tax, no courts, and no army. It agreed on a boycott, petitioned the king, and went home. When it met again in May 1775, fighting had begun. The Second Continental Congress then did what no colonial assembly could do for all of them. It adopted the army outside Boston, named George Washington to command it, issued paper money, opened relations with foreign powers, and, in July 1776, declared the colonies free and independent states. The Declaration was not a constitution. It ended the king’s authority. It did not erect a replacement.

For most of the war the real governments were the new states. They wrote constitutions, most of them between 1776 and 1780, and they wrote them against the governors they had just escaped. Legislatures were made strong. Executives were made weak, often chosen by the legislature, often without a veto, and often bound by councils. The states raised taxes, embodied militia, and fitted out their own troops. Congress could ask them for men and money. It could not compel either. A requisition was a bill sent to a sovereign, and a sovereign could pay late, pay in part, or not pay.

Congress itself remained a single house of delegates appointed by the state governments, each state voting as one. There was no separate executive. Work was done by committees, and later by boards, of members who were also legislators. The officer called president of the Congress was a presiding officer, chosen by the delegates for a short term. He signed papers and received ambassadors. He did not command the army, appoint the judges of a national judiciary, or execute a body of statutes. When the delegates went home, which they often did, the government thinned to whatever committee was still in the room. It moved when the British army approached. It had no permanent capital and no independent revenue.

The Articles of Confederation, sent to the states in 1777 and ratified only in 1781, wrote down the practice rather than replacing it. The Union was a league of states. Congress could make war and peace, send and receive ambassadors, and borrow. It could not levy a tax. It could not regulate the commerce of a state. Major acts required the votes of nine states. An amendment required all thirteen. There was no national executive and no general system of national courts, apart from the arrangements for prize cases. The Articles were in force for the last years of the fighting and for the peace. They were not in force at Lexington, or at the Declaration, or through Valley Forge. Until March 1781 the Congress governed by the authority of a revolution, not by a ratified constitution.

Money showed what that authority could not do. Congress printed continental dollars and spent them into a depreciation that became a proverb. It borrowed from France and, later, in Holland. It paid soldiers in promises. In 1781 it created the office of superintendent of finance and chartered the Bank of North America, a wartime device for a government that still could not tax. The states were asked to supply the rest. Some did. The army was clothed and fed unevenly, mutinied more than once, and was paid, in the end, badly. Washington held it together as a commander responsible to Congress. He was not a civil magistrate. The civil power remained in the states and in a Congress that could resolve but could not collect.

Foreign affairs had the same shape. Congress sent Franklin, Adams, and others, made a treaty of alliance with France in 1778, and in 1783 made the peace that recognized independence and set the boundaries. A treaty was a national act. Performance of it was not. The peace required that British creditors be able to recover their debts and that Loyalists not be further persecuted in their property. State legislatures were slow to comply, and Congress had no court and no sheriff that could make them. The United States could speak. It could not always make the speech good.

By the time the war ended, Americans had practiced two kinds of government and had refused a third. They had lived under royal and charter governments in which an assembly’s control of money was the check on an appointed executive. They had fought a war under a Congress that feared executive power enough to do without it, and under state constitutions written in the same fear. They had not yet accepted a national government that could tax, regulate commerce, maintain a court, and be executed by a single officer responsible for more than presiding. The Constitutional Convention was the argument over whether they would. The presidency was the part of the answer that had no predecessor in the president of the Congress. The next chapter begins when that office is first filled.

PreviousIntroduction NextGeorge Washington

1789–1797

George Washington

No party; identified with the Federalists

Before the office

He was born in 1732 at Pope’s Creek in Westmoreland County, a younger son of a planter who was comfortable and not great. Augustine Washington died when the boy was eleven. The older half-brothers had been sent to England to be educated. George was not. The want of that schooling stayed with him. As a boy he copied out a set of rules of civility, and he spent the rest of his life making his conduct match a standard he had not been born high enough to take for granted.

What he had instead was Lawrence, the half-brother at Mount Vernon, and the Fairfaxes of Belvoir, who took an awkward youth into a house that knew the army, England, and the western grants. He learned surveying because a Virginia boy could enter it without a college, and because Lord Fairfax had land beyond the Blue Ridge that needed measuring. Those trips were his first sight of the country that would occupy him as a soldier, a speculator, and later a president. Land was not a metaphor to him. It was the fortune a man without a great inheritance had to go and mark.

Lawrence died in 1752. Mount Vernon came to George by stages, first as a lease from Lawrence’s widow and then, in 1761, as his own. Before he was a settled planter he was a provincial officer in a disaster. In 1754 a party under his command killed a French officer, Jumonville, in the woods of the Ohio, and he then surrendered his own stockade, Fort Necessity. The next year he rode with Braddock into an ambush on the Monongahela and came out of it when most of the senior British officers did not. The defeat belonged to the empire. The composure was his, and Virginia made a reputation of it. He wanted a royal commission on the British establishment. He did not get one. The refusal taught him the distance between a colonial officer and the power he served.

He married Martha Dandridge Custis in 1759 and left the army. She was a widow with a large estate, two children, and enslaved people who were hers for life. He became a burgess, a farmer turning from tobacco toward wheat, a buyer of western land, and the master of a household that ran on forced labor. The temper was real. The private achievement was that he governed it. He made himself, by attention, into the figure of command the colony expected and that he had not been as a boy.

He was not a lawyer and not a pamphleteer. He was a soldier who believed the British army had denied him rank, and a planter who believed the British merchants held him in debt. When the quarrel with the empire became a war, Congress needed a Virginian at the head of a New England army, so that the fight would look like the continent’s. He took the command, refused a salary, and held a losing army together through winters that would have finished a man who needed victory in order to stay. The war supplied the education England had not. He learned Congress, supply, the French alliance, and the difference between applause and a government’s credit.

At the end he fixed the meaning of that command by giving it up. At Newburgh in 1783, when unpaid officers talked of turning the army on the Congress, he shamed them out of it. In December he resigned his commission at Annapolis and went home. The gesture told the country that the man who could have kept the force would not. The premises he carried into the presidency were already formed. A government had to be strong enough to pay what it owed and to command its own officers. A republic had to be led by men who could govern themselves. The West was the nation’s field. Parties were a faction to be suffered, not embraced. Foreign powers were to be kept at a distance. He did not invent those premises on the balcony in New York.

Slavery was not a theory he adopted in middle age. It was the labor of the place he was born and of the estate he married. He managed it, came to doubt its efficiency and its justice, and did not, while the government was being stood up, propose to end it. The Union mattered to him more than a settlement of that question, because without the Union there would be no government to settle anything. He would rather hold the states together than force a break. The same instinct made him fear parties. He had seen what a faction could do to an army and to a Congress. He did not believe the republic could afford two of them at the center.

In the office

Washington’s work was to show that the Constitution could function as a government. He did not invent a new order. He accepted the one the Convention had written, added a bill of rights to quiet reasonable fear, and used the powers he found there for credit, revenue, and peace.

The party

He took office before parties were admitted to be legitimate. The split between Federalists and Republicans formed under him, out of the fight over the Bank, the excise, and the French Revolution. He never stood as a party candidate, but by the second term the administration was the Federalist position: national credit, commercial connection with Britain, and a federal government able to enforce its own laws. The Republican opposition organized against that tendency without rejecting the Constitution he had put into operation.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    He signed assumption of the state Revolutionary debts, an excise on distilled spirits, and the rest of Hamilton’s revenue plan. When the excise was resisted, he rode with the militia to show that the federal government could collect what it had laid. He wanted peace revenue applied to the debt, not a permanent habit of borrowing.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    Commerce was a practical interest. He favored treaties that defined merchants’ rights and opened markets, without a settled system of industrial protection. Revenue and credit came before a protective policy.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    He signed the act creating the First Bank of the United States once he was persuaded the power attached to enumerated ends. The Mint was established. He did not himself manage the currency; he accepted a national bank as an instrument of the Treasury.

  4. 4. Labor

    Free labor, wages, and combinations of workmen were not federal questions. The economy he presided over still included enslaved labor in half the states, which the Constitution had left in place.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    Immigration was light and politically secondary. A short residence was required for naturalization. He did not build a restrictive system.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    He signed the Fugitive Slave Act of 1793 and the 1794 act limiting American participation in the international slave trade. He put no plan of abolition before Congress. In private he wished to see slavery ended by slow legislative degrees, and his will freed the people he owned in his own right after his wife’s death. Public restraint and private hope were both real, and they were not the same act.

  7. 7. Federalism

    He treated the federal government as energetic in defense, credit, and foreign commerce, and the states as the ordinary governors of property and local order. The Whiskey Rebellion was his proof that a federal tax could be collected inside a state.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    The Neutrality Proclamation of 1793 kept the United States out of the wars of the French Revolution. The Jay Treaty secured the evacuation of British posts and a troubled commercial peace. He left office warning against permanent alliances and recommending temporary commercial connections instead.

  9. 9. National defense

    The military establishment stayed small. He was willing to call militia and to keep a modest regular force sufficient to the frontier and to the laws, not a standing army on a European scale.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    He held that the national government should give bigotry no sanction and persecution no assistance. There was no establishment. He spoke of Providence in public without making a church.

  11. 11. Education

    Schools remained with towns, churches, and states. He thought well of a national university and did not get one.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    The public domain of the western territory was a national interest. Settlement, the Indian trade, and frontier posts were already the domestic business of the Union. He did not devise a later homestead system.

  13. 13. Energy

    Wood, water, and animal power did the work. There was no national fuel policy, and none was proposed.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    The Judiciary Act of 1789 organized the federal courts. Enforcement of federal criminal law was slight outside revenue, treason, and the few offenses the Constitution named.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    Relief of the poor belonged to towns, churches, and families. He proposed no federal system of support.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    Care of the sick was private or local. A marine hospital service for seamen was begun as a charge on their wages, not as a general medical system.

Of this presidency

  1. The Neutrality Proclamation

    In 1793 he declared the United States neutral in the war between France and Britain. The decision kept a weak republic out of a European war and angered those who thought the French alliance of 1778 still bound the country.

  2. The Whiskey Rebellion

    Western Pennsylvania resisted the excise. He assembled militia from several states and went out with them. The point was collection of a federal tax, not a new theory of government.

  3. The Jay Treaty

    The treaty was unpopular and, in his judgment, necessary. It removed British garrisons from the Northwest posts and bought time the country could not have spent in another war.

  4. The Farewell Address

    He warned against permanent alliances and against the spirit of party. The alliances he had avoided. The parties he disliked had already formed under his own administration.

PreviousThe Government They Left NextThe Bill of Rights and the Eleventh Amendment

1791–1795

The Bill of Rights and the Eleventh Amendment

The first change in the Constitution was not a president’s statute. Congress proposed amendments, the states ratified them, and, two years later, the Court decided a case the country then undid by amending the text again.

Amendment

The Bill of Rights

Ratified December 15, 1791

How it was made

Congress sent twelve articles to the states on September 25, 1789. Ten were ratified. They limit the national government: speech, religion, arms, searches, trials, and the reservation of undelegated powers to the states or the people. The article on congressional pay sat unratified until 1992. The article on the size of the House never was.

What it changed

The amendments did not enlarge the president or Congress. They fenced both, and they left the states, for the time, outside most of those fences. A later Court would read some of them into the Fourteenth Amendment and apply them to the states. That reading was not the law of 1791.

Decision

Chisholm v. Georgia

Decided February 18, 1793

How it was made

A South Carolina citizen sued Georgia in the Supreme Court for a Revolutionary debt. Georgia said a state could not be sued without its consent. The Court took the case.

Who sat

The Court was four to one. Every justice had been appointed by the sitting president.

With the opinion

  • John Jay — Washington
  • James Wilson — Washington
  • William Cushing — Washington
  • John Blair — Washington

In dissent

  • James Iredell — Washington

What it changed

A state could be sued by a citizen of another state in the federal court. The decision made the states answerable in a forum they had not expected. It did not last. The Eleventh Amendment was written to reverse it.

Amendment

The Eleventh Amendment

Ratified February 7, 1795

How it was made

Congress proposed it on March 4, 1794, and the states ratified it within a year. The judicial power does not extend to a suit against a state by a citizen of another state, or of a foreign state.

What it changed

An amendment took back what a decision had given. The Court could be corrected without replacing the justices, and the states recovered an immunity the decision had denied. Later readings of the amendment grew. The immediate change was the reversal of Chisholm.

PreviousGeorge Washington NextJohn Adams

1797–1801

John Adams

Federalist

Before the office

He was born in 1735 in Braintree, Massachusetts, the son of a farmer and shoemaker who was also a deacon and a selectman. The town was the government he knew first: a meeting, a church, a tax agreed by the neighbors, and a suspicion of any power that did not have to stand in front of them and explain itself. His father sent him to Harvard. He taught school, disliked it, and turned to the law, which suited a mind that wanted an argument with a rule under it.

The partnership of his life was Abigail Smith, whom he married in 1764. She was not a decoration of his career. She read what he read, managed the farm when he was gone for years, and told him the truth about his vanity, which was large and which he knew was large. The household was New England, not a plantation. There were no ranks of enslaved field hands under the Braintree house. The independence he later preached was an independence he had practiced on a small farm and in a law office.

The imperial crisis made him a public man without making him a demagogue. He watched James Otis attack the writs of assistance and took from that argument a lawyer’s conclusion: Parliament could not be unlimited and still be law. After the Boston Massacre he defended the British soldiers. The town hated the choice. He made it because a republic that would not give its enemies a trial was already done. The case fixed a habit. He would stand with the law against the crowd, and he would resent the crowd for making him do it, and he would do it anyway.

In the Continental Congress he was the manager of independence more than the author of its best sentences. He wanted the colonies declared free, a confederacy framed, and foreign help sought, and he pushed the three together while more cautious men waited on the king. Jefferson wrote the Declaration. Adams carried the floor. He believed the Revolution was a legal and moral act, not a rising of the poor against the rich. A republic, in his view, would last only as long as its people had virtue enough to bear it. He did not think virtue was automatic, and he did not think a majority was the same thing as the right.

The diplomacy that followed tested that stubbornness. In France he quarreled with Franklin, who was easier with the court and less interested in Adams’s sense of being slighted. In Holland he begged loans that the army could not fight without. At the peace he, with Jay, broke the instruction to do nothing without France, and made a separate preliminary treaty with Britain. He thought he had saved the boundary and the fishery. He also thought, not wrongly, that allies have interests of their own. The suspicion of permanent entanglement was not a slogan he learned in the presidency. He had practiced it in Paris.

He came home to serve as vice president under a man whose silence made Adams’s own need to be heard look like a fault. He called the office insignificant, and then he filled it with opinions about titles, ceremony, and the balance of the branches. He wanted a republic with a strong executive and an independent judiciary, not a pure democracy and not a monarchy, and he wanted the distinction understood. The Federalists claimed him. He never fit them comfortably, because he would not take a line from Hamilton and he would not pretend that a party was a principle. The man who reached the presidency in 1797 was already formed: a New England conscience, a lawyer’s nerve, a distrust of France and of Britain both, and a belief that the Union would be lost if either popular rage or a cabal of the great were allowed to run it.

In the office

Adams inherited Washington’s settlement and largely kept it: national credit, neutrality, and a federal government strong enough for external affairs. The difference was the Quasi-War with France, which pressed even a man of limited aims toward broader power over aliens and speech, and then receded when he chose peace.

The party

Adams was a Federalist in a way Washington had refused to be. The party had become a congressional bloc, identified with commercial connection to Britain, with national credit, and, in the crisis with France, with a stronger navy and laws against foreign agitation and domestic criticism. He led it and then split it. By making peace he broke with the High Federalists of his own cabinet and of Hamilton’s circle, who wanted the quarrel with France carried further. The Republican opposition, already formed under Washington, used that split to take the government in 1800.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    He did not undo Hamilton’s system. Defense spending rose with the naval conflict. Debt remained the frame of policy, to be carried and serviced rather than repudiated.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    Commercial treaties, not a protective system, were still the policy. The Convention of 1800 restored a basis for trade with France without a political alliance.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    The First Bank continued. He offered no alternative currency and no attack on the Bank.

  4. 4. Labor

    Wages, hours, and combinations of workmen were outside federal concern. He owned no slaves and did not make free labor a national program.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    The crisis produced the first real restriction. The Naturalization Act lengthened the residence required for citizenship. The Alien Friends and Alien Enemies Acts gave the executive power over foreigners. These were wartime measures of a party that feared French influence, not a settled immigration system.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    He never owned slaves and called the institution contrary to his principles. He also held that any ending of it must be gradual and cautious. As president he advanced no national measure against it. The priority was the Union and neutrality.

  7. 7. Federalism

    He accepted the division Washington had used. The Alien and Sedition Acts tested it. Virginia and Kentucky answered that a state might judge a federal law. He did not accept that doctrine, and he did not need it once the immediate danger passed.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    The undeclared naval war with France was the center of the term. He built a navy, authorized captures, and then sent envoys. The Convention of 1800 ended the Quasi-War and released the country from the permanent alliance of 1778. That was Washington’s advice carried out against his own party’s war wing.

  9. 9. National defense

    He supported a separate Navy Department and a limited military increase. He resisted a declaration of war and a great standing army. The navy was the service the crisis actually required.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    He believed in public morality and in no national church. Fast and thanksgiving proclamations continued the practice of his predecessor. Establishment remained with those states that still had one.

  11. 11. Education

    Schools stayed local. He proposed no federal system.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    The western posts and the Indian frontier remained national business. He added no great cession and no new land law of his own.

  13. 13. Energy

    No national fuel policy existed, and he proposed none.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    The Sedition Act made certain criticism of the government a federal crime. He signed it. It expired at the end of his term. The rest of federal criminal law stayed narrow.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    The poor remained a charge on towns and churches. There was no federal relief.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    Sickness was not a federal charge beyond the small provision already made for seamen.

Of this presidency

  1. The Quasi-War

    French seizures of American ships produced an undeclared naval war. Adams treated it as a limited defense of commerce, not as an occasion to join a European coalition.

  2. The Alien and Sedition Acts

    A Federalist Congress extended the wait for citizenship, authorized removal of certain aliens, and punished malicious writings against the government. He signed the acts. They became the opposition’s proof that crisis could stretch the government past speech.

  3. Peace with France

    He sent a second mission and accepted the Convention of 1800. The decision cost him his party and saved the country a declared war.

  4. The transfer of 1801

    He lost a bitter election and left office. The transfer itself was the particular. A party government changed hands without troops.

PreviousThe Bill of Rights and the Eleventh Amendment NextThe Virginia Four

1789–1825

The Virginia Four

Adams was the exception. Of the first five men to hold the presidency, four were planters from Virginia, and four held people in slavery. Washington was the first of them. He is sometimes set apart, as the national man who stood above section and above party. He was that, and he was also a Virginia planter. The office begins in that fact, not apart from it.

His estate was Mount Vernon, on the Potomac in Fairfax County. The crop had been tobacco and had become wheat. The labor was enslaved. He commanded the army and then the government as a man whose private income came from land worked by people he owned or, in the case of the Custis dower, controlled. Jefferson’s place was Monticello, in Albemarle. Madison’s was Montpelier, in Orange. Monroe’s was Highland, a smaller plantation in Albemarle, near Monticello. The four were not neighbors in one neighborhood. They were the same kind of man: a freeholder whose fields and household depended on enslaved labor, and whose public life was spent speaking for a republic of such freeholders.

They did not form a party under that name, and they did not always agree. Washington came to be identified with the Federalists. Jefferson and Madison organized the opposition to the government he headed, and Monroe belonged to that opposition. What they shared was older than the party quarrel. It was the state, the crop, and the labor. When Jefferson defeated Adams in 1800, the presidency went back to Virginia and stayed there. Jefferson served two terms, Madison two, Monroe two. From March 1801 until March 1825 the office did not leave the state. If Washington’s eight years are counted, Virginia held it for thirty-two of the republic’s first thirty-six. The four years outside it were Adams’s.

The succession had a shape. Jefferson had been Washington’s secretary of state. Madison was Jefferson’s. Monroe was Madison’s. The Republican party learned to treat the State Department as the step before the presidency, and for a quarter of a century that step was taken by a Virginian. The line broke when Monroe’s secretary of state was John Quincy Adams of Massachusetts, and the House, in 1825, elected him president. A Virginian, William H. Crawford, had been in the field. He was not chosen. The Virginia tenure of the office ended then. It had been the ordinary fact of the government’s first generation, not a coincidence of four separate careers.

Nothing in that dominance repealed the Constitution or made the presidency a Virginia office by law. Each of the four was elected, or in Washington’s case chosen, under the same rules that had elevated Adams. The policies differed, and the later chapters will say how. The social fact is simpler, and it is easy to misplace if Washington is left out of it. The government they conducted was national. The men who conducted it, for all but four of its first thirty-six years, were planters from one state.

PreviousJohn Adams NextThomas Jefferson

1801–1809

Thomas Jefferson

Democratic-Republican

Before the office

He was born in 1743 at Shadwell, on the edge of Albemarle County, the son of Peter Jefferson, a surveyor and mapmaker who had pushed the line of settlement west and then died when Thomas was fourteen. The boy inherited land, enslaved people, and the expectation that a Virginia gentleman would know the woods as well as the books. He was not a soldier. He was a reader, and the reading began early and never stopped. The worldview of the later president was built in Virginia, before he ever held national power, and it was built by a man who already lived on other people’s labor.

At William and Mary he found, in William Small and then in George Wythe, the teachers a provincial capital could offer a hungry mind. He read law with Wythe. He learned to think of government as a set of statutes that could be taken apart and written again, not as a mystery of kings. He was admitted to practice, entered the House of Burgesses, and began Monticello, the house on the mountain that was a project of a lifetime and a statement that he would live above the tobacco tidewater, in the county his father had surveyed. The mountain was his. The hands that leveled it were not.

He married Martha Wayles Skelton in 1772. The marriage brought him love, children, and the debts and enslaved people of the Wayles estate, which enlarged both his household and what he owed. He lived thereafter as a planter who was often short of cash and never short of acres or of the men and women who worked them. The contradiction was not hidden from him. He wrote, in these years, sentences about liberty that his own quarters refuted. He did not free the people who made the sentences possible. The habit of holding a principle he would not pay for was already his, before France and before the presidency.

The break with Britain gave him a public language. In 1774 he drafted a summary view of American rights that was too strong for some of the men who had asked for it: the king was a servant, Parliament was not their legislature, and emigration had created a right to govern themselves. Two years later, in Philadelphia, he drafted the Declaration. The committee and the Congress edited him. The core was his, and it was the core of the education Wythe and the books had given him. Men were equal in rights. Government was instituted to secure those rights. When it did not, they could leave it. He meant white men of property when he said it, and he wrote it as if he meant mankind. Both facts are required to understand him.

Back in Virginia he tried to make the new state match the paper. On the committee to revise the laws he drafted bills against primogeniture and entail, so that great estates would not pass forever in one line, and a bill for religious freedom, so that a man would not be taxed to support a church he did not believe. The statute on conscience was not enacted until after he had left the state. The aim was already clear. He distrusted an established church the way he distrusted a king, as a power that stood between a man and his own mind. He wanted a republic of freeholders, each secure on his land, none dependent on a patron or a priest.

The governorship, from 1779 to 1781, was the humiliation that sat beside the Declaration. The British invaded. The militia did not hold. He fled Richmond, and then Monticello, and an inquiry in the legislature asked whether he had done his duty. He was cleared, and he was wounded in a place the Declaration could not protect, his pride. His wife died in 1782. In 1784 he sailed for France. The Virginia years were over. They had already fixed him: a believer in written rights, in a light government, in the farmer against the funded debt and the established church, and in a Union that would leave slavery where he had found it, inside his own gate.

In the office

Jefferson called the election a revolution in principles and then governed inside the Constitution he had once viewed with reserve. The change he sought was of emphasis: lighter taxes, a stricter reading in ordinary cases, and a republic of farmers rather than of funded debt. He did not pull down the settlement he inherited.

The party

The party that took power in 1801 called itself Republican and was built against the Federalists: against the debt, the excise, the Sedition Act, and a British tilt in foreign policy. In office it became a governing party rather than a protest. Jefferson repealed the internal taxes and pardoned Sedition Act offenders, yet he left the Bank in place, used the navy, and stretched the Constitution to buy Louisiana. The Federalists shrank into a New England opposition. The Republican name now covered both the strict construction he preached and the national acts he performed.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    With Gallatin at the Treasury he repealed the internal taxes, including the whiskey excise, and relied on customs and land sales. The debt he inherited fell across two terms even after Louisiana was added to it. Offices were cut where he could cut them. Frugality changed the weight of the government more than its design.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    His first posture was peaceable commerce with all nations and entangling alliances with none. The embargo of 1807 reversed the method. To avoid war he stopped American ships from clearing for foreign ports. Trade was coerced by abstinence rather than protected by a navy sufficient to defend it.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    He had called the Bank of the United States unconstitutional. He left its charter alone. Gallatin used it. No new monetary constitution was written.

  4. 4. Labor

    He praised the independent farmer and distrusted workshops and a dependent class of workmen. Combinations, hours, and wages were not federal business. Enslaved labor remained the system of his own plantation and of the southern half of his party.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    He returned naturalization toward a shorter residence, undoing the Federalist extension of 1798. He wanted citizens who would become farmers, not a closed gate.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    He signed the act ending the legal importation of enslaved Africans in 1808, the first date the Constitution allowed. He did not propose emancipation. He believed black people and white people could not share a free republic on equal terms, and he looked to colonization as a distant hope. His will freed only a few of the Hemings family. Louisiana was acquired without a rule against slavery in the new country.

  7. 7. Federalism

    In opposition he and Madison had hinted that a state might interpose against a federal law. In office he wanted federal power used softly, except where he chose to use it hard: the embargo’s enforcement acts, and the purchase of Louisiana, which he himself doubted the Constitution authorized.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    The Barbary campaign was a limited use of force to protect trade. The great effort to stay out of the Napoleonic wars was the embargo. It punished American commerce, required harsh enforcement, and did not move Britain or France. He left his successor the failure.

  9. 9. National defense

    He preferred gunboats and militia to a large regular army and a fleet of ships of the line. West Point was founded. The navy that fought in the Mediterranean was small and, for that work, enough. It was not enough to protect neutral trade without the embargo.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    He wrote the Danbury Baptists that a wall of separation stood between church and state. He refused to proclaim national fasts. He did not disturb the establishments that still existed in a few states. Those were state laws.

  11. 11. Education

    He cared more about schools than any president of his generation and still left them to the states and to private founding. The national university was not created. The Military Academy was the federal exception, and it was a school for officers.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    Louisiana doubled the national domain. He also reduced the minimum price and size of a public-land purchase so smaller farmers could buy. Indian nations were pressed, by treaty and by debt, to cede land east of the Mississippi. He spoke of agriculture and practiced expansion.

  13. 13. Energy

    No fuel policy existed. The embargo’s effect on the coast did more to encourage small domestic manufactures than any energy act.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    He pardoned men convicted under the Sedition Act. Enforcement of the embargo created new federal offenses and a coast thick with informers. The slave trade by sea became a federal crime at the constitutional date.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    He distrusted a public debt and a pauper class, and he located relief in families and localities. There was no federal poor law.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    Medicine remained private. The marine hospital arrangement for seamen continued. He proposed nothing further.

Of this presidency

  1. The Louisiana Purchase

    He bought the western watershed of the Mississippi from France in 1803, then asked Congress for an amendment he did not get. The purchase stood anyway. Strict construction yielded to the map.

  2. The embargo

    From December 1807 American foreign trade was stopped by law, in order to avoid war. The coercion fell on American ports. It did not break the Orders in Council or the Berlin and Milan Decrees.

  3. The Barbary War

    He sent the navy to the Mediterranean against Tripoli rather than keep paying tribute. It was a war for the safety of merchant ships, limited and far from Europe.

  4. The end of legal importation

    On the first day the Constitution permitted, the foreign slave trade was prohibited. The act did not touch slavery in the states, and he did not pretend that it did.

PreviousThe Virginia Four NextMarbury and the Twelfth Amendment

1803–1804

Marbury and the Twelfth Amendment

Two changes in Jefferson’s first term reset the machinery of the Constitution. One was a decision about the Court’s own power. The other was an amendment written because the election of 1800 had tied.

Decision

Marbury v. Madison

Decided February 24, 1803

How it was made

William Marbury had been named a justice of the peace in the last hours of Adams’s presidency. His commission was signed and sealed and not delivered. He asked the Supreme Court for a writ ordering Madison, the new secretary of state, to deliver it. The case arrived under a statute that purported to give the Court original jurisdiction to issue such writs.

Who sat

Marshall wrote for a unanimous Court. Moore did not sit. The bench was Adams’s chief justice and Adams’s nephew of Washington, with three of Washington’s appointees.

With the opinion

  • John Marshall — Adams
  • William Cushing — Washington
  • William Paterson — Washington
  • Samuel Chase — Washington
  • Bushrod Washington — Adams

Not sitting

  • Alfred Moore — Adams

What it changed

The Court said Marbury had a right to the commission, and then refused to order delivery. The statute giving the Court that original jurisdiction contradicted Article III, and a statute contrary to the Constitution is not law. The president’s officer was not commanded. The Court’s power to set a federal statute aside was. Judicial review of acts of Congress dates from this refusal.

Amendment

The Twelfth Amendment

Ratified June 15, 1804

How it was made

Under the original Constitution each elector cast two votes for president, and the runner-up became vice president. In 1800 the Republican electors cast equal votes for Jefferson and Burr, and the House had to break the tie. Congress proposed separate ballots for president and vice president. The states ratified the article before the next election.

What it changed

Electors could vote a party ticket without risking a tie between their own two men. The vice presidency stopped being a prize for the defeated candidate. The amendment did not create parties. It made the Constitution fit an election the parties had already produced.

PreviousThomas Jefferson NextJames Madison

1809–1817

James Madison

Democratic-Republican

Before the office

He was born in 1751 at Port Conway and raised at Montpelier in Orange County, a plantation of his father’s, worked by enslaved people, in the Virginia piedmont. He was the oldest son and not the heir of a soldiering tradition. A frail constitution, which his family treated as a bar to the army, kept him out of the war that made Washington and Monroe. He became a politician of paper, committees, and statutes. The body that would not carry a musket carried the Constitution instead.

Princeton, under John Witherspoon, gave him what William and Mary had given Jefferson and something more argumentative. Witherspoon brought the Scottish common-sense philosophy into a college that was already a school for dissenters. Madison studied, stayed beyond the degree, and read himself into a conviction that the mind’s allegiance could not be forced. He went home learned, sickly, and without a profession he loved. The law did not hold him. Public business did.

The Revolution in Virginia was, for him, a problem of government rather than of the field. He sat in the convention that wrote the state’s constitution, then on the Council of State beside Patrick Henry and Jefferson, watching a wartime government that could resolve and could not supply. In the Continental Congress, still in his twenties and thirties, he saw the same failure at a larger scale. The Congress requisitioned. The states did not pay. A republic that could not feed its army or keep its treaties was not yet a government. The lesson was not abstract. He had watched it fail.

Religious liberty was the other half of his education, and it was local. Virginia meant to support teachers of the Christian religion by a tax. He wrote the Memorial and Remonstrance against it. Conscience, he said, was not a thing the majority could assess. The fight helped carry Jefferson’s statute. It also taught him a political method: a principle stated so that ordinary believers, not only philosophers, could see their own danger in the other side’s win. He was never only a theorist. He was a man who counted votes in a pew-filled state.

The Constitution was his answer to the failure he had watched. He came to Philadelphia with a plan for a national government that could tax, that could act on persons and not only on states, and that would divide its own power so that no branch could swallow the rest. He lost pieces of the plan and won the architecture. Then he explained it. In the papers written with Hamilton and Jay he argued that faction is sown into human nature, and that the remedy is not to hope for better men but to extend the sphere and divide the government. He meant it. He also, at the Virginia ratifying convention, promised a bill of rights to men who feared the plan, and in the first Congress he delivered the amendments. The builder of a strong federal government was also the man who wrote the limits.

Office under that government changed his allies and not his premise. He had worked with Hamilton to ratify. He broke with Hamilton over the debt, the bank, and the direction of the new state, and with Jefferson he built a party to oppose them. The break was real, and it was not a rejection of the Constitution he had just written. He thought Hamilton was using the instrument for a British kind of state, funded and connected to commerce and to a court party. He thought the remedy was a different majority, not a different frame. The man who became president in 1809 had already been the Constitution’s draftsman, its explainer, and the organizer of a party against the men who had been his partners in ratification. All three were one life.

In the office

Madison had done more than anyone except Washington to design the Constitution, and he used that frame rather than opening it. The war of 1812 was a new means toward an old end, the rights of a neutral trading republic. After the peace he accepted a bank and a tariff the prewar party would have refused, and he still vetoed a general power over internal improvements.

The party

He inherited Jefferson’s Republican party and its failed embargo. In peace the party still spoke the language of strict construction and small taxes. The war changed what it would sign. By 1816 Republicans had chartered a second national bank and passed a mildly protective tariff, and a wing of them wanted roads and canals on the same national theory. Madison vetoed that last step. The Federalists, identified with opposition to the war and with the Hartford Convention, came out of the peace as a ruined party. Republicanism was no longer a single doctrine. It was a majority that had discovered, in a war, the uses of the government it had once opposed.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    Customs collapsed under blockade, loans came hard, and the debt rose sharply. He did not invent a new tax system equal to the war. After the peace, revenue returned with trade, and the tariff of 1816 was written both to raise money and to shelter manufactures the embargo and the war had called into being.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    He began with non-intercourse and Macon’s Bill, still trying to play one European belligerent against the other. War was the abandonment of that commercial coercion. The peace restored trade without a paper victory on impressment. The tariff that followed was the first clearly protective one the Republican party was willing to pass.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    The First Bank’s charter was allowed to die in 1811. In 1815 he vetoed a wartime bank bill because he thought it would not work, and he set the old constitutional objection aside as already settled by practice. In 1816 he signed the Second Bank. Suspension of specie during the war had shown what the country lacked.

  4. 4. Labor

    The war and the embargo encouraged household and small-shop manufactures. Unions and hours were still not federal questions. Enslaved labor was untouched as a national system.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    The war did not produce a new naturalization policy. Aliens from the enemy country were a military question, not an occasion for a permanent gate.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    He owned enslaved people at Montpelier all his life and proposed no national emancipation. The ban on importation, already law, he treated as law. In private he still hoped for a gradual end joined to colonization, and he did not ask Congress for it. West Florida was occupied without any new rule against slavery.

  7. 7. Federalism

    New England’s resistance to the war, up to the Hartford Convention, was the sharpest state challenge of the term. He did not treat it as treason to be crushed by a new doctrine. His own strictness returned on the last day, when he vetoed the Bonus Bill for roads and canals and told Congress to amend the Constitution if it wanted that power.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    The war with Britain was fought for sailors and ships, and it was badly prepared. He also occupied West Florida on the claim that it had passed with Louisiana. The Treaty of Ghent ended the war without a cession and without a written end to impressment. The practice died because the European war died.

  9. 9. National defense

    He had inherited a reduced army and a gunboat navy. Congress was slow, the land war went badly until younger generals replaced the old, and the public buildings in Washington were burned. Frigates fought well. He left a somewhat larger regular force and a taste for coastal forts, not a military state.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    There was no national church. At Congress’s request he issued proclamations of thanksgiving and of a day of humiliation during the war. In retirement he decided he should not have done even that.

  11. 11. Education

    He urged a university in the District of Columbia. Congress did not establish it. Common schools stayed with the states.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    West Florida was the acquisition of the term. Public land remained a source of revenue and of settlement. The war broke Tecumseh’s confederacy, and cessions followed.

  13. 13. Energy

    No fuel policy was proposed. The relevant scarcity of the war was credit and goods, not a national source of power.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    Wartime treason and trade with the enemy were the federal criminal questions that mattered. He did not build a domestic police. The Hartford men were beaten by the peace and the election, not by prosecutions.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    The poor were a local charge. The war made prices and militia service hard on ordinary households, and the federal government had no relief system to answer that.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    Camp disease killed more soldiers than the set battles. Care of them was an army problem, not a civil medical system. He left no general health law.

Of this presidency

  1. The decision for war

    In June 1812 he asked Congress to declare war on Britain over impressment, seizures, and the Indian war he laid at British agents. It was the first declared war under the Constitution.

  2. The Second Bank

    He signed the charter in April 1816 after the war had shown the Treasury’s weakness without one. The man who had called the first bank unconstitutional treated the question as closed by practice.

  3. The Tariff of 1816

    Duties were raised for revenue and for the protection of new manufactures. A Republican Congress wrote the bill, and he accepted it.

  4. The Bonus Bill veto

    On his last day he refused a bill that would have spent the Bank’s bonus on roads and canals. The war had enlarged what he would sign. It had not, in his view, created a general power of internal improvement.

PreviousMarbury and the Twelfth Amendment NextJames Monroe

1817–1825

James Monroe

Democratic-Republican

Before the office

He was born in 1758 in Westmoreland County, into a minor planting family, not into Jefferson’s library or Washington’s command. His father died when James was sixteen. He entered William and Mary and left it for the war. The education that formed him was the army. At Trenton, the day after Christmas 1776, he was shot through the shoulder while leading a charge against the Hessian guns, and he carried the ball and the scar for the rest of his life. He wintered at Valley Forge. He was a lieutenant who became a captain, and he never forgot that the republic had been won by men standing in the snow, not by a pamphlet.

Jefferson took him up after the fighting and made him a lawyer. The relation was the spine of Monroe’s public life. He was the younger man, loyal, usable, and not the author of the ideas he served. He sat in the Virginia legislature and in the Congress of the Confederation. He saw the western lands and the weakness of a league that could not pay its soldiers. When the Constitution was written without him, he doubted it. At the Virginia convention he voted no. He wanted amendments before he would trust a government that strong. He then lived under the government he had opposed, and he rose inside it. The pattern is the man. He objected, and he served.

Washington sent him to revolutionary France and then recalled him, dissatisfied with a minister who had grown too warm toward the republic he was watching. The recall hurt. Monroe did not become a Federalist to heal it. He became governor of Virginia, and then, with Livingston, one of the envoys who took Napoleon’s offer of Louisiana, a purchase larger than the instructions they had carried. He was a diplomat of persistence more than of brilliance. He believed the safety of the United States lay in getting Europe out of the neighborhood and keeping American territory in American hands.

Under Madison he held the State Department and, in the worst year of the war, the War Department as well. He was in Washington when the British burned the public buildings. The humiliation was personal and national at once. A man who had been wounded at Trenton had to watch the capital fall because the country still could not quite field an army. He came out of that summer convinced that the Union needed a defense worthy of the independence he had fought for as a boy, and that the men of his own generation were the last who had seen both the winning of it and the nearly losing of it again.

Slavery sat under the whole life and was not a puzzle he solved. Highland, his place in Albemarle, was a plantation. He talked, as other Virginians of his kind talked, of colonization, of sending freed people out of the country they had been born in. He did not free the people at Highland as a policy, and he did not think the Union could be asked to do it. The soldier’s loyalty was to the republic he had bled for, which was a republic of the owners. He could hold two thoughts without breaking them against each other: that the Revolution had been about rights, and that the men and women in his own fields were property.

What he carried into the presidency was not a theory on the scale of Madison’s. It was a character. He had been shot for the cause, trained by Jefferson, rebuked by Washington, and trusted again when the war went bad. He believed the revolutionary generation’s last work was to hold the country together, to keep European powers off the continents, and to leave the constitutional settlement about where Madison had placed it. He was the reliable man of a great set. The reliability was the worldview.

In the office

Monroe was the last of the Virginia line, and he kept the settlement Madison had just restated: a federal government sufficient for defense, credit, and foreign commerce, and not possessed of a general power over roads and schools unless the Constitution were amended. Territory and slavery, not party competition, were what his years could not keep quiet.

The party

The Federalists had collapsed, and Monroe’s election met so little organized opposition that the years were called the Era of Good Feelings. One party now held the government, and it was no longer Jefferson’s party of 1801. It contained nationalists who wanted tariffs, a bank, and roads, and old Republicans who did not. Monroe governed with the bank and the tariff already in place, and he sided with the stricter view when Congress claimed a general power over internal improvements. The one-party calm hid a sectional split. Missouri opened it. The party that could not speak with one voice about slavery in the territories was already on the way to breaking in two.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    He inherited the postwar debt and a tariff that produced revenue. He did not launch a new tax system. Spending came down from the war footing. The Panic of 1819, following the Bank’s contraction, was met without a federal relief program.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    The tariff of 1816 remained, and duties were raised again near the end of the term. He treated the tariff as revenue with a protective effect, not as a plan to reconstruct the economy. There was no return to the embargo.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    The Second Bank stayed. Its mismanagement and then its sharp contraction deepened the panic. He did not destroy it and did not master it. Specie payments were a Treasury and Bank problem, not a new monetary constitution.

  4. 4. Labor

    The panic threw men out of work. Combinations of workmen and the law of strikes remained state matters. He offered no federal labor code. The Missouri fight was, among other things, a fight about whether western labor would be enslaved or free.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    No new restriction was enacted. Naturalization stayed as the Republicans had left it. Nativism was not yet a presidential question.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    He was a slaveholder. He signed the Missouri Compromise: Maine free, Missouri permitting slavery, and slavery prohibited in the rest of the Louisiana Purchase north of thirty-six degrees and thirty minutes. He doubted Congress could bind a new state with conditions it had not imposed on the old ones. He accepted the line through the territories. He looked to colonization as a voluntary and distant departure, not as emancipation, and the presidency freed no one at his plantations. Florida was acquired without a rule against slavery.

  7. 7. Federalism

    He held that a general system of roads and canals required an amendment. In 1822 he vetoed a bill that would have had the United States collect tolls and direct repairs on the Cumberland Road. He did not deny that roads were useful. He denied that the Constitution had given him that administration.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    The message of December 2, 1823, later called the Monroe Doctrine, said the United States would not join the wars of Europe and that the Americas were closed to new European colonization. It was a declaration, not a treaty, and it depended on the British fleet more than it admitted. It promised no alliance. Adams-Onís, negotiated in 1819 and ratified in 1821, took Florida from Spain, drew a boundary to the Pacific, and gave up the American claim to Texas.

  9. 9. National defense

    The army was reduced from the war establishment toward a modest regular force and a program of coastal forts. Jackson’s raid into Florida showed how a small army on a frontier could make foreign policy. Monroe did not disown the result. He used it.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    He issued no call for a national church. Religious liberty stayed as his predecessors had left it. The Missouri crisis was political and sectional, not a federal establishment of religion.

  11. 11. Education

    Schools remained with the states. He did not revive the project of a national university into a law.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    Florida was the acquisition. Public-land sales continued to feed the Treasury and the westward movement. Indian nations in the Southeast were under heavier pressure, by treaty and by the Florida campaigns. A general removal act was not yet law.

  13. 13. Energy

    No fuel policy existed. The commerce of the term moved by sail, river, and the first steamboats, none of them a federal energy system.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    Federal criminal law stayed limited. The question of slavery in the territories was fought in Congress, not in a new criminal code. International suppression of the slave trade at sea continued under earlier statutes.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    The panic left debtors to state stay-laws and local charity. He did not create a national relief. That absence was the policy.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    Yellow fever and the ordinary diseases of camps and ports were local or military concerns. There was no federal medical system.

Of this presidency

  1. The acquisition of Florida

    The Adams-Onís Treaty transferred East Florida and confirmed West Florida, at the price of Texas and of claims against Spain. Jackson’s campaign had already carried American troops onto Spanish ground.

  2. The Missouri Compromise

    He signed the acts that paired Maine and Missouri and drew the line of 1820 through the territories. The Senate’s balance was saved for a time. The argument was not.

  3. The Monroe Doctrine

    He separated the United States from the wars of Europe and warned European powers off new colonies in the Americas. The policy was an announcement, backed more by Britain’s navy than by his own.

  4. The Cumberland Road veto

    In 1822 he refused a bill that would have made improvement of the National Road a federal power of administration. Nationalist Republicans wanted the power. He told them to amend the Constitution.

PreviousJames Madison NextMcCulloch and Gibbons

1819–1824

McCulloch and Gibbons

Under Monroe the Court, still led by Adams’s chief justice, gave the national government the reading that a strict construction had resisted: implied powers, and a commerce power that reached navigation between the states.

Decision

McCulloch v. Maryland

Decided March 6, 1819

How it was made

Maryland taxed the notes of the Baltimore branch of the Second Bank. The cashier, McCulloch, refused to pay. The case asked whether Congress could charter the Bank, and whether a state could tax it.

Who sat

Unanimous. Marshall wrote. Jefferson’s and Madison’s appointees joined the chief justice the Republicans had once feared.

With the opinion

  • John Marshall — Adams
  • Bushrod Washington — Adams
  • William Johnson — Jefferson
  • Brockholst Livingston — Jefferson
  • Thomas Todd — Jefferson
  • Gabriel Duvall — Madison
  • Joseph Story — Madison

What it changed

Congress may use means convenient to the powers it is given, and a state may not tax the Bank out of existence. The decision did not invent the Bank, which Congress had already chartered. It settled that the charter was constitutional, and that state power stopped at the instrument. Jackson would later kill the Bank by veto. He did not get this decision overruled.

Decision

Gibbons v. Ogden

Decided March 2, 1824

How it was made

New York had granted a monopoly of steamboats in its waters. Ogden held the state grant. Gibbons ran a competing boat under a federal coasting license. The Court was asked which license controlled.

Who sat

The judgment was unanimous. Johnson agreed and wrote separately.

With the opinion

  • John Marshall — Adams
  • Bushrod Washington — Adams
  • Thomas Todd — Jefferson
  • Gabriel Duvall — Madison
  • Joseph Story — Madison
  • Smith Thompson — Monroe

Concurring

  • William Johnson — Jefferson

What it changed

Commerce among the states includes navigation, and a federal license prevails over a state monopoly on that commerce. The decision did not yet reach manufacturing or local trade. It did mean a state could not close a route Congress had opened. Later Courts would carry the commerce power much further. The starting point is this one.

PreviousJames Monroe NextJohn Quincy Adams

1825–1829

John Quincy Adams

National Republican

Before the office

He was born in 1767 in Braintree, the first son of John and Abigail, and the childhood that formed him was not a childhood in Massachusetts. At ten he crossed the Atlantic with his father. He went to school in Paris and in Leiden. At fourteen he was secretary and interpreter to Francis Dana on a mission to the court of Russia. He dined with Franklin, sat in the company of philosophers, and learned early that nations bargain and that a boy who could hold his tongue and his languages would be of use. The duty was his parents’ gift, and it was also their demand. He was raised to be the continuation of a public life, and he felt the weight of it before he had chosen a profession.

He came home, took a degree at Harvard, and read law because that was what an Adams did when he was not on a mission. He did not love the practice. He loved study, the diary he kept as a discipline, and the sense that a day not improved was a day he would have to answer for. In 1794 Washington sent him to the Netherlands. In London he married Louisa Catherine Johnson, the daughter of an American merchant and an English mother, a woman who would live much of her life in other people’s capitals and who paid, in health and in spirits, for the career he treated as an obligation. The marriage was real. It was not easy. The sons who came of it grew up under a standard several of them could not bear.

The Senate seat from Massachusetts showed the choice he would always make. He had come up among Federalists. When Jefferson’s embargo fell on New England shipping, the Federalists wanted him as their voice against it. He supported the embargo, because he thought the country was being pushed toward a war with Britain and a president had the right to keep American ships out of it. Massachusetts did not forgive him. He left the Senate. The lesson he drew was not that he had misjudged the politics. It was that a party is a poor master for a man who thinks he serves the Union. He would rather be alone than be managed.

The great forming years were the diplomatic ones under Madison and Monroe. He was minister to Russia through Napoleon’s invasion. He was one of the commissioners at Ghent who made the peace that ended the War of 1812 without losing territory. He was minister in London. Then, as secretary of state, he did the work that made him more than his father’s son. The treaty with Spain in 1819 drew a boundary to the Pacific and brought Florida in. The message of 1823, which carried Monroe’s name, was in its argument Adams’s: the Americas were not to be recolonized, and the United States would stay out of Europe’s wars. He believed the republic had a continental future, and he believed that future was the proper work of diplomacy, not of a standing quarrel with the Old World.

He also believed, for most of this life, that the Union as it existed was the thing to be served, slavery included. The man who later fought the gag rule in the House had, as secretary of state, helped to enlarge a republic in which slavery was legal, and he had not made abolition the price of Florida. The antislavery of his old age was serious. It was not the principle on which he had built his career. What had built the career was a colder and older idea, inherited from his parents and practiced in every capital he was sent to. The public good was a duty. Party was a temptation. The Union was the patient. A man who could be useful to it had no right to a private ease.

That idea made him a formidable secretary and an impossible party leader. When the House chose him president in 1825, after an election in which no one had a majority, he named Henry Clay secretary of state. The followers of Jackson called it a bargain. He treated the charge as an insult beneath an answer, which is not the same as an answer. He asked Congress for roads, a national university, and an observatory, and he asked it in language that thanked no faction and flattered no region. The country that was about to belong to Jackson did not want a president who had been a boy in the courts of Europe and who still thought the people should be improved by their government. He was already, before the inauguration, the man who would rather be right in his own diary than be followed.

In the office

Adams kept the commercial republic Monroe had left him, with the Bank in place, customs for revenue, and the ordinary life of the states outside federal hands, and he asked only to use that frame more actively for roads, a university, and protection. The constitutional order did not change. What broke was the old Republican party, under a tariff the planting states denounced as a sectional exaction rather than as an ordinary revenue law.

The party

Monroe’s Democratic-Republicans had no organized opposition left, and they still mixed nationalists who wanted a bank, a tariff, and roads with old Republicans who did not. Adams came from that party, but the election of 1824 broke it. His followers, called National Republicans, stood for the Bank, protection, and a national system of internal improvements, and their strength lay in New England and the commercial towns. The men who organized against him as Democrats drew the South and much of the West, and they treated that national program as a sectional favor to manufacturers and to men of money.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    The tariff supplied the revenue, and the debt continued to fall. He proposed no internal taxes and no general spending on poor relief or schools. A planned system of internal improvements was what he asked of Congress, and Congress mostly declined.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    He treated protection as national policy, not as an accident of a revenue law. In 1828 he signed the high tariff the South called the Tariff of Abominations. He wanted foreign trade opened by reciprocal treaties, refused a one-sided opening of the British West Indies, and left that colonial trade closed.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    The Second Bank continued, and he did not disturb its charter. He treated a national bank and a stable currency as settled machinery of the Treasury. Destruction of the Bank was his successor’s work, not his.

  4. 4. Labor

    Hours, wages, and combinations of workmen were not federal questions. Workingmen’s parties were beginning in a few cities, and they remained local. He offered no labor code.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    Naturalization stayed at the five-year residence fixed in 1802. Immigration was rising, and nativism was not yet a policy of the presidency. He enacted no new restriction.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    He was not a slaveholder, and he put no plan of emancipation before Congress. Slavery remained with the states, and the Missouri line remained the rule in the Louisiana Purchase. In the Indian country he tried to undo a fraudulent Creek cession and bargain again, while Georgia demanded the land at once and he did not make war on the state to stop it.

  7. 7. Federalism

    He held that Congress could build roads and canals of national character and could found a university, a step past Monroe, who had wanted an amendment before a general system of improvements. He did not claim a power to reorder slavery or the police of the states. When Georgia defied his Creek settlement, he left coercion of a state to Congress rather than to troops.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    He sought commercial treaties on reciprocal terms and held out, without success, for an equal trade with the British West Indies. He asked to send ministers to the congress of American states at Panama; Southern opposition, much of it from fear that Haiti and slavery would be discussed, delayed them, and the mission missed the meeting. He made no permanent alliance and fought no foreign war.

  9. 9. National defense

    The army and navy stayed on the modest peacetime footing Monroe had accepted, with coastal forts and a squadron against pirates in the Caribbean. He recommended a naval academy. Congress did not establish one.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    The federal government still had no religious establishment. Petitions to stop the Sunday mail were already arriving, and he did not turn the Post Office over to the Sabbath. Bible and tract societies worked outside the government.

  11. 11. Education

    In his first annual message he asked for a national university and for observatories. Congress refused both. Common schools remained with the states, the towns, and the churches.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    Public land continued to be surveyed and sold, and he preferred the proceeds for the debt and for national works rather than a rush to give the land away. He did not sign a general Indian removal law. Creek land in Georgia was the fight of the term, conducted by treaty and by a state’s defiance of the revised treaty.

  13. 13. Energy
