    Wood remained the ordinary fuel, and coal was a private trade just beginning to matter in Eastern forges and cities. Canals, the Erie among them, were works of states and companies. There was no federal fuel policy.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    The federal courts continued under the old judiciary acts. Piracy and the illegal African slave trade were the maritime offenses the navy still pursued. He created no new criminal code for the states.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    Relief of the poor sat with towns, churches, and local poorhouses. The federal government had no welfare system. He did not propose one.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    There was no federal medical system for the public. The marine hospitals for seamen, paid for by a charge on their wages, remained the narrow exception. Epidemics were left to cities and ports.

Of this presidency

  1. Election by the House

    No candidate won an electoral majority in 1824, and the House chose Adams under the Twelfth Amendment. He then named Henry Clay secretary of state. Jackson’s men called it a corrupt bargain. No proof of a sale of the office was produced, and the charge defined the term anyway.

  2. The program Congress would not pass

    He asked for a connected system of roads and canals, a national university, and an observatory, and he thought the Constitution already allowed them. Congress granted surveys and pieces of road. It did not grant the system, and it did not amend the Constitution to do so.

  3. The Tariff of 1828

    He signed the protective tariff of that year. Planting states called it the Tariff of Abominations and treated it as a tax laid on them for the benefit of Northern mills. It was still a tariff inside the old revenue system, and it was the grievance that made the next decade’s nullification fight.

  4. Georgia and the Creek treaties

    A Creek cession obtained through a faction, and stained by fraud, was set aside, and a new treaty restored part of what had been taken while still yielding a large tract. Georgia surveyed the country anyway and dared the government to interfere. Adams did not send an army against the state, and removal as a single national law was not yet his.

  5. The Panama mission

    He accepted an invitation to the congress Bolívar had called at Panama, a meeting of the new American states. The Senate fought the mission, in part because Haiti might be present and slavery might be discussed. The delegates did not arrive in time, and nothing was signed.

PreviousMcCulloch and Gibbons NextAndrew Jackson

1829–1837

Andrew Jackson

Democrat

Before the office

He was born in 1767 in the Waxhaws, on the border of the Carolinas, after his father was already dead. There was no estate and no college in the prospect. The Revolution took what family he had. One brother died in the fighting. He and the other were taken by the British, and when he refused to clean an officer’s boots the officer struck him with a sword, across the hand and the head. He carried the scars, and the hatred, for life. The brother died of the illness they caught in captivity. His mother went to Charleston to nurse other prisoners on the prison ships and died of the fever. At fourteen he was an orphan who had been cut by an empire and left with nothing to inherit but the insult.

He read law in Salisbury because the law was a trade an ambitious boy could enter without a family. He went west, to the settlements that became Tennessee, and he made himself useful as a prosecutor, a delegate to the convention that wrote the state constitution, a congressman, a senator, and a judge. He also speculated in land, which was how a man on that frontier got ahead, and he dueled, which was how a man on that frontier answered a slight. In 1806 he killed Charles Dickinson, a better shot who had insulted him, and he took a ball in the chest that stayed there. Honor, for him, was not Washington’s drilled self-command. It was the willingness to be shot and to shoot.

He married Rachel Donelson Robards before her divorce from her first husband was complete, and when the error was known they married again. He loved her. He treated every public mention of the marriage as an attack that had to be answered, if necessary with a pistol. The slander followed them to the White House, and she died, of a broken heart as he understood it, before he took the oath. The private rage and the public career were not separate. He believed a man’s name was his property, and that a government, like a man, had a right to answer an insult.

The Hermitage was a cotton plantation. The labor was enslaved. He was not a Virginia philosopher writing his way around the fact. He was a frontier planter and a buyer and seller of human beings who regarded the institution as part of the order he had climbed into. Nothing in his rise made him doubt it. The democracy he came to speak for was a democracy of white men, and the men at the bottom of it were still, in his view, above the people he owned and above the Indian nations on the land his constituents wanted.

The army made him a national figure in a way the Tennessee judgeship never could. In the Creek War he broke the Red Sticks at Horseshoe Bend and dictated a cession of land that reached far beyond the nation he had fought. At New Orleans in January 1815 he commanded a mixed force of regulars, militia, free black men, and Barataria sailors, and he shattered a British column. The peace had already been signed. The news had not arrived. The country did not care about the sequence. It had a victory and a general who had produced it by will. He then carried a campaign into Florida, hanged British subjects he believed were arming the Seminoles, and dared the administration to disown him. Monroe’s cabinet, with Adams defending the seizure, did not. Jackson learned that audacity, if it succeeded, would be ratified.

He ran for president in 1824 and lost in the House, though he had led the popular vote. He believed the office had been given away by a bargain between Adams and Clay. Whether the bargain was real mattered less, for the man he became, than his certainty that the people had been cheated and that he was the people. The worldview that entered the presidency in 1829 had been formed by the sword cut, the duel, the plantation, the victory at New Orleans, and that defeat. The Union was absolute. The white settler was its citizen. A national bank, a protective tariff for manufacturers, and an Indian nation holding land inside a state were, to him, the arrangements of men who had never been poor and never been struck. An executive who came from the voters could break those arrangements. A state that tried to break the Union could not.

In the office

Jackson governed inside the same Constitution: customs rather than an income tax, no federal system of schools or poor relief, no established church, and no permanent alliance. The bank, the Indian nations, and nullification were not differences of degree. He destroyed the national bank, signed a law to remove the Eastern nations across the Mississippi, and answered South Carolina’s ordinance with the claim that the Union was a government and not a league.

The party

Adams’s National Republicans were the improving wing of the old Republican party: the Bank, a protective tariff, and roads, with their strength in New England and the commercial towns. Jackson’s Democrats organized against that wing and gave the opposition a new name, joining Southern planters, Western farmers, and many urban workingmen against the Bank, a high tariff, and federal money for local improvements. They treated a strong executive as the check on those interests, which Adams’s more congressional nationalism did not. It was a party built to defeat the program Adams had asked for, and at the same time to deny that any state could nullify a federal law.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    The tariff remained the federal tax, and he cut at aid to projects he judged merely local. In 1835 the debt was paid off. He then signed a law depositing the Treasury’s surplus with the states, a distribution of money rather than a new establishment of spending.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    He accepted that the tariff of 1828 had to come down. The tariff of 1832 lowered it without satisfying South Carolina, and the compromise tariff of 1833 scheduled a gradual return toward a revenue standard. Protection survived as a declining schedule, not as Adams’s program.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    He vetoed recharter of the Second Bank in 1832, removed the federal deposits in 1833 into selected state banks, and in 1836 required gold or silver for purchases of public land. The federal charter expired in 1836. He left no national bank behind him.

  4. 4. Labor

    No federal law set hours or recognized unions. A ten-hour practice was won in a few public yards by petition, not by a national code. His quarrel with the Bank was fought as a quarrel with privilege, not as a labor program.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    He left the five-year naturalization rule in place and proposed no nativist restriction. Immigration from Ireland and Germany was increasing. It was not yet an issue on which his party governed.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    He was a slaveholder. He asked Congress to stop abolitionist papers in the Southern mail, which it did not, and near the end of his term the House began to table antislavery petitions. The statute that was his own was the Indian Removal Act of 1830, authorizing the exchange of Eastern lands for country west of the Mississippi, and he did not use the Cherokee decisions of the Supreme Court to restrain Georgia.

  7. 7. Federalism

    Against nullification he was a nationalist. His proclamation of December 1832 denied that a state could annul a federal law, and he signed the Force Bill so the customs could be collected. Against the Bank and against local roads he was a strict constructionist: the Maysville Road veto of 1830 refused a federal subscription to a road lying wholly inside one state.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    He fought no war with a European power. He pressed France, short of war, for payment of old spoliation claims, and he accepted a limited reopening of trade with the British West Indies on terms Adams had refused. He recognized the Republic of Texas on his last day in office and did not annex it.

  9. 9. National defense

    The regular army stayed small except where removal, and the Second Seminole War that began in 1835, put troops in the field. The navy remained a force for the protection of commerce. Coastal forts continued without a new military system.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    The federal government still had no establishment. Congressional reports in his term refused to halt the Sunday mail, treating that halt as a preference among sects. Temperance, Bible, and antislavery societies remained voluntary, and he did not adopt them as policy.

  11. 11. Education

    He did not revive Adams’s national university. Schools stayed with states, towns, and churches. The federal government took no charge of them.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    Removal was a land policy as well as an Indian policy. It cleared, in the government’s view, title for white settlement and for the speculations that followed. Public-land sales swelled, the specie order of 1836 was his answer to payment in bank paper, and he did not adopt a homestead law.

  13. 13. Energy

    Wood and, in the East, anthracite coal did the heating and the mill work, and both were private trades. Canals and the first railroads were enterprises of states and companies. He had no fuel policy.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    Federal criminal jurisdiction stayed narrow: the courts, the customs, the post, and the illegal foreign slave trade. He wanted the mails closed to antislavery publications and did not get a statute for it. The Cherokee controversy was left to treaties, to Georgia’s laws, and to the pressure of removal, not to a new criminal code.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    There was no federal relief of poverty. Towns, churches, and county poorhouses did that work. Neither the Bank War nor the land boom produced a national provision for men thrown out of work.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    Cholera struck the cities in 1832. Quarantine and nursing were municipal. The marine hospitals for seamen continued, and they were not a medical system for the public.

Of this presidency

  1. The Bank War

    Congress passed a recharter of the Second Bank in 1832, and he vetoed it as unconstitutional and as a monopoly. He then removed the federal deposits, dismissing a treasury secretary who refused, and placed them in selected state banks. The Senate censured him in 1834 and expunged the censure before he left office. The federal charter was allowed to die.

  2. The Indian Removal Act

    The act of 1830 authorized treaties exchanging Eastern homelands for land west of the Mississippi. Under that pressure the Choctaw, Creek, Chickasaw, and Cherokee were pushed toward removal, and the Supreme Court’s decision against Georgia’s power inside the Cherokee Nation did not move him to protect that nation. A treaty with a Cherokee faction was accepted before he left. The march that followed was carried out by his successor.

  3. Nullification

    South Carolina’s ordinance of 1832 declared the tariffs of 1828 and 1832 void inside the state. His proclamation denied the right, and the Force Bill authorized collection of the duties by military means if necessary. The same Congress passed Clay’s compromise tariff, scaling protection down over the decade. South Carolina withdrew its ordinance and, as a gesture, purported to nullify the Force Bill.

  4. The debt, the surplus, and the Specie Circular

    The debt inherited from the War of 1812 was paid off in 1835, and a surplus remained. Congress provided for depositing that surplus with the states. In 1836 he ordered that large purchases of public land be paid in gold or silver, a blow at bank paper that landed on the next administration when the panic came.

  5. Texas recognized, not annexed

    The Republic of Texas, independent of Mexico since 1836 and committed to slavery, asked to enter the Union. He did not annex it. On his last day he recognized its independence and left the question of admission to the men who followed.

PreviousJohn Quincy Adams NextWorcester v. Georgia

1832

Worcester v. Georgia

The Court held that Georgia’s laws did not govern the Cherokee Nation. The decision altered the legal relation of state, tribe, and Union. It did not alter the removal that followed, because the executive did not carry the judgment into Georgia.

Decision

Worcester v. Georgia

Decided March 3, 1832

How it was made

Georgia required a state license of white men living in the Cherokee country, and imprisoned Samuel Worcester, a missionary, for refusing it. He brought the conviction to the Supreme Court on a writ of error. A year earlier, in Cherokee Nation v. Georgia, the Court had turned the tribe itself away, calling it a domestic dependent nation and not a foreign state that could sue.

Who sat

The judgment was five to one. Johnson took no part. Jackson’s two appointees split: McLean with the Court, Baldwin against it.

With the opinion

  • John Marshall — Adams
  • Gabriel Duvall — Madison
  • Joseph Story — Madison
  • Smith Thompson — Monroe

Concurring

  • John McLean — Jackson

In dissent

  • Henry Baldwin — Jackson

Not sitting

  • William Johnson — Jefferson

What it changed

The Cherokee Nation was a political community whose land and self-government a state could not seize by statute. Treaties and the federal power over Indian affairs controlled. The legal change was real. The practical change was not. Georgia did not yield, and Jackson did not use the executive to make it yield. He wrote that the decision had fallen stillborn and could not coerce the state. Removal went on. A judgment of the Court is not, by itself, an act of government.

PreviousAndrew Jackson NextMartin Van Buren

1837–1841

Martin Van Buren

Democrat

Before the office

He was born in 1782 at Kinderhook, on the post road between New York and Albany, the son of Abraham Van Buren, who kept a tavern and a farm. The house was Dutch. The language of the table was Dutch, and English was the language he learned for the law and the county. Strangers came through the door every day, talked politics, and went on. He did not inherit a plantation or a commission. He inherited a room in which men already argued about offices. The profession he later invented had a counter in it before it had a theory.

There was no college. He read law with Francis Silvester in Kinderhook, a Federalist, and then with William P. Van Ness in New York, and he was admitted to the bar in 1803. The training was useful and it was also an education in the other party. He saw how a Federalist household held its clients and its scorn, and he went the other way. He married Hannah Hoes in 1807, a cousin from the same Dutch world, and he loved her. She died in 1819. He did not marry again. The private life after that was his sons and the party. There was no camp and no campaign of the sword in it.

New York politics made him. He rose through the surrogate’s office, the state senate, and the attorney generalship, and he learned that a man who could be heard in a parlor could not always be heard in a hall. The voice was small. The organization was not. Against DeWitt Clinton’s personal following he helped build the Bucktails, and out of them the Albany Regency: a set of men, a newspaper, and a rule that the party, not the great name, would say who held the offices. Patronage was not a scandal to him. It was how a republican party stayed a party between elections, instead of dissolving into the ambitions of its leaders.

He took that New York machine into national politics with a purpose he wrote down. In 1827 he told Thomas Ritchie of Virginia that the old party feelings were better than new ones, and that the natural combination was the planters of the South with the plain Republicans of the North. Jackson’s popularity could carry it. A convention could name it. If that combination failed, he said, the country would divide by geography, and by the prejudice between free states and slaveholding states. The party was not, in his mind, a substitute for principle. It was the arrangement that kept slavery from becoming the only principle anyone would vote on. He would rather revive the old Republican quarrel with Federalism than fight the new quarrel over the peculiar institution.

Jackson’s first term showed what the arrangement required of the man who kept it. As secretary of state he stood with the president in the quarrel over Peggy Eaton, when the cabinet wives would not receive her, and the standing bound him to Jackson and helped finish Calhoun. The Senate, with Calhoun’s casting vote, refused to confirm him as minister to Britain. Jackson made the insult a nomination to the vice presidency. By 1835 the Democracy had a national convention, a two-thirds rule, and a successor chosen by the party rather than by a congressional caucus or a hero’s nod alone. He had done what Washington had warned against. He had made the spirit of party into a system, and he thought the system was what held the Union together.

What he carried into the presidency was that system, and a temperament to match it. He was not a soldier and not a constitution-maker. He was a lawyer from a Dutch tavern who believed offices should be filled by the organization that won, that the government should not be a bank, and that a northern man could lead a southern party if he did not touch slavery in the states. The panic was not yet the fact of his life when he took the oath. The party was. He had spent thirty years building it so that a president could be a politician and still be legitimate.

In the office

Van Buren accepted Jackson’s constitutional frame and Jackson’s refusals: no new national bank, no federal assumption of state debts, no annexation of Texas, and no retreat from Indian removal. The panic made those refusals the center of domestic policy. The independent treasury was a harder version of the same quarrel over money, not a different kind of republic, and the forced removal of the Cherokee was the part of his inheritance that was not a matter of degree.

The party

He was Jackson’s Democrat, nominated by the party Jackson had built, and he did not restore the National Republican program of a bank and a high tariff. What shifted inside the Democracy was the Treasury: Jackson had placed federal money in favored state banks, and Van Buren, facing the Panic of 1837, set out to separate the government from every bank and keep its funds in its own hands. The coalition of South, West, and the urban Democracy held together. The depression gave the Whigs their national opening.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    Revenue fell with the panic, and the surplus already paid over to the states was not brought back. He would not assume the debts of states that had borrowed for canals and banks, and he would not lay an internal tax to fight the depression. Retrenchment, under the descending tariff of 1833, was his fiscal policy.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    He left the compromise tariff of 1833 on its downward path and sought no new protective system. Commercial treaties remained the ordinary foreign business. The quarrel with Britain was over the Canadian border, not over a new schedule of duties.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    He called Congress into special session in 1837 and refused both a new national bank and a return to selected state banks as the home of federal funds. Congress repealed the Specie Circular in 1838. In 1840 he signed the Independent Treasury Act, taking the government’s money into its own custody and pointing its receipts and payments toward specie.

  4. 4. Labor

    Unions and hours in private employment remained with the states. On March 31, 1840, he ordered that laborers and mechanics on federal public works be held to the ten-hour day. It was a rule for the government’s own yards, not a national hours statute.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    He did not change naturalization, and the panic checked newcomers without producing a federal restriction. Nativist politics were still local. He made no policy of exclusion.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    He was not a slaveholder, and he carried out the removal Jackson had authorized. In 1838 the army forced the Cherokee west on the journey later called the Trail of Tears, and many died on it. He treated the captive Africans of the Amistad as property to be given up, and he opposed annexing Texas while it would enlarge slaveholding country and risk war with Mexico.

  7. 7. Federalism

    The independent treasury was national custody of the government’s own money, not a national bank and not a national rescue of the states. He would not have Washington assume state debts. Slavery, debtor relief, and ordinary police stayed with the states, except where removal and the Seminole War put federal troops on the ground.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    After British forces burned the steamer Caroline, which had been supplying Canadian rebels, he protested and did not declare war. He sent General Scott to quiet the Aroostook confrontation on the Maine border in 1839 and left the boundary to a later treaty. He refused to annex Texas.

  9. 9. National defense

    The Second Seminole War, inherited and unfinished, was the military cost of the term. The rest of the army, and the navy, stayed small. He did not arm for a war with Britain or with Mexico.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    The federal government still had no religious establishment. He made no Sabbath rule for the mail and sponsored no church. The reform societies remained outside the administration.

  11. 11. Education

    Schools stayed with the states and the churches. He proposed no national university. He signed no grant of public land for a common-school system.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    The land boom collapsed with the panic, and sales did not return to the pitch of Jackson’s last years. Congress repealed the requirement that public land be paid for in specie. He founded no new land system, and he did not stop removal from opening Eastern acres to white purchase.

  13. 13. Energy

    Steamboat boilers, not a supply of fuel, were the federal contact with the new power. After fatal explosions, the steamboat act of 1838 required inspection of hulls and boilers. Wood and coal remained private trades, carried on canals and rivers the United States did not operate.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    The structure of federal justice was unchanged. Removal and the Seminole War were military, not a new criminal code. On the Amistad his administration took the side of the Spanish claimants, and the Supreme Court freed the captives only after he had left office.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    The panic was a national economic fact and a local charitable one. He proposed no federal relief for the unemployed and no assumption of debts for their sake. Towns, churches, and the poorhouse remained the provision.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    There was still no federal medical system for the public. Marine hospitals served seamen. Sickness on the removal marches was left to the army and to the people on the road.

Of this presidency

  1. The Panic of 1837

    Banks suspended specie payments within weeks of his inauguration. He called a special session and blamed an excess of bank paper, together with the disturbances of foreign credit. He offered no new national bank and no federal rescue of debtors or of the states.

  2. The independent treasury

    He asked Congress to divorce the Treasury from all banks, national or state, and to keep public money in public vaults. The act passed in 1840, after three years of defeat, and it moved federal receipts and payments toward specie. The Whigs repealed it the year he left office.

  3. The Trail of Tears

    Under the Treaty of New Echota, which a Cherokee minority had signed and the Senate had accepted, he sent the army to remove the Cherokee Nation. In 1838 General Scott carried the removal through. The journey west killed many, and it was a federal act, not a private migration.

  4. The Caroline and the Maine border

    The burning of the Caroline in 1837, with an American killed, brought the United States and Britain close to a fight over the Canadian rebellions. He answered with protest and neutrality, not with war. In the Aroostook country he used Scott as a peacemaker and left the map to be drawn by the next treaty with Britain.

  5. The Amistad

    In 1839 captive Africans seized the Spanish schooner Amistad, and the ship came into American custody. His administration argued that they should be delivered up under the treaty with Spain, and it prepared to send them away if the courts agreed. He was out of office when the Supreme Court held them free. The presidency had taken the side of the claimants, not of emancipation.

PreviousWorcester v. Georgia NextWilliam Henry Harrison

1841

William Henry Harrison

Whig

Before the office

He was born in 1773 at Berkeley, on the James River, the son of Benjamin Harrison, who signed the Declaration and governed Virginia. This was not Jackson’s orphaning and not Van Buren’s tavern. It was a planter’s house, enslaved labor under it, and a name that opened doors. He was sent toward medicine, at Hampden-Sydney and then in Philadelphia under Benjamin Rush. His father died in 1791. The estate and the debts ended the plan. He took a commission instead, an ensign’s, and went to the Northwest, where the republic was still an army arguing with the tribes over the land north of the Ohio.

He served under Anthony Wayne. At Fallen Timbers in 1794 he was a young aide in the battle that broke the Indian confederacy of the lakes and opened Ohio to settlement. The next year he married Anna Symmes, the daughter of the speculator and judge who held so much of the Miami country, and he married her before her father had agreed to it. The marriage lasted. The career that followed was the career of a territorial man: secretary of the Northwest Territory, delegate in Congress, and then, from 1801, governor of Indiana Territory, appointed by John Adams and left in the office by Jefferson. He was a gentleman acting as the agent of a settler government.

The governorship was a long purchase of land. Treaty after treaty, at Vincennes and Grouseland and elsewhere, transferred millions of acres from the tribes to the United States, often from chiefs who did not speak for the nations Tecumseh said could not sell what belonged to all of them. Harrison treated the treaties as law and the resistance as a British-encouraged conspiracy. In November 1811, while Tecumseh was away in the South, he marched on Prophetstown. The fight at Tippecanoe was confused and costly, and he burned the town. He came out of it with the name the country would still be using thirty years later. Old Tippecanoe was a title made in one morning on the Wabash.

The war completed it. After Hull surrendered Detroit, Kentucky and the West wanted a commander who would go north. Harrison took the militia, then a regular commission, and in October 1813 he won at the Thames, where Tecumseh was killed. He resigned when the secretary of war slighted him, and went home to North Bend, on the Ohio, below Cincinnati. Congress, the Senate, and a short mission to Colombia followed. Jackson recalled him from Bogota. He was, by then, a retired general with a large family, debts, and a fame that was real and also old. The man was a Virginia Harrison who had become an Ohio farmer of more pride than cash. He was not a theorist of the Constitution. He was a soldier who thought the tribes would give way, that Britain had armed them, and that a general who had won should be treated with respect.

The Whigs found a use for the respect. In 1836 he was one of several candidates against Van Buren and he lost. In 1840 he was the only one, and the party that wanted a bank, a tariff, and an end to Jackson’s kind of presidency sold him as a man of a log cabin and hard cider. The cabin was an insult they turned into a picture. He had been born at Berkeley. He let the picture stand, because he wanted the office and because the party, which Van Buren had taught the country how to build, was now better at the art than Van Buren was. What Harrison himself believed, and said when he finally spoke for himself, was closer to the old republican fear of the executive than to the cider. Congress should legislate. The veto was a conservative power, not a way to run the country. He would not serve a second term.

He took a cold at the inauguration, speaking for the better part of two hours without a coat equal to the day, and he died a month later of the illness that followed. The life that formed him was already complete: the signer’s son, Wayne’s aide, the governor who bought the Indian land, the general of the Thames, and at the last a candidate who allowed a party to describe him as someone he had not been. He did not live to sign the program that party had won the election to pass.

In the office

Harrison inherited Van Buren’s depression and a Whig promise to answer it with a bank and a tariff, still inside the commercial republic of customs, states, and no federal care of the poor. He held the office for one month. He formed a cabinet, called Congress into special session, and died, so that neither the Whig program nor the question of succession was settled by any statute of his own.

The party

Van Buren’s Democrats stood for the independent treasury, separation from a national bank, and the descending tariff of 1833, and the panic had made that record unpopular. Harrison’s Whigs were the party formed to defeat that Democracy, joining National Republicans who wanted a bank, protection, and distribution of the land revenue with Southerners who resented Jackson’s executive. The campaign of 1840 offered log cabins and hard cider rather than a published platform. The legislative program waiting in Congress was Clay’s American System, and Harrison died before any piece of it reached his desk.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    He inherited thin revenue and a Whig demand for a new financial arrangement, and he lived to enact neither a tax nor an appropriation of his own. His inaugural said he would leave revenue measures to Congress and use the veto sparingly. The special session he summoned was to meet at the end of May, after his death.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    He inherited the low schedule of the compromise tariff and the Whig intention to restore protection. No tariff bill reached him. Foreign commerce stood as Van Buren had left it.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    The Whigs meant to repeal the independent treasury and to charter a new national bank. He signed neither measure. He died with the Democratic monetary settlement still the law.

  4. 4. Labor

    The ten-hour rule on federal works remained an executive practice, and he did not revisit it. Unions and hours in private employment were still matters for the states. One month did not make them national.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    He proposed no change in naturalization. Nativist feeling was growing in the cities. It was not a policy of this term.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    He had owned enslaved people, and the Whig coalition that elected him contained both slaveholders and men who disliked the spread of slavery. He offered no bill on slavery, on the territories, or on Indian policy. The Seminole War was still open, and he did not redirect it.

  7. 7. Federalism

    Whig doctrine in 1841 preferred Congress to a Jacksonian executive, and his inaugural accepted that modesty. He drew no new line between the nation and the states. The line was about to be drawn by the vice president, not by him.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    He made no alliance and sent no expedition. The Maine boundary and the Caroline affair remained unsettled, in Webster’s hands at the State Department. Nothing was signed in the month he served.

  9. 9. National defense

    The army and navy were the establishments he inherited, with the war in Florida not yet closed. He authorized no new force. His death was not a military event.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    The federal government still had no religious establishment, and the month produced no moral statute. He was inaugurated with the usual public invocation. That was custom, not a church.

  11. 11. Education

    Schools remained with states, towns, and churches. He proposed no national university. He signed no school law.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    Distribution of land revenue and the claims of settlers were questions for the session he did not live to meet. No land act carries his signature. Preemption was not yet law.

  13. 13. Energy

    Wood, coal, and steam continued as private trades and as works of the states. He established no fuel policy. The steamboat-inspection law of 1838 stayed on the books, untouched.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    The federal courts sat as before. He created no offense and issued no policy of pardons that altered the law. Suppression of the foreign slave trade continued under statutes already in force.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    The poor of the depression remained the charge of towns, churches, and counties. He proposed no federal relief. His death did not change where that burden sat.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    He died in the White House on April 4, 1841, of an illness then identified as pneumonia, a month after taking the oath. There was no federal medical system that could have made the fact different. Care of the sick public stayed local, and the marine hospitals stayed a service for seamen.

Of this presidency

  1. A limited executive, promised

    His inaugural, nearly two hours in a cold rain, pledged one term, a sparing use of the veto, and deference to Congress on revenue. That was the Whig answer to Jackson’s presidency, stated as intention. He signed nothing that carried it into practice.

  2. The cabinet and the special session

    He appointed a Whig cabinet with Daniel Webster at the State Department and, on March 17, called Congress to meet on May 31 to deal with revenue and finance. Clay, who led the party in Congress and was not in the cabinet, expected that session to restore the bank and the tariff. Harrison was dead before it convened.

  3. Death in office

    He took the oath on March 4 and died on April 4, the first president to die in the office. The cause was recorded as pneumonia. No statute, treaty, or veto bears his administration’s mark beyond the summons to Congress and the appointments of the first weeks.

  4. The succession he did not define

    The Constitution said the powers and duties of the office should devolve on the vice president. It did not say, in terms that had ever been tested, whether that man became president or only acted as one. Harrison’s death put the question. His successor answered it.

PreviousMartin Van Buren NextJohn Tyler

1841–1845

John Tyler

Whig

Before the office

He was born in 1790 at Greenway, in Charles City County, the son of another John Tyler, who was governor of Virginia and a Jeffersonian of the old kind. The house was a planter’s house. The labor was enslaved. The politics of the table were states’ rights, a suspicion of banks, and a belief that Virginia was a commonwealth and not a county of some national improvement scheme. He went to William and Mary, read law, and was admitted to the bar before he was twenty. In 1813 he married Letitia Christian. She was the wife of his private life and, later, of a White House she was too ill to preside over. The public man was already set in the direction of his father.

He moved through the offices Virginia gave a man of that name: the legislature, the national House, the governorship, the Senate. At each of them he voted as a strict constructionist. He opposed the national bank, federal roads and canals, and a protective tariff understood as a favor to manufacturers. He was not a dissenter from the Virginia line of Jefferson and Madison. He was a younger keeper of it, at a time when that line was losing its hold on the men who called themselves Republicans and were becoming Democrats under Jackson.

Jackson broke him from the party of his fathers without making him a Whig of Clay’s sort. He opposed the proclamation against South Carolina and the Force Bill that followed it, and in the Senate he cast the lone vote against that bill. He could tolerate a quarrel with a tariff. He could not tolerate a president using the army to collect one. In 1836 the Virginia legislature instructed its senators to vote for the expunging of the Senate’s censure of Jackson. He believed a senator ought to respect such an instruction, and he would not cast the vote. He resigned. The resignation was the man: a doctrine of instruction, a doctrine of states’ rights, and a refusal to let either be convenient.

The Whigs took him because he had left Jackson, not because he had come to them. He was a southern balance on a ticket whose other name was a northern military hero, and whose campaign said almost nothing about a bank. He did not think he was signing Clay’s program by accepting the vice presidency. He thought he was standing against the executive Jackson had made. Letitia had already suffered a stroke. He cannot have expected the office he was about to inherit. The theory he carried, if the man above him died, was not in doubt in his own mind. The vice president who succeeded would be president. The Constitution said the office devolved on him. It did not say he became a clerk of the dead man’s cabinet.

Under that theory sat an older belief about the country, which was not the belief of a man preparing to be a figurehead. The Union he meant to keep was a Union of states that governed slavery themselves, and a Union that could still grow. Texas, already independent and already a slaveholding republic, was in his view a natural piece of that growth, not a rupture. He did not think expansion was a gift to the North’s manufacturers or a project of a national bank. He thought it was how a planting republic stayed a planting republic instead of being cornered by the free states. The accident of Harrison’s death did not give him that map. Virginia had.

When the news came he was at Williamsburg. He reached Washington, took the oath, and met a cabinet that had been allowed, under Harrison, to settle questions by a majority of its own members. He told them he would hear their advice and would not be dictated to. He was president, and the administration would be his. The sentence was the branch he had been growing toward for twenty years: a strict reading of the Constitution, used, at the moment it mattered, to claim the whole office rather than a share of it. He had no party that agreed with both halves of that claim. He had not had one before he took the oath.

In the office

Tyler took the office in full on Harrison’s death and then used the veto to defeat the national bank the Whigs had won the election to restore. He still governed a commercial republic of tariffs, land sales, and state control of slavery, schools, and the poor. The acts that were not differences of degree were those vetoes, which drove him out of his party, and the annexation of Texas, which added a slaveholding republic by joint resolution after a treaty had failed.

The party

Harrison’s Whigs had won the election Tyler inherited, and their leaders expected a president who would sign a bank, a protective tariff, and a distribution of the land revenue. Tyler had come into that party as a Virginia man of states’ rights, opposed to Jackson’s force against South Carolina and to Jackson’s way of using the executive, not as a convert to Clay’s American System. When he vetoed the bank bills, the congressional Whigs expelled him, and the cabinet he had inherited resigned, Webster excepted. He finished the term with no party, taking Whig votes when he agreed with them and Democratic votes when the question was Texas.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    He inherited a deficit and the Whig plan to pair a higher tariff with distribution of land revenue to the states. He vetoed tariff bills that kept distribution alive on terms he rejected, and in 1842 he signed a protective tariff under which that distribution stopped. Customs, not a new internal tax, remained the revenue.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    The compromise tariff of 1833 had run down to its low point, and the tariff of 1842 raised duties and restored protection, which he accepted once the quarrel over land revenue was settled his way. The Webster-Ashburton Treaty quieted a territorial dispute with Britain. It did not remake the tariff.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    He signed the repeal of Van Buren’s independent treasury at the special session of 1841, and federal money went back into state banks. Twice that year Congress passed a national bank, the second time as a fiscal corporation in the District of Columbia, and twice he vetoed it. He would not charter the institution Clay required.

  4. 4. Labor

    No national labor law was proposed or passed. The ten-hour practice on federal works continued without new legislation. Hours and unions in private employment remained with the states and the courts.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    Naturalization was unchanged. Immigration was heavy, from Ireland and from Germany, and he built no federal bar against it. Nativism was loud in some cities and absent from his statutes.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    He was a Virginia slaveholder, and the Texas he moved to annex was a slaveholding republic. The joint resolution left slavery standing there and permitted Texas to be divided later into further states. He did not touch slavery in the old states, and the Seminole War ended in 1842 short of the removal of every remaining band.

  7. 7. Federalism

    His bank vetoes rested on a states’-rights reading: he held that Congress had no power to charter a national bank. In the Dorr Rebellion he sided with Rhode Island’s charter government and did not send troops into the state, though he was prepared to do so if the charter government required it against a real insurrection. Annexation was the national act, done by Congress and the president over those who said only a treaty, and a two-thirds Senate, could add a foreign republic.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    Webster-Ashburton, in 1842, settled the northeastern boundary with Britain and bound each nation to keep a naval force against the Atlantic slave trade, closing the border crisis Van Buren had left open. He sent no army abroad. The foreign decision of the term was Texas: a treaty of annexation failed in the Senate in 1844, and a joint resolution passed in his last days.

  9. 9. National defense

    The Second Seminole War was brought to a close in 1842, without a large standing army left behind it. He attended to the navy and to coastal defense and did not create a new military system. The explosion of a gun on the Princeton in 1844 killed the secretaries of state and of the navy and did not change the establishment.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    The federal government still had no religious establishment, and he proposed none. Churches were already dividing over slavery, the Methodists and the Baptists among them, during these years. Those divisions were not his statutes.

  11. 11. Education

    Schools remained a charge of states, towns, and churches. He signed no bill for a national university and no general school law. Public land was not granted as a national system of education.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    The Preemption Act of 1841 let settlers buy, at the government’s minimum price, land they had already occupied, and it promised the states a share of land revenue that ceased when the tariff went up. Florida was admitted as he left office. Texas, under the joint resolution, was to keep its own public lands.

  13. 13. Energy

    Coal and wood moved by canal, river, and a lengthening railroad network built by states and by companies. He had no fuel policy. Steam mattered to him as an instrument of the navy, not as an energy program.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    A federal bankruptcy act in 1841 let debtors surrender their property and take a discharge. It was repealed in 1843 after widespread complaint. Criminal law otherwise stood as it had, with the agreement to keep a squadron against the African slave trade added to the settlement with Britain.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    He proposed no federal poor law. The bankruptcy act was a discharge for debtors, not a support of the poor, and it did not last. Relief stayed with towns, churches, and county institutions.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    No federal medical care was created for the public. The marine hospitals continued for seamen. Sickness in the last of the Florida campaigns remained an army concern, not the start of a health service.

Of this presidency

  1. He became president

    Harrison died on April 4, 1841. Tyler took the oath on April 6 and insisted that he was president, not an acting president, for the remainder of the term. The cabinet stayed for a time. What had been an untested clause became, by his insistence, the practice of the office.

  2. The bank vetoes

    With the independent treasury already repealed, the Whig Congress passed a national bank, and he vetoed it. It passed another, narrowed and placed in the District of Columbia, and he vetoed that too. All but Webster then left the cabinet, and the congressional Whigs expelled him from the party. He governed the rest of the term without one.

  3. The Webster-Ashburton Treaty

    In 1842 the treaty with Britain fixed the Maine boundary that had produced the Aroostook confrontation, and it carried the line on through the northern lakes. An article committed both countries to squadrons off the African coast against the slave trade. Webster remained through the ratification and resigned the next year.

  4. Texas by joint resolution

    A treaty of annexation, pressed after Calhoun became secretary of state, failed in the Senate in 1844 for want of two-thirds. After Polk’s election on an expansionist platform, Tyler asked Congress for a joint resolution, which needs only a majority, and he signed it on March 1, 1845. He dispatched the offer to Texas before leaving office. Slavery was left in place, and the formal admission of the state was completed under his successor.

  5. The Dorr Rebellion

    Rhode Island still governed itself under its colonial charter, and Thomas Dorr led a rival government claiming a wider suffrage. Tyler recognized the charter government and promised federal force if insurrection required it. He did not march troops. The rebellion collapsed, and the state then wrote a constitution of its own.

PreviousWilliam Henry Harrison NextJames K. Polk

1845–1849

James K. Polk

Democrat

Before the office

He was born in 1795 in Mecklenburg County, North Carolina, and the childhood that formed him was in Tennessee. When he was ten his father, Samuel Polk, took the family to the Duck River country in Maury County. Samuel was a surveyor and a farmer who had done well, and he was Andrew Jackson’s kind of man. Jane Knox Polk, the mother, was a stricter Presbyterian than the son ever became. He did not have the conversion she wanted. The distance stayed. What he took from the house was work, a suspicion of show, and the Democratic politics of the Tennessee frontier, not the anxiety of her church.

He was a sickly boy. In 1812 he endured a surgery for stones, awake, and came out of it alive and never robust. The body did not make a soldier. It made a clerk of his own ambition. He went to the University of North Carolina, finished at the head of the class in 1818, and read law in Nashville under Felix Grundy. He was admitted to the bar and he married Sarah Childress of Murfreesboro on the first day of 1824. She had been educated, she was as political as he was, and she became the person he trusted when he trusted anyone. They had no children. The marriage was the partnership of the career.

He was a planter as well as a lawyer. The cotton was worked by people he owned, and he added to that property when he could. Nothing in the Jacksonian lesson he was learning required him to treat slavery as a question the Union had to settle. It was the labor of his class in middle Tennessee. He would later want new land in the Southwest, and he did not want that land closed to the institution he lived by. The desire was already in the life before it was a program.

The politics were Jackson’s, practiced in the House of Representatives. He entered Congress in 1825, in time to be useful in the quarrel with Adams and Clay, and he became the man who carried Jackson’s business on the floor. He chaired Ways and Means. He was Speaker from 1835 to 1839, through the years when the bank was dead and the party still had to be held together. They called him Young Hickory. The name was fair as to loyalty and unfair as to temperament. Jackson was a fighter who trusted his temper. Polk was a fighter who trusted a list. He prepared, he counted, and he did not forget a slight or an object.

Tennessee then humbled him. He left the Speakership to run for governor, won in 1839, and lost the office in 1841 and again in 1843. A national career that had looked finished was, for a moment, a defeated governor at home. The defeat is part of what he carried into 1844. He had no personal following large enough to nominate itself. He had a record as Jackson’s lieutenant, a willingness to take Texas and the whole of Oregon when Van Buren and Clay were both hedging, and a promise that he would serve one term and go home. The promise made him safe for men who wanted the nomination later. The convention, deadlocked, took him. He was the first dark horse, and he did not regard the fact as an accident.

He came to the oath with the term already measured. One administration, four things: a tariff for revenue and not for protection, a treasury kept out of the banks, the Oregon boundary settled with Britain, and California in the possession of the United States. Texas was being annexed as he arrived. The rest he meant to finish because he would not be running again, and because a president who knew the date of his own departure could spend the office instead of saving it. The war message came later. The decision that Mexico would yield the Pacific coast, or be made to, was the decision of the man who had written the list.

In the office

Polk kept the Democratic fiscal creed, a revenue tariff and a treasury kept clear of the banks, and he spent the power of the government on territory, dividing Oregon with Britain and taking California and the Southwest by a war with Mexico that he requested and Congress declared. That war was not a difference of degree. The land it added produced the Wilmot Proviso and split his own party over whether slavery would be allowed in the country just acquired.

The party

Tyler had been elected a Whig, had been read out of that party, and had annexed Texas with Democratic votes after a treaty failed. Polk was a Jacksonian Democrat, nominated because he would take Texas and Oregon and Van Buren would not, and he defeated Clay, who would not take Texas at the risk of war. The Democracy he led still wanted a revenue tariff and an independent treasury, the domestic creed of Jackson’s later years. What had shifted inside the party was the weight of expansion: it committed itself to continental territory, and before the term ended that commitment split it over slavery in the land to be gained.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    He wanted economy, a lower tariff, and no federal program of internal improvements. The war with Mexico was financed by loans and treasury notes, not by an income tax or a new system of excises. In 1846 he vetoed a rivers and harbors bill as local spending presented as commerce.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    The Walker Tariff of 1846 cut the rates set in 1842 and returned the country to a revenue tariff with only moderate protection. He treated that reduction as a commitment equal to Oregon and to the treasury. It angered the protectionists of Pennsylvania, and it did not settle the sectional quarrel.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    In 1846 he restored the independent treasury that Van Buren had built and the Whigs had repealed. Federal funds were again to be kept by the government itself, not in a national bank and not as a policy of favored state banks. He did not revive the Bank of the United States.

  4. 4. Labor

    No federal statute governed unions, wages, or the hours of private labor. The proviso fight was nevertheless a fight about labor, because Wilmot would have closed the Mexican cession to enslaved labor and left it to free labor. Polk opposed the ban, and it never became law.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    He left naturalization as it stood. Famine in Ireland and unrest in Germany were filling the ports during the war. He proposed no nativist statute, and no nativist party yet controlled the government.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    He was a slaveholder from Tennessee. He brought in Texas as a slave state and opposed Wilmot’s proviso, which would have barred slavery from any territory taken from Mexico; the House passed it more than once, the Senate did not, and the cession was left with no rule. He did not propose to disturb slavery in the states where it already existed.

  7. 7. Federalism

    He read national power broadly enough to make war and acquire the Mexican Cession, and narrowly enough to veto internal improvements. He would not let the proviso settle slavery in the new country by a vote of Congress. The old states kept their domestic institutions, and the silence about the new territories guaranteed that the quarrel would continue.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    He claimed the whole of Oregon and then accepted the forty-ninth parallel, rather than fight Britain while he fought Mexico. He sent a minister to buy California and New Mexico and to place the Texas boundary at the Rio Grande; Mexico would not receive him, and after a clash north of that river he asked Congress for war, which was declared in May 1846. The Treaty of Guadalupe Hidalgo, in 1848, confirmed the Rio Grande line and ceded California and the Southwest for fifteen million dollars and the assumption of American claims against Mexico.

  9. 9. National defense

    The war was fought by volunteers as much as by the small regular army: Taylor in northern Mexico, Scott from Veracruz to Mexico City, and joint expeditions by land and sea to California. Disease in the camps killed more soldiers than battle did. He did not leave behind a standing army on a European scale.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    The federal government still had no establishment, and the war did not create one. Some clergy denounced the conflict as aggression against a weaker neighbor. Their churches were not instruments of the administration.

  11. 11. Education

    He signed no national school law, and education remained with the states and the churches. The lands taken from Mexico were not granted out as a federal school system. A university at the capital was still only an old proposal.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    Texas, the Oregon country south of the forty-ninth parallel, and the Mexican Cession were the land policy of the term. He would not give away the older public domain by distribution or by a homestead law, and he vetoed local river and harbor projects. Gold was discovered in California as the term closed, and he confirmed the reports to Congress.

  13. 13. Energy

    Wood and coal remained fuels bought in private markets, and the war ran on sail, steam, and animals the government hired or purchased rather than produced. He created no energy administration. Railroads continued to spread under state charters.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    The war placed military justice beside the ordinary federal courts. The foreign slave trade remained illegal, and the new territories arrived without a new criminal code. The proviso was a legislative fight, not a new list of federal crimes.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    No federal relief of poverty was enacted. Pensions were a payment for military service, not a support of the poor as a class. The unemployed, and the immigrants fleeing famine, remained the care of cities, churches, and voluntary societies.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    There was no federal medical system. Surgeons accompanied the army, and disease in camp was the heaviest killer of the war, without producing a public health service. The marine hospitals for seamen continued as before.

Of this presidency

  1. Oregon at the forty-ninth parallel

    His inaugural treated the American title to the whole of Oregon as clear, and there was talk of war with Britain if the line were not drawn far north. He submitted the British offer of the forty-ninth parallel to the Senate and accepted it in 1846. Britain kept Vancouver Island. The Oregon country was organized as a territory, with slavery excluded, before he left office.

  2. The war with Mexico

    After Mexico refused his envoy, he ordered troops into the disputed country between the Nueces and the Rio Grande. When Mexican forces collided with them, he told Congress that American blood had been shed on soil he held to be American, and Congress declared war. Scott took Mexico City. The peace of Guadalupe Hidalgo added California and the Southwest and fixed the southern boundary of Texas at the Rio Grande.

  3. The Wilmot Proviso

    In August 1846 David Wilmot of Pennsylvania moved to bar slavery from any territory acquired from Mexico, as a condition of a war appropriation. The House agreed, and the Senate did not. The proviso was offered again and never became law. It divided Democrats of the North from Democrats of the South more sharply than the tariff or the treasury had ever done.

  4. Tariff and treasury

    In the summer of 1846 he signed the Walker Tariff, lowering the protective rates of 1842, and restored the independent treasury. Those two acts completed the domestic program he had taken from the Jacksonian Democracy. He did not ask for a second term.

  5. The Department of the Interior

    On the last day of his presidency he signed the act creating the Department of the Interior. It gathered the land office, Indian affairs, pensions, and patents into one department. The tasks were old. The gathering of them was new, and it was administration, not a new theory of federal power.

PreviousJohn Tyler NextZachary Taylor

1849–1850

Zachary Taylor

Whig

Before the office

He was born in 1784 in Orange County, Virginia, and he was carried to Kentucky as an infant. His father, Richard Taylor, had been an officer in the Revolution and became a planter near Louisville. The connection to the old Virginia names was real. James Madison was a cousin. The education was not the education of that cousin. Zachary had little schooling and no ambition for the law. In 1808 he took a commission in the infantry. The army was the profession, and he stayed in it for forty years.

He married Margaret Mackall Smith in 1810. She followed the posts when she had to and hated the public part of his fame when it came. The family life of a frontier officer was a string of quarters, fever, and children buried young. One daughter, Sarah Knox, married Jefferson Davis and died of malaria within a few months. The grief broke the friendship with Davis for years. It did not take Taylor out of the service. In 1812 he held Fort Harrison, in the Indiana Territory, against a night attack, and the army brevetted him for it. That was the first of the small, ugly frontier fights that made his name long before Mexico.

Between wars he became a planter of the Old Southwest, which is the other half of the life. He acquired cotton and sugar land in Louisiana and Mississippi and the enslaved people who worked it. He was often not there. The plantations ran by overseer and by letter, while he was on the Sabine or in Florida or at some barracks. He was not a theorist of slavery and not an apologist who wrote about it. He was an owner. The institution was his property and the labor of his class, and he did not propose that the federal government should touch it in the states. The Union, for him, was the country the army served. Slavery was the private order of the country he went home to.

The nickname came out of Florida. In the Second Seminole War he fought at Lake Okeechobee and wore the campaign without much regard for uniform. The soldiers called him Old Rough and Ready. He looked like a farmer who had been given a command, and he cultivated the look, but the habit under it was a regular officer’s habit: hold the position, do not explain more than you must, and do not let politicians move you if the order is unclear. Black Hawk’s campaign had been a brief earlier version of the same work. Florida was the long one. Neither made him a national figure. Mexico did.

Polk ordered him to the Rio Grande. In May 1846 he fought at Palo Alto and Resaca de la Palma and went south. At Monterrey he took the city and granted an armistice the president disliked. At Buena Vista, in February 1847, he beat Santa Anna with a force badly outnumbered, and Davis, the widower of his daughter, was among the men under him. Then Polk took most of the army away and gave the decisive campaign to Winfield Scott. Taylor stayed in the north of Mexico, angry, idle, and famous. The administration had made a hero it could not trust. The Whigs, who had opposed the war, took the hero anyway. It was the same bargain they had made with Harrison, and Taylor was even less of a party man than Harrison had been.

He had spent his life outside elections. He let his name be used, said he would make no pledges, and then, in a letter from Baton Rouge in April 1848, put down the sentence that made the managers able to nominate him. He was a Whig, but not an ultra Whig. He would not be the mere president of a party. The veto he would keep for a clear breach of the Constitution, not for Clay’s program or for anyone else’s. What he carried into the office was a soldier’s Union, a planter’s stake in slavery where it already existed, and no list of the sort Polk had written. The crisis waiting for him was the land he himself had helped to conquer, and the question of whether slavery would go into it. He had not prepared a doctrine for that. He had prepared a habit of holding the ground he was given.

In the office

Taylor inherited the land Polk had taken and the sectional crisis that land produced, and he did not live to settle it. He left the revenue tariff and the independent treasury standing, and he offered no new system of banks, schools, or relief of the poor. His own policy was not a difference of degree: he urged Congress to admit California, and New Mexico when it was ready, immediately as states, and he opposed both a congressional ban written as the price of the war and the compromise Clay offered in its place.

The party

Polk’s Democrats had made the war and taken the land, and they had then divided over whether slavery might enter it. The Whigs nominated Taylor, a Louisiana slaveholder and the general who had won the northern campaign in Mexico, rather than Clay and rather than a platform of the American System. Voters who had disliked the war nevertheless voted for its most famous soldier. Inside the Whig party he was not Clay’s instrument: he wanted California and New Mexico admitted as states at once, and he set himself against the compromise Clay was assembling to satisfy the South.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    He inherited the debts of the war, California gold beginning to reach the Treasury, and the Walker Tariff. He proposed no income tax and brought forward no new protective schedule. Sixteen months produced no fiscal reconstruction.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    The revenue tariff of 1846 remained the law of foreign commerce. He opened no new system of protection. His diplomacy looked toward a canal across Central America, not toward a revision of duties.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    The independent treasury stayed in force. He did not revive a national bank, though many Whigs still wanted one. Gold from California was changing the specie in circulation without a monetary statute of his own.

  4. 4. Labor

    Unions, wages, and hours were not on the agenda of his months in office. The argument that the new country should be kept for free labor was the labor question inside the crisis. He met it by urging statehood, not by a labor law.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    Naturalization was unchanged. Irish and German immigration remained large, and nativist societies were spreading in the cities. He made no federal policy either of invitation or of exclusion.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    He was a slaveholder with no patience for talk of disunion, and he did not propose to abolish slavery where the states already had it. California had written a constitution prohibiting slavery, and he asked Congress to admit the state on that constitution. He would not tie that admission to a harsher fugitive-slave law or to territorial governments left open to slavery.

  7. 7. Federalism

    He treated the Union as something a state could not abandon, and he was prepared to use troops if Texas tried to seize the New Mexican country it claimed east of the Rio Grande. He wanted the people of California and of New Mexico to settle slavery in their own constitutions rather than have Congress legislate a bargain over territories. That was national force against disunion, and state decision instead of Clay’s omnibus.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    The Clayton-Bulwer Treaty, which he ratified in 1850, bound the United States and Britain neither to colonize Central America nor to take exclusive control of a future canal across the isthmus. He proclaimed against expeditions fitted out in American ports to invade Cuba. He began no war.

  9. 9. National defense

    The army was the force left after the Mexican War, reduced, and posted in part in the new territories, where it could also meet a Texan move toward Santa Fe. The navy stayed small. He did not rearm for a sectional war, and he was willing to use the troops he had.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    The federal government still had no religious establishment. Union meetings and sermons about slavery were loud in these months, and they were not his legislation. He made no Sabbath law and chartered no church.

  11. 11. Education

    Schools remained with the states and the churches. He signed no national education law. Whatever California meant to do for its schools was written in its own constitution, not in a federal statute.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    California gold, and a Mexican cession not yet given stable government, were the land facts, and he answered them by urging statehood rather than by a new land statute. No homestead act passed. He would not concede Texas’s claim to the country around Santa Fe.

  13. 13. Energy

    Wood and coal, steamboats and the early railroads, remained the business of private owners and of the states. He had no fuel policy. The gold rush was a mineral stampede, not an energy program.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    The Fugitive Slave Act of 1793 was still the limited federal law, poorly enforced in parts of the North, and he signed no replacement. The courts and the army were what he had ready against filibusters and against a collision in New Mexico. He died before any new federal process for reclaiming fugitives was enacted.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    Relief of the poor stayed with towns, churches, and counties. Neither the gold rush nor the crisis in Congress produced a federal poor law. He proposed none.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    Cholera had passed through the country in 1849, and cities met it without a federal medical service. He died on July 9, 1850, of an acute gastric illness the physicians called cholera morbus. The marine hospitals remained a provision for seamen and nothing wider.

Of this presidency

  1. Immediate statehood

    He urged California, already sitting in convention and writing a free constitution, to come into the Union at once, and he wanted New Mexico to follow by the same path rather than linger as a territory. Congress would accept or reject the states. It would not, on his plan, write a slavery code for them in advance.

  2. Against Clay’s compromise

    From January 1850 Clay laid before the Senate a combined settlement: California free, territorial governments with no ban on slavery, a payment to Texas for its New Mexican claim, an end to the slave trade in the District, and a stronger fugitive-slave law. Taylor opposed the package. He would not purchase Southern assent by coupling California to the rest.

  3. Texas and New Mexico

    Texas claimed the country east of the Rio Grande up to its source, including Santa Fe, and talked of asserting the claim with force. He sided with New Mexico and was prepared to send the army to hold the line. The collision did not come while he lived. It was the nearest thing to a domestic use of troops in his term.

  4. Clayton-Bulwer

    The treaty with Britain, ratified in the last months of his life, said that neither power would monopolize a canal across the Central American isthmus or colonize that region. It was a limit on rivalry, not an alliance. The canal itself was not built.

  5. Death before a settlement

    He fell ill after the Fourth of July ceremonies in 1850 and died on July 9. The immediate-statehood plan died with him. His successor accepted the compromise he had refused, and the cabinet he had appointed resigned to make way.

PreviousJames K. Polk NextMillard Fillmore

1850–1853

Millard Fillmore

Whig

Before the office

He was born in 1800 in a log cabin in Cayuga County, New York, the son of Nathaniel Fillmore, a tenant farmer who lost more than one farm, and of Phoebe Millard. There was no estate and no commission waiting. As a boy he was apprenticed to a cloth dresser, bound to a trade he hated, and the schooling was whatever could be caught between the work. He left the apprenticeship. He taught school to live, and he read law when he could find a lawyer who would let him. The admission to the bar, in 1823, was the first piece of property that was really his. Everything afterward was built on that.

Abigail Powers was the other beginning. She was a schoolteacher, the daughter of a Baptist minister, two years older than he was, and she had the books. He married her in 1826, when he could at last support a wife. She remained the educated half of the household. The practice grew, first in East Aurora and then, from 1830, in Buffalo, where he became a lawyer of standing and a man with a house, clients, and money in a city that was itself new. He was not a planter and he held no slaves. The self-made part of the story was true. It also made him careful. A man who had climbed out of a bound apprenticeship did not like disorder, and he did not like a politics that treated the law as something to be broken for a feeling.

The politics he entered were the Anti-Masonic politics of western New York, after a man named Morgan disappeared and the lodge was blamed. It was a popular movement against a secret society, and it was also a way up for young men who were not Van Buren’s Albany Regency. Fillmore went to the legislature as an Anti-Mason. When that party faded into the Whigs, he went with it, and the Whig program fitted the life. A protective tariff, a national bank or at least a stable currency, and roads and canals were the politics of a commercial town and of a lawyer who had seen what a market could do for a poor county. In Congress, in the 1830s and early 1840s, he was one of Clay’s men. As chairman of Ways and Means he carried the tariff of 1842. He was never the hero of a campaign. He was the man who could draft the bill.

New York would not let him be only a national Whig. Thurlow Weed and William Seward ran the state party as a machine, and Fillmore was useful to them until he was in their way. He wanted the governorship in 1844 and lost it. He came back as comptroller in 1848, the officer who watched the state’s money, and the office made him large enough that a national ticket had to reckon with him. The reckoning was the vice presidency under Taylor. A Louisiana slaveholder at the top of the ticket, a New York Whig under him, and New York’s electoral votes made safe from the Seward wing by giving Fillmore the place. He took it. He was not Taylor’s friend, and he was not admitted to Taylor’s counsels. Seward had the president’s ear. The vice president had the Senate chair and a resentment.

What he believed by then was already the belief he would govern with. The Union was a constitutional bargain, not a feeling. Slavery was an evil, and it was also an evil the Constitution had recognized and left to the states. A northern man who did not own a slave was not, in his view, thereby released from the clauses that protected the owners. Abolitionist agitation struck him as a threat to the government he had spent twenty years trying to make Whig and solvent. He would rather endure the institution, and give it the protection the bargain required, than pull down free government in order to be rid of it.

Taylor’s death made the sentence a policy. Fillmore had not expected the office, any more than Tyler had, and he had even less of a personal legend to carry him into it. He was a Buffalo lawyer, a tariff man, and a New York Whig whom his own state party had sidelined. He came to the oath convinced that the crisis over the land Polk had taken could be closed by a statute, and that a president’s duty was to execute the statute once it passed. The habit was the habit of the apprentice who had got free by following the rules of the profession, not by breaking them.

In the office

Fillmore did not replace the commercial republic. The tariff, the independent treasury, and the habit of leaving schools, churches, and the poor to states and towns all remained. He did break with the man he succeeded on the question that was tearing the Union, and he signed the Compromise of 1850, including a fugitive-slave law that put federal officers to the work of returning enslaved people from free states.

The party

He was a Whig, as Taylor had been, and he reversed Taylor’s course inside the party. Taylor, a Southern soldier and a slaveholder, had resisted Clay’s compromise and preferred to admit California and New Mexico at once under constitutions of their own making. Fillmore, a New York Whig who held no slaves, accepted the compromise, signed each part of it, and enforced the fugitive-slave law. Northern Whigs would not live with that law, Southern Whigs were not reconciled by it, and the party that had elected Taylor never elected another president.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    He left the Walker Tariff and the independent treasury in place, and California gold supported the revenue. The compromise paid Texas ten million dollars to give up its claim on New Mexican territory, a specific appropriation, not a new tax. He did not build a peacetime system of general spending.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    He made no general revision of the tariff. He authorized Commodore Matthew Perry to take a squadron, and a letter from the president, to Japan and to ask that ports be opened to American ships. Perry sailed before the term ended, and the treaty was signed under the next administration.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    The independent treasury continued, and gold from California enlarged the stock of specie. He did not charter a national bank. The old Whig hope of restoring one had no revival in his statutes.

  4. 4. Labor

    No federal law set private hours or recognized unions. The labor meaning of the compromise was sectional: California came in free, and Utah and New Mexico were organized with no congressional prohibition of slavery. Enforcing the fugitive law was the administration’s work, not a code for free workmen.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship
