    Naturalization stayed at five years’ residence. He signed no nativist restriction, though societies hostile to immigrants were spreading in the cities after the famine migration. The nativist nomination he accepted later was not an act of this term.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    He signed the Compromise of 1850: California free, territorial governments for Utah and New Mexico with no ban on slavery, an end to the slave trade in the District of Columbia, a money settlement with Texas, and a Fugitive Slave Act that denied alleged fugitives a jury and the right to testify and ordered federal officers to return them. He enforced that act, including against rescues in Northern cities. Slavery inside the existing slave states he left untouched.

  7. 7. Federalism

    The fugitive law was a national command inside free states, set against the personal-liberty resistance that had grown up under the act of 1793. He treated the compromise as a final settlement and secession as inadmissible. Ordinary police, schools, and the law of property otherwise remained with the states, which made the fugitive statute the sharp exception.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    Perry’s voyage to Japan was a display of naval force in the service of trade, not a war and not an alliance. He held to the Clayton-Bulwer understanding with Britain and proclaimed against expeditions leaving American ports to seize Cuba. He did not annex Cuba, and he did not turn American sympathy for the failed Hungarian revolution into an intervention.

  9. 9. National defense

    The army and navy remained peacetime forces, and the navy’s notable duty was the preparation and sailing of Perry’s squadron. He raised no war establishment. Marshals, and in particular cases troops, were used to carry out the fugitive law.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    The federal government still had no religious establishment. Churches in the North and in the South preached both for the compromise and against it. He adopted neither pulpit as a national faith.

  11. 11. Education

    Common schools stayed with states, towns, and churches. He signed no bill for a national university and no general grant of land for schools. The railroad land grant of 1850 was for a road, not for a school fund.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    The compromise drew the Texas boundary, paid Texas for the claim it released, and left Utah and New Mexico as territories beside a free California. In September 1850 he also signed a land-grant act giving alternate sections to Illinois, Mississippi, and Alabama to help build a railroad from the Lakes toward the Gulf, a grant for a road rather than a homestead law. The Senate rejected treaties that federal agents had just made with Indian communities in the California gold country.

  13. 13. Energy

    Coal was gaining on wood in cities and ironworks, and both fuels were bought and sold privately. In 1852 a stronger steamboat law required inspection of boilers and the licensing of pilots and engineers, after a series of fatal explosions. That was a safety rule for steam, not a national fuel policy.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    The Fugitive Slave Act was the criminal statute of the term. A commissioner could send an alleged fugitive back without a jury, the accused could not testify, and rescue or concealment was a federal offense. He supported enforcement, including the return of Thomas Sims from Boston to slavery in Georgia and prosecutions after the Jerry rescue in Syracuse.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    No federal system of relief was created. The money in the compromise went to Texas, not to the poor. Towns, churches, and voluntary societies remained the support of the destitute, immigrants in the cities included.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    There was no federal system of medicine for the public. The marine hospitals for seamen continued. Yellow fever and the remnants of cholera were left to cities and to local boards of health.

Of this presidency

  1. Signing the Compromise

    Taylor’s death removed the chief executive obstacle to Clay’s settlement. The single omnibus bill failed, Stephen Douglas carried the pieces separately, and Fillmore, having replaced Taylor’s cabinet and named Webster secretary of state, signed them in September 1850. He then treated the package as a final settlement of the sectional quarrel. The quarrel was not finished.

  2. The Fugitive Slave Act

    The act of September 18, 1850, put federal commissioners over the return of alleged fugitives, forbade a jury and the testimony of the accused, and paid a commissioner ten dollars if he ordered a return and five if he did not. Harboring or rescuing carried fine and imprisonment, and citizens could be called on to assist. He enforced it. Thomas Sims was marched out of Boston under guard, and the government prosecuted rescuers after a crowd in Syracuse freed a man the law had seized.

  3. The slave trade in the District

    One statute of the compromise abolished the slave trade, not slavery itself, in the District of Columbia. The jails and the pens used for that trade were to close. Enslaved people already in the capital were not freed by the act. The distinction was deliberate, and it was his signature.

  4. The mission to Japan

    He sent Commodore Perry with a squadron and a letter asking Japan to open ports and protect shipwrecked American sailors. Perry left the United States in 1852. He reached Japan, and the treaty was made, only after Fillmore had left office. The decision to send him was Fillmore’s.

  5. The end of the Whig party as a national force

    Enforcement of the fugitive law estranged the Northern wing, and the admission of a free California did not satisfy the Southern wing. In 1852 the party nominated General Scott, not Fillmore, and lost to the Democrats. He was the last Whig president.

PreviousZachary Taylor NextFranklin Pierce

1853–1857

Franklin Pierce

Democrat

Before the office

He was born in 1804 at Hillsborough, New Hampshire, the son of Benjamin Pierce, who had fought in the Revolution, kept a tavern, and been governor of the state. The house was Democratic in the old way: the Union as the work of that war, Jefferson’s party as the party of the people, and no patience for a politics that would set one section’s conscience over the bargain the states had made. Franklin was handsome, quick, and liked. He was not a scholar. At Bowdoin he was in the class of 1824 with Longfellow and with Nathaniel Hawthorne, and Hawthorne, not the college honors, was the friendship that lasted. Hawthorne would one day write the life that helped elect him.

He read law and was admitted to the bar in 1827. The rise in New Hampshire was easy, because the name was his father’s and the manner was his own. He sat in the legislature and was Speaker of it while he was still young. He went to Congress in 1833 and to the Senate in 1837. In 1834 he married Jane Means Appleton, daughter of a president of Bowdoin, a woman of a strict and fragile piety who hated Washington, hated the drinking, and did not want the life he wanted. He loved her. He also resigned the Senate in 1842 and went back to Concord, to a law practice that paid, in part because she could not bear the capital and in part because he could not bear what he was becoming in it. The bottle was already a fact of the marriage and of the reputation.

They buried children. One son died in infancy. Another died of typhus as a small boy. The last, Benjamin, lived. Jane’s health did not. Pierce’s idea of a good life, by the middle of the 1840s, was a Concord lawyer’s life, the party, and his friends, not the presidency. He had seen enough of Washington to know that Jane would experience any return as a punishment. The Mexican War pulled him out of that settlement without making him a Taylor. He enlisted, was made a brigadier, and went with Scott’s army. At Contreras his horse fell and injured his knee. He was back in the fight. Political enemies later said he had fainted from fear. The injury was real, and so was the usefulness of the story to men who needed him to look small. He came home a general of a minor kind, and a Democrat who had served.

The party that nominated him in 1852 was a party that could not agree on a bigger man. The convention balloted into the forties. Pierce was a northern Democrat the South could accept, because he had never made trouble about slavery and because he would treat the Compromise of 1850 as a final settlement, including the law that returned fugitives. He had not sought the nomination in the open. When it came, Jane fainted. He took it. He was New Hampshire’s favorite son in the sense that the state knew him and liked him, and he was a national candidate in the sense that he offended no section that the Democracy needed. He beat Winfield Scott. The platform was the compromise. He meant to be the president who carried it out and then let the subject alone.

Two months before the inauguration the train carrying the family came off the track near Andover. The car turned over. Pierce and Jane survived, and they watched their last child die. Jane, who had already thought the ambition was a sin, took the death as a judgment. She did not come to the ceremony. He delivered the address from memory, in a capital that was looking at a mourner. The private catastrophe did not change the doctrine. It took the ease out of the man who had lived on ease. What remained was the party obligation and the belief he had brought from Hillsborough.

That belief was simple and he stated it at the oath. Involuntary servitude, where the states kept it, was a right the Constitution recognized. The compromise measures were constitutional and were to be carried into effect without hesitation. Sectional excitement, ambitious or fanatical, was the danger to the Union, not the existence of slavery itself. He was a northern man who held no slaves and who proposed to act as if the South’s constitutional claim were no different from any other claim. He thought that was loyalty. He thought the question was at rest. The life that taught him to think so was his father’s party, his wife’s grief, and a career spent being agreeable to every Democrat in the room.

In the office

Pierce took office treating the Compromise of 1850 as a finality and the federal government as a government of limited objects. He then signed the Kansas-Nebraska Act, which repealed the Missouri Compromise and reopened the territorial question that finality was supposed to have closed. He did not widen the objects of government: he would not use federal land for the poor, and he would not touch slavery inside a state.

The party

Fillmore was a Whig, and his party still called the Compromise of 1850 a final settlement while carrying, in fragments, the older Whig program of a protective tariff, a national bank, and internal improvements. That coalition was already splitting between Northern members who hated the Fugitive Slave Act and Southern members who required it. Pierce’s Democrats were the national party that remained: a revenue tariff, no national bank, territorial expansion, and popular sovereignty. They made repeal of the Missouri Compromise a test of party loyalty, and the Whig organization did not survive the test.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    Customs produced a surplus, and the debt from the Mexican War was being paid down. He lowered duties rather than find new things to spend on. The veto of a land grant for the indigent insane was his limit in one sentence of action: charity was not a federal power.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    The Walker Tariff of 1846 stood until his last day, when he signed the Tariff of 1857 and cut rates again. That was a revenue tariff, not Whig protection. A reciprocity treaty with British North America in 1854 opened a free list of natural products and settled a fisheries dispute, a commercial bargain rather than an alliance.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    The Independent Treasury continued, and he chartered no national bank. California gold enlarged the supply of specie, and state banks issued the paper. He constructed nothing new.

  4. 4. Labor

    Wages, hours, and strikes stayed with the states. He offered no federal code for free workers and no recognition of unions. The labor question he did treat as national was slavery in the territories, and he called that a choice for the settlers.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    Irish and German immigration was heavy, and the American Party rose against Catholics and the foreign-born. Pierce’s Democrats wanted the immigrant vote and opposed a longer wait for citizenship. He signed no restrictive law.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    He enforced the Fugitive Slave Act, and in 1854 federal troops took Anthony Burns out of Boston and back to slavery in Virginia. He signed the Kansas-Nebraska Act, repealing the Missouri line and opening those territories to slavery if the settlers voted it in. He recognized the proslavery legislature in Kansas and proposed no change to slavery in the states.

  7. 7. Federalism

    He used marshals and soldiers to execute the fugitive-slave law inside Northern states, and he held that Congress ought not bar slavery from a territory. Schools, poor relief, and ordinary police he left to the states, which is what the Dix veto said. He did not concede a right of secession, and he built no power that could have stopped one.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    There was no European alliance. He bought from Mexico a strip along the southern border for a railroad, completed the treaty Perry had negotiated with Japan, and made the Canadian reciprocity treaty. He wanted Cuba by purchase, and when the Ostend dispatch suggested taking it he disavowed the threat and sent no fleet.

  9. 9. National defense

    The army stayed small and scattered on the frontier, and it was used at times to enforce federal law, as in the Burns rendition. Surveys for a Pacific railroad were not a military buildup. The navy remained a modest cruising force.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    He made no establishment and issued no program of national religious observance. Days of thanksgiving stayed with the states, as they had through most of the years since Madison. Churches and reform societies worked without a federal charter or a federal ban.

  11. 11. Education

    Common schools and colleges belonged to states, churches, and private founders. He signed no land-grant college act and created no office of education. Public land was not, in his practice, a fund for a national school system.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    The Gadsden Purchase added territory along the Mexican border. Kansas and Nebraska were organized and opened, with slavery left to the settlers. Treaties went on reducing Indian land, and a Pacific railroad was surveyed at public expense and not built.

  13. 13. Energy

    Coal and wood fired the mills and the early railroads. The federal government did not produce fuel, price it, or allocate it. The railroad surveys were about a route, not about an energy system.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    The federal criminal law that marked the term was the Fugitive Slave Act, executed by marshals and, in Boston, by troops. The armed factions in Kansas were never reduced to a settled peace. Ordinary offenses remained with the states.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    On May 3, 1854, he vetoed the grant of public land to the states for the indigent insane. He held that a power to provide for one class of the poor would be pressed into a power to provide for all of them, and that the Constitution had not given it. Relief stayed with towns, counties, and private charity.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    That same veto refused the one serious proposal to use the public domain for asylums. There was no federal medicine for civilians. Army surgeons and the marine hospitals for seamen were the whole of federal medical work.

Of this presidency

  1. The Kansas-Nebraska Act

    On May 30, 1854, he signed the act that organized Kansas and Nebraska and repealed the Missouri Compromise line of 1820. Slavery in the new territories was left to the settlers, and Democratic support was made a test of party loyalty. Men from the free states and the slave states then fought over the polls and the land. He recognized the proslavery territorial legislature.

  2. The Gadsden Purchase

    James Gadsden negotiated a purchase from Mexico so a southern railroad could reach the Pacific below the worst of the mountains. The Senate cut both the tract and the price, and Pierce took the reduced bargain. It was expansion for a route. It was not a war, and it did not settle which labor system would ride the railroad.

  3. The Ostend Manifesto

    In October 1854 the ministers to Britain, France, and Spain wrote from Ostend that the United States should buy Cuba and might seize it if Spain’s refusal put the peace at risk. The dispatch leaked. The administration repudiated the threat. Pierce still wanted the island by purchase, and he sent no expedition.

  4. The rendition of Anthony Burns

    Federal officers seized Anthony Burns in Boston under the Fugitive Slave Act. An attempt to rescue him failed. Pierce sent troops, and Burns was carried back to Virginia. He treated the rendition as the plain duty of a constitutional government. The spectacle recruited more opponents of that government than it quieted.

PreviousMillard Fillmore NextJames Buchanan

1857–1861

James Buchanan

Democrat

Before the office

He was born in 1791 in a log cabin at Stony Batter, near Cove Gap in Pennsylvania, into a Scotch-Irish family that did not stay poor. His father kept a store and bought land. The boy was raised at Mercersburg, sent to Dickinson College, and sent down from it for disorder. He was taken back, and he graduated in 1809. The lesson he kept was not the disorder. It was the recovery by rule. He read law in Lancaster, was admitted in 1812, and became one of the ablest and best-paid lawyers in the state. The Constitution, for him, was a document a careful man could master and then live by. He never stopped thinking that a hard question had a clause that would answer it.

He began as a Federalist, which in Pennsylvania was the minority of property, the bar, and the suspicion of the War of 1812. When invasion threatened he turned out with a militia company, a brief service that let him say he had not been only an opponent of the war. The party of his youth died. He did not retire with it. By the end of the 1820s he was Andrew Jackson’s man, and the rest of the public life was Democratic: congressman, minister to Russia, senator from Pennsylvania for eleven years, secretary of state under Polk. He helped settle Oregon at the forty-ninth parallel. He managed the diplomacy of a war he had not started. He wanted Cuba. He wanted the presidency, and the party kept giving it to someone else.

The private life closed early. In 1819 he was engaged to Ann Coleman, the daughter of the richest ironmaster in the county. A quarrel, his absence in a lawsuit, and the gossip of Lancaster broke it. She died within days, and her family would not have him at the burial. He never married. He did not talk about it. The household of his later years was his niece, Harriet Lane, whom he raised and who would stand as hostess in London and then in the White House. The manner that carried him through forty years of office was a bachelor’s manner: courteous, secretive, fond of company that did not ask him what he felt. The wound was not a political position. It was the reason the political position was all he had.

Under Pierce he went to London as minister, and there he put his name to the Ostend Manifesto. With the ministers to Spain and France he declared that the United States should buy Cuba, and that if Spain refused and the island became a danger to the Union, taking it would be justified. Pierce backed away from the paper. Buchanan did not back away from the idea. Cuba was slave country. Acquiring it was, in his view, a legitimate object of a Democratic administration and a service to the Union, not a provocation. The South noticed. When the party needed a candidate in 1856 who had been out of the country during the fight over Kansas-Nebraska, he was the man. He had not written the act. He had spent the years being agreeable to the men who had.

He took the nomination as a lawyer takes a case he has waited for. The country was already past the point where a case could settle it, and he did not see that. He believed the territorial question was a judicial question. The Supreme Court had it. Good citizens would submit to whatever the judges said. He had lived his life among men who submitted to documents: a treaty, a statute, a will, a court. Slavery, in that frame, was property the Constitution recognized, and agitation against it was the disorder, not the institution. He held no plantation. He was a Pennsylvania Democrat who had spent his career in the party that required northern men to reassure southern ones, and he had never found the reassurance difficult.

The reading he would give Congress in December 1860 was the reading he brought to the oath. Secession would be a breach. Coercion of a state would be a power the Constitution had not given to Congress or to him, and a power not given was a power he would not use. Forts could fall while he looked for the clause that authorized him to hold them by force. He was not a traitor in his own mind. He was the Lancaster lawyer at the end of a long Democratic life, still certain that the enumerated powers were the whole of the government, and that a president who went beyond them would be worse than a president who watched the Union come apart.

In the office

He entered in March 1857 expecting a judgment of the Supreme Court to settle slavery in the territories. It widened the breach instead. After Lincoln’s election he called secession unconstitutional and, in the same papers, denied the federal government a power to coerce a state, and forts and customhouses were taken while he held to that pair of opinions.

The party

Pierce’s Democrats had still presented themselves as one national party, with popular sovereignty as the formula that would hold the sections. Buchanan took that party and the Dred Scott decision and treated slave property in the territories as a constitutional right, not as a local choice that might go against slavery. When Kansas rejected the Lecompton constitution he tried to force it through, and Douglas and the Northern wing broke with him. By 1860 the party ran two tickets, and he left office denying both a right of secession and any power to stop secession.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    The Tariff of 1857 had just cut rates, and the Panic of that year cut imports and revenue with them. He answered with no new tax and no relief budget. On March 2, 1861, with Southern members already gone, he signed the Morrill Tariff and raised duties on the way out.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    He lived under the low Democratic tariff Pierce had signed, and the panic made its yield too small. His own preference was moderate revenue duties, not a reconstruction of industry. The Morrill bill he signed at the end was a protective tariff passed because secession had removed the men who would have killed it.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    The Independent Treasury and specie were his system. State banks suspended payment in the panic, and he proposed neither a national bank nor a federal paper money. Greenbacks were left to the war.

  4. 4. Labor

    The panic put men out of work, and he offered neither a labor statute nor public works as relief. Strikes and wages stayed with the states. The labor system he would use national power to protect was slavery in the territories.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    He signed no restriction. The nativist surge of the mid-1850s was receding, and the Democrats remained the party that wanted the immigrant vote. The naturalization term was unchanged.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    He took Dred Scott as the end of the argument: Congress could not ban slavery in a territory, and black persons were not citizens. He urged admission of Kansas under a proslavery constitution the free-state majority had refused to write. He blamed Northern agitation, asked for an amendment protecting slavery, and would not use force when states seceded to defend it.

  7. 7. Federalism

    His message of December 1860 said that a state had no right to secede and that neither Congress nor the president had a power to coerce one. He would collect the revenue and hold public property so far as the existing laws allowed, and he would not make war on a state. When South Carolina fired on the ship sent to resupply Fort Sumter, that distinction left him with no next act.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    He asked Congress for authority to intervene in Mexico’s civil war and to secure transit routes, and Congress refused. He sent no army and made no European alliance. A naval demonstration against Paraguay settled a claim without a fight.

  9. 9. National defense

    The regular army stayed small and was posted mostly in the West. He did not mass it to hold the Southern forts. In January 1861 the Star of the West, sent to resupply Fort Sumter, was turned back by shore batteries, and he did not follow with the navy.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    He made no establishment and imposed no religious test. He did not cast the sectional crisis as a war of churches. Days of thanksgiving and fasting remained largely with the states.

  11. 11. Education

    Schools stayed with states, churches, and families. He created no department of education and signed no grant of land for colleges. Public lands, in his homestead veto, were a source of revenue, not a school fund.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    In June 1860 he vetoed a homestead bill, calling free land unfair to men who had paid and costly to the revenue. The veto also kept a flood of free-state settlers out of the territories, which was why the South wanted the bill dead. A military expedition brought Utah to terms without a battle, and the Indian wars in the West went on.

  13. 13. Energy

    Coal and the railroads were private, and the panic stopped a great deal of building. He did not manage fuel, and he signed no Pacific railroad charter. That law waited until the war had removed the Southern votes against it.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    He embraced Dred Scott, which was the Court’s act and then his policy. As the lower South seceded, federal courts and customhouses there stopped, and he did not restore them by force. He thought secessionists in the wrong and did not treat them as rebels to be subdued.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    The Panic of 1857 was a national distress met without a national relief. Counties, churches, and the debtor laws of the states carried it. He proposed no support for the unemployed and no public works to hire them.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    There was no federal system of medicine. Fevers and the care of the sick poor remained local or private. The marine hospitals continued for seamen only, and he did not extend them.

Of this presidency

  1. Dred Scott

    The Court decided Dred Scott two days after his inauguration. He was not a bystander. He had urged justices to reach the question of slavery in the territories, and the inaugural told the country to accept the result as final. He then took the opinion — that Congress could not ban slavery in a territory, and that a black man was not a citizen — as the policy of his administration.

  2. The Lecompton constitution

    A convention the free-state majority had boycotted wrote a proslavery constitution for Kansas. He asked Congress to admit the state under it. Douglas and most Northern Democrats refused. Congress sent the question back to Kansas, and Kansas voted the constitution down.

  3. The secession winter

    South Carolina seceded on December 20, 1860, and six more states of the lower South followed by the first of February. Forts, arsenals, and customhouses were seized. He argued for a constitutional amendment to protect slavery and waited on compromise. He handed Fort Sumter, and the decision for war, to Lincoln.

  4. No coercion

    He called secession a revolution, not a right. He also held that the Constitution gave no department a power to make war on a state. After the Star of the West was fired on and turned back, he did not escalate. For the rest of his term the Union was, in his doctrine, perpetual, and it was not to be defended by arms.

PreviousFranklin Pierce NextDred Scott v. Sandford

1857

Dred Scott v. Sandford

The Court tried to end the argument over slavery in the territories by a constitutional ruling. The ruling answered more than the parties had to have answered, and it did not hold.

Decision

Dred Scott v. Sandford

Decided March 6, 1857

How it was made

Dred Scott sued for his freedom in federal court, claiming that residence at Fort Snelling, in the part of the Louisiana Purchase where the Missouri Compromise had forbidden slavery, had made him free. The Court could have stopped at jurisdiction. It went on to the compromise itself.

Who sat

Seven to two. Taney wrote the lead opinion. Five presidents’ appointees were in the majority, and one of Jackson’s, McLean, dissented with Fillmore’s Curtis.

With the opinion

  • Roger Taney — Jackson
  • James Wayne — Jackson
  • John Catron — Jackson
  • Peter Daniel — Van Buren
  • Samuel Nelson — Tyler
  • Robert Grier — Polk
  • John Campbell — Pierce

In dissent

  • John McLean — Jackson
  • Benjamin Curtis — Fillmore

What it changed

Taney held that a black person could not be a citizen who might sue in federal court, and that Congress could not ban slavery in a territory. The Missouri Compromise line was declared void. Curtis answered that free black citizens had existed in several states at the founding, and that Congress had governed slavery in the territories since the Northwest Ordinance. The decision nationalized a question the compromise had tried to localize. It was undone not by a later opinion in the same terms but by war and by the Thirteenth and Fourteenth Amendments. A Court can close a statute. It cannot, by that act, close a crisis the country will not accept.

PreviousJames Buchanan NextAbraham Lincoln

1861–1865

Abraham Lincoln

Republican

Before the office

He was born in 1809 in a log cabin at Sinking Spring, in Hardin County, Kentucky, the son of Thomas Lincoln, a farmer and carpenter, and of Nancy Hanks. The family moved, lost ground to bad titles, and in 1816 crossed into Indiana, in part to get clear of a slave state. Nancy died of milk sickness two years later. Thomas married Sarah Bush Johnston, a widow who brought her own children, a few books, and the first steady kindness the boy had known. The schooling was a few months in all. What he had was what he read, and he read everything he could borrow. The poverty was real. So was the decision, early, that he would not live as his father had lived.

In 1830 the family moved again, to Illinois. He went down the Mississippi on a flatboat, saw New Orleans, and then settled at New Salem, a village that soon died. He clerked, he split rails, he failed in a store and spent years paying the debts, he was postmaster and surveyor. In the Black Hawk War the men of his company elected him captain. He saw no battle, and he said years afterward that the election had given him more pleasure than any success he had known up to then. He lost his first race for the legislature and won the next one. He was a Whig in a Democratic state. Henry Clay was the model: a tariff, a bank, internal improvements, and a Union that might put slavery in the way of extinction without tearing itself up to do it. He taught himself the law, was licensed in 1836, and moved to Springfield.

The law made him. On the Eighth Circuit he became a lawyer other lawyers wanted, and the clients, including the railroads, paid. By the 1850s he was not a poor man. He was a self-made one, and the political economy he trusted was the one he had lived: a laborer should be able to become a hirer of labor, and a system that locked a class into labor forever was wrong even when the Constitution protected it. In 1842 he married Mary Todd, of a Lexington family that held slaves. She was ambitious, proud, and often miserable, and she wanted the distinction he wanted. The marriage was hard and it held. One son died in Springfield before the presidency. The private life was not the legend of a saint. It was a household, a debt paid, and a practice.

He went to Congress for one term, 1847 to 1849, and used it to challenge Polk’s account of how the Mexican War began. The Spot Resolutions did not make him famous. They marked him. He would not call an aggressive war a defensive one to oblige the president who wanted the land. He did not run again. He thought the political life was over, and he gave himself to the law. What brought him back was the Kansas-Nebraska Act. At Peoria in 1854 he said what he would keep saying: slavery was a moral, social, and political wrong; he had no lawful right and no wish to interfere with it in the states where it existed; he would oppose its spread into the territories with everything he had. The distinction was the man. It was also the limit of the party that was about to form around it.

He helped make that party in Illinois, lost a Senate seat to Lyman Trumbull when his own supporters had to give way, and in 1858 ran against Douglas. Accepting the nomination, he told the Republicans that a house divided against itself cannot stand, and that the government could not endure permanently half slave and half free. He did not expect the Union to dissolve. He expected it to become all one thing or all the other. He lost the election and won the audience. At Cooper Union in 1860 he argued that the founders had treated slavery as an evil to be contained, and that the Republicans were the conservatives. The Chicago convention took him because Seward was too hot for the lower North and because Lincoln had spent six years saying the same thing without frightening the men who still wanted a Union.

He came to the office still inside that argument. He did not propose to abolish slavery where it stood. He did not believe the Constitution gave him that power, and he said so. He did believe the Union was perpetual, that secession was not a constitutional right, and that a republic which had stopped believing the Declaration’s promise was already changing into something else. The ambition had been large since New Salem. The policy he carried in was not a new kind of government. It was the Whig program of tariff, homestead, and railroad, joined to a refusal to let slavery become national. The war was not yet the policy. The refusal was.

In the office

The war was a break in the use of national power, not a small step past what Buchanan had declined to do. To hold the Union, Lincoln raised an army without precedent in the republic, taxed and borrowed on a new scale, suspended the writ of habeas corpus, and proclaimed emancipation in the states that were fighting him. He did these things as war measures, and they were not the peacetime government.

The party

The Republican party, formed in 1854 against the Kansas-Nebraska Act, had replaced the Whigs as the party of the North: former Whigs, Free Soilers, anti-Nebraska Democrats, and a portion of the old nativist vote, joined to keep slavery out of the territories and to pass a protective tariff, a homestead law, and a Pacific railroad. It did not propose immediate abolition in the states where slavery already existed, and it had almost no Southern wing. Buchanan’s Democrats had treated slave property in every territory as a constitutional right and had denied the Union any power to coerce a state. Lincoln’s party rejected the first of those positions and, once the war began, used the power Buchanan had refused.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    Customs could not carry the war, so Congress laid excises and the first federal tax on incomes, and the Treasury sold bonds on a scale no earlier administration had tried. The debt became a standing structure, not a sum to be cleared in a quiet decade. He financed a war and did not found a peacetime system of relief.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    The Morrill Tariff, which Buchanan had signed on the way out, was the Republican schedule, and later acts raised it. Protection was party doctrine, and the war made it revenue as well. The blockade was the other half of the policy: Southern cotton was to be kept in, and foreign ships were warned off.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    The Legal Tender Act of 1862 created greenbacks, a paper money not redeemable in gold on demand. The national banking acts of 1863 and 1864 taxed state bank notes out of circulation and put a uniform national bank-note currency in their place. Specie payment stopped, and nothing in the Independent Treasury of the 1850s resembled the result.

  4. 4. Labor

    The Enrollment Act of 1863 imposed a national draft, with a commutation fee and the hire of substitutes, and the New York riots that July were put down by troops. Wages and hours in private shops were still not federal law. An act of 1864 encouraged immigration so employers could fill the shortage of hands.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    The act of 1864 allowed passage to be advanced against future wages, and the homestead law reached foreigners who had declared their intent to become citizens. He signed no exclusion by nationality. The nativism of the 1850s did not govern a country that was drafting men.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    For the first year he held the war to be a war for the Union, and he revoked military emancipations in Missouri and the Department of the South that ran ahead of that line. On January 1, 1863, he proclaimed freedom for enslaved people in places still in rebellion, left slavery in the loyal border states untouched, and opened the army and navy to black soldiers; he then pressed the Thirteenth Amendment, which Congress passed in January 1865. Colonization, which he was still urging in 1862, was not what he argued in his last public speech, when he asked for a limited black suffrage in the restored South.

  7. 7. Federalism

    He denied that a state could leave the Union, and he governed the rebellion by blockade, military officers, and emancipation under the war power. He suspended habeas corpus over Taney’s protest in Merryman and did not yield; Congress later authorized the suspension. A president who could raise a national army, free people by proclamation, and try civilians before military commissions was using powers the peacetime republic had not used.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    He gave up the Confederate envoys taken off the Trent rather than add a British war to the rebellion, and Seward warned France out of Mexico without sending an army while the war at home continued. No European power recognized the Confederacy. He made no alliance to purchase that result, and he did not live to enforce the Monroe line against Maximilian.

  9. 9. National defense

    After Sumter he called out seventy-five thousand militia, then took volunteers by the hundred thousand, and in 1863 conscripted. The navy’s blockade was the other arm of the same effort. The regular army of the 1850s was not this force, and taking it apart was left to his successor.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    There was no established church and no religious test for office. He proclaimed days of national fasting and, on October 3, 1863, a day of thanksgiving on the last Thursday in November, as recommendations to the people rather than as a church calendar. The second inaugural read the war as a judgment on a nation implicated in slavery, theology in a state paper rather than an establishment.

  11. 11. Education

    The Morrill Act of 1862 gave each loyal state public land, scaled to its representation in Congress, to endow colleges of agriculture and the mechanic arts. It created no department that ran the common schools, and those schools stayed with the states. States then in rebellion were left out until they returned.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    The Homestead Act offered western farms to settlers who would occupy and improve them, and the Pacific Railway Acts paid a continental line in land and federal bonds. After the Dakota war in Minnesota he commuted most of the death sentences a military commission had returned and approved the hanging of thirty-eight men. Sand Creek, in 1864, was the killing of Cheyenne and Arapaho by territorial volunteers; Washington investigated it afterward and had not stopped it.

  13. 13. Energy

    Coal and the railroads carried the war economy. The government pushed a Pacific line with land and credit and did not own the mines or set a price of fuel. There was no department of energy and no public system of power.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    He treated the Confederacy as a war, which justified a blockade, and as a rebellion, which left its leaders open to a charge of treason and to pardon. The amnesty proclamation of December 1863 offered forgiveness to most who would swear loyalty, with classes of men excepted. Suspension of the writ and trial by military commission put civilians under a justice the ordinary federal courts did not control.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    There was no general poor law. The Freedmen’s Bureau, which he signed on March 3, 1865, was to feed refugees and freed people, oversee labor contracts, and manage abandoned lands. It was not a provision for every poor citizen, and outside that war measure relief stayed with states, towns, and voluntary societies.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    The United States Sanitary Commission was a voluntary body the War Department recognized. It inspected camps and supplied hospitals, while army medicine was reorganized for the wounded. Neither was a medical system for civilians, and he created no department of public health.

Of this presidency

  1. The war for the Union

    After Fort Sumter he called the militia, proclaimed a blockade, and held the border by politics and by force. Congress, meeting in July, confirmed the steps he had taken while it was adjourned. Conscription, suspension of habeas corpus, and military rule of occupied districts followed. He fought first to restore the Union. Emancipation was added when he judged that the Union required it.

  2. The Emancipation Proclamation

    On September 22, 1862, after Antietam, he warned that on January 1 he would free enslaved people in any state still in rebellion. The proclamation of that day did so. It did not free anyone in Delaware, Maryland, Kentucky, Missouri, or in districts already under Union control, and it rested on the war power of the commander, not on a claim that the Constitution had always authorized abolition. He spent what was left of his presidency pressing the Thirteenth Amendment, which abolished slavery in the whole country and was ratified after he was dead.

  3. Homestead and the Pacific railway

    The Homestead Act of May 20, 1862, and the Pacific Railway Act of July 1, 1862, were the economic program a Southern bloc had been able to stop before secession. One offered 160 acres to citizens and intending citizens who would live on the land and improve it. The other, enlarged in 1864, chartered a road to the Pacific and paid the companies in public land and a federal loan of bonds. Free settlement and a subsidized corporation were both uses of national resources the prewar government had debated and not made.

  4. Gettysburg

    On November 19, 1863, at the cemetery, he defined the Union as a nation committed from its origin to human equality, and he said the dead had left the living a duty to finish the war. The address enacted no statute and freed no one. It rejected the theory that the United States was only a compact a state might abandon.

  5. The assassination

    On the night of April 14, 1865, John Wilkes Booth shot him at Ford’s Theatre. He died the next morning, five days after Lee’s surrender. Booth acted in the Confederacy’s name and meant others in the administration to die with him; Seward was wounded the same night and lived. The murder did not restore slavery or the Confederacy. It made Andrew Johnson president of the peace.

PreviousDred Scott v. Sandford NextThe Thirteenth Amendment

1865

The Thirteenth Amendment

Congress proposed the amendment while Lincoln was alive. The states finished ratifying it after he was dead. It is the first amendment that gave the national government a power over a domestic institution the original Constitution had protected.

Amendment

The Thirteenth Amendment

Ratified December 6, 1865

How it was made

On January 31, 1865, after an earlier failure in the House, Congress sent the states an article abolishing slavery and involuntary servitude, except as punishment for crime, and giving Congress power to enforce it. Ratification was completed under Andrew Johnson. The Emancipation Proclamation had freed people in places the Union arms had not yet secured, as a war measure. The amendment made abolition constitutional law in every state, including those the proclamation had not reached.

What it changed

Slavery ceased to be a form of property the federal government was bound to respect. The enforcement section was new: Congress could legislate directly against the institution, not only against the international trade. The exception for criminal punishment remained, and Southern states soon used it. The amendment changed the Constitution. It did not, by itself, make the freed people citizens or voters. Those were the next two articles.

PreviousAbraham Lincoln NextAndrew Johnson

1865–1869

Andrew Johnson

National Union / Democrat

Before the office

He was born in 1808 in Raleigh, North Carolina, as poor as any man who ever reached the office. His father died when he was small. His mother took in washing. He was bound out as an apprentice to a tailor, and he never sat a day in a school. He learned his letters while he worked, and when he ran away from the shop the notice that followed him offered a reward of ten dollars. The grievance of that beginning never left him. He had been owned, for a term of years, by a master’s contract, and the people who looked down on a tailor were the people he spent the rest of his life trying to beat.

He crossed the mountains into Greeneville, Tennessee, opened a shop, and in 1827 married Eliza McCardle. She had the schooling he lacked, and she taught him to write a cleaner hand and to read with more system. A man was hired to read aloud while Johnson sewed. The shop became a political place. He was alderman, then mayor, then a legislator, then a congressman, governor, and senator, and at every step he ran as the mechanic against the plantation. He was a Jacksonian of the strictest kind: the Union, the Constitution as written, a cheap government, and free land for the man who would work it. The homestead was his cause for years before Lincoln signed it. He held a few slaves in Greeneville. That fact did not, in his mind, put him with the aristocrats. It confirmed him. A tailor might own labor and still hate the class that had shut men like him out of power.

He believed slavery was the South’s institution and white government was the only government he meant to defend. He also believed secession was a crime. When Tennessee left the Union he stayed in the Senate, the only senator from a seceded state who did. His property was taken, his family was harassed, and he called the doctrine what he thought it was, a heresy that would dissolve any government that admitted it. Lincoln sent him back in 1862 as military governor. He required oaths, jailed the disloyal, and rebuilt a loyal government with a hard hand. The radicals thought they had found a southern man who would punish the planters. He thought he was saving his state for the white Union men who had never left it.

In 1864 the Republicans needed a War Democrat on the ticket, and they took him. The party name that year was National Union, which described the bargain and not a new faith. He was still the Greeneville Democrat. At the inauguration in March 1865 he was ill, he drank, and the speech he made was a humiliation the city did not forget. Six weeks later Lincoln was dead and the tailor was president. He did not think the office required him to become a Republican. He thought it required him to finish the war’s one necessary result, the death of secession, and then to restore the states to their old place in the Union, governed by the white men who lived in them.

That was the whole of the program he brought to the oath. Pardons for the mass of Confederates who would swear loyalty. The planter leaders humbled, or forgiven when it suited him, but not left as a permanent ruling class if he could help it. The freed people released from slavery and not made into a political nation by act of Congress. A state, once it had renounced secession and accepted the end of slavery, was a state again, and Congress had no warrant to keep it out or to write its civil law. He had spent thirty years saying the Constitution did not authorize a federal charity or a federal rearrangement of society. The Freedmen’s Bureau and a civil-rights bill looked to him like both.

He could not see that the war had changed the warrant. The Union he had refused to leave was, for him, the Union of 1860 with the rebellion beaten and the slaves freed, not a Union in which the general government would stand between a state and the people it had just emancipated. When he vetoed the Civil Rights Bill he said no such system had ever been proposed, and that the safeguards it gave the colored race went beyond any the government had given the white. The sentence was not a new cruelty invented in the White House. It was the tailor’s democracy, intact: the common white man as the people, the Constitution as the fence around the states, and no power in Washington to build a different fence because a war had been won.

In the office

He inherited the victory and the break in national power the war had made, and he tried to close that break at once. Congress refused, and imposed military reconstruction over his vetoes. For four years the federal government remained far inside the Southern states, on a scale the prewar republic had not used, while the president and the Republican Congress fought over who would hold that power and to what end.

The party

The National Union name of 1864 was a wartime fusion, a Republican president with a War Democrat from Tennessee on the ticket to hold the border, and Johnson was that Democrat. In the office he governed as a states’-rights man of the old school, restoring the Southern states quickly, accepting white governments, and vetoing federal protection of the freed people. Congressional Republicans broke with him in 1866, passed their program over his vetoes, and impeached him. The party fact of the term is that break, not the survival of Lincoln’s coalition.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    The war debt dominated the Treasury, and he wanted economy, a quick return to civil expenses, and contraction of the paper currency. He did not ask for taxes to support a standing social program. The spending he fought was the Freedmen’s Bureau, which he called charity without a constitutional warrant and a machine for ruling the South.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    The protective tariff left by the war stayed in force. He was a low-tariff Democrat by his whole past and did not have the votes to rewrite the schedule. Republican Congresses kept protection, and the tariff was not the ground on which he and they divided.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    Secretary McCulloch retired greenbacks toward a return to specie, and Johnson supported the direction. In 1868 Congress ordered the contraction stopped. He founded no new bank and opened no silver question, and a smaller issue of paper and a cheaper government were the old Democratic habits he still practiced.

  4. 4. Labor

    In 1868 he signed an eight-hour day for laborers and mechanics employed by the United States, and for nobody else. Private shops were untouched. In the South the labor question was the contract that replaced slavery, and he wanted that contract left to the restored states.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    The Burlingame Treaty of 1868 recognized free migration between the United States and China. He signed no exclusion act. European immigrants continued to be naturalized under the old rule, which still confined naturalization to white persons.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    He required the states he restored to ratify the Thirteenth Amendment, and he opposed every federal guarantee beyond abolition. Black Codes in those states bound freed people to labor and cut down their standing in court, and white mobs killed black people in Memphis and New Orleans in 1866; he did not treat the codes or the killings as a reason to withhold recognition. He vetoed the Civil Rights Act, urged the South to reject the Fourteenth Amendment, and described equal citizenship as a danger to white government.

  7. 7. Federalism

    He held that the states had never left the Union and that the president, not Congress, should restore them on his terms. Congress, over his vetoes, divided the former Confederacy into military districts and required new constitutions with black male suffrage. He obstructed that settlement where he could, above all through the army, and could not repeal a federal power over those states that stayed, for the whole term, far past any prewar claim.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    Seward’s purchase of Alaska from Russia was the acquisition of the term. Johnson pressed France to withdraw from Mexico, made no alliance, and sent no army. The French left, Maximilian was captured and executed by Mexican republicans, and relations with Europe otherwise stayed diplomatic.

  9. 9. National defense

    The volunteer armies were sent home quickly, and the regular force shrank toward a frontier size. What remained in the South was used by Congress’s direction more than by his. He wanted those soldiers out of politics, while Indian fighting in the West resumed as the wartime regiments disbanded.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    He made no establishment. Northern churches and missionary societies, with the Bureau he was trying to kill, did much of the teaching and relief of the freed people. He did not turn that work into a national church, and thanksgiving proclamations continued as civic custom rather than as a new religious power.

  11. 11. Education

    Bureau schools and church societies taught large numbers of freed people, and his assault on the Bureau was an assault on that effort. In 1867 he signed a Department of Education whose work was to collect statistics; within a year it was cut down to a minor office and it never ran a school. Common education remained with the states.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    By pardon and by order he returned most of the abandoned coastal plantations that wartime arrangements had set aside for freed people. There was no general confiscation of Confederate land. The Southern Homestead Act of 1866 opened some public land in the South to settlement, while Lincoln’s homestead law continued in the West and the Indian wars started again.

  13. 13. Energy

    Coal and the railroads recovered as private business. He created no fuel policy and no publicly owned system of power. The federal relation to that economy was land grants and mail contracts already in motion, not an energy program.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    He did not bring Jefferson Davis or the other Confederate chiefs to trial. Special pardons covered men of wealth whom his own proclamation had excepted, and the amnesty of December 25, 1868, covered the rest. Racial killing in the South produced no federal enforcement act while he was president, and the criminal proceeding that defined the term was his own impeachment.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    The Freedmen’s Bureau was the federal relief of these years: rations for freed people and white refugees, supervision of contracts, hospitals, and courts. He vetoed its renewal as a charity the Constitution did not authorize and as an army quartered on the South. Congress overrode him, and there was still no national poor law for the country at large.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    Bureau hospitals gave freed people some medical care, the nearest approach to a federal health service in these years, and a temporary one that followed from emancipation rather than a system for citizens at large. The Sanitary Commission of the war had been voluntary and was finished. Everyone else relied on private doctors and local charity.

Of this presidency

  1. The vetoes

    He vetoed the Freedmen’s Bureau bill in February 1866 and the Civil Rights Act in March. The civil-rights bill made the freed people citizens and secured their right to contract, sue, testify, and own property; Congress overrode that veto in April, the first override of a major law, and overrode a second Bureau bill in July. Reconstruction acts and the Tenure of Office Act went the same way. He called each of them an invasion of states he had already declared restored.

  2. The Fourteenth Amendment

    Congress proposed the amendment in June 1866: a national citizenship, restraints on the states, a cut in representation where male suffrage was denied, a bar on leading Confederates holding office, and a voiding of the Confederate debt. He denounced it and advised the Southern legislatures to reject it. All of them but Tennessee did so, and he lost the elections of 1866 on that advice. The amendment was ratified in 1868 because Congress made ratification the price of readmission.

  3. Impeachment

    The Tenure of Office Act, passed over his veto, forbade removal of a Senate-confirmed officer without the Senate’s consent. He suspended Secretary of War Stanton, who was acting with Congress, and in February 1868 removed him. The House impeached him. The Senate tried him with the Chief Justice presiding and, on May 16, 1868, acquitted him by a single vote. He served out the term without recovering Reconstruction.

  4. The Black Codes

    Legislatures restored under his proclamations passed codes that tied black workers to written contracts, turned vagrancy into a means of forced labor, and barred black testimony against white men. He treated these as the ordinary police laws of states whose restoration he had completed. His veto of the Civil Rights Act said federal protection of the most basic civil rights was both unwise and unconstitutional. The codes, more than his speeches, showed what that restoration produced, and Congress wrote the civil-rights bill and the Fourteenth Amendment against them.

PreviousThe Thirteenth Amendment NextMilligan and the Fourteenth Amendment

1866–1868

Milligan and the Fourteenth Amendment

In the same years the Court told the government it could not try civilians by military commission where the ordinary courts were open, and the country rewrote who a citizen is.

Decision

Ex parte Milligan

Decided April 3, 1866

How it was made

Lambdin Milligan, a civilian in Indiana, had been sentenced to death by a military commission for conspiracy against the Union. The civil courts in Indiana were open. He asked the Supreme Court for habeas corpus.

Who sat

All nine agreed that Milligan’s conviction could not stand. They split five to four on the reason. Davis’s majority said neither president nor Congress could use a military court in those circumstances. Chase’s concurrence said Congress could have authorized it, and had not.

With the opinion

  • David Davis — Lincoln
  • Samuel Nelson — Tyler
  • Robert Grier — Polk
  • Nathan Clifford — Buchanan
  • Stephen Field — Lincoln

Concurring

  • Salmon Chase — Lincoln
  • James Wayne — Jackson
  • Noah Swayne — Lincoln
  • Samuel Miller — Lincoln

What it changed

A wartime president’s use of a military commission against a civilian, away from the fighting, was declared illegal. The limit was judicial, not statutory. Lincoln’s own appointees divided on how far the limit ran, and the chief justice he had named would have left Congress a door the majority closed.

Amendment

The Fourteenth Amendment

Ratified July 9, 1868

How it was made

Congress proposed it in June 1866. Johnson urged the states to reject it. Ratification was made a condition of restoring Confederate states to Congress, and it was completed in 1868. The article defines national citizenship by birth or naturalization, forbids a state to abridge the privileges or immunities of citizens of the United States, to deprive any person of life, liberty, or property without due process, or to deny equal protection of the laws. It reduces a state’s representation in the House if the right to vote is denied to adult male citizens. It disqualifies certain insurgent officeholders until Congress removes the disability. It repudiates Confederate debt and confirms the Union debt. Section 5 gives Congress power to enforce the article.

What it changed

Birth in the United States, not state grace, makes a citizen. A state, not only Congress, is now under constitutional restraints a person can invoke, and Congress has a text to legislate from. The amendment did not itself integrate schools, reapportion legislatures, or require the states to let black men vote. Later Courts and later statutes did that work with this text, and for a long time they declined to. The change in 1868 was the text and the power. The use of them was contested from the first case.

PreviousAndrew Johnson NextUlysses S. Grant

1869–1877

Ulysses S. Grant

Republican

Before the office

He was born in 1822 at Point Pleasant, Ohio, and christened Hiram Ulysses Grant. His father, Jesse, was a tanner who talked and pushed. His mother, Hannah, was a silent Methodist who did not. The boy hated the tannery, the blood and the smell of it, and loved horses with a competence that was already a kind of authority. When a congressman appointed him to West Point he got the name wrong, Ulysses S. Grant, and the young man let the name stand. He graduated in 1843 in the middle of his class. He was not a scholar. He was the best rider on the post, and he was quiet in a way that made louder men mistake him for someone who had nothing to say.

The Mexican War gave him a trade and a doubt. He served as a quartermaster under Taylor and then in Scott’s march to Mexico City, which is to say he learned how an army eats, moves, and does not fall apart. He admired Taylor’s plainness. He did not admire the war. He would later call it one of the most unjust a stronger nation had ever waged against a weaker one, and he did the work anyway. In 1848 he married Julia Dent. She had grown up at White Haven, in Missouri, in a house that held slaves. His own people in Ohio were against the institution. The marriage crossed that line and held for the rest of his life. She was the person who steadied him. When she was not there, he came apart.

The coming apart was the next ten years. At a miserable post in California, far from her, he drank. The drinking was a binge, not a daily soak, and it was enough. In 1854 he resigned rather than face what his commanding officer was preparing for him. Out of the army he failed at farming on a scrap of land he called Hardscrabble, failed at selling wood, and ended as a clerk in his father’s leather store in Galena, working under his younger brothers. He was past thirty-five and he looked finished. In those Missouri years he held one man in slavery, William Jones, and in 1859, when money was the thing he did not have, he freed him instead of selling him. It was a small act. It was also the opposite of the market he was living in.

The war returned him to the only work he had ever done well. He asked for a regiment, got the 21st Illinois, and then a brigade. At Fort Donelson he told a Confederate commander there were no terms but unconditional surrender, and the newspapers made the initials into a name. Shiloh nearly ruined him. He was surprised, he held, and he was not removed. Vicksburg was the opposite of a gambler’s luck: a campaign of rivers, ditches, and patience that cut the Confederacy in two. In 1864 Lincoln made him lieutenant general and sent him after Lee. The summer of that year cost more blood than the country had imagined a general would spend. He did not maneuver the Army of Northern Virginia out of a position and call it a victory. He destroyed it. At Appomattox he gave the men their paroles and their horses, so they could go home and plant a crop. The generosity was real. So was the fact that he had required the surrender first.

Johnson thought the general who had been generous at the surrender would be generous about the peace, which to Johnson meant white governments restored at once. Grant was general of the army, and the president tried to use him in the fight over Stanton and the Tenure of Office Act. Grant would not be used. He sided with the Congress that meant to keep the verdict of the war, and Johnson took it as a betrayal. What had changed in Grant was not a theory learned from a book. The war had made emancipation one of his instruments, Black soldiers had served under his command, and he had watched the men he paroled go home to a South that would take the freedom back if nobody stopped it. He was still not a politician. He was a soldier who had decided the peace had to be kept with the same attention he had given the siege.

The Republicans nominated him in 1868 because he was that soldier, and because Johnson’s party, whatever it called itself, would not enforce the laws the war had produced. He accepted in a short letter. He would administer all the laws in good faith, with economy, and with the view of giving peace, quiet, and protection everywhere. Then the line the campaign painted on banners: let us have peace. He did not mean Johnson’s peace. He meant a peace in which the laws, including the ones that protected the freed people, were actually kept. The man who wrote it had failed at every civilian trade he had tried, had drunk himself out of the peacetime army, and had freed one slave when he could have sold him. The presidency was the next assignment, and he took it the way he had taken Vicksburg, as a piece of work that had to be finished.

In the office

Prosecution of the Ku Klux Klan and enforcement of the Fifteenth Amendment put national criminal law onto private violence, which the prewar government had left to the states. He carried that work further than Johnson would have allowed. By the end of the second term the Northern will to sustain it had failed, and a return toward a more limited federal role in the South had begun, without a repeal of the amendments.

The party

Johnson had set the presidency against the Republican Congress. Grant’s election returned the office to the party that had imposed military reconstruction and that now stood for the Fifteenth Amendment, federal prosecution of political terror, the protective tariff, and payment of the war bonds in coin. That was a different use of the party from anything Johnson would accept. The commitment then weakened inside Republican ranks themselves: the Liberal revolt of 1872 and the fatigue of the second term were a retreat from enforcement, not a change of the party’s name.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    The Public Credit Act of 1869 pledged that the bonded war debt would be paid in coin. Ordinary spending sat between the wartime peak and the antebellum government: army, navy, Indians, pensions, and the civil list. The panic of 1873 produced no federal relief budget.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    The tariff remained protective. A reduction of rates in 1872 was repealed in 1875 when customs revenue sagged. He was not a free trader, and the Republican party was not becoming one.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    He vetoed the inflation bill of 1874, which would have added to the greenbacks, and he signed the Resumption Act of 1875, fixing January 1, 1879, as the day paper would be redeemed in coin. The Coinage Act of 1873 dropped the standard silver dollar from the coins to be minted. He wanted specie and national bank notes rather than a new issue of paper, and redemption itself came under Hayes.

  4. 4. Labor

    He ordered that the eight-hour day already granted to federal workers not be used as a pretext to cut their daily wage, and private industry was outside that order. The depression after 1873 idled large numbers of men, and he offered no wage law and no board of arbitration. The great railroad strike fell to his successor.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    The naturalization act of 1870 admitted immigrants of African descent to citizenship and left Chinese immigrants ineligible. The Page Act of 1875, which he signed, restricted the entry of Chinese women and of coerced labor from Asia. European immigrants were still drawn by open land, and a general Chinese exclusion was not yet law.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    He enforced the Fifteenth Amendment with criminal statutes and, in South Carolina, with troops and a suspension of habeas corpus, and he signed the Civil Rights Act of 1875 against exclusion from inns, public conveyances, and places of amusement. The second term was a retreat: he intervened less often, and he treated Northern weariness of election-season calls for troops as a fact he would not forever override. The statutes remained while the practice of using them thinned.

  7. 7. Federalism

    The Enforcement Acts made a private conspiracy against constitutional rights a federal crime, which older practice had left entirely to the states. He used that power, and he used the army to sustain Republican state governments, and then he used both less. By 1876 the drift back toward state control of Southern politics was plain, and the amendments that still bound those states were not repealed.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    The Treaty of Washington in 1871 sent the Alabama claims against Britain to arbitration and avoided both a war and an alliance. His project to annex Santo Domingo died in the Senate. The Virginius affair of 1873 brought a sharp dispute with Spain over Cuba and ended without a war, and with Maximilian already dead Mexico remained a neighbor rather than a protectorate.

  9. 9. National defense

    The army was small, and it was occupied with duty in the South and with wars on the Plains, including the campaign that ended at the Little Bighorn in 1876. Congress cut at the appropriation, and the navy was left to decay. There was no return to the mass army of 1865, and no quiet establishment of forts alone.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    In 1875 he asked for a constitutional amendment to keep public money from religious schools. It did not pass, and there was no establishment. His Indian peace policy did put church-nominated agents into federal posts, which mixed denominations into administration without making a national church.

  11. 11. Education

    The Bureau’s school work ended when the Bureau ended. He founded no national system of common schools, and his proposal to bar tax money to sectarian schools failed in Congress. Such public schools as the reconstructed states built were state institutions.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    Homestead entries went on, and so did railroad land grants, with Credit Mobilier as the scandal of one subsidized line. His peace policy handed Indian agencies to religious societies and pressed the nations onto reservations. War continued with the Modoc, on the southern Plains, and against the Sioux, so that reservation and campaign remained the policy together.

  13. 13. Energy

    Coal and the railroad corporations were the system that mattered, and both were private. Federal land grants and bond subsidies, most of them already given, were the government’s relation to that system. He created no office to manage fuel or set its price.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    The Department of Justice, created in 1870, carried the Klan cases into the federal courts, and those prosecutions were the serious federal criminal work of the term. They broke the first Klan as an organization and did not end lynching or election murder. The Whiskey Ring showed the same government unable to keep its own revenue officers honest.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    He let the Freedmen’s Bureau die. The panic of 1873 and the long depression after it brought no federal relief, no jobs appropriation, and no poor law. Charity, city aid, and the vagrancy statutes of the states were what the unemployed had.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    In 1870 the Marine Hospital Service for seamen was reorganized under a supervising surgeon, a tighter administration of a small and old service. It was not medicine for the public. Care of the civilian sick stayed private, and epidemics stayed with cities and states.

Of this presidency

  1. The Fifteenth Amendment and the Enforcement Acts

    The amendment had been sent to the states before he was inaugurated and was ratified in February 1870. He treated it as a law to be executed rather than as advice to the South. The Enforcement Acts of 1870 and 1871 made conspiracy against the new constitutional rights a federal crime and provided for supervision of elections. The Department of Justice, created in 1870, brought the prosecutions.

  2. The Ku Klux Klan Act

    The act of April 20, 1871, authorized the army against combinations that attacked the freed people’s rights and, where such combinations overawed a state, a suspension of habeas corpus. That October he suspended the writ in nine counties of South Carolina and sent troops. Federal trials there broke the Klan as a mass organization. The convictions were real, and they were not a permanent peace.

  3. The Whiskey Ring and Belknap

    Credit Mobilier, exposed in 1872, was Congress’s scandal: insiders of the subsidized Union Pacific took the construction profits and placed shares with members and with the vice president. The Whiskey Ring was the administration’s. Distillers and revenue officers stole the tax on spirits; Grant’s private secretary, Orville Babcock, was indicted; and a deposition from the president helped the jury acquit him. Secretary of War Belknap sold appointments to Indian traderships, resigned when the scheme broke in 1876, and was impeached after he had left office. The Senate did not convict. Grant was not shown to have taken the money. He had kept men who did.

  4. The Treaty of Washington

    The treaty of May 8, 1871, sent to arbitration at Geneva the claims for Confederate cruisers fitted out in British yards, and it also settled a boundary and a fisheries dispute. The tribunal awarded damages against Britain. There was no alliance and no war. A claim was pressed, and then it was submitted to a judgment.

PreviousMilligan and the Fourteenth Amendment NextThe Fifteenth Amendment and the Slaughterhouse Cases

1870–1873

The Fifteenth Amendment and the Slaughterhouse Cases

The last of the three Reconstruction articles forbade racial exclusion from the vote. The first great case under the Fourteenth Amendment then refused to read that article as a transfer of ordinary civil rights from the states to the nation.

Amendment

The Fifteenth Amendment

Ratified February 3, 1870

How it was made

Congress proposed it in February 1869. The right of citizens to vote shall not be denied or abridged by the United States or by any state on account of race, color, or previous condition of servitude. Congress may enforce the article. It does not forbid denial of the vote for other reasons: sex, literacy, poll taxes, residence.

What it changed

Racial exclusion from the suffrage became unconstitutional, and Congress received an enforcement power it used in the statutes of the early 1870s. The amendment did not stop a state from accomplishing a similar result by tests written in neutral words. Those devices lasted until statutes and decisions of the 1960s. The constitutional prohibition was nonetheless a change the original Constitution had not contained, and that the Fourteenth Amendment had not stated.

Decision

The Slaughterhouse Cases

Decided April 14, 1873

How it was made

Louisiana had given one company a monopoly of slaughtering in New Orleans. Rival butchers said the monopoly destroyed their trade and violated the new Fourteenth Amendment. It was the Court’s first reading of that amendment.

Who sat

Five to four. Miller wrote. Lincoln’s appointees split. Grant’s split. The chief justice Lincoln had named was in dissent.

With the opinion

  • Samuel Miller — Lincoln
  • Nathan Clifford — Buchanan
  • David Davis — Lincoln
  • William Strong — Grant
  • Ward Hunt — Grant

In dissent

  • Stephen Field — Lincoln
  • Salmon Chase — Lincoln
  • Noah Swayne — Lincoln
  • Joseph Bradley — Grant

What it changed

Miller held that the privileges or immunities of national citizenship are a short list, and that the ordinary rights of earning a living remain with the states. The due process and equal protection clauses were left for later cases, and were described as aimed chiefly at discrimination against the freed people. The amendment stayed in the Constitution. Its broadest clause was closed on the day the Court first construed it. The dissenters would have made the amendment a general limit on state interference with a citizen’s calling. That reading lost, five to four, and the loss shaped federalism for decades.

PreviousUlysses S. Grant NextRutherford B. Hayes

1877–1881

Rutherford B. Hayes

Republican

Before the office

He was born in 1822 in Delaware, Ohio, after his father had already died. Sophia Birchard Hayes raised him. The money and the direction came from her brother, Sardis Birchard, a banker in the town that became Fremont. There was no cabin legend in it and no apprenticeship. There was a sister, Fanny, who read what he read and pushed him, and there was a careful education: Kenyon College, where he was valedictorian, and the Harvard law school. He came back to Ohio a lawyer of the old Whig kind, earnest, a little stiff, and convinced that a republic was a moral enterprise before it was a machine.

He practiced in Cincinnati, on the river, where the Fugitive Slave Act was not a theory. He took cases for men who had run, and he joined the Republicans when the Kansas-Nebraska Act made the Whig compromise unbearable. In 1852 he married Lucy Webb. She had been to college. She was an abolitionist and a Methodist, and she would not have liquor in her house. The marriage was the center of his life. Children died young.
