The household that remained was serious, religious, and political in the same breath. She was not an ornament of the career. She was one of the reasons the career had the shape it had.

The war made him a soldier without making him a Grant. He took a majority in the 23rd Ohio, fought in western Virginia and the Shenandoah, and was badly wounded at South Mountain in 1862. A young commissary in the regiment was William McKinley. Hayes rose to a brevet major general by being present and being brave, not by a genius for the large campaign. He came out of it a Republican who had bled for the Union and who thought the peace owed something to the men, white and black, who had held it. He went to Congress while the amendments were being written, and he supported them. Then Ohio made him governor. He was a hard-money man in a state full of men who wanted more greenbacks, and he was a reformer in a party that lived on offices. He backed the vote for black men in Ohio and paid for it, and he was elected again.

By 1876 the party needed a candidate who was not Grant and not Blaine. Grant’s second term had stained the name. Blaine was the favorite of the regulars and was carrying a railroad scandal. Hayes was a governor who had fought, who had promised nothing corrupt, and who would pledge a single term. He took the nomination as a duty. He believed the civil service had to be taken back from the senators who treated offices as their own, and he believed the paper money of the war had to be brought back to coin. Those were Whig habits in a Republican year: sound currency, competent administration, and a suspicion of the men who made politics a business.

The election did not give him a clean title, and he knew the country would say so. Tilden had more votes in the popular count. The returns in Louisiana, Florida, and South Carolina were fought over by rival boards, in states where black men had been beaten away from the polls and where both parties had used whatever power they had. An electoral commission of eight Republicans and seven Democrats gave him every disputed vote. He believed the certified returns were his and that terror, not a bargain, explained the confusion. His enemies said the presidency had been sold. What he had concluded, while the count was still being fought, was that the troops still sitting in the last Republican statehouses could not be the permanent government of the South. The North would not sustain it. He would not pretend that it could.

He took the oath meaning to end that occupation and to call what followed home rule, and he took it also meaning that the amendments remained the law. In the inaugural he told the southern states that he wanted the interests of the white and of the colored people both and equally, and a civil policy that would wipe out the color line and the distinction between North and South. He thought conciliation would do what the bayonet had stopped being able to do. He thought white southerners, restored to the ordinary dignity of self-government, would keep the rights the war had written down. That hope was the branch he brought into the office. It was a hope about the same Constitution Grant had been enforcing. It was not Grant’s method, and it left the freed people to governments that did not share it.

In the office

In April 1877 he withdrew the last federal troops from the work of holding Republican statehouses in South Carolina and Louisiana, and the military phase of Reconstruction ended. The national government turned back toward a more limited hand in the politics of the states. It did not repeal the Thirteenth, Fourteenth, or Fifteenth Amendments, and he vetoed bills that would have stripped away the federal election laws.

The party

He was a Republican, as Grant had been, and the name of the party did not change, but the commitment did. Grant’s Republicans had still prosecuted the Klan and had used troops, unevenly, to uphold Republican governments in the South. Hayes withdrew the last of those troops, called the result home rule, and left the black vote to Democratic state governments that did not mean to honor it. Reform of the civil service and a hard currency set him apart from the Stalwarts without making him the continuer of Grant’s Reconstruction.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    He went on paying the debt and defended the gold resumption Grant had set on the calendar. He added no new tax and opened no relief budget for the depression. The fight with Congress over money was about silver, not about a larger civil government.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    The protective tariff stayed where the war and the Republican Congresses had put it. He was a moderate protectionist and secured no general revision. Trade was inheritance, and currency, troops, and offices were the fights he actually had.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    On January 1, 1879, the Treasury resumed specie payment on the greenbacks, and the gold reserve held. He vetoed the Bland-Allison bill, which ordered a limited monthly purchase and coinage of silver, as a step off that standard. Congress overrode the veto, and he carried out the purchases he had refused to approve.

  4. 4. Labor

    When the railroad strike of July 1877 stopped the trunk lines, he sent regular troops, at the request of governors, to protect the mails and federal property and to get trains moving. It was the first use of the army in a labor rising on a national scale. He proposed no law on wages, hours, or the right to combine.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    In 1879 he vetoed a bill limiting ships from China to fifteen Chinese passengers, because it broke the Burlingame Treaty, and he sent commissioners to renegotiate. The treaty concluded in 1880 allowed the United States to regulate, limit, or suspend Chinese immigration and did not authorize a flat and permanent ban. He would restrict by a revised treaty and not by a statute that violated the one in force.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    Taking the troops away let Democratic Redeemers finish off the last Republican state governments, and the black voting those governments had rested on was not saved by the goodwill he said would replace the army. He still vetoed riders meant to repeal the federal election laws, so the statutes outlived him. The amendments remained written, and the occupation that had given them practical force did not.

  7. 7. Federalism

    Home rule, in his usage, meant that Washington would stop deciding who held a Southern government. He signed the army bill that contained the Posse Comitatus Act of 1878, which barred the regular army from acting as a civilian posse unless the Constitution or a statute expressly allowed it. Authority over ordinary politics moved back toward the states, but not all the way, because he used the veto to keep the national election laws.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    There was no European alliance and no new doctrine. He allowed a limited military pursuit of raiders across the Mexican border, which Mexico protested and which was later narrowed, and he did not intervene in Mexico’s government. The Chinese question was handled by negotiation, and nothing in the term was a foreign war.

  9. 9. National defense

    He used the small army in the strike and in Indian campaigns, among them the pursuit of the Nez Perce in 1877, and he stopped using it to decide elections of state officers. The navy was not rebuilt. What remained was a frontier constabulary and a neglected fleet.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    He made no establishment, and public worship stayed voluntary. The argument over tax support for church schools, which Grant had opened, produced no amendment in his term. Polygamy in Utah he left as a territorial question for a later criminal statute.

  11. 11. Education

    He commended common schools, in the South especially, as a substitute for the troops he was withdrawing, and Congress gave him no federal school fund. The land-grant colleges went on under the Morrill Act. Day-to-day schooling remained a state and local charge.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    Homestead and preemption continued, and the Timber and Stone Act of 1878 let settlers buy certain nonfarm land, a disposal of the public domain that was much abused. Indian policy was still reservation and war together. Secretary Schurz reformed pieces of the Indian service and did not stop the campaigns, and the Nez Perce were turned back short of Canada.

  13. 13. Energy

    The strike of 1877 began on the railroads and spread into the coal fields. Both the roads and the mines were private. He used troops to reopen movement and did not take over a line, regulate a rate, or set a price of coal.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    He accepted the Posse Comitatus limit on the army and refused to accept repeal of the election statutes. Enforcement of those statutes slackened with the political decision of 1877. Ordinary crime remained a state business, and Reconstruction’s criminal law, though still on the books, had less and less force behind it.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    The depression that opened in 1873 was not over. He proposed no federal relief and no public employment of the jobless. Counties, churches, and private societies carried them, and the troops of 1877 protected property and the mails rather than wages.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    The yellow-fever epidemic of 1878 along the lower Mississippi produced the Quarantine Act of that year, a limited port authority for the Marine Hospital Service. It was a quarantine, not a system of hospitals for the sick poor. Medical care otherwise stayed private and local.

Of this presidency

  1. The withdrawal of 1877

    The election of 1876 was disputed, and an electoral commission with a Republican majority gave Hayes every contested vote. He took the office, and in April 1877 he removed the federal soldiers still sustaining the Republican claimants in South Carolina and Louisiana. Those governments fell at once. He called the change local self-government and said law would protect black citizens better than troops would. The governments that replaced them did not secure the vote the amendments described.

  2. Specie resumption

    He held the Treasury to the date in the Resumption Act Grant had signed. On the first day of 1879 the greenbacks became redeemable in gold, and the run that had been predicted did not empty the reserve. Congress then passed Bland-Allison over his veto. A limited silver purchase was added to a currency that was, at last, convertible.

  3. The Great Railroad Strike

    Wage cuts on the Baltimore and Ohio in July 1877 spread from road to road and from city to city. Militia in some places fired on crowds and in others would not act. Hayes sent regulars to reopen the lines and protect federal property. He did not judge the wage, and he did not claim a peacetime power to govern labor. He claimed a power to restore the mails and the traffic.

  4. The election-law vetoes

    Democratic majorities tried to repeal the laws for federal supervision of elections, and to forbid troops at the polls, by riders on bills the government needed in order to function. He vetoed those bills more than once, and the statutes survived his term. In the same years he signed the Posse Comitatus Act. The two decisions belonged together: no army as the maker of a state government, and no surrender of every federal law on the vote.

PreviousThe Fifteenth Amendment and the Slaughterhouse Cases NextJames A. Garfield

1881

James A. Garfield

Republican

Before the office

He was born in 1831 in a log cabin in Orange Township, Ohio, and his father died before the boy was two. Eliza Ballou Garfield kept the family on almost nothing. He worked, and in his teens he hired on as a driver on the Ohio Canal. He fell overboard and nearly drowned, and he left the towpath. The canal boy of the later campaign was not an invention. It was also not the whole of him. What he wanted, with a hunger that never let up, was an education, and he got one by teaching while he studied. At the Western Reserve Eclectic Institute in Hiram he found the Disciples of Christ and a life of preaching and classrooms. Then Williams College took him, and he finished in 1856 near the head of the class. Mark Hopkins, the president there, was the teacher he measured every other teacher against. He came home to Hiram and soon ran the school.

In 1858 he married Lucretia Rudolph, who had been a student beside him and who was at least as strong as he was. The marriage had a break in it. In the war years he was unfaithful, she found it out, and the household nearly ended. They put it back together, and the later life was the life they made after that, not the courtship before it. She would be the person who watched him die. Before any of that he was a preacher, a teacher, and a young Ohio Republican of the radical kind: slavery was the wrong that had to be contained and then destroyed, and the party that would do it was his.

He raised the 42nd Ohio and in January 1862 won a small, clean fight at Middle Creek, in eastern Kentucky, which made his name in a war that had few clean fights. He served on Rosecrans’s staff and was at Chickamauga. While he was still in uniform the district elected him to Congress, and he took the seat in December 1863. He stayed in the House for seventeen years. The radical of the war became the man the House trusted with money: appropriations, the tariff, the long argument over whether the greenback would be brought back to gold. He was on the electoral commission that seated Hayes. He was also touched by the Crédit Mobilier scandal, a small transaction that left a large stain, and he survived it because the party needed his mind more than it needed his innocence. The mind was real. He read constantly, he spoke without notes, and he could hold both the equality the amendments had written and the unity a party required if it meant to keep winning.

That double hold is what the subtitle of his life comes down to. He had been a radical. He had wanted the freed people armed with the vote and protected in it. By the end of the 1870s he no longer believed an army could sit on the South, and he did not propose to send it back. He did believe there was no middle ground, under American institutions, between slavery and equal citizenship, and no future in a disfranchised peasantry. Education, in his view, was what would make the suffrage safe, not a repeal of the suffrage. He could say this and still be the man who had helped end the military occupation. The two positions were not, to him, a contradiction. They were the radicalism adapted to a country that had grown tired.

He went to the Chicago convention in 1880 to nominate John Sherman, not himself. The Stalwarts wanted Grant for a third term, and Roscoe Conkling was their manager. The other wing wanted Blaine. Ballot after ballot changed nothing. On the thirty-sixth the men who would not have Grant turned to the congressman who had been putting Sherman’s name in nomination, and who had asked them not to do this. He was stunned, and he was not unwilling for long. To keep the Stalwarts from bolting, the ticket took Chester Arthur, Conkling’s collector at the New York customhouse, for vice president. Garfield beat Winfield Scott Hancock by a narrow popular vote and a clear electoral one. He had spent his life preparing for a great place and had not expected this particular one. He took it as the scholar of the party, not as a general and not as a boss.

What he carried to the oath was that scholarship and that ambition, and a refusal to be the property of the faction that had been given the vice presidency to keep it quiet. The presidency was not a clerkship of the Senate. He said, in the inaugural, that the elevation of the black race from slavery to citizenship was the most important political change since 1787, and that there could be no permanent disfranchised peasantry in the United States. He did not have a program for sending troops to make the sentence true. He had the sentence, the gold standard, the protective tariff, and four months of health.

In the office

He was president from March 4 until his death on September 19, 1881, and from early July he was dying of a gunshot wound. He did not reopen the break of the war or of Reconstruction. The government of his few working weeks was the more limited one left by the withdrawal of 1877, with the three amendments still in the Constitution and without the troops that had once made them effective in the South.

The party

Hayes had already changed what the Republican party would do: the name survived, and the military occupation of Southern politics did not. Garfield did not reverse that choice. The fight of his months in office was inside the party, between Stalwarts around Conkling, who had wanted Grant for a third term and who held the spoils of the New York customhouse, and Half-Breeds around Blaine, whom he made secretary of state. He used the appointments against Conkling, and neither faction proposed to send the army back to the Southern statehouses.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    He inherited a Treasury that was paying debt and taking in more from customs than it spent. He proposed no new tax, and no fiscal statute was passed while he could govern. The surplus and the argument over the tariff were left to Arthur.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    He was a protectionist, as the Republican party had been since Lincoln. No tariff act was completed in the months he served. Trade policy did not move.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    Specie payments, resumed under Hayes, continued without a crisis. He opposed any fresh inflation of silver or of greenbacks and signed no monetary law. The standard he found was the standard he left.

  4. 4. Labor

    No national strike reached him, and no labor bill did either. Wages and hours in private employment remained with the states. The eight-hour rule for federal workers was already on the books and was not extended.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    Western demands for Chinese exclusion were loud and not yet enacted as a statute. He signed no restriction and left no immigration law of his own. The treaty Hayes had obtained, which permitted a suspension of immigration, was the instrument Arthur would use.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    The inaugural acknowledged a national duty to the freed people’s rights and offered the laws already passed, and education, rather than a return of troops. He nominated black Republicans to federal office, Frederick Douglass among them. He did not reoccupy the South, and the practical retreat of 1877 was not undone.

  7. 7. Federalism

    He accepted the settlement Hayes had made. The states, including the Southern states now under Democrats, ran their domestic governments, and he launched no new enforcement campaign under the amendments. His real dispute about federal power was smaller: whether a senator or the president would name the officers of the port of New York.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    Blaine, as secretary of state, began a more active commercial diplomacy toward Latin America and spoke of a conference of American states. No treaty and no alliance came of it before the shooting. There was no European entanglement, and Arthur let the initiative drop when he replaced Blaine.

  9. 9. National defense

    The army remained a small frontier force, still in the field against Apache bands and in other Indian wars. The navy was obsolete wood. He proposed no program of steel ships, and that start was Arthur’s.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    There was no establishment. He had preached for the Disciples of Christ and put neither that church nor any other into federal law. He treated polygamy in a territory as a fit subject of national criminal law, because Utah was not yet a state, and the statute that acted on that view was passed under his successor.

  11. 11. Education

    He asked Congress for federal aid to common schools, above all in the South, as a way to make citizenship mean something without soldiers. No such bill passed. Schools stayed with states and localities, and a recommendation was the whole of the record.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    Homestead and preemption continued without a statute of his. Indian reservation policy did not change in the weeks he was able to direct it. No new system of the public lands was enacted.

  13. 13. Energy

    Coal, iron, and the railroads remained private, and he did not regulate them. No fuel crisis and no railroad strike marked the term. The government bought coal and moved the mail, and it did not administer energy.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    Prosecutions for the Star Route frauds, inflated contracts for western mail routes left over from Hayes’s years, were getting under way when he was shot. He wrote no new criminal code. His murder was tried afterward in an ordinary court, as the act of one man, not as an episode of war.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    He proposed no system of relief. The poor remained a charge on counties, churches, and families. A Treasury running a surplus was not, in the weeks he had, turned toward the support of the unemployed.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    There was no federal service of medicine for civilians. The physicians who attended him were private men, and their probing of the wound infected it and killed him, a personal disaster rather than a policy. Quarantine and the marine hospitals went on as the small establishment he had inherited.

Of this presidency

  1. Half-Breeds and Stalwarts

    Republicans in 1881 were split between Stalwarts, organized around Roscoe Conkling and the hope of another term for Grant, and Half-Breeds, associated with James G. Blaine. The Stalwarts defended the spoils of the customhouse. The Half-Breeds wanted those offices taken from Conkling’s machine, and they called the taking reform. Garfield had been nominated between the factions. In office he chose Blaine’s side.

  2. The fight over the customhouse

    He named William H. Robertson, an enemy of Conkling, collector of the port of New York. Conkling and Thomas Platt resigned their Senate seats and asked the New York legislature to send them back. It refused. Garfield had insisted that the president, not a senator, appoints the officers. He was shot before that quarrel could be written into a civil-service statute.

  3. The assassination

    On July 2, 1881, Charles J. Guiteau shot him in the Washington railroad station. Guiteau was a failed office seeker, and unsound, and he called himself a Stalwart and said that Arthur was now president. Garfield lived until September 19. Infection from the wound and from the treatment of it killed him. The country read the murder as the price of the spoils system, which overstated Guiteau’s mind and did not overstate the system he had battened on.

  4. A term cut off

    Almost none of a program became law. He had recommended examinations for office, a sound currency, and federal help for common schools, and he had allowed Blaine to open a Latin American commercial policy. The recommendations either died with him or passed, unfinished, to Arthur. The term shows how little can be completed when the office is cut off in its first summer, and how much a murder can decide about the next administration.

PreviousRutherford B. Hayes NextChester A. Arthur

1881–1885

Chester A. Arthur

Republican

Before the office

He was born in 1829 in Fairfield, Vermont, the son of William Arthur, a Baptist preacher who had come from Ireland and who moved from one small pulpit to the next, and of Malvina Stone. The boyhood was parsonages, not a ward. He went to Union College and finished in 1848, taught school, and then read law in New York. The young lawyer was not yet a collector. He was, for a while, an idealist of the antebellum city. In the Lemmon case he was one of the counsel arguing that people held as slaves in Virginia became free when their owner brought them into New York. He won that kind of work, and he liked the company of men who did it.

In 1859 he married Ellen Herndon, the daughter of a naval officer. She was the private life he kept apart from the politics, and she was southern in her family’s feeling in a way that did not match his early cases. The marriage held. She died in January 1880, suddenly, of pneumonia, the year before the convention put him on a ticket. He did not marry again. The grief was not a pose. His sister, Mary McElroy, would stand in the White House in Ellen’s place. The man the country later saw, careful of his clothes and of his dinners, was also a widower who did not intend to explain himself.

The war did not make him a soldier in the field. Governor Edwin Morgan made him quartermaster general of New York, with the rank of brigadier, and he spent those years feeding, clothing, and equipping regiments. He was good at it. He also learned, at close range, how contracts worked and which men controlled them. After the peace the lesson went into politics. Roscoe Conkling’s machine in New York had use for a lawyer who was presentable, loyal, and not squeamish about an office. Arthur became one of its ornaments. In 1871 Grant made him collector of the port of New York, the richest patronage in the United States. The customhouse employed hundreds. They were assessed for the party. They worked elections. The salary and the fees made him a wealthy man, and the title of general, which he kept, made him a personage.

Hayes looked at the customhouse and saw the spoils system itself. In 1878 he removed Arthur. The removal was the making of the Stalwart martyr: a gentleman turned out by a reformer, dignified, injured, and more Conkling’s man than he had been before. He had never been elected to anything. When the 1880 convention needed a Stalwart to balance Garfield, it took the collector Hayes had fired. Arthur accepted. He was the vice presidency as a favor to a boss. Four months after the inauguration, Charles Guiteau shot Garfield and announced that a Stalwart would now be president. The words were a madman’s. They were also too close to the faction’s own language. Arthur was horrified, and the country did not believe the office was safe in his hands.

What the country could not see was that the collector had not eaten the whole of the man. The preacher’s son was still in him, and the lawyer who had argued the Lemmon case, and a vanity that could not bear to be thought a puppet or a thief. He liked comfort. He liked being called general. He had lived for years by the rule that offices were the property of the organization that won. He also had a line, personal rather than doctrinal, past which he would not be pushed once the responsibility was his alone and not Conkling’s. The machine had put him where he was. It could not tell him, after a murder, to behave as if the murder had been a promotion.

He came to the oath with no program and with that line. He would not send troops back to the South. He would not become a theorist. Before the first winter was out he told Congress that original appointments ought to rest on ascertained fitness, that tenure ought to be stable, and that the higher places ought to go to officers who had earned them. The sentence undid the customhouse that had made him. He would sign it into law because the alternative was to be, in history, only the man Guiteau had seemed to speak for. The gentleman was real. The boss had been real too. The office required him to choose, and the choice surprised the men who thought they owned him.

In the office

He inherited the more limited peacetime government left after 1877, and he did not send troops back into the Southern states or give the amendments any new machinery of enforcement. Inside that narrower field he signed a civil-service law, a revised Chinese exclusion act, and the first money for a steel navy. The federal government was again at its older work of revenue, offices, the border, and ships, and the three Civil War amendments remained law without a Reconstruction government behind them.

The party

Arthur was the Stalwart the machine had demanded: collector of the port of New York, removed by Hayes, and placed on the ticket in 1880 to placate Conkling. That faction expected him to return the offices and to treat civil-service reform as a Half-Breed weapon. He did not. He signed the Pendleton Act, administered it as a real statute, and left the party where Hayes had placed it, on protection and gold payments, without a return to military rule in the South.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    Customs brought in more than the government spent, so the problem was a surplus, not a debt crisis. He preferred to cut the tariff rather than spend the money on local projects, and he vetoed a swollen rivers and harbors bill on that ground. Congress overrode the veto even as the debt continued downward, and the wartime income tax was not brought back.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    He asked for a commission and for duties revised toward revenue. The commission of 1882 recommended serious reductions. The tariff he signed in 1883 lowered some rates, raised others, and left protection essentially standing, a bargain so mixed that it was called the Mongrel Tariff.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    Gold redemption of the greenbacks, won under Hayes, continued, and he did not disturb it. The silver purchases required by Bland-Allison went on because the statute required them. He was neither a free-silver man nor an inflationist, and the task was custody of a standard already resumed.

  4. 4. Labor

    In 1884 he signed a Bureau of Labor, placed in the Interior Department, to collect statistics on wages and conditions. It regulated nothing and arbitrated nothing. He faced no national strike of the 1877 kind, and the eight-hour rule was not extended from federal workers to private industry.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    On April 4, 1882, he vetoed a twenty-year suspension of Chinese labor immigration as a breach of the treaty of 1880, which allowed regulation or suspension and not a bar lasting a generation. On May 6 he signed a ten-year exclusion, the first federal statute aimed at a single nationality. The same year a general immigration act imposed a head tax and barred paupers, convicts, and persons judged insane, closing the open migration of the Burlingame years for Chinese laborers and narrowing it for the destitute of any country.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    He did not revive military Reconstruction, and after the Supreme Court in 1883 struck down the public-accommodations sections of the Civil Rights Act of 1875 he proposed no new enforcement statute in their place. Black men remained in a thinner federal service than Reconstruction had built, and the Republican party of his term was not the party of the Enforcement Acts. The amendments stayed in the Constitution, and the South stayed with the governments Hayes had allowed to take it.

  7. 7. Federalism

    National action in his term ran to examinations for office, exclusion at the ports, and ships, not back to military districts or to federal supervision of ordinary Southern life. The states kept schools, labor, criminal justice, and the poor. He did not repeat Pierce’s claim that every new federal activity was forbidden, and he did not take the government back into the work Reconstruction had done.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    He made no European alliance. Reciprocity treaties negotiated under Secretary Frelinghuysen were not in force when he left, and his successor withdrew them. A treaty with Korea was concluded and an isthmian canal was discussed without being begun, and the diplomacy started no war.

  9. 9. National defense

    With Secretary William Chandler he obtained, in the naval law of 1883, authority for the steel cruisers Atlanta, Boston, and Chicago and the dispatch boat Dolphin. They were the beginning of a new navy, not yet a fleet. The army stayed small and was still used in Indian campaigns, and a mass wartime establishment was not rebuilt.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    He signed the Edmunds Act of 1882, which made polygamy in the territories easier to prosecute and barred polygamists from juries and from office. Congress could legislate for a territory in a way it could not for a state. The act was a criminal law, and it established no church.

  11. 11. Education

    A bill for federal aid to common schools was debated and did not pass. He recommended the object and signed no system. The land-grant colleges continued on the Morrill grants while the common schools remained with the states, and the Civil War amendments neither created a federal school system nor required one.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    Homestead, preemption, and cash sale of the public domain continued under statutes already in force. He signed no general allotment of Indian reservations; that act came after him. Policy toward the Indian nations was still the reservation, the agency, and intermittent war, and the lands were being granted and sold rather than held as a permanent public estate.

  13. 13. Energy

    Steel for the new ships and coal for the railroads were bought from private mills and mines. He did not regulate the pits, the freight rates, or the price of fuel. The nearest thing to an energy decision was the order for cruisers that American yards were only beginning to be able to build.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    The Star Route cases came to trial in his term, and the leading defendants were acquitted. The Pendleton Act banned political assessments on officeholders and did not reach every fraud in the departments. The Edmunds Act sharpened the prosecution of polygamy and Chinese exclusion created federal offenses of unlawful entry, while ordinary crime otherwise remained with the states.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    Barring immigrants likely to become a public charge was a rule at the port, not a provision for the poor already in the country. He spent none of the surplus on relief. Counties, churches, and private charity remained the support of the destitute, and a labor bureau that counted wages did not pay them.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    No medical service for the civilian population was created. Marine hospitals and the quarantine authority enacted in 1878 continued, for seamen and for ports. Excluding immigrants classed as insane was a police measure at the dock, not care for anyone already living in the country.

Of this presidency

  1. The Pendleton Act

    He signed the Pendleton Civil Service Reform Act on January 16, 1883. It set up a bipartisan commission, required open competitive examinations for the classified service, and forbade assessing federal employees for party funds. At the start the classified list was only a modest fraction of the offices, chiefly the larger post offices and customhouses. He named the commission and let the examinations operate. Patronage was not abolished. It was no longer the only rule of admission.

  2. Chinese exclusion

    Congress first passed a suspension of Chinese laborers for twenty years. He vetoed it on April 4, 1882, because a prohibition of that length broke the treaty of 1880, under which the United States might regulate or suspend immigration and had not bargained for a bar of a generation. Congress cut the period to ten years. He signed the Chinese Exclusion Act on May 6. Laborers were barred, Chinese persons were declared ineligible for naturalization, and those already here needed certificates if they left and wished to return. He had refused the harsher term. He accepted the exclusion.

  3. The first steel cruisers

    The navy he inherited was wooden and obsolete. In 1883 Congress authorized Atlanta, Boston, Chicago, and the dispatch vessel Dolphin, the first steel ships of the navy that was built over the next generation. Arthur did not commission a battle fleet. He got the hulls begun, which was the first serious naval construction since the Civil War.

  4. The tariff commission

    He treated the Treasury surplus as a reason to lower the tariff, not as a purse for rivers, harbors, and local claims, and he asked for a commission to revise the rates. The commission recommended substantial cuts. The act of March 3, 1883, which he signed, did not follow the report: some duties fell, others rose, and protection survived. The rivers and harbors veto, which Congress overrode, was the same argument in another bill.

PreviousJames A. Garfield NextThe Civil Rights Cases

1883

The Civil Rights Cases

Congress had used the Fourteenth Amendment to forbid racial exclusion from inns, theaters, and public conveyances. The Court held that the amendment did not give Congress that power over private persons.

Decision

The Civil Rights Cases

Decided October 15, 1883

How it was made

Five prosecutions under the Civil Rights Act of 1875 were heard together. The act required equal access to accommodations, transport, and places of amusement. The defendants were private innkeepers, theater owners, and a railroad, not state officers.

Who sat

Eight to one. Bradley wrote. Matthews had been nominated by Hayes and was commissioned after Garfield renewed the nomination. Harlan, Hayes’s other appointee, dissented alone.

With the opinion

  • Joseph Bradley — Grant
  • Morrison Waite — Grant
  • Samuel Miller — Lincoln
  • Stephen Field — Lincoln
  • William Woods — Hayes
  • Stanley Matthews — Garfield
  • Horace Gray — Arthur
  • Samuel Blatchford — Arthur

In dissent

  • John Marshall Harlan — Hayes

What it changed

The Fourteenth Amendment restrains states, Bradley held, not private choices. Congress may correct a state’s denial of a right. It may not write a general code of racial equality for inns and theaters. The Thirteenth Amendment, he said, ended slavery and the badges of slavery, and exclusion from a theater was not that. The act of 1875 fell. For eighty years the national government had no general power to reach private racial exclusion in public accommodations. Congress returned to that ground in 1964 on the commerce power, and the Court then upheld it. Harlan’s dissent was the road not taken in 1883.

PreviousChester A. Arthur NextGrover Cleveland

1885–1889

Grover Cleveland

Democrat

Before the office

He was born in 1837 in Caldwell, New Jersey, and christened Stephen Grover. His father, Richard Falley Cleveland, was a Presbyterian minister who moved from church to church and died when the boy was sixteen. There were many children and no money for a college. Grover, as he called himself, went to work. An uncle in Buffalo gave him a start, and he read law in the office of Rogers, Bowen and Rogers, the old way, copying papers and sitting up with the books. He was admitted to the bar in 1859. The man who came out of that office was large, blunt, and already suspicious of anyone who wanted a favor. He was not poor in the sense of a cabin legend. He was a bachelor lawyer who worked harder than the men around him and saw no reason to apologize for it.

Erie County made him sheriff in 1870. The office was usually a place to put friends. He treated it as a duty, including the part other sheriffs handed to a deputy. Twice, when a man had been sentenced to hang, Cleveland sprang the trap himself. He did not enjoy it. He thought the law had assigned the act to him and that passing it on would be cowardice. The story followed him for the rest of his life, and it was true. It was also the whole of his politics in one scene. He would do the unpleasant thing rather than find a softer man to do it, and he would not dress the choice up as mercy.

Buffalo then elected him mayor, and he vetoed. A street-cleaning contract that smelled of the council was sent back. The city discovered that it had a Democrat who would not deal. In 1882 the state made him governor by a majority large enough to frighten Tammany Hall, which had wanted a Democrat it could use and had got one it could not. At Albany he vetoed again: bills that raided the treasury for pensions and projects, a bill to cut the fare on the elevated trains because the cut was popular and, in his view, a taking. He worked at a standing desk. He had almost no small talk. The sentence his friends put on him was that a public office is a public trust, and for once the sentence was not larger than the man.

There was a private fact, and it came out when he ran for president. In Buffalo he had been involved with a widow, Maria Halpin, and a child had been born. He had accepted the paternity and had provided for the boy. He had not married her. When the newspapers got it, the men around him wanted a denial or a smear. He told them to tell the truth. The campaign against James G. Blaine became, on one side, the tariff and the railroads, and on the other a chant about the child. He did not answer the chant with a lie. Nevins would later call the public habit courage. The private habit was narrower and less attractive, and it was the same muscle: he would rather be caught in the fact than be caught rearranging it.

The party that nominated him in 1884 had not elected a president since Buchanan. It was the white South, and it was Northern men who were tired of the pension list, the protective tariff, and the Republican use of the customhouse. Cleveland was useful because he was not a Confederate and not a boss. He was a reformer only in the negative sense. He did not arrive with a program for new bureaus. He arrived with a belief that the government was already doing too many favors, that the Treasury surplus was a standing invitation to steal, and that the veto was the honest man’s ordinary tool. The first Democrat in a quarter of a century was not a new kind of Democrat. He was the old one, without the secession.

He took the oath a bachelor, which he would not remain, and a vetoer, which he would. The Texas drought was still in the future, and so was the message in which he would tell Congress that he could find no constitutional warrant for sending seed grain to suffering farmers, because though the people support the government the government should not support the people. Nothing he had done in Buffalo or at Albany made that sentence a surprise. The courage, such as it was, had always been the courage to refuse.

In the office

Cleveland came in to stop private bills and to shrink a Treasury surplus he believed was corrupting Congress. The federal government was already far larger than it had been before the Civil War, and in 1887 it gained a commission over interstate railroads, a further use of the commerce power rather than a new frame of government. He left the construction of Jim Crow to the states.

The party

Chester A. Arthur's Republicans were the party of the Union veteran and the protective tariff, even after Arthur had accepted civil-service reform and a confused revision of duties. Cleveland's Democrats drew their strength from the white South and from Northerners who wanted fewer favors and a lower tariff. They did not propose another constitution. They proposed to veto more, to take less at the customhouse, and to leave schools, droughts, and elections with the states.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    Tariff and excise receipts still outran ordinary spending, and he treated the surplus as a temptation rather than as a fund for new national services. He vetoed hundreds of private pension bills and a general dependent-pension bill, preferring to cut revenue rather than to lengthen the list of federal beneficiaries.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    His annual message of December 1887 was given entirely to the tariff, which he argued had become a tax larger than the government required. A reduction bill passed the Democratic House and died in the Republican Senate. Protection stayed in force, and it was the question on which he lost the electoral vote in 1888.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    He was a gold Democrat. The Bland-Allison Act still required monthly purchases of silver, and Congress would not repeal it, so the Treasury defended redemption without a new banking statute. The national banks founded in the Civil War continued, and there was still no central bank.

  4. 4. Labor

    In 1888 he signed the law that made the Bureau of Labor an independent Department of Labor under a commissioner, without a seat in the cabinet and without power to set wages or recognize unions. It was an office of inquiry. The strikes of the middle of the decade, including the eight-hour campaigns of 1886, were left to cities, states, and employers.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    Early in the term he signed the contract-labor law, which forbade bringing in workers already bound by foreign agreements. In 1888 the Scott Act closed a door the exclusion laws had left ajar, barring Chinese laborers who had left the country from returning even when they held certificates.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    He made no effort to restore federal protection of Black voters in the South. The Civil Rights Cases had already narrowed the reach of the Fourteenth Amendment before he took office, and he accepted a Union in which schools, inns, and polling places were left to state law. His conspicuous use of national power over a subject people was the Dawes Act, directed at Indians, not at white supremacy in the former Confederacy.

  7. 7. Federalism

    The veto of aid for drought-stricken Texas farmers denied that relief of that kind was a federal duty, and bills for national aid to common schools never became law. In the same season he signed the Interstate Commerce Act, after the Supreme Court had held that states could not regulate the interstate part of a railroad rate. Limited government, as he practiced it, stopped where the commerce clause plainly began.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    He preferred negotiation to display, and a fisheries quarrel with Britain went to diplomacy rather than to the fleet. Hawaiian reciprocity was renewed with a clause giving the United States exclusive use of Pearl Harbor, a harbor right and not annexation. No treaty to annex the islands came before him in this term; the overthrow of the monarchy and his withdrawal of an annexation treaty belong to the presidency that began in 1893.

  9. 9. National defense

    Secretary of the Navy William C. Whitney carried on the shift from wood and sail to steel hulls that Arthur had begun. The navy grew in quality rather than into an instrument of overseas conquest, and the army remained a small force scattered on the frontier. The term contained no foreign war.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    He signed the Edmunds-Tucker Act of 1887, which dissolved the legal corporation of the Mormon church and tightened federal prosecution of polygamy in Utah Territory. That was an exercise of Congress's complete power over a territory, not an establishment of religion in the states. In the states, worship and its public recognition stayed outside federal administration.

  11. 11. Education

    Common schools remained with the states and the local districts. He gave no support to general federal appropriations for them, and the Blair bills failed. The Bureau of Education stayed a small statistical office inside the Interior Department.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    He ordered cattle companies to pull down illegal fences on the public range and pressed the recovery of lands he believed had been acquired by fraud. The statute that endured was the Dawes Act of 1887, which allotted reservation land to individuals, opened what the government called surplus to outside buyers, and treated tribal title as an obstacle to citizenship. The practical result was a long transfer of Indian land out of Indian hands.

  13. 13. Energy

    Coal fired the railroads and the cities and was mined and sold by private companies, some of them working public land. There was no department of energy and no federal policy of price or output. Where Washington touched the business at all, it was through the railroad that hauled the coal, not through the mine.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    Murder, theft, and riot remained for the states to define and punish. Federal criminal law covered counterfeiting, the mails, the territories, the exclusion statutes, and polygamy. He founded no national police, and he did not reopen federal prosecution of racial violence in the South.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    A small appropriation to buy seed grain for Texas farmers ruined by drought drew his most famous veto. He held that relief of individual suffering, however genuine, was not a duty of the general government, and that citizens must not be taught to look to Washington for support. Union pensions were the great payment already in the law, and he tried to confine even those to examined, service-connected claims.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    The Marine Hospital Service continued to care for sick merchant seamen and to assist quarantine. There was no federal system of medicine for the rest of the people, and no pure-food statute. The illnesses of the poor were left to cities, counties, and charity.

Of this presidency

  1. Pension vetoes

    Cleveland vetoed hundreds of private bills awarding pensions to named Union veterans, commonly because the Pension Bureau had already rejected the claim and Congress was overriding the bureau as a favor. In 1887 he also vetoed a general dependent-pension bill that would have paid for disability without requiring that the disability come from the war. The Grand Army of the Republic did not forgive the habit. He did not abolish the pension system. He refused to let private acts and loose definitions turn it into an open civil list.

  2. The Texas seed bill

    Congress voted a modest sum to purchase seed grain for counties in Texas that drought had stripped of the means to plant. Cleveland returned the bill. His message denied that the Constitution had commissioned the federal government to relieve distress of that ordinary kind, and insisted that the people support the government rather than the government the people. The amount was small and the language was hard. It remained his rule for poverty, in prosperity and later in panic alike.

  3. The Interstate Commerce Act

    The act of 1887 followed the Supreme Court's decision that a state could not regulate rates on the interstate portion of a rail journey. It required interstate rates to be reasonable and just, forbade rebates and pooling, and restricted the practice of charging more for a short haul than for a long haul over the same line. It established the Interstate Commerce Commission, the first independent federal regulatory commission. Cleveland signed a measure he had not written. The commerce power, long exercised chiefly through navigation and the tariff, now had a standing domestic agency.

  4. The Dawes Act

    The General Allotment Act of 1887, which he signed, broke much reservation land into individual parcels and treated the remainder as surplus available for sale to non-Indians. Allotment was offered as the path to farming and to citizenship, and tribal government was treated as the thing to be outgrown. The statute did what its sponsors intended to the land system and not what they promised to the nations. Over the following decades Indian communities lost the greater part of the acreage they still held when the act passed.

  5. The tariff message of 1887

    On December 6, 1887, Cleveland used his whole annual message for the tariff and the surplus. He argued that duties kept up after the Treasury's needs were met were unnecessary taxation, and that the idle surplus invited waste. The House passed the Mills bill to reduce rates in 1888. The Republican Senate would not agree. He had drawn the line on which the next election was fought, and Benjamin Harrison won the electoral vote while Cleveland held a plurality of the popular vote.

PreviousThe Civil Rights Cases NextBenjamin Harrison

1889–1893

Benjamin Harrison

Republican

Before the office

He was born in 1833 at North Bend, Ohio, in the house of his grandfather, William Henry Harrison. The name was a presidency and a warning. The grandfather had died a month into the office, and the boy grew up inside a family that already treated public life as an inheritance and a risk. His great-grandfather had signed the Declaration. His father, John Scott Harrison, farmed, sat in Congress as a Whig, and distrusted the men who lived off offices. The household was Presbyterian and not rich in proportion to the name. Benjamin was small, exact, and proud. He did not have the easy warmth the family legend seemed to promise. He had the seriousness.

He finished at Miami University in 1852 and the next year married Caroline Scott, daughter of a Presbyterian minister who ran a school for girls at Oxford. She was educated, musical, and stronger in company than he was. In 1854 they moved to Indianapolis, where he meant to make a living at the law rather than wait for the name to provide one. The practice grew because he was a fierce and prepared courtroom lawyer, not because he was liked. He could be icy. He taught a Sunday school and would not set the Sabbath aside for politics. The Republican party, when it came, fit him without a conversion. It was the Union, a tariff that protected American wages, and the moral vocabulary he already used on Sundays.

In 1862 the governor of Indiana wanted a regiment and a respectable colonel. Harrison raised the 70th Indiana and led it in Sherman’s campaign to Atlanta. At Peachtree Creek his brigade held. He was brevetted a brigadier. In the fall of 1864 he was sent home to speak for Lincoln and the war, and he went back to the army when the speaking was done. He was not Grant and he was not his grandfather. He was a volunteer general of the careful kind, brave enough, and glad to return to a law office that paid. Indiana called him Little Ben, which described his height and, in some mouths, a doubt that the height was the only small thing about him. The doubt underestimated the will.

The political rise was slow and then sudden. He lost a race for governor in 1876. In 1881 the legislature sent him to the Senate, and for six years he was a Republican of the strict observance: a high tariff, pensions the country owed the men who had fought, and a refusal to treat the Fifteenth Amendment as a dead letter in the states that were cancelling it. He favored federal help for schools and federal supervision of elections. When the Indiana legislature turned Democratic it retired him. The retirement was useful. A senator still in office is a faction. A former senator who had been a general, who could carry Indiana, and who was the grandson of a president was a candidate. In 1888 the convention took him.

He did not go out on the road. He stood on the porch of his house on North Delaware Street and received the delegations the party sent, and he talked to them at a length Cleveland would not have tolerated. The tariff was the argument. Cleveland had asked Congress to cut protection and shrink the surplus. Harrison said the surplus could be used, for the soldiers and for the dignity of the government, and that the protective duty was the difference between American labor and the cheaper labor of Europe. He lost the popular vote and won the electoral college, Indiana and New York deciding it. He was inclined to call the result providential. The men who had managed the close states thought the providence had required a good deal of help. He took the office anyway as a vindication of the Republican program, not as a bargain he owed the bosses.

What he carried to the oath was that program and the older family belief that the Union’s settlement was not finished while a class of citizens could be kept from the polls by concert. Cleveland had been willing to leave elections, schools, and droughts with the states. Harrison told the country, in the inaugural, that a community which denies a portion of its members their plain rights under the law has severed the only safe bond of social order, and that an unlawful expedient cannot become a permanent condition of government. He meant the suppression of the black vote. He also meant to sign a tariff, a pension, and a navy. The grandson had not come to repeal his party. He had come to use it.

In the office

The Fifty-first Congress was the first whose appropriations passed a billion dollars, and it spent the surplus Cleveland had hoped to end by cutting duties. Antitrust, a heavier silver purchase, the McKinley Tariff, and a broad Union pension pushed taxing, spending, and commerce powers further than Cleveland would go, without rewriting the Constitution. In the same years army troops killed a Lakota band at Wounded Knee, and the last serious bill of that era for federal supervision of elections died in the Senate.

The party

Cleveland's Democrats had stood for a lower tariff, for the white South, and for vetoes in the name of a narrow federal duty. Harrison's Republicans were still the party of the protective tariff and of the Union veteran in the North, and under him they also became the party that would spend the surplus and multiply national statutes. Pensions, the Sherman Acts, a higher tariff, forest reserves, and a battleship navy were all ordinary laws under the existing Constitution. What changed was the amount of national legislation, not the instrument that authorized it.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    The Treasury surplus that Cleveland had called a public danger became, for Harrison's Congress, the means of a busier government. Pensions, naval ships, and rivers-and-harbors projects carried appropriations, across the life of the Fifty-first Congress, past a billion dollars. The spending was denounced as extravagance and defended as payment of national obligations, and it was not yet an income tax or a welfare state.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    The McKinley Tariff of 1890 raised protective rates to a height that became the symbol of the Congress, while putting raw sugar on the free list and paying a domestic sugar bounty. A reciprocity clause let the president reimpose duties on certain free goods if other countries refused American goods fair treatment. Voters punished the party in the 1890 elections and the House majority was lost, but protection remained Republican doctrine.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    The Sherman Silver Purchase Act required the Treasury to buy four and a half million ounces of silver a month, nearly the whole domestic output, and to issue notes redeemable in gold or silver. It replaced the smaller monthly purchases of the Bland-Allison Act and was the price of Western votes for the tariff, and in practice note-holders demanded gold. Harrison did not create a central bank, and he left Cleveland a reserve already under strain.

  4. 4. Labor

    The Homestead strike of 1892, at Carnegie steel works on the Monongahela, was fought out with Pinkerton guards and Pennsylvania militia, and Harrison did not send the army. The Sherman Antitrust Act contained no exemption for unions, but in his term it was not turned into a labor policy. Wages, hours, and the right to organize stayed matters of state law and private power.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    The Immigration Act of 1891 put inspection under federal officers, excluding, among others, persons with contagious disease and those likely to become a public charge, and Ellis Island opened as the main station in 1892. The Geary Act of the same year extended Chinese exclusion for another ten years and required Chinese residents to carry certificates. Restriction was national policy before any European quota existed.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    Harrison supported the Federal Elections Bill, which would have placed congressional elections under closer federal supervision in order to protect Black voters, and it passed the House only to fail in the Senate, set aside by a coalition that preferred the silver bill to a fight with the South. While that bill died, Mississippi adopted a constitution designed to remove Black men from politics, and federal troops at Wounded Knee killed a large number of Lakota, many of them women and children. His presidency talked of voting rights it could not enact and presided over the army's last mass killing of the Indian wars.

  7. 7. Federalism

    More subjects received national statutes than under Cleveland: railroad combinations, silver, immigration inspection, land-grant colleges, and forest reserves. The police, the schools, and the rules of marriage remained with the states. The Lodge elections bill was the point at which a national protection of the franchise was refused, and after its failure the states of the South were left to write disfranchisement into their own constitutions.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    James G. Blaine's Pan-American conference sought trade and influence in the hemisphere without a permanent alliance, and an attack on sailors from the cruiser Baltimore in Chile ended in a Chilean apology and indemnity after Harrison had shown he was willing to threaten force. In February 1893, with days left in the term, he sent the Senate a treaty to annex Hawaii after the overthrow of Queen Liliuokalani. The Senate had not consented when he left office.

  9. 9. National defense

    Secretary of the Navy Benjamin F. Tracy's program produced the Naval Act of 1890, authorizing the first three American battleships of the new steel fleet. The army stayed small, except when it was used against the Ghost Dance and at Wounded Knee, and Harrison fought no foreign war. He did leave a navy built to be seen abroad.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    The federal campaign against Mormon polygamy, already severe under the Edmunds-Tucker Act, continued until the church formally renounced plural marriage in 1890, clearing the political path toward Utah statehood, which came after Harrison had left office. He created no national church and proposed no religious test. Moral regulation of the territories was still understood as a congressional power.

  11. 11. Education

    The Second Morrill Act of 1890 gave further federal money to land-grant colleges and withheld it from any state that did not provide for Black students either in those colleges or in separate institutions, and the practical result in the South was a set of segregated public colleges, not integration. Common schools otherwise remained a state and local charge. No national school system was attempted.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    Six new states entered the Union: North Dakota, South Dakota, Montana, Washington, Idaho, and Wyoming. The Forest Reserve Act of 1891, in a provision little noticed at passage, authorized the president to set aside public forest land, and Harrison proclaimed the first reserves, while allotment of Indian reservations went on under the Dawes Act. The public domain was at once being conserved in forests, opened in new states, and taken from tribal control.

  13. 13. Energy

    Silver mining, not a fuel policy, was what moved Congress, and the silver-purchase law was written for that Western industry, while coal remained a private business. There was no department of energy. Naval expansion assumed coal as the fleet's fuel and did not yet set aside oil reserves.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    The Sherman Act made certain restraints of interstate trade federal misdemeanors, but Harrison's attorneys general brought few cases and established no habit of enforcement. Ordinary crime stayed with sheriffs, police, and state courts. No federal prosecution followed Wounded Knee, and the soldiers were not tried for the killing.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    The Dependent Pension Act of 1890 pensioned Union veterans who had served ninety days and were unable to perform manual labor, whether or not the disability was caused by the war, and it was the great federal payment to individuals in the term, a payment for service rather than a poor law. Confederate veterans received nothing from Washington. Drought, unemployment, and old age outside the Grand Army remained local or private burdens.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    Federal medical inspection of immigrants became a real gate at Ellis Island, and the Marine Hospital Service remained the nearest thing to a national health office. There was no program of care for the civilian sick and no pure-food law. Quarantine and hospitals for the poor were still city and state functions.

Of this presidency

  1. The Sherman Antitrust Act

    The act of July 2, 1890, declared illegal every contract, combination, or conspiracy in restraint of trade or commerce among the states or with foreign nations, and it forbade monopolizing such trade. It was a criminal statute as well as a basis for injunctions, and it allowed private suitors to recover triple damages. Harrison signed it. His administration barely used it. The sugar-trust case that later gutted its application to manufacturing was decided under Cleveland, and the celebrated dissolutions came under Roosevelt and Taft.

  2. The Sherman Silver Purchase Act

    Two weeks after the antitrust law, Harrison signed the silver-purchase act that Western Republicans had demanded. The Treasury was to buy four and a half million ounces of silver every month and pay with notes the holder could redeem in gold or silver. The metallic reserve behind those notes was, in the hands of a frightened public, gold. The act kept silver in the monetary system without establishing free coinage. Its repeal in 1893 split the Democrats and defined Cleveland's second term.

  3. The McKinley Tariff

    The tariff that bore William McKinley's name as chairman of the House committee raised protective duties on most manufactured goods and was attacked at once as a tax on consumers for the benefit of trusts. Reciprocity provisions, urged by Blaine, allowed bargaining with countries that bought American goods, and domestic sugar growers received a bounty when raw sugar was made free. The rates helped cost the Republicans the House in 1890. They did not cost the party its belief that protection was national policy.

  4. The Billion Dollar Congress

    Under Speaker Thomas B. Reed, the Fifty-first Congress adopted rules that let a determined majority legislate, and it appropriated more than a billion dollars. Pensions, the new navy, and internal improvements accounted for much of the sum. Opponents named it the Billion Dollar Congress and treated the figure as proof of Republican excess. Harrison did not apologize for the activity. The session showed what his party would do with unified government and a surplus, and the next elections took the House away.

  5. Dependent pensions

    Cleveland had vetoed a general service pension. Harrison signed one. After ninety days of Union service, a veteran disabled for manual work could draw a pension without proving that the war had caused the disability, and the roll of dependents widened with it. The act was popular with the organized veterans of the North and expensive to the Treasury. It was also a sectional statute. The men who had fought for the Confederacy were outside it.

  6. The Lodge elections bill

    Representative Henry Cabot Lodge's Federal Elections Bill would have allowed federal circuit courts to appoint supervisors for elections to Congress where a portion of the voters petitioned, a direct answer to fraud and intimidation against Black voters in the South. Harrison urged it. The House passed it in 1890. In the Senate, silver Republicans joined Democrats to lay it aside so that the silver-purchase bill could go forward. No comparable federal elections law was enacted again until the middle of the next century.

  7. Wounded Knee

    On December 29, 1890, troops of the Seventh Cavalry opened fire on a band of Miniconjou and Hunkpapa Lakota at Wounded Knee Creek on the Pine Ridge reservation, during the army's campaign against the Ghost Dance. Well over a hundred Lakota were killed, a great share of them women and children, and the army's own dead included men hit by its own fire. Harrison was president. He had not ordered a massacre in so many words, and he did not afterward treat the command as criminal. The killing was a federal military action, and it effectively ended the armed resistance of the Plains wars.

PreviousGrover Cleveland NextGrover Cleveland

1893–1897

Grover Cleveland

Democrat

Before the office

He had already been president, and the country had put him out without quite rejecting him. In 1888 he won more votes than Benjamin Harrison and lost the office in the electoral college, on the tariff. He had told Congress, in December 1887, that the protective system was the source of the surplus and of the corruption that fed on it. The protected interests and the veterans’ organizations answered him at the polls. He left in March 1889 convinced that he had been beaten for being right, which is the most dangerous conviction a man can carry into a second chance.

The years between were the first ordinary years of his marriage. He had married Frances Folsom in the White House in 1886, a young woman he had known since her childhood as the daughter of his dead law partner. Out of office he practiced law in New York. A daughter was born. He fished. He did not become a different politician. The Democracy that wanted him back in 1892 was already arguing with itself about silver, and he would not take the nomination as a man who might be talked into free coinage. He beat Harrison. He came to the oath a second time with the same vetoes in his pocket and with a Treasury that was about to fail the gold standard he thought was the government’s good name.

What the four years out of office had added was not a new principle. It was the knowledge that the principle could cost him the presidency and that he would pay it again. In August 1893, with banks failing and the gold reserve running out, he told Congress to repeal the silver-purchase law Harrison’s party had passed and to make it plain that the United States would pay what it owed in money other nations recognized. He was asking his own party to abandon the inflation a large part of it wanted. He had told the truth about a child in 1884 and about a tariff in 1887. He meant to tell it about the dollar. The courage Nevins named had not softened. The party he had led back into power was about to break on it.

In the office

He returned as the panic of 1893 was draining the gold reserve and throwing men out of work, and he met it by repealing silver purchases and selling bonds, not by federal relief. Injunctions and regular troops were then used against a nationwide railroad boycott, a harder application of the commerce power rather than a new constitution. Southern disfranchisement and segregation advanced without his interference, and the Hawaiian annexation treaty he withdrew was the one Harrison had sent at the opening of this term.

The party

Harrison's Republicans had raised the tariff, widened Union pensions, and bought silver in order to hold the West. Cleveland's Democrats still claimed a lower tariff, the Solid South, and a government that would not support the people. The panic made that platform split his own party. He forced the repeal of the Sherman silver law with Republican votes, and the Democracy divided between his defense of gold and the free-silver politics that soon nominated Bryan.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    The surplus of the 1880s disappeared with the depression, and customs revenue fell with imports. Cleveland would not answer the shortage by silver inflation or by work relief, and he covered the Treasury's gold obligation with bond sales, including the unpopular arrangement of 1895 in which a private banking syndicate supplied gold and helped stop the immediate drain. Economy, for him, still meant refusing new beneficiaries.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    The Wilson-Gorman Act of 1894 was written as a reduction of the McKinley rates and emerged from the Senate loaded with protective amendments, and Cleveland denounced the result and let it become law without his signature. It did include a peacetime income tax of two percent on incomes above four thousand dollars, which the Supreme Court struck down the next year in Pollock as an unapportioned direct tax. The tariff he left behind satisfied neither free traders nor protectionists.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    His first domestic act of consequence was the repeal, in a special session, of the Sherman Silver Purchase Act, completed in November 1893, because he regarded the silver notes as a pipe through which the gold reserve leaked and was willing to break his party to close it. Free coinage of silver at sixteen to one, the demand of the agrarian Democrats and the Populists, was the policy he existed to stop. There was still no central bank, and gold redemption was maintained by bond issues and by will.

  4. 4. Labor

    When the American Railway Union's boycott of Pullman cars stopped rail traffic across much of the country in 1894, his attorney general obtained a federal injunction and Cleveland sent troops to Chicago over the objection of Governor John P. Altgeld. Eugene V. Debs was jailed for violating the injunction, and the Supreme Court, in In re Debs, upheld that use of the federal courts. No statute of the term gave workers a right to organize.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    Hard times revived demands for restriction, Chinese exclusion remained in force, and at the end of the term Congress passed a bill requiring a literacy test of adult immigrants. Cleveland vetoed it on March 2, 1897, arguing that the country still needed their labor and that illiteracy was not a fair measure of fitness. The veto stood, and a literacy test did not become law until Congress overrode another Democratic president twenty years later.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    This term, not the first, was when Jim Crow received its decisive constitutional permission and when several Southern states rewrote their suffrage: Plessy v. Ferguson, decided in 1896, upheld a Louisiana separate-car law, and South Carolina adopted a disfranchising constitution in 1895. Cleveland proposed no elections bill and no attack on lynching. The Democratic Party of his second election was the party of the white South, and he governed as its candidate.

  7. 7. Federalism

    He would not feed the unemployed from the Treasury, and he told Congress that demands like Coxey's Army mistook the national government for a relief agency, yet he would move federal troops into a state to protect the mails and interstate commerce without the governor's consent. The income tax in the tariff act was a national claim on private income, and the Court rejected it. Limited government, in this term, was a policy toward the poor, not a refusal to use force or the commerce power.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    He spent his first year undoing Harrison's Hawaiian treaty, and in the Venezuelan crisis of 1895 his secretary of state told Britain that the United States was practically sovereign in the Americas while Cleveland asked Congress for a commission on the boundary of British Guiana if Britain would not arbitrate. The message was severe enough to raise talk of war, and Britain agreed to arbitration. He kept the country neutral in the Cuban rebellion against Spain and left the decision for war to his successor.

  9. 9. National defense

    There was no declared war, the navy continued its slow modernization, and the army was used at home, against strikers, rather than against a foreign state. His Venezuelan message showed that he would risk a serious quarrel with Britain in the name of the Monroe Doctrine. He did not build a doctrine of overseas colonies, and he did not want one.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    Utah entered the Union in January 1896 after the Mormon church had abandoned plural marriage as a condition of statehood, so the long federal coercion in the territory ended in admission rather than in a permanent religious establishment. He made no general Sunday law and founded no chaplaincy beyond military custom. Public morality in the states remained local.

  11. 11. Education

    The depression produced no national school program, and common education stayed with states and districts that were themselves short of money. Land-grant colleges continued under the Morrill statutes already in force. He did not revive the Blair bill or treat illiteracy as a federal charge, a view consistent with his veto of the immigrant literacy test.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    Allotment of Indian land under the Dawes Act went on, and the depression did not stop the passage of reservation land into private non-Indian ownership. On February 22, 1897, in the last days of the term, he proclaimed thirteen new forest reserves covering more than twenty million acres in the West, to the fury of Western senators. The reserves were a real extension of Harrison's forest policy, made by a president otherwise famous for saying no.

  13. 13. Energy

    Coal mines and oil fields were private, and many of both shut or cut wages when orders vanished in 1893. The federal government did not allocate fuel, fix its price, or operate mines. Railroads that lived on coal haulage were the national concern, and they were approached as a problem of interstate commerce and public order, not of energy supply.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    The distinctive federal criminal instrument of the term was the labor injunction, enforced by contempt, against Debs and other officers of the railway union, and ordinary policing of the strike was also done by the army under presidential order. Antitrust prosecution, by contrast, failed in the courts when the Supreme Court held in 1895 that the sugar trust's manufacturing was not interstate commerce. The United States could jail a union leader more readily than it could break that combination.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    Unemployment in the panic was the worst since the 1870s, and Coxey's Army of jobless men marched on Washington to ask for federal public works, which Cleveland refused. Leaders of the march were arrested for walking on the Capitol grass. Private charity, municipal relief, and vagrancy laws carried what was carried, and his seed-bill doctrine of the first term held in a national depression.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    The Marine Hospital Service and local quarantines remained the federal and state machinery for epidemic disease. There was no national medical insurance and no food-and-drug statute. Depression poverty worsened illness without producing a public-health program, and care of the sick poor stayed outside the federal budget.

Of this presidency

  1. The panic of 1893
