    Railroad failures, a break in the stock market, and a wave of bank suspensions opened within months of his inauguration. Unemployment spread through mills and mines, and business failures multiplied. Cleveland diagnosed the panic as a monetary fright caused by silver, not as a collapse that the Treasury should relieve with work or credit for farmers. The political result was a Republican landslide in 1894 and, two years later, a Democratic party he no longer led.

  2. Repeal of the silver-purchase law

    He called Congress into special session and demanded repeal of the Sherman Silver Purchase Act as the condition of saving the gold reserve. The repeal passed in November 1893 only because enough Republicans voted for it to overcome silver Democrats. William Jennings Bryan, still in the House, fought him and lost. The vote did not restore prosperity at once. It did mark the end of Cleveland's authority inside his own party, which in 1896 nominated Bryan on a free-silver platform Cleveland would not support.

  3. The Pullman strike and the Debs injunction

    Workers struck the Pullman company town outside Chicago, and the American Railway Union, led by Eugene V. Debs, answered with a boycott of trains carrying Pullman cars. Attorney General Richard Olney, a former railroad lawyer, sought a sweeping federal injunction on the grounds of the mail and the Sherman Act, and Cleveland ordered federal troops into Chicago although Governor Altgeld insisted Illinois could keep order. Debs was convicted of contempt and imprisoned. The Supreme Court sustained the injunction. The episode fixed, for a generation, the picture of a Democratic president using soldiers and courts against labor while refusing money to the unemployed.

  4. The Wilson-Gorman tariff

    The House bill associated with William L. Wilson would have lowered the McKinley duties in earnest. The Senate, under Arthur P. Gorman and a crowd of interested senators, restored protection in hundreds of items. Cleveland refused to sign the bill and refused to veto it, so it became law in 1894 without his name. Its income tax of two percent above four thousand dollars was voided by Pollock v. Farmers' Loan and Trust Co. in 1895. He had proved that a Democratic president could not, by himself, make his party a free-trade party.

  5. The Venezuelan boundary message

    A long dispute over the line between British Guiana and Venezuela became, in Cleveland's hands, a test of the Monroe Doctrine. Secretary of State Richard Olney's note of 1895 asserted a sweeping American authority in the hemisphere, and Cleveland's message to Congress that December warned that a British refusal to arbitrate would be a matter the United States could not ignore. Congress authorized a boundary commission. Britain, occupied elsewhere and unwilling to fight the United States over a jungle frontier, accepted arbitration. The later award did not simply give Venezuela what its advocates had claimed. The message nonetheless announced that Washington would press European powers in American boundary disputes.

  6. Withdrawal of the Hawaiian treaty

    Queen Liliuokalani was overthrown in January 1893, while Harrison was still president. Harrison sent an annexation treaty to the Senate on February 15. Cleveland, inaugurated on March 4, withdrew that treaty on March 9, five days into this term and not during the presidency of 1885 to 1889. He sent James H. Blount to investigate, concluded that the monarchy had been toppled with the improper aid of the American minister and American forces, and tried to restore the queen. The provisional government refused. The Republic of Hawaii was proclaimed in 1894, and Cleveland would not annex it. Annexation waited until 1898, under McKinley, and it was done by joint resolution rather than by the treaty he had taken back.

PreviousBenjamin Harrison NextPollock and Plessy

1895–1896

Pollock and Plessy

In two terms of the same Court the justices closed a federal income tax and opened a constitutional permission for racial separation. Both results were later reversed, one by amendment and one by a later decision.

Decision

Pollock v. Farmers’ Loan & Trust Co.

Decided on rehearing, May 20, 1895

How it was made

The income tax of 1894 taxed incomes from land and from personal property, as well as wages. A shareholder sued to stop his company paying it. The Constitution requires direct taxes to be apportioned among the states by population. The question was whether a tax on income from property is a direct tax.

Who sat

Five to four on rehearing. The first hearing had split four to four, Jackson being absent and ill. His return made the fifth vote against the tax. Cleveland’s appointees split. Harrison’s split.

With the opinion

  • Melville Fuller — Cleveland
  • Stephen Field — Lincoln
  • Horace Gray — Arthur
  • David Brewer — Harrison
  • George Shiras — Harrison

In dissent

  • John Marshall Harlan — Hayes
  • Henry Brown — Harrison
  • Howell Jackson — Harrison
  • Edward White — Cleveland

What it changed

A federal tax on income from real estate and personal property, unapportioned, was unconstitutional. The practical result was that the income tax Congress had just enacted could not be collected in the form written. The government did not get that power back from this Court. It got it from the Sixteenth Amendment.

Decision

Plessy v. Ferguson

Decided May 18, 1896

How it was made

Louisiana required separate railway cars for white and black passengers. Homer Plessy, who was seven-eighths white by the state’s own measure and black by its law, sat in a white car and was arrested. He said the statute denied him the equal protection of the laws.

Who sat

Seven to one. Brown wrote. Brewer did not sit. Harlan dissented alone, as he had in the Civil Rights Cases.

With the opinion

  • Henry Brown — Harrison
  • Melville Fuller — Cleveland
  • Stephen Field — Lincoln
  • Horace Gray — Arthur
  • George Shiras — Harrison
  • Edward White — Cleveland
  • Rufus Peckham — Cleveland

In dissent

  • John Marshall Harlan — Hayes

Not sitting

  • David Brewer — Harrison

What it changed

A state could require separate facilities by race if the facilities were formally equal. The decision did not invent Jim Crow, which legislatures were already writing. It gave those statutes a constitutional shelter that lasted until Brown. Equality, the Court said, did not require the end of distinction. Harlan answered that the Constitution did not permit a dominant class to brand another by law. His was one vote.

PreviousGrover Cleveland NextWilliam McKinley

1897–1901

William McKinley

Republican

Before the office

He was born in 1843 in Niles, Ohio, the son of an iron founder and of Nancy Allison McKinley, a Methodist of the strict kind. The family was large and respectable and not rich. He went to the academy at Poland, started at Allegheny College, and came home when the money and his health gave out. He taught school. In 1861 he enlisted as a private in the 23rd Ohio, the regiment Rutherford Hayes would command. Hayes noticed the boy who got coffee and food up to the line at Antietam under fire, and made him a commissary sergeant and then a lieutenant. By the end of the war McKinley was a brevet major, twenty-two years old. He had seen enough of a real war to respect it and not to love it. The man who later said he wanted no wars of conquest had already been in one.

He read law, opened a practice in Canton, and married Ida Saxton in 1871. She was the daughter of a Canton banker, educated, and for a short time a well woman. Two daughters were born and both died, one in infancy and one as a small child. Ida’s health broke. The epilepsy and the invalidism that followed organized the rest of his life. He would not be long away from her. At dinners he sat where he could cover a seizure with a handkerchief and go on with the conversation as if nothing had happened. The tenderness was genuine, and it was also a discipline. He became a man who managed difficulty by staying calm, staying present, and never making a scene. Politics got the same treatment.

Ohio sent him to Congress in 1876, and the House, with one term gerrymandered away, was his school until 1891. He was the tariff. As chairman of Ways and Means he wrote the bill of 1890 that raised protective duties and that carried his name, and the bill then cost his party the House and cost him his seat. He did not treat the defeat as a reason to change his mind. Protection, in his view, was the wage of the American workingman, and a revenue tariff of the Cleveland kind was a theory that forgot who got hurt. He had defended miners in court when he was a young lawyer. He was not a labor radical. He thought a high duty was how a Republican kept faith with the men who worked in the mills his father had helped build.

He came home and was elected governor, twice. The depression of the 1890s made the governorship a school in caution. He would not inflame a strike and he would not hand the state to the men who wanted one. In 1896 the party was staring at William Jennings Bryan, free silver, and a real chance of losing the industrial North. Mark Hanna raised the money and built the organization. McKinley stayed on his porch in Canton, as Harrison had stayed on his, and received the delegations. The argument was gold and the tariff. He was not Hanna’s doll. Hanna was the manager of a candidate who already knew what he thought and who would not be talked into a phrase he had not weighed. He won a majority of the popular vote, the first a president had received since Grant.

Cuba was already bleeding when he took the oath, and he did not take it in order to start a war. Spain’s reconcentration of the Cuban population offended him. So did the destruction of American property and the disorder ninety miles from Florida. He had spent thirty years inside a party that believed the United States should be strong, solvent, and protected, and that believed Washington’s advice against entanglement was still good sense. The inaugural said it as plainly as he ever said anything. No wars of conquest. No territorial aggression. War only after every agency of peace had failed. He meant it. He also meant the tariff and the gold dollar, which were the reasons the porch in Canton had been full of men.

The Methodist and the major were the same person. He did not drink, he did not bluster, and he did not think a president should be interesting at the expense of being safe. Ida’s illness had taught him that the day is managed, not seized. The party’s near-loss to Bryan had taught him that sound money and protection were not hobbies. They were the coalition. What he had not yet been taught, because it had not happened, was how small the distance is between a war to stop a cruelty and a peace that keeps the islands. He came in believing that distance was wide, and that a careful man could stand on the near side of it.

In the office

McKinley was elected to defend protection and the gold dollar, and within two years he was directing a war that gave the United States Puerto Rico, Guam, and the Philippines and a protectorate over Cuba. Those acquisitions rested on the war power and on the power to govern territory, not on a rewritten Constitution, but the change in degree was plain: subject populations overseas were now held without statehood or full citizenship. At home he left Southern disfranchisement and segregation in place.

The party

Cleveland's second-term Democrats had broken over silver and had refused both Hawaiian annexation and a war for Cuba. McKinley's Republicans restored the protective tariff and put the gold standard into statute, uniting the industrial North with voters who had rejected Bryan's free silver. They then took the country into war with Spain and kept the islands the war delivered. The party of the tariff became, without a new constitution and without abandoning its old voters, a party of overseas empire.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    The Dingley Tariff restored customs as the center of Republican finance, and the Spanish war was paid for by bonds and by temporary wartime taxes rather than by a permanent new system. Prosperity returned with the gold discoveries and with recovery from the panic, and the Treasury moved back toward surplus, while veterans' pensions continued as the standing domestic payment. He did not invent a peacetime income tax, the Court having buried the one in the Wilson-Gorman Act.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    The Dingley Act of 1897 raised duties again after the unsatisfactory Wilson-Gorman revision and returned Republican policy to high protection, with reciprocity provisions McKinley was prepared to use. He came, by the end of his life, to speak of reciprocal trade agreements as the next step, and he was shot at Buffalo the day after a speech along those lines. The law he actually left was still a protective tariff, and colonial trade with the new islands was a complication the old tariff system had not been built to handle.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    The Gold Standard Act of 1900 declared the gold dollar the unit of value and committed the Treasury to redeem its notes in gold, ending the bimetallic ambiguity that had dominated politics since the 1870s. Silver coinage did not disappear, but free coinage of silver was finished as a legislative possibility, and Bryan ran against him again in 1900 on silver and anti-imperialism and lost. A central bank was still in the future.

  4. 4. Labor

    Recovery did more for employment than any statute of his, and he offered no program of union recognition, hours, or arbitration comparable to what his successor improvised in the coal strike. He was a friend of the protective system and of the employers who supported it. Industrial combination went forward, culminating in the formation of United States Steel in 1901, while labor law remained overwhelmingly state law.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    European immigration, increasingly from southern and eastern Europe, rose again with prosperity, and he proposed no quota and no literacy test, while Chinese exclusion stayed in force. The new colonial subjects were a different category from immigrants: Filipinos and Puerto Ricans did not become citizens by the transfer of sovereignty. Entry to the mainland and citizenship were both withheld as questions for later Congresses.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    The war was fought by a segregated army, and the empire was debated in the language of capacity, with opponents and supporters alike speaking of Filipinos as a people unfit, for the present, to share citizenship. At home McKinley toured the South in the interest of sectional reconciliation and did not ask for a revival of federal election supervision. Wilmington's white supremacist coup in 1898 and the spread of new Southern constitutions drew no federal intervention, and Black citizens remained outside the government he was willing to disturb.

  7. 7. Federalism

    Domestic regulation advanced little beyond the institutions he inherited, and the great federal growth was external. Puerto Rico, Hawaii, Guam, and the Philippines were governed from Washington and were not states, and the Insular Cases, beginning in 1901, held that the Constitution did not follow the flag in every particular. States kept their police power, their schools, and their racial codes: empire was national, and Jim Crow was left local.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    He led the country into war with Spain in April 1898, after the Cuban rebellion, the destruction of the Maine in Havana harbor, and a public demand he finally accepted, and the Teller Amendment disclaimed any American intention to annex Cuba even as the Treaty of Paris took Puerto Rico, Guam, and the Philippines, the last for twenty million dollars. The Platt Amendment then gave the United States a right to intervene in Cuba and a naval station at Guantanamo. He also sent troops to China during the Boxer rising without a declaration of war, while Secretary of State John Hay circulated the Open Door notes asking the powers to keep Chinese trade open and China's territory intact.

  9. 9. National defense

    The war was short against Spain and long against Filipinos who had expected independence. Dewey's squadron destroyed the Spanish fleet at Manila Bay, a volunteer and regular force took Santiago and Puerto Rico, and the army that won those easy victories was then committed to a conquest of the Philippines that was still unfinished when McKinley died. The new navy proved itself, and a small colonial army became a permanent requirement.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    Supporters of keeping the Philippines spoke of a duty to Christianize and civilize, a missionary argument McKinley used in private conversation with churchmen and did not enact as a church establishment, and Catholic friar lands in the islands became a political problem for a Protestant American administration. At home he recommended no religious test and no national church. Public morality remained, as before, mostly a state subject.

  11. 11. Education

    In the new possessions, American authorities set up public schools as an instrument of rule, a federal educational project that had no counterpart in the states. Domestic common schools stayed with states and counties, and land-grant colleges continued under earlier laws. He created no national university and no cabinet department of education.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    Hawaii was annexed by joint resolution in July 1898, during the war, not by reviving the treaty Cleveland had withdrawn. The public-land system of the continental West was not his subject in the way it became Roosevelt's, and Indian allotment continued on the mainland without a new policy from him. What changed the map was sovereignty: island territory, a Cuban base, and a refusal, under the Teller Amendment, to take title to Cuba itself.

  13. 13. Energy

    Coal remained the navy's fuel and the industrial fuel, produced by private mines, and the war did not produce a fuel administration. There was no department of energy and no federal oil reserve yet set aside for the fleet. Control of coaling stations, at Guantanamo and in the Pacific, mattered more to his strategy than any domestic energy law.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    He was shot on September 6, 1901, by Leon Czolgosz, an anarchist, at the Pan-American Exposition in Buffalo, and he died on September 14. The murder made anarchism a subject of federal anxiety, but the criminal law that punished it was still largely state law, while in the Philippines military commissions and harsh field punishment were the justice of conquest. He left no revision of the domestic criminal code equal to the change in the country's territory.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    The return of employment after 1897 reduced the political pressure for federal relief without any change in principle, and pensions for Union veterans continued to be the national cash payment. Colonized populations received schools and sanitation campaigns more readily than income support. There was still no federal poor law.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    Army medical work in Cuba, including Walter Reed's demonstration that yellow fever was transmitted by mosquitos, was a genuine federal contribution to medicine, born of occupation. The Marine Hospital Service and immigration inspection remained the standing civilian agencies. There was no national health insurance and no pure-food act, both of which waited for the next presidency.

Of this presidency

  1. The Dingley Tariff

    Passed in July 1897, the Dingley Tariff restored high protection after the muddle of Wilson-Gorman and fulfilled the central domestic promise of the 1896 campaign. Average duties on protected goods rose sharply, and specific rates were written to shield manufacturers who had blamed the depression on foreign goods. Reciprocity clauses gave McKinley some room to bargain. The act reaffirmed that the Republican party, whatever else it was about to become abroad, was still the party of the protective tariff at home.

  2. The Gold Standard Act

    The act of March 14, 1900, made the gold dollar the sole standard of value and required the Treasury to maintain gold redemption of United States notes. It was the legislative end of the free-silver struggle that had dominated the previous decade. Bimetallism survived as a memory and as Bryan's platform, not as the working rule of the currency. McKinley did not found the Federal Reserve. He settled the question the Reserve would later administer: the dollar was a gold dollar.

  3. War with Spain

    Congress declared war in April 1898, after the Maine exploded in Havana harbor with the loss of more than two hundred and fifty American sailors, a blast popularly charged to Spain and never proved to be a Spanish mine. The fighting in Cuba and Puerto Rico and against the Spanish squadron in the Philippines was brief. The Teller Amendment, adopted before the war, pledged that the United States would not annex Cuba. Victory was easy, and the political decision about the Spanish empire's remains was not. McKinley chose to keep what Spain ceded in the Pacific and the Caribbean, Cuba alone excepted, and that choice opened a second war.

  4. The Philippines, Puerto Rico, and Guam

    By the Treaty of Paris, ratified in 1899, Spain ceded Puerto Rico and Guam and sold the Philippines for twenty million dollars. Filipino forces under Emilio Aguinaldo, who had fought Spain and expected a republic, were at war with the United States by February 1899. The conquest killed far more Filipinos than Americans and was marked by atrocities, including torture of prisoners, before it was declared over under the next president. Puerto Rico received civil government under the Foraker Act of 1900 without statehood or, yet, citizenship. The Supreme Court soon held that these unincorporated territories need not receive every constitutional guarantee. The United States had become an imperial power in plain fact.

  5. Hawaii annexed

    Cleveland had withdrawn Harrison's annexation treaty in 1893 and refused another. McKinley favored annexation, could not get a two-thirds vote in the Senate for a treaty, and accepted a joint resolution, the Newlands Resolution, which Congress passed in July 1898 while the war with Spain made the islands look like a strategic necessity. Annexation was therefore a statute, not a ratification of the treaty Cleveland had taken back. An organic act in 1900 made Hawaii a territory. Statehood was not offered.

  6. The Open Door

    In 1899 Secretary Hay asked the powers that held spheres in China to keep those spheres open to the commerce of other nations, and during the Boxer Rebellion in 1900 he added a call to preserve China's territorial integrity. The notes were not a treaty, and the other powers did not bind themselves in any firm way. McKinley also contributed American troops to the international expedition that relieved the legations in Peking. The Open Door was a policy of equal trade and of opposition to partition, enforced by diplomacy and, in 1900, by a small share of a foreign march on a Chinese capital.

  7. Assassination

    Czolgosz shot McKinley at point-blank range in a receiving line at Buffalo on September 6, 1901. The president died eight days later, and Theodore Roosevelt took the oath. The crime ended a presidency that had just been reelected on the twin grounds of sound money and overseas expansion. It did not reverse either. The gold act stood, and the empire stood, to be administered by a successor who had become famous charging up a hill in the war McKinley had requested.

PreviousPollock and Plessy NextTheodore Roosevelt

1901–1909

Theodore Roosevelt

Republican

Before the office

He was born in 1858 in a brownstone in New York, and he was not expected to be strong. The asthma nearly killed him as a child. His father, Theodore Roosevelt Sr., a philanthropist and a man the boy treated as a conscience, built him a gymnasium and told him the mind would not be enough. He made the body by force of will: boxing, riding, the outdoors, a program he never afterward distinguished from morality. His mother was a Bulloch from Georgia. The war that had divided the country had sat at his own dinner table, and his father, who had not gone to fight, hired a substitute. The son spent the rest of his life refusing to be the man who stayed home.

Harvard polished him and did not tame him. In 1880 he married Alice Hathaway Lee, and he entered the New York Assembly as a young man the professionals first mistook for a dude with glasses and a high voice. He learned the machine, fought it, and made himself useful to reform without becoming a man who could only scold. Then, on February 14, 1884, in the same house, his mother died and his wife died, the wife after giving birth to a daughter. He marked the day in his diary with a cross and the sentence that the light had gone out of his life. He left the child with his sister and went to the Badlands of Dakota to raise cattle. The winter of 1886–87 killed the herd. It did not kill the identity. He remained, in his own mind, a man who had decided that action was a cure.

The second marriage was the life that lasted. In December 1886, before that killing winter had finished its work, he married Edith Kermit Carow in London. He had known her since childhood. The house at Sagamore Hill filled with children, and the politics resumed as if the ranch had been a chapter and not an exit. Harrison made him civil service commissioner, and Cleveland, who agreed with him about almost nothing else, kept him there because he actually enforced the law Arthur had signed. He walked the New York police at night when he was a commissioner and turned a corrupt department into a newspaper serial. McKinley made him assistant secretary of the navy. He used the office to prepare a fleet for a war his chief still hoped to avoid, and when the war came he resigned, raised a regiment of cowboys and college men, and led it up Kettle Hill. The charge was real. The publicity was also real. He came home a hero who had been, six months earlier, a subordinate itching for a fight his president did not want.

New York made him governor, and he governed as if the bosses were an obstacle rather than an employer. Thomas Platt, who ran the Republican machine, found him expensive: corporation taxes, a show of independence, too much noise. The remedy the machine invented was the vice presidency, an office in which an inconvenient man could be buried under McKinley. Roosevelt understood the burial and accepted it anyway, because he could not resist a nomination and because he did not expect the president to die. On a porch in Minnesota, still vice president, he was already saying in public what he had written to a friend the year before. Speak softly and carry a big stick. He meant a navy, and he meant a country that would not bluster and would not be pushed.

McKinley was shot in Buffalo in September 1901. Roosevelt was in the Adirondacks. He came down the mountain and took the oath in a borrowed house, the youngest man ever to hold the office, and a man the conservative Republicans immediately feared. They were not wrong about the temperament. They were wrong if they thought he had come to found a different party. He was a Republican of the protective tariff and the gold standard and the islands McKinley had taken. What he added was the conviction that the national government was obliged to use the powers it already had: against a trust that crushed competition, against a railroad that set a confiscatory rate, against the waste of the public forests, and for a navy large enough to make the proverb true.

The difference from McKinley was not a new constitution. It was speed, pleasure in the fight, and a willingness to be seen using the office. McKinley had managed difficulty by staying calm and hoping peace would hold. Roosevelt had answered a boyhood weakness, a double funeral, and a failed ranch by insisting that the strenuous life was the only honest one. He took the presidency by an assassin’s bullet, which he had not sought, and he took it as a man who had already decided that power unused was a kind of failure. The stick was the navy and the statute book. The soft voice was never the part he found natural.

In the office

Roosevelt made regulation and conservation the ordinary work of a Republican presidency that had previously emphasized the tariff, the pension list, and, under McKinley, war overseas. Rate-setting for railroads, inspection of meat and drugs, and a multiplication of forests and monuments were uses of the commerce power and of federal ownership, not amendments to the frame of government. Jim Crow at home and American rule in the Philippines remained in place while that domestic authority grew.

The party

McKinley's Republicans had stood for the high tariff, the gold dollar, and the islands taken from Spain. Roosevelt kept the party, the gold standard, and the empire, and he added a domestic regulation McKinley had not practiced: railroad rates, selected antitrust suits, inspection of food, and a great enlargement of the public forests, which he called the Square Deal. It was not a new party and not a new constitution. It was the commerce power and the federal lands put to steady administrative use.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    Customs and excises still paid for the government, and he did not make the tariff the fight of his presidency, though he came to favor a downward revision he left to Taft. He spoke for taxes on large incomes and inheritances and got neither a statute nor an amendment. Spending rose with the navy and with reclamation, without a general program of relief, and the budget was still assembled piece by piece in Congress rather than as one executive document.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    The Dingley Tariff stayed law, while his distinctive trade regulation was domestic: the Elkins Act of 1903 attacked railroad rebates, and the Hepburn Act of 1906 gave the Interstate Commerce Commission power to set maximum rates. He also sued the Northern Securities combination and won a decree dissolving it in 1904. The great decrees against Standard Oil and American Tobacco were entered later, under Taft, though the Standard Oil suit had been filed while Roosevelt was president.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    He accepted the gold standard McKinley had written into law and did not reopen free silver. The panic of 1907, set off by the failure of trust companies in New York, was halted by a private rescue organized by J. P. Morgan, with Roosevelt's agreement not to attack United States Steel for buying Tennessee Coal and Iron during the crisis. The Aldrich-Vreeland Act of 1908 then allowed emergency currency, a stopgap rather than the Federal Reserve.

  4. 4. Labor

    In the anthracite coal strike of 1902 he did what Cleveland had not done in 1894: he pressed the employers, threatened to operate the mines with the army, and obtained arbitration rather than simply breaking the strike, so that the miners gained a wage increase and a shorter day without recognition of the union. He supported an employer's liability law for interstate railroads, enacted in a form the Court would accept only on a second try. He had no sympathy for Debs or for socialism, and he did not propose a general right to bargain.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    European immigration remained large and was not put under a quota. After San Francisco segregated Japanese pupils, he bargained for an end to that insult in exchange for the Gentleman's Agreement of 1907, under which Japan stopped issuing passports to laborers bound for the mainland, while Chinese exclusion continued. He preferred a diplomatic limit on Japanese migration to a statute that would humiliate a rising navy in the Pacific.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    He invited Booker T. Washington to dinner at the White House in 1901, and the Southern outcry did not turn the gesture into a civil-rights policy. In 1906, after a shooting affray at Brownsville, Texas, he summarily discharged without honor the Black soldiers of three companies of the Twenty-fifth Infantry, one hundred and sixty-seven men, denying them a trial. He did not challenge disfranchisement or lynching with legislation, and in the Philippines as at home he shared the period's assumption that some peoples were not ready for self-government.

  7. 7. Federalism

    Railroad rates, food and drugs in interstate commerce, and combinations in restraint of that commerce became routine subjects of national commissions and prosecutions. Public lands and forests were federal property, and he managed them as such, sometimes ahead of a statute. He did not take over schools, ordinary policing, or the suffrage, and the states kept Jim Crow because he would not spend federal power against it.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    He took Panama in order to build a canal, announced that the United States would police the hemisphere against European debt collections, mediated the end of the Russo-Japanese War at Portsmouth in 1905, and sent troops back into Cuba under the Platt Amendment in 1906. The Philippines were pacified into civil government under American sovereignty, not released. His foreign policy was the Monroe Doctrine rewritten as intervention, plus a readiness to sit as broker among great powers.

  9. 9. National defense

    Elihu Root's reforms, begun under McKinley and carried through under Roosevelt, gave the army a general staff and a modern militia framework, and the navy he favored was advertised by the cruise of the battle fleet around the world from 1907 to 1909. He fought no European war. He did occupy Caribbean ground and finish a colonial war he had inherited.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    He spoke in the moral vocabulary of a liberal Protestant without proposing an established church or a federal religious test, and Sunday laws, school Bible reading, and prohibition remained state questions. Missionary rhetoric still colored talk of the Philippines, but the civil government he installed there was not a church. Polygamy was already legally settled before Utah's statehood.

  11. 11. Education

    Common schools stayed with the states, and he created no national system of them. The Forest Service and the reclamation projects trained a small class of federal officers and settlers, not children, and agricultural extension and vocational grants on a national scale came after he left. He valued expertise in government and did not federalize the classroom.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    The Reclamation Act of 1902 put proceeds of Western land sales into irrigation works, and forest reserves were transferred to the Department of Agriculture in 1905 and placed under Gifford Pinchot's Forest Service, which Roosevelt enlarged far beyond the forests Harrison and Cleveland had set aside. The Antiquities Act of 1906 let him proclaim national monuments, and he set aside the first national wildlife refuges. These were uses of the public domain, and they did not restore to the tribes the land the Dawes Act was still alienating.

  13. 13. Energy

    Anthracite coal heated the cities of the East, and the 1902 strike was the energy crisis of the term, settled by federal pressure rather than by federal ownership. He withdrew some coal-bearing public lands from casual entry. Oil had not yet replaced coal in the navy; the naval petroleum reserves associated with later scandals were set aside under Taft and Wilson, and there was no department of energy.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    Antitrust prosecutions, civil and criminal, were his addition to federal justice, aimed at combinations rather than at street crime, and he used the disciplinary power of the commander in chief harshly at Brownsville. Ordinary murder and theft remained state crimes. He built no national police force, and the Bureau of Investigation was only beginning inside the Department of Justice.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    The Square Deal promised a fair chance, not a federal dole, and reclamation offered irrigated land to settlers who could work it. The panic of 1907 produced no unemployment appropriation. Veterans of the Civil War were still pensioned under the old laws, and that system was not extended into a general provision for old age or for the poor.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    The Pure Food and Drug Act and the Meat Inspection Act, both signed on June 30, 1906, were the first general federal protections of the food and drug supply in interstate commerce, a response to Harvey Wiley's chemical work and to the public effect of Upton Sinclair's novel about the packinghouses, which Congress read as a sanitary scandal. They were inspection and labeling statutes, not a system of medical care. The Public Health Service remained a quarantine and marine-hospital agency.

Of this presidency

  1. The coal strike and the Square Deal

    In May 1902 the anthracite miners struck, and by autumn Eastern cities faced a winter without coal. Roosevelt called the operators and the union leader John Mitchell to Washington, failed to move the operators by persuasion, and let them know he would seize the mines and run them with troops if they would not accept a commission. They accepted. The miners won a ten percent wage increase and a nine-hour day, and the union was not recognized. He gave the name Square Deal to this kind of intervention, a claim to stand among capital, labor, and the public rather than to break a strike in the name of the mails as Cleveland had done.

  2. The Hepburn Act

    The Elkins Act of 1903 had already forbidden rebates, the secret discounts railroads gave favored shippers. The Hepburn Act of 1906 went further. It empowered the Interstate Commerce Commission to set maximum railroad rates, put the burden of court challenges on the carriers, and extended the commission's reach to sleeping-car companies, pipelines, and related services. Roosevelt made its passage a personal fight with the conservative wing of his own party. Rate regulation, which in 1887 had been a timid prohibition of unjust discrimination, was now an affirmative federal power.

  3. Pure food and meat inspection

    On the same day in 1906 he signed the Pure Food and Drug Act, banning the interstate shipment of adulterated or misbranded food and drugs, and the Meat Inspection Act, requiring federal inspection of meat sold across state lines. Sinclair had written The Jungle to recruit readers to socialism and had instead turned the stomach of the country. Roosevelt, who distrusted Sinclair's politics, trusted the evidence of filth in the yards enough to force a bill the packers and their senators had stalled. This was the start of a national sanitary police power over commerce. It was not a promise of medical treatment.

  4. Forests, monuments, and reclamation

    He treated conservation as a central duty of the presidency, not as a hobby attached to Western settlement. The Reclamation Act financed dams and canals in the arid states. Pinchot's Forest Service, after 1905, managed a reserve system Roosevelt enlarged far beyond the forests Harrison and Cleveland had set aside. Under the Antiquities Act he proclaimed national monuments, beginning with Devils Tower, and he created wildlife refuges, beginning with Pelican Island. Western members of his own party protested the withdrawals. He answered that the public domain was not a pile to be given away before the next generation arrived.

  5. Panama

    Colombia rejected the Hay-Herrán treaty that would have granted a canal zone across its province of Panama. In November 1903 a revolt on the isthmus succeeded after American warships prevented Colombian troops from landing to suppress it. The United States recognized the new republic at once and signed the Hay-Bunau-Varilla treaty, taking the zone in perpetuity for ten million dollars and an annual payment. Roosevelt did not pretend the revolution was a spontaneous gift. He treated the acquisition as his own act and defended it as such. The canal was the strategic center of the empire McKinley had begun.

  6. The Roosevelt Corollary

    The annual message of December 1904 added a corollary to the Monroe Doctrine. Where Caribbean states fell into disorder or defaulted on European debts, the United States would exercise an international police power so that European governments would have no excuse to intervene. The first full use was a customs receivership in the Dominican Republic, arranged in 1905, under which American officials collected the revenue and apportioned it to creditors. Monroe had warned Europe to plant no new colonies. Roosevelt claimed a right to govern the finances of neighbors. The two doctrines shared a hemisphere and not a meaning.

  7. The Great White Fleet

    In December 1907 sixteen battleships left Hampton Roads to circle the globe, painted white, and returned in February 1909. The cruise was aimed as much at Japan, after tensions over immigration and the Pacific, as at a general public. Congress was brought to pay for it because the president had already ordered the ships as far as the Pacific and dared the legislators to strand them there. No shot was fired. The voyage stated that the navy built in the 1890s was now an ocean fleet, and that American power sat in the same water as Japan's.

PreviousWilliam McKinley NextLochner v. New York

1905

Lochner v. New York

The Court set aside a state’s limit on working hours in bakeries. The ground was not that Congress had occupied the field. It was that the Fourteenth Amendment protected a liberty of contract the statute had infringed.

Decision

Lochner v. New York

Decided April 17, 1905

How it was made

New York limited bakery work to ten hours a day and sixty hours a week. Joseph Lochner was fined for employing a worker longer. He said the law deprived him and the worker of liberty without due process.

Who sat

Five to four. Peckham wrote the opinion. Harlan dissented, joined by White and Day. Holmes dissented alone. Roosevelt’s two appointees were both in dissent. The majority was an older Court.

With the opinion

  • Rufus Peckham — Cleveland
  • Melville Fuller — Cleveland
  • David Brewer — Harrison
  • Henry Brown — Harrison
  • Joseph McKenna — McKinley

In dissent

  • John Marshall Harlan — Hayes
  • Edward White — Cleveland
  • William Day — T. Roosevelt
  • Oliver Wendell Holmes — T. Roosevelt

What it changed

A state health law fell because the Court found it a regulation of the bargain between employer and worker, not a fair exercise of the police power. Legislatures kept passing such laws. For thirty years a changing Court struck a number of them and upheld others, with no stable line. The Fourteenth Amendment, written for the freed people, had become a limit on economic regulation. That use of it receded in 1937. It was the law of this decision until then.

PreviousTheodore Roosevelt NextWilliam Howard Taft

1909–1913

William Howard Taft

Republican

Before the office

He was born in 1857 in Cincinnati, the son of Alphonso Taft, who had helped found the Republican party in that city, served Grant as attorney general, and never let his children forget that a Taft was supposed to be useful. The mother, Louise Torrey, was as strong as the father and more affectionate. William was large, cheerful, and academically serious in a way that pleased them both. He finished second in his class at Yale in 1878 and took his law degree in Cincinnati in 1880. What he wanted, and said he wanted, was a judgeship. Not a following. Not a war. A court, where the question could be decided and the deciding would be enough.

In 1886 he married Helen Herron, whom everyone called Nellie. She had stood in the White House as a girl and had decided she would live there. He would have been content with the Cincinnati bench. She was not, and the marriage was the reason his life left the path he had chosen for it. He loved her, he deferred to her ambition more than he admitted, and he remained, under the offices she helped him take, a lawyer who thought the presidency was a distraction from the Supreme Court. The children came. One of the sons, Robert, would later carry the family name into the Senate as the voice of the Republican resistance to a later expansion of the government. The father was already, in the 1880s, the man who believed a court could do justice without making a speech about it.

The early offices were the ones he was built for. He was a prosecutor. He was collector of internal revenue for Cincinnati, and he resigned rather than dismiss competent men to make room for the party’s friends. At twenty-nine he was a judge of the superior court. Harrison brought him to Washington in 1890 as solicitor general, where he argued the government’s cases and learned that he could do it, and two years later put him on the Sixth Circuit. For eight years he was an appellate judge. He was not a romantic about strikes. He granted injunctions when he thought violence or a boycott had stepped outside the law, and he believed that taking the injunction away from the federal courts in labor disputes would create a privileged class. The belief was judicial, not theatrical. It was also why labor never trusted him, even when he was enforcing statutes labor had wanted.

McKinley pulled him off the bench in 1900 and sent him to the Philippines, first at the head of the commission and then as civil governor. He disliked the military habit of treating the islands as a conquered camp. He built courts, schools, and a civil administration, and he came to think of the work as the best he had ever done. Roosevelt, who wanted able men near him and who trusted Taft’s loyalty more than he trusted most men’s judgment, made him secretary of war in 1904. The title was smaller than the job. He was the troubleshooter: Panama, Cuba, the friction with Japan, whatever the president did not have time to finish. He carried out Roosevelt’s policies and defended them, and he did not yet understand that loyalty of that kind is a debt the recipient does not always remember in the same amount.

By 1908 Roosevelt had decided not to run again and had decided that Taft should. Taft did not hunger for it. Nellie did. The party did, because he was the heir who would not frighten the conservatives and who had been standing next to the progressives for four years. He beat Bryan. He took the result as an obligation to continue the work, and he took it with a different theory of how the work should be done. Roosevelt had regulated by publicity, by a threat, by a bargain in the White House, by an executive order that created a forest. Taft thought those methods were personal, and that personal methods died with the man. The same ends, if they were lawful, had to be put into statutes and lawsuits that the next president could not undo by being a different sort of person.

The inaugural said the scope of a modern government had widened far beyond the old laissez faire writers, and that the widening had met popular approval. He was not repealing Roosevelt. He was saying the new work was legitimate, and that he would do it as a lawyer. Supervision of the railroads, prosecution of unlawful combinations, conservation of what the federal government actually owned: all of that stayed. What he would not do was govern by being the most interesting man in the room. He had wanted a court since Cincinnati. He had accepted a presidency because his wife, his predecessor, and his party had all told him the court could wait. The tragedy of the term, which he could not see on March 4, was that the predecessor would decide the waiting was a betrayal.

In the office

Taft meant to carry on regulation by lawsuit and by orderly statute, and in antitrust he filed more cases in four years than Roosevelt had filed in nearly eight. The government he left was still the regulatory republic of the previous term, with a tariff the progressives thought too high and with an income-tax amendment ratified as he departed. Conservation, railroad supervision, and Sherman Act prosecutions widened national administration without a new constitution, and without a serious federal challenge to Jim Crow.

The party

Roosevelt had made Republican domestic policy mean discretionary regulation, conservation by presidential order, and a personal presidency. Taft stayed a Republican of the protective tariff and of the courts, and he held that the same ends had to be reached by statute and by lawsuit rather than by his predecessor's threats and bargains. He brought more antitrust prosecutions than Roosevelt and lost the progressive wing over the tariff and the dismissal of Gifford Pinchot. The party did not formally split until 1912, but this term is when the two wings ceased to trust each other.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    He wanted economy and a more orderly way of estimating expenditures, and he did not get the executive budget that came a decade later. The Payne-Aldrich Tariff was his chief revenue law, and it included a small tax on corporate income when an unapportioned personal income tax was still forbidden by Pollock. He supported the amendment that would remove that barrier, while ordinary spending remained pensions, the navy, and the routine of administration, not relief.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    The Payne-Aldrich Act of 1909 revised the Dingley rates only modestly downward and left many industrial schedules highly protective, and progressive Republicans who had expected a real cut treated Taft's defense of the bill as a betrayal. A reciprocity agreement with Canada, which he did want, failed when Canadian voters rejected it. Protection was intact when he left, ready for Wilson to attack and for Harding to restore.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    The gold standard continued, he proposed no free silver and no central bank, and although the National Monetary Commission studied a reserve system after the panic of 1907, the Federal Reserve Act was Wilson's. Taft's own monetary innovation was indirect: he backed the Sixteenth Amendment, which made a federal income tax constitutional and thereby changed what the Treasury could live on. Ratification came in February 1913, in his last weeks.

  4. 4. Labor

    He enforced the Sherman Act against corporations more vigorously than he built a labor program of his own, and he neither mediated a great strike in Roosevelt's manner nor enacted a labor exemption that courts would honor. The Mann-Elkins Act of 1910 strengthened interstate-commerce supervision, including over telegraph and telephone lines. Hours, the recognition of unions, and child labor in factories remained, for his term, outside a successful federal code.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    Immigration from Europe stayed high, and he signed no quota. In 1913 he vetoed an immigration bill whose central device was a literacy test, as Cleveland had vetoed one in 1897, while Chinese exclusion and the Gentleman's Agreement with Japan remained the Asian limits. The literacy test became law only when Congress later overrode Wilson, so Taft's veto kept European immigrants unnumbered through his term.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    He did not propose to revive federal supervision of Southern elections, he did not support an anti-lynching law, and Republican efforts to court white Southerners continued to thin Black officeholding in the region. Jim Crow constitutions stood. He administered the empire Roosevelt had left without offering the Philippines independence or Puerto Ricans statehood, and racial hierarchy was not a subject he chose to spend capital on.

  7. 7. Federalism

    Antitrust suits and the Mann-Elkins Act carried forward the national regulation of interstate business, and two constitutional amendments altered the federal design itself: the income-tax amendment, ratified before he left, and the direct election of senators, sent to the states in 1912 and ratified just after he left. He vetoed Arizona's admission while its proposed constitution allowed voters to recall judges, admitted the state when the clause was removed, and watched Arizona restore it afterward. He believed federal power should be large where the statute was clear and tightly reasoned where it was not.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    Dollar diplomacy, associated with Secretary of State Philander Knox, used American loans and financial advisers to displace European creditors in the Caribbean and to seek a place in Chinese finance, and in Nicaragua the policy was backed by marines, landed in 1912, who remained long after Taft had gone. He negotiated broad arbitration treaties with Britain and France, and the Senate, led by Lodge, cut them down until he abandoned them. The diplomacy was not isolation and not a League, but banks, and sometimes troops, inside the Roosevelt Corollary's hemisphere.

  9. 9. National defense

    He kept up naval construction while the army returned to a modest peacetime footing, and there was no great-power war. The military actions of the term were the small interventions dollar diplomacy required, especially in Nicaragua, not a mobilization. He also set aside oil-bearing public lands for naval fuel as the fleet began to burn oil as well as coal, a matter of defense logistics that later became a scandal under another president.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    No religious establishment was proposed, and no national Sunday or school-prayer statute was his. The Mann Act of 1910, forbidding the interstate transportation of women for immoral purposes, was the term's federal venture into sexual morality, written as an attack on commercial prostitution and reaching, by its language, much further than that stated target. Churches and their schools remained state concerns.

  11. 11. Education

    Common schools and universities, outside the land-grant colleges already funded, stayed with the states, and he created no cabinet department of education. The Children's Bureau, established in 1912, began federal investigation of infant mortality and child labor without operating schools. Vocational and extension grants on a large scale were left to the next administration.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    The quarrel with Roosevelt over conservation was about method and about Pinchot, not about a decision to throw open the forests. Taft withdrew oil and coal lands on a footing he considered lawful, and the Weeks Act of 1911 authorized the purchase of private land on the headwaters of navigable streams in the East, where there was no public domain to reserve. New Mexico and Arizona became states in 1912, Indian allotment continued, and the public lands were still being both withheld and disposed of.

  13. 13. Energy

    Coal and water power on public land were the heart of the fight between Interior Secretary Richard Ballinger and Pinchot, and Taft took Ballinger's side on the Alaska coal claims and a less aggressive style of withdrawal, then fired Pinchot for taking the quarrel to Congress. At the same time he withdrew petroleum lands and, in 1912, designated naval petroleum reserves in California so the fleet would have oil. Teapot Dome in Wyoming was not his proclamation, since Wilson set that reserve aside in 1915, and there was still no department of energy, only a growing federal custody of fuel lands.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    The Sherman Act was his principal criminal and civil weapon, used against industrial combinations more often than Roosevelt had used it, and the Mann Act added a federal sex crime defined by crossing a state line. Ordinary violence, including lynching, stayed in state hands that often would not punish it. He did not construct a national police, and the prosecutions that mattered politically were the trust cases.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    There was no federal relief of unemployment and no general pension beyond the Civil War system. Postal savings, enacted in 1910, let small depositors use the post office as a bank and was the term's modest innovation for people of small means. The Children's Bureau studied infant death and poverty without being given money to end either, and want remained a local and private burden.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    In 1912 the Public Health and Marine Hospital Service was reorganized as the Public Health Service, with a somewhat wider field of investigation and quarantine, but it did not become a system of care for the population. Pure food and meat inspection, already law, continued. The federal grants for maternal and infant hygiene that later marked a new departure were not enacted in his term.

Of this presidency

  1. The Payne-Aldrich Tariff

    Sereno Payne's House bill cut duties. Nelson Aldrich's Senate restored protection on a long list of industrial goods, and the law Taft signed in 1909 was much closer to Aldrich than to the downward revision progressives had been promised. In a speech at Winona, Minnesota, Taft called it the best tariff bill the Republican party had ever passed. The sentence fixed the insurgent revolt. The act did create a tariff board and a one percent tax on corporate net income, so it was not a mere copy of Dingley. It was still a protective tariff, and it convinced Roosevelt's wing that Taft had gone over to the Old Guard.

  2. Ballinger and Pinchot

    Richard Ballinger, secretary of the interior, reopened certain withdrawn water-power sites and was accused of favoring claimants to rich Alaska coal lands, including interests tied to the Guggenheims. Gifford Pinchot, still chief of the Forest Service, carried the attack beyond the administration to Congress and the press. Taft concluded that Ballinger had acted lawfully and that Pinchot had been insubordinate, and he dismissed Pinchot in January 1910. A congressional investigation did not break Ballinger, who resigned anyway in 1911. Roosevelt took the dismissal as proof that his successor had abandoned conservation. Taft thought he had rescued it from extralegal heroics, and the Weeks Act and his oil withdrawals show that the reserves were not handed back to the first comer.

  3. Dollar diplomacy

    Knox's policy substituted American capital for European loans in small Caribbean states and sought a share of railroad loans in China, on the theory that financial supervision would produce order and keep rivals out. Where the theory met resistance, as in Nicaragua, Taft sent marines and left them in place to support a government friendly to the arrangement. Dollar diplomacy was therefore not a peaceful alternative to the Roosevelt Corollary. It was the corollary's aim pursued through bankers, with troops in reserve and sometimes in the streets. The Chinese consortium, meanwhile, largely failed. The Caribbean occupations did not.

  4. Antitrust prosecutions

    Taft's attorney general filed more cases under the Sherman Act in one term than Roosevelt had filed in nearly two. In 1911 the Supreme Court ordered the dissolution of Standard Oil and of American Tobacco, applying a rule of reason that distinguished restraints from reasonable agreements, and the government went on to sue United States Steel. That steel suit reached the purchase of Tennessee Coal and Iron, which Roosevelt had personally cleared during the panic of 1907. Roosevelt regarded the suit as a personal affront. Taft regarded it as evidence that he did not sort trusts into good and bad by private letter. The cases were his, not Roosevelt's later borrowed glory.

  5. The Sixteenth Amendment

    Congress proposed the income-tax amendment in 1909, during the tariff fight, as a substitute for a statutory tax that Pollock had seemed to forbid unless it was apportioned among the states by population. Taft supported the amendment, which authorizes a tax on incomes from whatever source without apportionment. It was ratified on February 3, 1913, before he left office. He did not sign the modern income-tax schedule. That came later in 1913, inside Wilson's tariff. The amendment was the constitutional change; the rates were another presidency's work.

  6. The Seventeenth Amendment

    In 1912 Congress sent the states an amendment transferring the election of senators from the legislatures to the people of each state. Taft was not the leader of the long campaign for direct election, which progressives and a number of states had already forced in practice by preferential schemes. The amendment went out during his term and was ratified in April 1913, after Wilson had been inaugurated. It changed a structural feature of federalism that had lasted since 1789. The Senate ceased to be the constitutional embassy of the state legislatures.

PreviousLochner v. New York NextThe Sixteenth and Seventeenth Amendments

1913

The Sixteenth and Seventeenth Amendments

In one year the Constitution took a tax power the Court had denied and took the election of senators away from the state legislatures. Both articles had been sent to the states under Taft. One was finished before he left, and one just after.

Amendment

The Sixteenth Amendment

Ratified February 3, 1913

How it was made

Congress proposed it in 1909, after Pollock. The article gives Congress power to lay and collect taxes on incomes, from whatever source derived, without apportionment among the states. It was ratified while Taft was still president.

What it changed

An amendment overruled a decision. The federal government could tax incomes, including income from property, without dividing the burden by state population. The modern federal budget follows from that power. The amendment did not set a rate. It removed the constitutional objection Pollock had raised.

Amendment

The Seventeenth Amendment

Ratified April 8, 1913

How it was made

Congress proposed it in 1912. Senators had been chosen by state legislatures, as the original Constitution provided. Deadlocks in those legislatures had left seats empty, and the choice had become a party bargain invisible to the voter. The article moves the election to the people of the state. It was ratified a month after Wilson took office. The proposal was Taft’s Congress, not Wilson’s.

What it changed

A state legislature lost the power to name the state’s senators. Senators became dependent on a popular electorate, and the Senate became a second popular house with longer terms rather than a council of the states. Federalism lost an institutional link it had had since 1789. The states did not lose their borders or their other powers. They lost this one.

PreviousWilliam Howard Taft NextWoodrow Wilson

1913–1921

Woodrow Wilson

Democrat

Before the office

He was born in 1856 in the manse at Staunton, Virginia. His father, Joseph Ruggles Wilson, was a Presbyterian minister from Ohio who had cast his lot with the Southern church, and his mother, Jessie Woodrow, was a minister’s daughter. The boy grew up in Augusta, Columbia, and Wilmington, in the Confederacy’s towns and then in the towns of the defeat. The war was not a campaign he fought. It was the street outside a preacher’s house, and the racial order that survived the defeat was the order he never seriously questioned. The father drilled him in sentences. The words did not come easily at first. What came, and stayed, was the belief that a clear sentence was a moral act and that a man who could speak clearly had a duty to lead.

Princeton took him, class of 1879. He tried the law at the University of Virginia and in a brief, unhappy practice in Atlanta, and he discovered that he did not love cases. He loved the explanation of how a government actually works. At Johns Hopkins he wrote the book that made his name, Congressional Government, published in 1885: an argument that Congress had swallowed the presidency, and that the country needed something nearer a British cabinet, a leader who could speak for a party and drive a program through. The same year he married Ellen Axson, of Georgia, a painter and the person who could live with his certainty. Three daughters followed. He taught at Bryn Mawr and at Wesleyan, and in 1890 Princeton brought him back as a professor. He was, by then, the most eloquent academic student of American institutions in the country, and he had already decided those institutions were too leaderless.

In 1902 the trustees made him president of Princeton. He reformed the curriculum and was good at it. He then picked fights he defined as moral: a plan to replace the undergraduate eating clubs with residential colleges, and a struggle with Dean Andrew West over where the graduate college would stand and who would control it. The alumni who loved the clubs, and West, who was as stubborn as he was, beat him. He came out of the defeat with his prose intact and his habit confirmed. An opponent was not a man with a different interest. An opponent was a man who had chosen the lower side. He could carry an audience of educated people a very long way with that habit. He could also break an institution with it. Both uses were still ahead of him.

The Democratic bosses of New Jersey offered him the governorship in 1910 because a Princeton president looked clean and usable. He took the nomination and then would not be used. In two legislative sessions he got a direct primary, workmen’s compensation, and regulation of the public utilities, and he broke the boss who had expected to own him. The party, which had not won the presidency since Cleveland and which Bryan had led to three defeats, suddenly had a progressive who was not Bryan. In 1912 Taft and Roosevelt split the Republicans. Wilson’s answer to Roosevelt’s New Nationalism was the New Freedom: do not accept the great combinations and hire the government to supervise them; restore competition, cut the tariff, and build a currency that was not a Wall Street favor. He won fewer than half the popular votes and an overwhelming electoral college. A divided opposition had made a minority president, and he intended to behave as if the majority were his.

He was also a child of the states that had seceded, in a way no man elected president had been since Zachary Taylor. The Democracy that put him in was the Solid South plus a Northern progressive vote. He did not arrive with a program to disturb the settlement his region had made with the Fifteenth Amendment. The segregation of the federal offices was a decision of the term, not of the campaign. The man who would permit it had been formed in the manses of Virginia, Georgia, and the Carolinas, and his large moral vocabulary had not been built to include that question. Cooper’s Wilson is a domestic reformer of real stature and a Presbyterian who believed God had purposes in history. The purposes, as he understood them in 1913, ran to the tariff, the banks, and the trusts. They did not run to the color line.

He took the oath meaning to be the president his first book had asked for. He would go before Congress in person, which no president had done since Adams, and he would lead a party majority while he had one. The inaugural said the country had been crude and heartless in its haste to be great, and that it had built a machinery in which only the men at the levers could look out for themselves. The remedy he named was not pity and not a personal will of the Roosevelt kind. It was to cleanse, reconsider, and restore. He had the certainty of the Princeton fights already in him, and he had Ellen, without whom the certainty was harder to bear. The war was not yet the subject. The League was not yet the subject. The habit of treating a legislative program as a covenant was the subject, and he had had it since the manse.

In the office

Wilson's first term put a Democratic mark on national regulation, cutting the tariff, creating the Federal Reserve, and adding antitrust and labor statutes under the commerce power. The war then enlarged that government again, by conscription, boards that managed fuel, food, and opinion, and criminal laws against disloyal speech, without replacing the Constitution. The Senate rejected his League, and the same presidency segregated the federal offices and left lynching and disfranchisement to the states.

The party

Taft's Republicans were the party of a still-high tariff, of antitrust by lawsuit, and of an income-tax amendment they had proposed but not yet used to fight a war. Wilson's Democrats joined the Solid South to a Northern progressive vote and spent that majority on a lower tariff, the Federal Reserve, and national statutes on trusts and on some conditions of labor. It was a more active Democracy than Cleveland's. The South's presence was felt in Washington itself, where the administration segregated federal offices, and the war then built a state Cleveland would not have recognized.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    The Underwood tariff of 1913 cut duties and imposed the first income tax under the new amendment, at modest rates and with a high exemption, and wartime revenue acts then drove those rates far above the 1913 schedule while Liberty Loans placed a mass debt in the hands of the public. The Treasury became an engine of the war rather than a collector of customs with a surplus to argue about. Much of the emergency taxing receded afterward, and the income tax did not.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    Underwood was the real downward revision Cleveland had failed to get and Taft had refused: it still protected some industries, and it was not a free-trade law, but it broke the high Republican tariff that had stood, with one muddled interruption, since the Civil War. The war itself suspended ordinary trade policy, as shipments, loans, and blockade dominated and American goods poured toward the Allies long before the declaration. The statutory protectionist system returned only when the Republicans came back.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    The Federal Reserve Act of December 1913 created twelve regional reserve banks and a Federal Reserve Board, with power to discount commercial paper and to issue notes that could expand and contract. It was a central banking system designed to look unlike the Bank of the United States that Jackson had killed, and agrarian Democrats accepted it because it was regional and public as well as banker-influenced. During the war the Treasury dominated the new system in order to float the loans, and the country, which had not had such an institution since the 1830s, has had it ever since.

  4. 4. Labor

    The Clayton Act declared that the labor of a human being was not a commodity and limited injunctions in labor disputes, though within a few years the Supreme Court read those sections narrowly, and the Adamson Act of 1916 imposed an eight-hour day on interstate railroads in order to stop a national strike. Keating-Owen attempted to bar child-made goods from interstate commerce and was struck down. In wartime the National War Labor Board traded no-strike pledges for standards that included collective bargaining, a bargain that ended with the war and left no peacetime federal right to organize.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    He vetoed a literacy-test bill, as Cleveland and Taft had done, and in 1917 Congress passed the Immigration Act over his veto. The act imposed the literacy test and created an Asiatic barred zone shutting out most of Asia and the Pacific islands not already excluded, though not Japan, which remained under the Gentleman's Agreement, while European immigrants were still not held to a numerical quota. That limit arrived in 1921 under Harding, so Wilson resisted restriction and lost.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    The Solid South that anchored his party was a Jim Crow South, and he did not challenge its disfranchisement or its lynchings. His administration segregated federal offices in Washington that had employed Black and white clerks together since Reconstruction, separating Black workers and closing the better places to them. Black troops served in a segregated army and returned to the riots of 1919, which produced no civil-rights statute, while occupations in Haiti and the Dominican Republic carried the same presumption of rule over peoples he did not treat as political equals.

  7. 7. Federalism

    The Reserve banks, the Trade Commission, the Adamson law, and the child-labor act extended national administration, and the Court held in Hammer v. Dagenhart that Congress could not use the commerce power to forbid goods because of how local factories had made them. During the war the government conscripted men, operated the railroads, and directed fuel and food, a centralization without peacetime precedent that was largely dismantled after the armistice, while grants for agriculture and vocational education began a habit of federal aid with state administration. The states kept ordinary criminal law, marriage, and schools, and they kept Jim Crow.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    From 1914 into early 1917 he kept a formal neutrality while trade and credit leaned heavily toward the Allies, and he answered the sinking of the Lusitania with protest rather than with war. He asked Congress for war in April 1917 after Germany resumed unrestricted submarine warfare and the Zimmermann telegram proposed a German alliance with Mexico. Beside that European decision he occupied Veracruz in 1914, sent a punitive expedition after Pancho Villa, purchased the Danish Virgin Islands, and imposed military occupations on Haiti and the Dominican Republic.

  9. 9. National defense

    The Selective Service Act of 1917 raised an army of millions by conscription, and the American Expeditionary Forces fought in France under Pershing in 1918, while the navy's contribution was convoying and the blockade more than a fleet action. The armistice of November 11, 1918, stopped the fighting without a ratified treaty, and when the Senate refused the Treaty of Versailles the country remained formally at war until the next president signed a separate peace. The wartime army was then demobilized, but the precedent of the draft was not.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    He spoke as the son of a Presbyterian manse, in a moral register, and he established no church. The Eighteenth Amendment, prohibiting intoxicating liquor, was proposed by Congress in 1917, ratified in 1919, and took effect in 1920, all during his term. He vetoed the Volstead Act that gave the amendment its criminal code, and Congress overrode the veto, so prohibition was constitutional law he had not led, enforced by a statute he had refused to sign.

  11. 11. Education

    The Smith-Lever Act of 1914 and the Smith-Hughes Act of 1917 put federal matching money into agricultural extension and into vocational education in the public schools, administered by the states. They were grants-in-aid, not a national school board or a common curriculum, and ordinary elementary schooling remained local. The war added training for soldiers and propaganda for civilians, both temporary, and a federal university system was not created.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    He signed the Organic Act of the National Park Service in 1916, giving the parks a bureau of their own instead of a scattered custody, while the public domain in the West continued under the land laws already in force, including allotment. In 1915 he designated the Teapot Dome field in Wyoming as a naval petroleum reserve, after Taft had set aside the California naval reserves. Island territory acquired under McKinley stayed under the flag, and he added the Virgin Islands by purchase without adding continental colonies.

  13. 13. Energy

    The Fuel Administration, under Harry Garfield, allocated coal and encouraged economies, including a temporary daylight-saving rule, so that war industries and the fleet would be supplied, and oil mattered more than in any previous war. Naval reserves were held for that reason, and the controls ended with the emergency. There was no department of energy and no peacetime public ownership of mines or wells: private companies produced the fuel, and the government directed it only while the war lasted.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    The Espionage Act of 1917 and the Sedition Act of 1918 made a federal crime of obstructing the draft and, under the later act, of disloyal, abusive, or scurrilous speech about the government, the flag, or the armed forces, and Eugene V. Debs was imprisoned for a speech at Canton, Ohio. The post office barred publications from the mail, the Committee on Public Information managed wartime opinion, and Attorney General A. Mitchell Palmer's raids in 1919 and 1920 arrested thousands of radicals for deportation. These were acts of the national government against speech and association, not excesses of a mob that Washington merely failed to stop.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    No general relief of poverty was enacted, and what the war created was a system of allotments and war-risk insurance for servicemen and their dependents, a federal payment tied to military service rather than to need in civil life. Demobilization in 1919 brought unemployment that cities and charities were left to meet. He did not propose a peacetime poor law, so the seed-bill philosophy was gone in wartime finance and still intact for the civilian poor.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    The influenza pandemic of 1918 and 1919 killed hundreds of thousands of Americans, and the federal instruments on hand were the Public Health Service and the army's medical corps, not a national system of care, though wartime laws also funded a campaign against venereal disease near the camps. There was no health insurance and no peacetime program of clinics for mothers and infants. That maternal program was enacted in 1921, after he had left, and he did not sign it.

Of this presidency

  1. The Underwood tariff

    The Underwood-Simmons Act of 1913 cut protective rates substantially and supplied the lost customs revenue with a graduated income tax, the first under the Sixteenth Amendment. The tax began at a low rate above a comfortable exemption and rose, still modestly by later standards, on large incomes. Wilson treated the reduction as the moral center of the New Freedom, a strike at the alliance of tariff and trust. The war soon made the income tax, not the tariff cut, the fiscally important half of the statute. Republican protection was out of power. It was not out of the party system.

  2. The Federal Reserve

    Signed in December 1913, the Federal Reserve Act was the monetary settlement that had failed since Jackson destroyed the second Bank. Twelve reserve banks, owned in form by member banks and supervised by a presidentially appointed board, could rediscount commercial paper and issue Federal Reserve notes. The structure was a compromise between those who wanted a single central bank and those who feared Wall Street. It did not prevent every later panic. It did end the arrangement under which the country depended on the Treasury's gold pile and on J. P. Morgan's personal intervention.

  3. Clayton and the Trade Commission
