    The Federal Trade Commission Act of 1914 created a commission to police unfair methods of competition, replacing Roosevelt's Bureau of Corporations with a body Wilson preferred to personal trust-busting. The Clayton Act of the same autumn prohibited price discrimination, tying contracts, and certain interlocking directorates, and it announced protections for labor unions and farm cooperatives against the Sherman Act. Wilson had campaigned against regulated monopoly and for competition enforced by law. In practice the commission and the lawsuits lived side by side. Duplex Printing Press Co. v. Deering, decided in 1921 while he was still president, showed that the labor clauses would not be read as a full exemption.

  4. The Keating-Owen child-labor law

    In 1916 he signed the Keating-Owen Act, which barred from interstate commerce goods made in factories employing children under fourteen, or in mines employing children under sixteen, or made by children worked beyond specified hours. It was the first direct federal attack on child labor. In Hammer v. Dagenhart the Supreme Court held, in 1918, that Congress was regulating local production under the disguise of commerce and voided the act. A later attempt to reach the same end by a prohibitive tax was struck down after Wilson had left office. Child labor returned to the states until the New Deal found a path the Court would accept.

  5. War in 1917 and the Fourteen Points

    He had won reelection in 1916 on the claim that he had kept the country out of war. In April 1917 he asked for a declaration against Germany, and Congress gave it. American forces were decisive in the campaigns of 1918 more by weight and timing than by a long independent war. On January 8, 1918, he stated the Fourteen Points: open covenants, freedom of the seas, freer trade, disarmament, adjustment of colonial claims, self-determination in Europe, and a league of nations. The armistice was negotiated with those points in view. The treaty that followed bargained many of them away, and he bargained with it.

  6. Defeat of the League

    The Treaty of Versailles, which he helped write in Paris and which contained the Covenant of the League of Nations, never received the Senate's consent. Senator Henry Cabot Lodge's reservations, above all on the article that could commit members to defend one another's territory, would have required congressional approval before American force was pledged. Wilson refused to accept the treaty so reserved and urged Democrats to vote it down rather than take Lodge's version. The Senate rejected the treaty with reservations and rejected it without them, in November 1919 and again in March 1920. He had suffered a stroke on the speaking tour meant to save the covenant. The United States never joined the League.

  7. The Espionage and Sedition Acts

    The Espionage Act of 1917 punished interference with the draft and false statements intended to hinder the war, and it let the postmaster exclude dissenting papers from the mail. The Sedition Act of 1918 went on to punish disloyal, profane, scurrilous, or abusive language about the form of government, the Constitution, the flag, or the uniform. Debs received ten years for praising men who resisted the draft. The Supreme Court affirmed wartime convictions, including his, under a test that allowed speech to be punished when it was found to create a clear and present danger. Hundreds of people were prosecuted. This was a national sedition law in a country that had not had one since 1801, and Wilson did not repent of it.

  8. Segregation of the federal offices

    Beginning in 1913, cabinet officers in the Treasury, the Post Office, and the Navy separated Black and white employees in federal workplaces in Washington, including restrooms and lunchrooms that had been shared. Personnel practices were altered so that race could be read off an application, and Black clerks were screened off, demoted, or pushed out. When William Monroe Trotter led a delegation to protest in 1914, Wilson defended the separation as a benefit that would reduce friction, and he resented being contradicted. He allowed the policy and his department heads advanced it. It was not required by any statute. It was a deliberate act of his administration.

  9. Woman suffrage

    He came late to the Nineteenth Amendment, supporting it in 1918 as a war measure after years of treating the vote as a state question. Congress sent the amendment to the states in June 1919. It was ratified in August 1920, while he was still president, and women voted nationwide in the next election. A constitutional amendment is proposed by Congress and ratified by the states. It does not depend on a presidential signature, and he did not sign this one. His eventual support mattered in a close wartime Congress. The legal change belonged to the amendment itself, not to a statute of the New Freedom.

PreviousThe Sixteenth and Seventeenth Amendments NextSpeech, Prohibition, and the Vote

1919–1920

Speech, Prohibition, and the Vote

The war and its immediate aftermath produced a decision about speech and two amendments that reached deep into private life: one forbidding a trade, and one forbidding the states to keep women from the ballot.

Decision

Schenck v. United States

Decided March 3, 1919

How it was made

Charles Schenck, a Socialist official, had mailed leaflets urging men to resist the draft. He was convicted under the Espionage Act of 1917. He said the conviction punished speech.

Who sat

Unanimous. Holmes wrote. White, Cleveland’s appointee, was chief justice. Wilson’s three appointees joined a opinion that upheld a wartime conviction for a pamphlet.

With the opinion

  • Oliver Wendell Holmes — T. Roosevelt
  • Edward White — Cleveland
  • Joseph McKenna — McKinley
  • William Day — T. Roosevelt
  • Willis Van Devanter — Taft
  • Mahlon Pitney — Taft
  • James McReynolds — Wilson
  • Louis Brandeis — Wilson
  • John Clarke — Wilson

What it changed

The First Amendment does not protect speech that creates a clear and present danger of an evil Congress may prevent. In wartime, Holmes said, words that would be tolerated in peace can be punished. The decision gave the government a test, and the test was used to sustain convictions. Holmes and Brandeis would later argue for a stricter version of it. In this case they did not dissent.

Amendment

The Eighteenth Amendment

Ratified January 16, 1919

How it was made

Congress proposed it in December 1917. One year after ratification, the manufacture, sale, and transportation of intoxicating liquors for beverage purposes was forbidden inside the United States. Congress and the states were given concurrent power to enforce it. The amendment wrote a police regulation into the Constitution itself, which none of the first seventeen articles had done.

What it changed

A private trade that had been a matter for state law became a national constitutional ban. Enforcement required a federal police apparatus the older revenue system had not been. The ban failed in practice and was repealed, completely, fourteen years later. While it lasted it was not a statute a later Congress could quietly drop. It was constitutional text.

Amendment

The Nineteenth Amendment

Ratified August 18, 1920

How it was made

Congress proposed it in June 1919. The right of citizens to vote shall not be denied or abridged by the United States or by any state on account of sex. Several states already allowed women to vote. The article made the rule national, including in the states that had refused.

What it changed

Sex ceased to be a lawful ground for excluding a citizen from the vote. The electorate roughly doubled where women had been barred. The amendment did not speak to juries, offices, or employment. It changed the composition of the sovereign that elects Congress and the president, which is a change in the government and not only in the list of voters.

PreviousWoodrow Wilson NextWarren G. Harding

1921–1923

Warren G. Harding

Republican

Before the office

He was born in 1865 in Blooming Grove, Ohio, the son of a homeopathic doctor who also farmed and of Phoebe Dickerson, a midwife. There was no distinction in the family and no legend he had to carry. He was handsome, friendly, and happiest when a room was in agreement. He went to a small college at Iberia, taught a term, and at nineteen put what money he could find into the Marion Star. The paper became the Republican voice of a county seat because he made it reliable, local, and unwilling to stay angry. He was a booster. He joined the lodges. He played in the town band. He liked to be liked, and in Marion that was a sufficient politics for a long time.

In 1891 he married Florence Kling, who had been married before and whose father, the rich man of the town, opposed the match and stayed cold for years. She was five years older, sharp about money, and much more ambitious than he was. She took the business side of the Star and made it pay. People in Marion called her the Duchess, not always kindly. He loved the ease of the marriage less than she loved the future she intended for him, and he was not faithful. The longest of the affairs was with Carrie Phillips, the wife of a friend, and it ran for years. He did not have the vanity of a man who wanted to be caught. He had the carelessness of a man who thought a private kindness could be kept in a separate drawer from a public smile. The drawer did not stay shut.

Ohio sent him to the state senate and then made him lieutenant governor, and he was good at both in the only way he was good at politics. He smoothed. He remembered names. He stood with the regular Republicans of the McKinley school: a protective tariff, a friendliness to business, a dislike of the agitator. In 1914 the state sent him to the United States Senate. He was popular there and not important. He missed votes, followed the party, and made no mark except the mark of a man other senators enjoyed. When Wilson brought the League back from Paris, Harding was against the covenant as it was written. He was not a thinker about Europe. He thought the country was tired of being instructed, and he thought a senator’s job was to say so without making a personal enemy of the man on the other side of the desk.

The 1920 convention deadlocked between Leonard Wood and Frank Lowden, two men with factions and enemies. Harding had a manager, Harry Daugherty, who had said the delegates would eventually pick a man in a hotel room late at night, and that the man might be Harding. Something like that happened. The senators who could not have Wood or Lowden wanted a candidate who looked like a president, who had offended nobody, and who would listen. Harding was nominated on the tenth ballot. He had already given the speech that named the mood. In Boston, in May, he said America’s present need was not heroics but healing, not nostrums but normalcy, not submergence in internationality but sustainment in nationality. The educated laughed at the word. The country did not. He beat James Cox in a landslide, in the first election in which women voted everywhere.

What he meant by normalcy was not a repeal of the twentieth century. He meant a lower temperature. The League as Wilson had drawn it was out. The wartime boards were to come down. The tariff was to go back up. Taxes were to come down from their war height. He did not propose to abolish the income tax, the Federal Reserve, or prohibition, all of which he inherited and all of which he accepted as the government he had found. He was a party man of the 1890s living in 1921, and he thought the party’s old creed, plus a little peace and quiet, was what the people had voted for. On that he was not foolish. The foolishness was in thinking that a government which had learned to run a war could be handed back to friends and would behave.

He took the oath wanting to be a conciliator, not a Wilson and not a Roosevelt. The cabinet he gathered showed both the wish and the limit. Charles Evans Hughes, Herbert Hoover, and Andrew Mellon were serious men, and he was proud to have them. Harry Daugherty as attorney general, and Albert Fall at the Interior, were the other kind: cronies of the poker game and the Senate cloakroom, men he trusted because they were his. Murray’s account of the era does not make Harding the engineer of the thefts that followed. It makes him the man who could not imagine that a friend would use the office. He had spent his life arranging rooms so that no one was uncomfortable. The presidency was the first room in which that talent was not enough, and he did not live to see how completely it had failed.

In the office

Harding was elected to take down the wartime state and to stay out of the League, and he did both, restoring a high tariff and ending the war by a separate resolution rather than by Versailles. What remained was still a larger federal government than Cleveland had defended: the income tax, the Federal Reserve, prohibition, and the island possessions all stayed, and Congress added an executive budget and the first numerical immigration quotas. He died in 1923 before the oil-reserve leases of his interior secretary were fully exposed as bribery, but the leases themselves were acts of his administration.

The party

Wilson's Democrats had cut the tariff, fought the European war, and asked the Senate to join a League of Nations. Harding's Republicans rejected the League, made a separate peace, and returned to tariff protection as the party's settled economic creed. They dismantled much of the wartime administration while keeping the income tax, the Federal Reserve, and prohibition, all of which any later president would inherit. The coalition was again the Northern and Western party of business and of distance from European political commitments, not a restoration of Cleveland's veto.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    The Budget and Accounting Act of 1921 required the president to submit one executive budget and created the Bureau of the Budget and the General Accounting Office, making the presidency, not the separate departments, the author of the spending plan. Andrew Mellon, at the Treasury, began the postwar reduction of the very high wartime tax rates without abolishing the income tax. Harding vetoed a soldiers' bonus bill in 1922, the Senate sustained the veto, and economy was the announced rule.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    An emergency tariff in 1921 raised agricultural duties in response to the collapse of farm prices after the war, and the Fordney-McCumber Tariff of 1922 then restored general protection, reversing the Underwood policy and adding a limited power for the president to adjust rates on the recommendation of a tariff commission. Republican protection was back in statute. It did not restore the world trade of 1913, and European retaliation and war debts sat outside the schedule.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    The Federal Reserve, still young, had already presided over a violent deflation in 1920 and 1921, and Harding did not remodel it. Gold remained the standard, silver was not reopened as a political cause, and what the administration offered farmers was tariff protection and easier agricultural credit, not a change in the dollar. Monetary politics had moved from the coinage fights of the 1890s to the quieter management of a central bank.

  4. 4. Labor

    Wartime boards that had sheltered collective bargaining were gone, and in the shopmen's strike of 1922 Attorney General Harry Daugherty obtained an injunction so broad that it forbade almost any encouragement of the strike, a return to the Cleveland pattern and a rejection of the wartime truce. Unemployment in the sharp depression of 1921 was met by a conference, chaired by Herbert Hoover, that urged local and voluntary action. There was no federal unemployment insurance and no statute granting a general right to organize, so labor's legal position was weaker than it had been in 1918.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    The Emergency Quota Act of 1921, which he signed, limited annual immigration from each European country to three percent of the number of foreign-born persons of that nationality living in the United States in 1910. It was temporary, it exempted the Western Hemisphere, and it left Asian exclusion to the older laws, but it was the first numerical national-origins quota. The permanent system, with tighter numbers and the 1890 census as its base, was enacted in 1924 under Coolidge, not under Harding, yet the principle that European immigration would be capped by nationality was his statute.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    In a speech at Birmingham in October 1921 he said that Black citizens should have political and economic equality, an unusual thing for a president to say in the Deep South, and he did not follow it with a voting-rights law. The Dyer anti-lynching bill passed the House in 1922 and died under a Southern filibuster in the Senate, which he did not break. The destruction of the Greenwood district in Tulsa in June 1921, in his first year, drew no federal action that restored the victims or punished the killers, and Jim Crow stood untouched by the administration's gestures.

  7. 7. Federalism

    The budget act strengthened the national executive against the old congressional scramble for appropriations, and Sheppard-Towner together with the highway act of 1921 continued grants-in-aid, federal money administered through state programs, a device Wilson had already used for agriculture and vocational schools. Prohibition required federal criminal enforcement in states that would not help, and that friction was constant. He did not try to nationalize ordinary labor law or the suffrage rules of the South, and the states kept Jim Crow.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    He refused membership in the League of Nations, which the 1920 election had already condemned, and in July 1921 a joint resolution declared the war with Germany and Austria-Hungary at an end, with separate treaties following. The Washington Naval Conference of 1921–1922 was his alternative to collective security: limitation of battleships, a Pacific consultation treaty that ended the Anglo-Japanese alliance, and a nine-power restatement of the Open Door in China. He would cooperate by conference and not by the covenant Wilson had lost, while occupations in Haiti and, for a time, the Dominican Republic continued from the previous decade.

  9. 9. National defense

    The Five-Power Treaty fixed a capital-ship ratio of five for the United States and Britain, three for Japan, and smaller allowances for France and Italy, and it paused the building of new battleships, while the army was cut back toward a peacetime establishment after the vast wartime force had already been sent home under Wilson. Harding thus accepted naval limitation as national policy while keeping a fleet meant to be equal to Britain's. Coast defense and the small regular army, not a European garrison, were the military posture, and the oil reserves were supposed to fuel that navy, which is why their lease mattered.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    Prohibition was in force for the whole of his term, under the Eighteenth Amendment and the Volstead Act, and official policy was enforcement even though enforcement was uneven, widely evaded, and already a source of organized crime. He proposed no change in the relation of church and state. Public morality had become, through prohibition, a federal criminal duty whether local majorities wanted it or not.

  11. 11. Education

    Public schools remained with the states and the districts, while federal vocational and extension grants continued from Wilson's statutes and Sheppard-Towner paid for instruction of mothers as much as for clinics, still through state acceptance of a grant. He created no department of education and no national curriculum. The schooling of children was not a subject of his presidency in the way quotas, the tariff, and the naval treaties were.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    The scandal of the term was a disposition of public mineral land: naval petroleum reserves that Taft and Wilson had withheld for the fleet were transferred from the Navy to the Interior Department by an order Harding signed, and then leased without competitive bidding. Ordinary conservation receded from the intensity of Roosevelt's years, and Secretary of the Interior Albert B. Fall was hostile to the older restraints. The public domain was again treated, by at least one of his appointees, as a store of favors.

  13. 13. Energy

    Coal strikes in 1922 disrupted supply, and the administration's instrument was the labor injunction, not a fuel administration of the wartime kind. Oil was the strategic fuel of the new navy, which is why Elk Hills in California and Teapot Dome in Wyoming had been reserved, and Harding's transfer of those reserves to Interior, followed by Fall's secret leases to Edward Doheny and Harry Sinclair, put that fuel into private hands for bribes that were proved later. There was no department of energy, only a cabinet officer leasing the navy's oil land.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    Prohibition enforcement and political corruption were the federal criminal facts of the term. Charles R. Forbes, head of the Veterans Bureau, took kickbacks on hospital supplies and contracts, resigned while Harding was alive, and was later imprisoned, while Fall's oil leases, a crime against public property exposed chiefly after the president's death, ended in Fall's later conviction. The injunction against the shopmen made peaceful support of a strike into a contempt, and Harding's own distinctive act of justice ran the other way: he shortened the sentences of Debs and other wartime prisoners.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    The depression of 1920–1921 was sharp, and the unemployment conference he called recommended community chests, local public works, and voluntary restraint by employers, not a federal dole. Veterans' care was the federal social expenditure that existed, gathered into the Veterans Bureau, and it was the bureau Forbes looted, while farm distress was answered with a tariff and credit rather than with price supports of the kind Coolidge would later veto. Poverty as such remained outside the federal budget.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    On November 23, 1921, he signed the Sheppard-Towner Act, granting federal money to states for programs on the health of mothers and infants, administered through the Children's Bureau, the first substantial federal welfare grant of its kind and one the American Medical Association attacked as an intrusion on medical practice. It was not a national health service, its funds were allowed to lapse at the end of the decade, and the Veterans Bureau's hospitals, the administration's other health effort, were real and were corrupted by Forbes. Wilson had not signed Sheppard-Towner, and Harding did.

Of this presidency

  1. Normalcy

    In the 1920 campaign he offered a return to normalcy, by which he meant an end to wartime controls, a quieter reform politics, a high tariff, lower taxes than the war had required, and a refusal of the League. The word was his, and the public understood it. Normalcy did not repeal the income tax, the Federal Reserve, woman suffrage, prohibition, or the overseas possessions. It meant that the Republican party would administer those inheritances in a conservative temper and would not extend the wartime state into a permanent one. He won in a landslide over James M. Cox.

  2. The Budget and Accounting Act

    Before 1921 each department sent its own estimates to Congress, and no one officer was responsible for a total. The act Harding signed required a single budget from the president, lodged a Bureau of the Budget in the Treasury to prepare it, and created the General Accounting Office to audit spending for Congress. Charles G. Dawes, the first budget director, used the new machinery to cut expenditures. The statute is less famous than the scandals and more lasting. It is the reason later presidents submit a budget at all.

  3. The Washington Naval Conference

    Secretary of State Charles Evans Hughes opened the conference in November 1921 by proposing that the powers scrap battleships on a fixed ratio, and the Five-Power Treaty of 1922 wrote that proposal into limits on capital ships. A Four-Power Treaty among the United States, Britain, Japan, and France replaced the Anglo-Japanese alliance with an agreement to consult on Pacific disputes. A Nine-Power Treaty pledged respect for China's independence and for the Open Door. The Senate consented. The treaties did not limit cruisers, submarines, or armies in any comparable way, and they did not create a league. They were Harding's evidence that the country could bargain with great powers without Wilson's covenant.

  4. The emergency tariff and Fordney-McCumber

    Farm prices had collapsed while European agriculture recovered, and the Emergency Tariff Act of May 1921 raised duties on a list of agricultural products as a temporary shield. Fordney-McCumber, in September 1922, made high protection general again, covering industry as well as farms, and allowed the president to raise or lower a duty within a limited range if the Tariff Commission found that costs of production required it. The Underwood reductions were undone. The flexible provision was used, in practice, more often to raise rates than to cut them. The Republican party had returned to the tariff position it had held under Harrison and McKinley.

  5. Teapot Dome and the other scandals

    Albert B. Fall, secretary of the interior, persuaded Harding and Secretary of the Navy Edwin Denby to transfer the naval oil reserves from the Navy to Interior. In 1922 Fall secretly leased Teapot Dome in Wyoming to Harry Sinclair and Elk Hills in California to Edward Doheny, without competitive bidding. Teapot Dome had been reserved by Wilson in 1915; the California reserves had been reserved by Taft. Fall received money and bonds from the lessees. Harding died on August 2, 1923, in San Francisco, before the Senate inquiry had established that bribery in public. He was not shown to have taken a bribe himself. He had already been shaken by another appointee, Charles R. Forbes of the Veterans Bureau, whose corruption came to light while the president was alive. The oil scandal is a scandal of his administration and of men he appointed, fully exposed after he was dead.

  6. Commutation of Debs

    Eugene V. Debs was in the Atlanta penitentiary under a ten-year sentence Wilson had refused to disturb, imposed for a speech against the draft. On Christmas 1921 Harding commuted the sentence so that Debs could be released, and he commuted the sentences of a number of other prisoners convicted under the Espionage Act. It was not a pardon. Civil rights, including citizenship, were not restored. Debs left prison still under the disability of the conviction, and he called on Harding at the White House on his way home. The act did not repeal the Espionage Act. It did mark a Republican president undoing, for one famous prisoner, a piece of Wilson's wartime justice.

  7. The Emergency Quota Act

    Signed in May 1921, the quota act capped immigration from each eligible nationality at three percent of the foreign-born of that nationality counted in the 1910 census, with a ceiling for the whole of a few hundred thousand a year. The Western Hemisphere was left outside the quotas. Asia remained governed by exclusion and by the barred zone of 1917. Congress wrote the law as an emergency, to hold the line until a permanent formula could be passed. Harding signed the emergency, not the National Origins Act of 1924. The older American assumption, that Europeans might come without a numerical ceiling, ended in his term.

  8. Sheppard-Towner

    The Promotion of the Welfare and Hygiene of Maternity and Infancy Act, known as Sheppard-Towner, became law on November 23, 1921, over his signature. It offered federal grants to states for clinics and instruction aimed at the deaths of mothers and infants, and it was administered by the Children's Bureau rather than by a new medical establishment. Opponents called it socialized medicine. It was in fact a small, optional, time-limited grant, and Congress let it expire in 1929. It belongs to Harding's term and not to Wilson's, which had ended without it, and it is the first federal health-welfare subsidy of its kind.

PreviousSpeech, Prohibition, and the Vote NextCalvin Coolidge

1923–1929

Calvin Coolidge

Republican

Before the office

He was born on the Fourth of July, 1872, in Plymouth Notch, Vermont, and the date was the only flourish the place ever offered him. His father, John Coolidge, kept the store, farmed, sat as a justice of the peace, and served in the legislature. His mother, Victoria, died of tuberculosis when the boy was twelve. His only sister died a few years later. The hills did not make a performance out of grief, and neither did he. He was slight, red-haired, and already economical with words. People who met him later thought the silence was a trick. It was a childhood. Vermont did not pay a man for explaining himself, and he never afterward saw a reason to start.

Amherst College took him, class of 1895. He was not a social figure. A professor of moral philosophy, Charles Garman, gave him the habit of treating a principle as something you could state in a sentence and then keep. He read law in a Northampton office, was admitted to the bar in 1897, and stayed in that town for the rest of his private life. The practice was modest. In 1905 he married Grace Goodhue, who taught at the Clarke School for the Deaf and who could fill a room he would have left empty. She was the warmth in the household. Two sons were born. He remained, beside her, the man who would sit through a dinner and contribute a sentence if a sentence was required. The marriage worked because she did not try to turn him into someone else, and because he knew what she was doing for him.

The offices came one at a time, and he took each one as if it were the whole job. Northampton councilman, city solicitor, clerk of courts, a term in the Massachusetts house, mayor, state senator. In 1914 the senate made him its president, and he told it to have faith in Massachusetts: economy, property, the town meeting, the day’s work. He was lieutenant governor, then governor. Nothing in the climb was brilliant. All of it was steady, and all of it was Republican in the old New England sense rather than in the sense of a national manager. He did not want a larger government. He wanted the one that existed to be solvent, quiet, and obeyed.

In September 1919 the Boston police walked out. The commissioner had refused them a union affiliation with the American Federation of Labor, and the men left the city unguarded. Looting followed. Coolidge backed the commissioner, called out the state guard, and would not reinstate the strikers. Samuel Gompers, the head of the Federation, asked him to put the men back. He answered by telegram. There was no right to strike against the public safety by anybody, anywhere, any time. The sentence was not a theory of labor. It was a theory of the public order: a city is not a shop, and the men hired to keep it are not free to abandon it and then ask for their places back. The country, tired of strikes and of Wilson’s last years, heard a governor who would say no in one line. That line made him national.

The 1920 convention, having nominated Harding, chose Coolidge for vice president because the delegates wanted the telegram more than the bosses wanted their own man. He presided over the Senate, lived in a hotel, and did little, which he considered the correct use of the office. He was in Plymouth, visiting his father, when Harding died in San Francisco. In the early morning of August 3, 1923, by the light of a kerosene lamp, John Coolidge, a notary, swore his son in as president. The scene was plain enough to become a legend, and it was also irregular enough that Coolidge took the oath again in Washington so no one could say the night had not counted. He did not treat the repetition as drama. He treated it as getting the papers right.

What he brought to the office was Harding’s party without Harding’s friends. The tariff, the distance from the League, the wish to cut the war taxes, and the refusal of federal relief were already the Republican settlement. Coolidge did not propose to reopen them. He proposed to stop adding to them, and to stop mistaking a companion for a qualification. Thrift, for him, was not a mood. It was the moral claim that the government should take what it needed and then go quiet, and that a president who talked less might govern less and by governing less do less harm. The police strike had taught the country he would draw a line. The lamp in Plymouth taught it that he did not need a crowd in order to take a duty. The rest of the reputation, the silence and the jokes, came after. The line was already drawn.

In the office

Coolidge became president on Harding's death and was elected in his own right in 1924. He kept the Republican settlement of the early 1920s: lower income-tax rates, a high tariff, and a narrow reading of what the federal government owed the farmer or the unemployed. The scale of the federal domestic role did not change.

The party

Coolidge continued the Harding Republicanism of tax cuts and restraint, and he was free of the scandals that had marked the previous administration. The party still treated a protective tariff, a surplus budget, and private settlement of wages and farm prices as national economic policy. It did not become a party of federal relief.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    The Revenue Acts of 1924, 1926, and 1928 cut wartime income-tax rates, the 1926 act setting the top rate at 25 percent, and the budget stayed in surplus while the war debt was paid down. He vetoed the soldiers' bonus of 1924, which Congress enacted over the veto as deferred certificates rather than as relief for the unemployed.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    He left the Fordney-McCumber Tariff of 1922 in place and supported protection as the ordinary policy of the party. He signed no new general tariff. Rate changes under the flexible provision of the existing law were adjustments inside a protective system, not a turn toward freer trade.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    He left the gold standard and the Federal Reserve Act as he found them and proposed no reform of the banking structure. He did not treat the late-1920s rise in stock prices as a subject for new federal legislation.

  4. 4. Labor

    The Railway Labor Act of 1926, which he signed, recognized the right of railroad workers to bargain and established federal mediation for that industry. He proposed no general right to organize, no minimum wage, and no federal unemployment insurance.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    On May 26, 1924 he signed the Immigration Act, known as the Johnson-Reed Act. It imposed national-origins quotas, calculated at first from the 1890 census, and it barred aliens ineligible for citizenship, which ended Japanese immigration. He signed it although he would have preferred not to single Japan out.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    The Indian Citizenship Act of 1924, which he signed, made citizens of Native Americans born in the United States. It did not end federal wardship or guarantee the vote where states withheld it. He obtained no federal anti-lynching law, and the immigration quotas wrote a national-origins preference into admissions.

  7. 7. Federalism

    He treated prices, wages, and poor relief as matters for states, markets, and private charity, and his vetoes of farm-price legislation and of government operation of Muscle Shoals followed that view. He did accept particular federal works, the Mississippi flood-control system and the Boulder Canyon project, as construction rather than as a general federal power over the economy.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    The United States stayed out of the League of Nations. He supported the Dawes Plan for German reparations and the Kellogg-Briand Pact, a general renunciation of war that created no alliance and no means of enforcement. Marines remained in Nicaragua.

  9. 9. National defense

    The army stayed small, and the navy stayed inside the limits of the Washington naval treaty of 1922. The Geneva naval conference of 1927 failed to extend those limits. He fought no war.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    He proposed no establishment and no federal support of churches. Prohibition, already in the Constitution, was the public moral rule of the term, and he treated it as law to be enforced rather than as a program of his own. Worship remained a private and local matter.

  11. 11. Education

    Schools stayed with the states and localities. The federal Bureau of Education remained a small office in the Department of the Interior, and he asked for no national program of school aid.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    The Flood Control Act of 1928 committed the federal government to levees and related works on the lower Mississippi after the disaster of 1927. He did not open a broader regime of federal land planning. Parks, forests, and the public domain continued under the laws already in force.

  13. 13. Energy

    He signed the Boulder Canyon Project Act in 1928, authorizing a federal dam and works for power and water on the Colorado River. The same year he pocket-vetoed the bill for government operation of Muscle Shoals. Oil policy was the prosecution of the Teapot Dome leases, not a new system of production control.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    Enforcement of prohibition was the visible federal criminal work of the term. Attorney General Harlan Fiske Stone reorganized the Bureau of Investigation and placed J. Edgar Hoover at its head in 1924. Ordinary crime remained with the states.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    He proposed no federal cash relief for the poor or the unemployed. Support of that kind stayed with families, charities, and local government. The veterans' certificates were pay for wartime service, not a poor law.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    No national health insurance was proposed or enacted. The Sheppard-Towner grants for maternal and infant care, a small federal program begun in 1921, were extended once and then allowed to expire. The Public Health Service went on with its limited work in quarantine and marine hospitals.

Of this presidency

  1. The Revenue Acts

    Andrew Mellon stayed at the Treasury, and Coolidge signed the tax-reduction acts of 1924, 1926, and 1928. Wartime income-tax rates came down, and the act of 1926 fixed the top rate at 25 percent. The federal budget remained in surplus and the debt incurred in the war was reduced. The cuts were not accompanied by any new federal relief or regulatory establishment.

  2. The McNary-Haugen vetoes

    Congress passed the McNary-Haugen farm bill in 1927 and again in 1928, and he vetoed it both times. The bill would have set up a federal board to buy surplus crops and dispose of them abroad, covering the loss with an equalization fee on the producers. He rejected it as price-fixing by statute. No farm subsidy system was enacted in its place while he was president.

  3. The Immigration Act of 1924

    He signed the Johnson-Reed Immigration Act on May 26, 1924. Quotas by national origin replaced the looser emergency limits of 1921, and the formula was drawn at first from the foreign-born population of 1890, which favored northern and western Europe. Persons ineligible for citizenship were excluded, and under the naturalization law that category shut out Japanese immigrants. Restriction, not recruitment, was the policy he put his name to.

  4. The Kellogg-Briand Pact

    In August 1928 Secretary of State Frank B. Kellogg signed the Pact of Paris, and the Senate ratified it in January 1929 while Coolidge was still president. The parties renounced war as an instrument of national policy. The treaty set up no enforcement, created no alliance, and required no disarmament. It did not change the size of the army or the navy.

  5. "I do not choose to run"

    On August 2, 1927 he stated, "I do not choose to run for President in nineteen twenty-eight." He did not reopen the question. The Republican nomination passed to Herbert Hoover, who had served in the cabinet Coolidge inherited from Harding.

PreviousWarren G. Harding NextHerbert Hoover

1929–1933

Herbert Hoover

Republican

Before the office

He was born in 1874 in West Branch, Iowa, into a Quaker meeting so small the town could be walked in a few minutes. His father, Jesse, a blacksmith, died when the boy was six. His mother, Hulda, a Quaker minister, died when he was nine. Relatives put him on a train to Oregon, to an uncle who was a doctor and a stern keeper of the discipline. There was no money that mattered and no audience for grief. He hoed, he studied, and he did not explain himself. The self-reliance people later called a philosophy was, first, the only arrangement an orphan had been offered. He kept it. He also kept the Quaker habit of doing the useful thing without making a testimony out of his own feelings.

Stanford took him in its first years, and he worked his way through to a degree in geology in 1895. Lou Henry was the only woman in the department. He married her in 1899, and she went where the work was. The work was mines: the goldfields of Australia, then China, where they were caught in the siege of Tientsin during the Boxer rising and he took hold of whatever practical problem the civilians had. By his thirties he was a rich man in London, an engineer who organized ore, labor, and capital on several continents. The money was not inherited and not a speculation he talked about. It was the proof, to him, that competence compounded. He did not think a life organized that way owed an apology.

The war made him a public man without an election. In 1914 he built the Commission for Relief in Belgium, a neutral machine of ships, diplomats, and accounts that fed a country under German occupation. Wilson then made him Food Administrator, and he ran the American food supply as an engineer runs a works: voluntary, public, and strict. After the armistice he fed Europe. In 1921 he fed the Russian famine, though he despised the Bolshevik government that had helped to cause it. The distinction was the one he never gave up. You organize the food. You do not adopt the politics of the people you are feeding, and you do not teach them that the next meal comes from a government they need not repay with effort. Help that left a man standing was mercy. Help that left him waiting was a harm.

Harding and Coolidge put him at Commerce, a department nobody had thought important, and he made it the busiest office in two quiet administrations. Standards, aviation, the radio, the hours of the steel mills shortened by agreement rather than by a statute he would have distrusted. He wrote the creed down in 1922 and called it American individualism: association, competence, the engineer, the government as coordinator rather than as owner. He was a progressive in the sense that he believed problems could be solved and that the twentieth century required organization. He was not Wilson and he was not Roosevelt. He thought a European dole was what happened to a people that had forgotten how to organize itself, and he had been an orphan long enough to hate the lesson.

He had never held an elective office when the party nominated him in 1928. It did not need him to have held one. He was the man who had fed Belgium and the man who had managed prosperity from a department the Constitution barely contemplated. He beat Al Smith in a landslide, and the size of the vote confirmed a confidence he already felt. Accepting the nomination, he said the country was nearer the final triumph over poverty than ever before in the history of any land, and that the poorhouse was vanishing. He meant it as a report. The market broke seven months after he took the oath. The man who gave the report did not yet have that fact. He had two continents of famine relief behind him, and a method he believed was the American one.

He came in reserved, rich, and more acquainted with hunger than any president who had preceded him. He intended to be useful inside the limits Coolidge had kept, and he intended to be more active than Coolidge, because inactivity offended the engineer. He did not intend to build a relief state. The refusal was not ignorance. It was West Branch and Belgium, drawn out to a national conclusion: the government may organize credit, association, and food in a crisis, and it must not become the source on which a free people depends for a living. The depression would ask him to abandon the conclusion. He had spent fifty years becoming the man who would not.

In the office

Hoover took office in 1929 expecting to administer prosperity inside the limits Coolidge had kept. The crash that autumn, and the depression that followed, pushed him into federal lending he had not planned. He still refused a system of direct federal relief, and the large change in the national government came after he left.

The party

Hoover's party still rejected a large federal relief state. When the Depression broke that position, the administration improvised credit for banks, farms, and homeowners rather than turn the Republicans into a party of direct federal support for the unemployed. The relief state was built by the Democrats who followed him, not by him.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    He came in on a surplus and a low-tax schedule and left with a collapsed revenue and a deficit driven by that collapse. The Revenue Act of 1932, which he signed, raised taxes sharply in an attempt to balance the budget, taking the top income-tax rate from 25 percent to 63 percent. Economy in expenditure was his stated rule, and it did not restore the revenue.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    He signed the Smoot-Hawley Tariff in June 1930, almost eight months after the stock-market crash of October 1929, raising duties on a wide range of goods that other countries answered with restrictions of their own. The tariff did not cause the crash. It added a contraction of trade to a depression already underway.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    He defended the gold standard while thousands of banks failed, and he did not insure deposits or separate commercial banking from the securities business. A banking act of 1932, also called Glass-Steagall, only widened the collateral the Federal Reserve could accept, while the Reconstruction Finance Corporation lent to banks to keep them open. Deposit insurance and the abandonment of the domestic gold standard came under the next president.

  4. 4. Labor

    He signed the Davis-Bacon Act of 1931, requiring prevailing local wages on federal construction, and the Norris-LaGuardia Act of 1932, which barred federal courts from enjoining peaceful labor disputes and outlawed yellow-dog contracts. Those were limits on injunctions and on a wage race in public works, not a national system of union elections. The Wagner Act was not his.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    The national-origins quotas enacted in 1924 came fully into effect in the first months of his term, and in 1930 the State Department told consuls to apply the public-charge clause strictly, which cut immigration sharply without a new statute. Federal authorities, together with state and local governments, also carried out a repatriation of people of Mexican origin, including some American citizens. The numbers are disputed, and the coercion was real.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    He offered no civil-rights program and obtained no federal anti-lynching law. His nomination of John J. Parker to the Supreme Court, defeated in the Senate, was opposed by the NAACP as well as by labor, and his approach to the South did not include a defense of Black voting. The repatriation campaigns fell hardest on people of Mexican descent.

  7. 7. Federalism

    His public theory was associational: voluntary cooperation, local government, and federal help aimed at credit rather than at households. The Farm Board, the Reconstruction Finance Corporation, and the Home Loan Banks were federal institutions, and they were still not a national poor law. He regarded direct federal relief to persons as a breach of that line.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    He supported the London Naval Treaty of 1930 and, in 1931, a one-year moratorium on intergovernmental debts and reparations. After Japan seized Manchuria he backed the Stimson Doctrine, a refusal to recognize territorial gains made by force, and he did not follow it with sanctions or troops. Withdrawal of the Marines from Nicaragua was completed as he left office, and he did not take the country into the League.

  9. 9. National defense

    He sought limitation and economy in the armed forces rather than rearmament, and the London treaty continued the naval ceilings of the 1920s for a time. The army remained small, and the Manchurian crisis did not produce a buildup. The treaty system was already failing when he left.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    He was a Quaker, and he made no change in the rule against an establishment of religion. Prohibition was the moral legislation he defended in public, even after the Wickersham reports showed that enforcement had broken down. The Eighteenth Amendment was repealed after he had left office.

  11. 11. Education

    He signed no federal aid-to-education statute and created no national school system. He favored better social statistics and research, including a national survey of recent social trends, which was not a school program. Classrooms remained a state and local charge.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    The agricultural depression, and then drought, dominated the land question, and he answered with credit and marketing devices rather than with production controls or a soil-conservation service. Construction of the dam authorized in 1928 began on the Colorado during his term. He did not rewrite the public-land laws.

  13. 13. Energy

    Work began on the Colorado River dam he had long wanted, a federal hydroelectric and water project under a statute Coolidge had signed. He continued to oppose federal operation of Muscle Shoals as government in the power business. There was no national regulation of oil production and no regional power authority of the kind created the next year.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    In 1929 he appointed the National Commission on Law Observance and Enforcement, the Wickersham Commission, to examine prohibition and the administration of justice. Its reports described a wide failure of enforcement, and he did not move from those reports to repeal. In 1932 he signed the Federal Kidnapping Act after the Lindbergh abduction, making interstate kidnapping a federal crime.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    He resisted direct federal relief to the unemployed and relied first on private giving and on local government, urged on by his emergency committees. The Reconstruction Finance Corporation lent to institutions, and only late, under the Emergency Relief and Construction Act of 1932, did he accept loans to the states for relief, loans the states were expected to repay. He did not accept a federal dole, and the instruments he would use did not meet the scale of the unemployment.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    No national health insurance was proposed. The maternity-and-infancy grants expired in 1929 and were not revived. In 1930 he consolidated veterans' pensions, homes, and hospitals into the Veterans Administration, a reorganization for those who had served, not a medical system for the public.

Of this presidency

  1. The Agricultural Marketing Act

    In June 1929 he signed the Agricultural Marketing Act, creating the Federal Farm Board and a revolving fund to support cooperatives and to steady prices by purchasing surpluses. It was his alternative to the McNary-Haugen bills Coolidge had vetoed: no equalization fee and no mandatory control of planting. When prices collapsed, the Board's purchases could not hold them. The experiment ended in loss and left no lasting subsidy system.

  2. Smoot-Hawley

    The stock market crashed in October 1929. He signed the Smoot-Hawley Tariff in June 1930, over the public protest of a large body of economists, having hoped earlier for a more limited revision aimed at agriculture. Duties rose across a long schedule, and foreign governments retaliated. The act did not cause the crash, which had already occurred. It deepened the fall in trade after the depression was underway.

  3. The Reconstruction Finance Corporation

    In January 1932 he signed the act creating the Reconstruction Finance Corporation. It lent public money to banks, railroads, and other institutions whose failure he believed would pull the rest of the economy down. Lending to firms and banks was as far as he would go for most of that year. It was not a grant of relief to the unemployed, and it did not stop the banking collapse that met his successor in March 1933.

  4. The Bonus Army

    In the summer of 1932 veterans camped in Washington to demand immediate cash for the bonus certificates that were not due until 1945. He authorized the clearance of federal buildings the marchers were occupying. The Army, commanded by Douglas MacArthur, then drove the veterans from the city and burned the camp on the Anacostia flats, going past a clearance of the buildings alone. The episode fixed, in public, the picture of an administration that would not pay the bonus and would not treat the march as a claim on federal relief.

  5. The Federal Home Loan Banks

    On July 22, 1932 he signed the Federal Home Loan Bank Act. It created regional banks to discount home mortgages for member savings institutions, on the analogy of the Federal Reserve's lending to commercial banks. The aim was to thaw mortgage credit. The system did not insure deposits, and it did not stop foreclosures on the scale the depression had produced.

  6. Direct relief refused

    Throughout the collapse he argued that a federal dole would weaken local responsibility and private charity, and he stood against bills for direct federal relief to households. The step he finally took, in the summer of 1932, was to let the Reconstruction Finance Corporation lend to states for relief and self-liquidating public works. Those were loans, not grants, and they were late. The line he would not cross was a national system of relief paid to the unemployed as such.

PreviousCalvin Coolidge NextThe Twentieth and Twenty-first Amendments

1933

The Twentieth and Twenty-first Amendments

The Twentieth Amendment shortened the lame-duck months of a defeated Congress and a defeated president. The Twenty-first repealed the Eighteenth outright. One was finished under Hoover. The other was finished under Roosevelt. Congress had proposed them both before the administration changed.

Amendment

The Twentieth Amendment

Ratified January 23, 1933

How it was made

Congress proposed it in March 1932. Terms of the president and vice president would end on January 20, and terms of senators and representatives on January 3, instead of March 4. A new Congress would meet in January, before it had to count electoral votes. If a president-elect died or failed of qualification, the amendment named the line. Hoover was still president when the states finished.

What it changed

A defeated Congress lost most of the long session in which it had continued to legislate after the voters had replaced it. The country no longer waited thirteen months between a presidential election and the inauguration of the winner, which in 1932–33 had meant four months of a repudiated administration during a banking collapse. The calendar of the government changed. The powers did not.

Amendment

The Twenty-first Amendment

Ratified December 5, 1933

How it was made

Congress proposed repeal in February 1933, while Hoover was still in office, and required ratification by state conventions rather than by legislatures, the only time Article V has been used that way. The conventions finished the work in December, under Roosevelt. Section 1 repeals the Eighteenth Amendment. Section 2 leaves a state free to prohibit transportation or importation of liquor into its own territory.

What it changed

A constitutional ban was removed by a later amendment, which is the only time that has happened. The federal police power written in 1919 ended. The states kept a power to stay dry. Repeal is the clearest evidence in the book that an amendment is not permanent merely because it is an amendment.

PreviousHerbert Hoover NextFranklin D. Roosevelt

1933–1945

Franklin D. Roosevelt

Democrat

Before the office

He was born in 1882 at Hyde Park, on the Hudson, the only child of an elderly country gentleman and of Sara Delano, who was younger than her husband, richer in her own right, and not inclined to share the boy. James Roosevelt was a Democrat of the old rural kind, a squire who did not think democracy required him to live like other people. Sara thought even less of the requirement. Groton, under Endicott Peabody, taught the son that a privileged man owed a duty. Harvard polished him. He passed the bar without bothering to finish the law degree at Columbia. Nothing in the youth was hard. He was charming, a little light, and already fond of being Franklin Roosevelt, a cousin of the president and a man for whom rooms arranged themselves.

In 1905 he married Eleanor Roosevelt, Theodore’s niece, and the president gave the bride away. She was serious where he was easy, and she had been lonely in a way Hyde Park did not produce. The marriage became a political partnership and, after he fell in love with Lucy Mercer and Eleanor found the letters in 1918, a partnership with the private part withdrawn. They did not divorce. His mother and his career were against it, and so, in the end, was the work they could still do together. Eleanor became a public woman in her own right, with her own causes and her own information, and he learned to use what she brought him. The charm remained. The uncomplicated marriage did not.

He entered the New York Senate in 1911 as a progressive Democrat against Tammany, which was the respectable rebellion available to a Hudson River man. Wilson made him assistant secretary of the navy, the office Theodore had used as a step, and he loved the ships and the imitation. In 1920 the party put him on the ticket with James Cox, and Harding buried them. He was not yet forty, he had run for vice president, and he had the look of a man whose rise would be pleasant and continuous. In August 1921, at Campobello, polio took his legs. He was never again able to walk unaided. Sara wanted him at Hyde Park as an invalid with a famous name. He refused the life she offered. With Eleanor, and with Louis Howe, who had attached himself to the career and would not give it up, he spent the next years learning to appear in public as a standing man: braces, a son’s arm, the smile already in place before the crowd could see the effort.

Warm Springs, in Georgia, which he bought and turned into a place for other people with the disease, showed him a rural poverty Hyde Park had only driven past. The illness did a second thing, and it is the thing Brands’s account does not sentimentalize into a saint’s conversion. It made him patient, and it made him guarded. A man who cannot walk into a room has to arrange the room before he enters it. He became a politician who kept his real position in reserve, who could mean two things by a sentence, and who hated being pitied more than he hated an opponent. The lightness did not disappear. It became an instrument. People who had known the young man thought the disease had deepened him. It had also taught him concealment, which is a political education of its own.

New York elected him governor in 1928, as the boom was ending, and reelected him when the depression was the only subject. At Albany he tried relief the state could pay for, and he watched Hoover insist that a federal dole would unmake the people it fed. Roosevelt did not accept the distinction. He had been rich all his life, and he had been helpless in a bed, and he drew from the second fact a conclusion Hoover had refused to draw from West Branch: when the private means fail, the government is what a people has. In 1932 he flew to Chicago, breaking the old custom of waiting at home to be told he had been nominated, and he pledged himself to a new deal for the American people. The phrase was not yet a program. It was a rejection of the man in the White House. The federal government would relieve, it would experiment, and it would not apologize for the size of the effort by calling dependence a moral danger.

He was a traitor to his class in the precise sense that he came from it and then used its confidence, its manners, and its presidency against its economic preferences. The men at Hyde Park’s dinner tables had not expected one of their own to make Washington the provider of work, of bank deposits, and of old-age insurance. He had not, in 1932, written the statutes. He had decided the question Hoover had treated as closed. The Constitution he intended to use was the one he had found: taxing, spending, the commerce power, and a Congress frightened enough to follow a man who could still make a crowd feel that the fear was the problem. What he added was the willingness to keep adding until the Court, the South, or the war told him to stop. On the day he pledged the new deal, none of the three had told him anything of the kind.

In the office

Roosevelt served twelve years, from the closed banks of 1933 until his death in the last year of the Second World War, and the presidency cannot be reduced to the first hundred days. The New Deal made the federal government a permanent instrument of relief, social insurance, and economic regulation, a large expansion that Hoover had refused. The war enlarged that government again, into a tax, conscription, and production state on a scale without earlier American precedent.

The party

Roosevelt's Democrats became the party of the national relief and regulatory state. Urban voters and organized labor moved into the coalition, and the white South stayed in it through his four elections even as northern Black voters shifted toward the Democrats. The party that had once stood for a lower tariff and a stricter reading of federal power now built Social Security, the Wagner Act, and a wartime government of draft, tax, and production.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    Relief, public works, and farm programs raised federal spending and the deficit above the pre-depression peacetime norm, and a drive to cut that deficit in 1937 coincided with a sharp recession, after which spending rose again. The war then enlarged expenditure on another scale entirely. The income tax, once confined to the well-off, was extended to ordinary wages, withholding began in 1943, and wartime top rates were set above ninety percent.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    The Reciprocal Trade Agreements Act of 1934 authorized the president to cut tariffs by as much as half through agreements with other countries, a turn away from Smoot-Hawley under Secretary of State Hull. It did not establish free trade. During the war, allocation and Lend-Lease, not the tariff schedule, governed the movement of American goods.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    He closed the banks, reopened the solvent ones under the Emergency Banking Act, and in the Banking Act of 1933 separated commercial banks from investment banking and created federal deposit insurance. The United States left the domestic gold standard in 1933, the Gold Reserve Act of 1934 set the dollar at thirty-five dollars an ounce, and the Banking Act of 1935 strengthened the Board of the Federal Reserve. Through the war the Reserve supported Treasury bond prices, a peg his successor eventually had to end.

  4. 4. Labor

    Section 7(a) of the National Industrial Recovery Act offered a first, brief federal protection of organizing, and the National Labor Relations Act of 1935 then guaranteed the right of private employees to organize and bargain and created the National Labor Relations Board. The Fair Labor Standards Act of 1938 set a federal minimum wage and maximum hours and restricted child labor. In wartime he used a War Labor Board and, after Congress overrode his veto of the Smith-Connally Act in 1943, authority to seize struck plants producing for the war.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    The national-origins quotas stayed in force, and he did not open them to refugees from Nazi Germany, so that the liner Saint Louis was turned away in 1939 and a War Refugee Board came only in 1944. The Bracero agreement of 1942 brought Mexican farm workers in on temporary contracts. In 1943 Congress repealed Chinese exclusion and replaced it with a token quota, as a gesture to an ally.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    Black voters in the northern cities shifted toward the Democrats, and Executive Order 8802 of 1941 barred discrimination by defense contractors and set up a Committee on Fair Employment Practice. He still would not force an anti-lynching bill past the southern chairmen who ran Congress, and he left the armed forces segregated. The wartime removal and confinement of Japanese Americans, most of them citizens, was his administration's act.

  7. 7. Federalism

    Federal grants, federal labor law, federal crop controls, and a mixed federal-state system of social insurance made Washington a presence in matters states and counties had handled or had left alone, and the change from the Hoover years was large and was not confined to 1933. The Court struck down the National Industrial Recovery Act as reaching beyond interstate commerce, then accepted the Wagner Act and the wages-and-hours law, so the practical reach of federal economic power widened even after the first recovery codes fell. Wartime conscription, rationing, and plant seizures went further still.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    He did not take the United States into the League of Nations, recognized the Soviet Union in 1933, and announced a Good Neighbor policy that renounced armed intervention in Latin America. As war spread in Europe and Asia he moved from neutrality legislation to cash-and-carry, the destroyers-for-bases exchange, and Lend-Lease, and after the attack on Pearl Harbor he asked Congress for a declaration of war. Germany and Italy declared war on the United States, and Congress declared war in return.

  9. 9. National defense

    The Selective Training and Service Act of 1940 imposed the first peacetime draft, and after Pearl Harbor the armed forces grew to more than twelve million, supplied by conscription and by a war industry under federal priority. He authorized the scientific and industrial project that built the atomic bomb. The defense establishment of 1945 had no earlier American parallel in size.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    He made no change in the constitutional place of religion and proposed no establishment. Freedom of worship was one of the Four Freedoms he named in 1941, a war aim rather than a statute. Disputes over flag salutes and conscientious objection were fought in the courts, not settled by a religious program of his own.

  11. 11. Education

    General federal aid to elementary and secondary schools failed, caught on race and on aid to church schools. The Civilian Conservation Corps and the National Youth Administration included teaching and student work, on a relief basis. The large education statute of the presidency was the GI Bill of 1944, which paid for training and higher education for people who had served, and which changed the scale of federal support for that education without creating a national school system.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    The Civilian Conservation Corps, the Soil Conservation Service, and the Taylor Grazing Act of 1934 put the federal government into erosion control, tree planting, and the end of the unregulated open range on the public domain. The original Agricultural Adjustment Act, struck down in 1936, paid farmers to cut production, and later farm acts continued that approach, while national parks were expanded and the Tennessee Valley Authority remade a river basin. This was a large federal role in land use.

  13. 13. Energy

    The Tennessee Valley Authority, created in 1933, was a federal corporation for navigation, flood control, fertilizer, and the generation and sale of electricity, the project Coolidge had refused at Muscle Shoals and Hoover had opposed, and the Rural Electrification Administration of 1935 financed lines the private utilities had not built. The Public Utility Holding Company Act of 1935 broke up utility empires, and the Connally Hot Oil Act supported state limits on oil production. In wartime, petroleum was allocated by federal authority, and the energy role of the national government became large.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    A cluster of statutes in 1934 made it a federal crime to rob a federally connected bank, to flee a state to avoid prosecution, and, building on the Lindbergh Act, to carry a kidnapping across state lines. The Bureau of Investigation grew into the Federal Bureau of Investigation, and Alcatraz became a federal prison for inmates the government would not leave in ordinary penitentiaries. He treated interstate crime as a federal responsibility, and ordinary offenses inside a state stayed with the states.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    The Federal Emergency Relief Act of 1933 made direct federal grants to the states for the unemployed, the step Hoover had refused, and the Civil Works Administration, the Works Progress Administration, and the Civilian Conservation Corps turned much of the effort into federal work. The Social Security Act added old-age insurance, a federal-state system of unemployment compensation, and categorical aid for the aged poor, the blind, and dependent children. Mass unemployment ended with the war more fully than with the relief rolls, and the relief commitment itself was a large and lasting change.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    Social Security was not a health-insurance program. Inside the Committee on Economic Security he considered medical insurance and set it aside, judging that it would endanger the bill, and he told Congress he was not then recommending it, though the act did include grants to the states for public health and for maternal and child health. No national system of medical insurance was enacted, and veterans' care through the Veterans Administration was not that system.

Of this presidency

  1. The bank holiday and Glass-Steagall

    On March 6, 1933 he closed the banks. Congress convened and passed the Emergency Banking Act, and solvent banks reopened under federal supervision, with a public explanation of which institutions were sound. The Banking Act of June 1933, known as Glass-Steagall, separated commercial banking from investment banking and created the Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation. The panic of depositor runs was stopped by those acts. They were the start of the New Deal's financial structure, not the whole of it.

  2. Social Security

    He signed the Social Security Act on August 14, 1935. It created a federal old-age insurance system financed by a payroll tax on employers and employees, a federal-state unemployment-compensation tax, and grants for assistance to the aged poor, the blind, and dependent children. Amendments in 1939 added benefits for survivors, and monthly insurance payments began in 1940. The statute was social insurance and categorical relief. It was not medical care, which he had considered and deferred.

  3. The Wagner Act

    The National Labor Relations Act of 1935, the Wagner Act, guaranteed employees in private industry the right to organize and to bargain collectively, defined unfair labor practices by employers, and established the National Labor Relations Board to hold elections and hear cases. It replaced the collapsed bargaining clause of the recovery act with a permanent statute. Combined with the wages-and-hours law of 1938, it made labor relations a federal subject on a scale the injunction fights of the 1920s had not.

  4. The court-packing plan

    In February 1937, after the Supreme Court had struck down the National Industrial Recovery Act and the Agricultural Adjustment Act, he asked Congress for power to appoint an additional justice for each justice over seventy who did not retire. The proposal would have enlarged the Court. The Senate rejected it, and the membership stayed at nine. Later that year the unreformed Court upheld the Wagner Act and the Social Security Act. The plan failed.

  5. Lend-Lease

    In March 1941, nine months before Pearl Harbor, he signed the Lend-Lease Act, authorizing him to transfer arms and supplies to any country whose defense he found vital to the United States. Britain was the first great recipient. After Germany invaded the Soviet Union, aid was extended there, and China was included as well. The United States was not yet a declared belligerent. The act made it an arsenal for the wars already being fought.

  6. The internment of Japanese Americans

    On February 19, 1942 he signed Executive Order 9066, authorizing military commanders to exclude persons from designated zones. Under that order his administration removed about 120,000 people of Japanese ancestry from the West Coast, most of them citizens of the United States, and confined them in camps for the duration of the war. Congress made violation of the exclusion orders a crime, and the Supreme Court upheld the policy. The removal was his administration's act. No comparable mass exile was imposed on German Americans or Italian Americans.

  7. The GI Bill

    On June 22, 1944 he signed the Servicemen's Readjustment Act, the GI Bill. It paid for education and training, provided a readjustment allowance for the unemployed veteran, and guaranteed loans for homes, farms, and businesses. Millions used it after demobilization, and federal support of higher education acquired a scale it had not had. The act was a veterans' statute. It was not a national health-insurance program.

  8. Yalta

    At Yalta in February 1945 he met Churchill and Stalin for the last of the wartime conferences. They agreed that the Soviet Union would enter the war against Japan after Germany's defeat, that the proposed United Nations would seat the great powers as permanent members with a veto, and that a Declaration on Liberated Europe would promise free elections. Poland's government and borders were left to a settlement dominated by the presence of the Red Army. The promise of free political choice was not kept in the Soviet sphere.

  9. Death in the fourth term

    He had been elected in 1932, 1936, 1940, and 1944, the only president chosen four times. He died on April 12, 1945, at Warm Springs, Georgia, in the first months of that fourth term. Germany had not yet surrendered, Japan had not yet surrendered, and the United Nations Charter had not yet been signed. The Twenty-second Amendment, ratified in 1951, limited later presidents to two elections, with an exception only for the man then holding the office. Harry S. Truman succeeded him the same day.

PreviousThe Twentieth and Twenty-first Amendments NextThe Court, the New Deal, and Korematsu

1935–1944

The Court, the New Deal, and Korematsu

The Court first struck the center of the early New Deal, then upheld a later statute of the same kind, and did so before Roosevelt’s appointees were a majority. Near the end of the war a different bench, mostly his, sustained the exclusion of Japanese Americans from the West Coast.

Decision

Schechter Poultry Corp. v. United States

Decided May 27, 1935

How it was made

The National Industrial Recovery Act let the president approve codes of fair competition written for each industry. The Schechter brothers, Brooklyn poultry jobbers, were prosecuted under the live-poultry code. They said Congress had given its legislative power away, and that their local trade was not commerce among the states.

Who sat

Unanimous. Hughes wrote. Brandeis and Cardozo, who often upheld regulation, joined the justices who usually did not. No Roosevelt appointee sat. He had not yet named one.

With the opinion

  • Charles Evans Hughes — Hoover
  • Willis Van Devanter — Taft
  • James McReynolds — Wilson
  • Louis Brandeis — Wilson
  • George Sutherland — Harding
  • Pierce Butler — Harding
  • Harlan Fiske Stone — Coolidge
  • Owen Roberts — Hoover
  • Benjamin Cardozo — Hoover

What it changed

The recovery codes fell. Congress could not hand the president an open power to make law for an industry, and the commerce power did not reach the slaughter of chickens after they had come to rest in a state. The decision is why the second New Deal was written differently from the first. It is also why Roosevelt, after the election of 1936, asked Congress for authority to appoint an additional justice for every member of the Court over seventy. That bill failed.

Decision

West Coast Hotel Co. v. Parrish

Decided March 29, 1937

How it was made

Washington State set a minimum wage for women. The Court had struck a similar federal law for the District of Columbia in 1923. Elsie Parrish sued her hotel for the difference between what she had been paid and the state’s minimum. The case was argued before the court-packing bill was announced, and decided after.

Who sat

Five to four. Hughes wrote. Roberts, who had been with the other side in a minimum-wage case the year before, joined the majority. Not one justice in the majority had been appointed by Roosevelt. Van Devanter retired later in 1937, and Hugo Black, Roosevelt’s first appointee, took that seat.

With the opinion

  • Charles Evans Hughes — Hoover
  • Louis Brandeis — Wilson
  • Harlan Fiske Stone — Coolidge
  • Owen Roberts — Hoover
  • Benjamin Cardozo — Hoover

In dissent

  • Willis Van Devanter — Taft
  • James McReynolds — Wilson
  • George Sutherland — Harding
  • Pierce Butler — Harding

What it changed

A state minimum wage for women stood. Liberty of contract, as Lochner had used it, no longer overrode wage regulation of this kind. Two weeks later, in National Labor Relations Board v. Jones & Laughlin Steel, the same five upheld Congress’s protection of union organizing in a manufacturing company, on the ground that the stoppage of its production would affect commerce among the states. Schechter’s narrow reading of commerce did not survive. The federal government could regulate the labor relations of production. The Court that did this was not yet Roosevelt’s Court. It was the old Court, minus one vote.

Decision

Korematsu v. United States

Decided December 18, 1944

How it was made
