After Pearl Harbor the president authorized military commanders to exclude persons from the West Coast. Congress made violation of such an order a crime. Fred Korematsu, an American citizen born in Oakland, remained and was convicted. He said the exclusion, which fell on persons of Japanese ancestry, denied him the equal protection of the laws.

Who sat

Six to three. Black wrote. Three of Roosevelt’s own appointees dissented. Roberts, the Hoover appointee who had supplied the fifth vote in 1937, dissented here.

With the opinion

  • Hugo Black — F. Roosevelt
  • Harlan Fiske Stone — Coolidge; chief justice by F. Roosevelt
  • Stanley Reed — F. Roosevelt
  • Felix Frankfurter — F. Roosevelt
  • William Douglas — F. Roosevelt
  • Wiley Rutledge — F. Roosevelt

In dissent

  • Owen Roberts — Hoover
  • Frank Murphy — F. Roosevelt
  • Robert Jackson — F. Roosevelt

What it changed

The Court sustained a racial exclusion of citizens, resting on a military judgment it said it would not second-guess in war. The decision did not create the camps. The executive order and the statute had done that. It gave them a constitutional approval, and it refused to treat ancestry, in that setting, as a reason to look harder. Jackson’s dissent said the Court had validated a principle that lay about like a loaded weapon. The case remained a citation until a later Court discredited it. The government that fought the war with this order was the same government the 1937 cases had allowed to regulate the economy. Capacity proved to be capacity for more than one kind of act.

PreviousFranklin D. Roosevelt NextHarry S. Truman

1945–1953

Harry S. Truman

Democrat

Before the office

He was born in 1884 in Lamar, Missouri, and grew up in Independence, a county seat with a memory of the border war and no interest in a boy who wanted to explain himself. His father, John Truman, traded livestock, moved when a trade went bad, and died in 1914 still chasing the next one. His mother, Martha Ellen, was the stronger of the two and lived long enough to see her son in the White House. Harry wanted West Point. His eyes ended that. He read the histories and the lives anyway, and he played the piano, and he worked: a railroad timekeeper, a bank clerk in Kansas City, then the farm at Grandview because the family needed him on it. There was no college. There was a pair of glasses and a habit of doing the next job without announcing that it was beneath him.

The war gave him the only rank he ever trusted. In 1917 he went with the Missouri National Guard, though he was past thirty, and he came out a captain of artillery. Battery D of the 129th was a Kansas City outfit that had already run off one officer. In the Vosges and in the Argonne he held them. He cursed them, he stood up when the fire came, and he brought them home. Independence called him Captain Harry for the rest of his life, and he preferred it to any later title. The battery was the proof he had not had from books: he could be afraid and still decide, and men would follow the decision. Everything he did afterward, including the morning they told him Roosevelt was dead, he measured against that.

He had loved Bess Wallace since they were children. Her family was the better family in Independence, and her mother never really conceded the point. He married Bess in 1919 and lived, for years, in the Wallace house, where he was the man who had married up and was expected to remember it. One child, Margaret, was born in 1924. The marriage was the private government of his life. He wrote Bess when he was away as if the letter were the day. With Eddie Jacobson, a partner from the army, he opened a haberdashery in Kansas City. The depression of 1921 closed it. He spent more than a decade paying debts he could have escaped. McCullough’s Truman is not a man who thinks a failed shop is a story of the business cycle. He thinks a debt is a debt. The habit is small, and it is the habit he took into offices where the sums were no longer his.

Jackson County politics ran through Tom Pendergast’s machine in Kansas City. The machine needed a Protestant veteran who was honest enough to be shown to the voters, and Truman needed a way up that a failed merchant could actually use. He became a county judge, which in Missouri meant he administered the county, and then presiding judge. He built roads and a courthouse. He did not get rich. The organization that elected him was corrupt, and he knew it, and he stayed loyal to it past the time when loyalty was comfortable. When Pendergast went to federal prison, Truman did not pretend he had never known the man. The compromise was the one he had made at the start: take the ladder that exists, do not steal, and do not abandon the people who put you on it. It was not a pure record. It was a Missouri record, and he would not dress it up as something else.

In 1934 the same machine helped send him to the Senate, and the Washington press named him the senator from Pendergast and waited for him to disappear. He voted the New Deal, worked harder than the men who mocked him, and in his second term chaired the committee that investigated waste in the war plants. The Truman Committee made his name for the opposite reason the machine had made it. He saved the government money, he was not afraid of a contractor, and he was not afraid of his own administration. In 1944 the party wanted Henry Wallace off the ticket. The bosses settled on the border-state senator who was acceptable to labor and not impossible for the South, a New Dealer who did not sound like one of Roosevelt’s professors. Roosevelt took him and told him almost nothing. He was vice president for eighty-two days. He did not know the country had built an atomic bomb.

On the evening of April 12, 1945, Eleanor Roosevelt told him the president was dead. He asked if there was anything he could do for her. She said he was the one in trouble now. The next day he told the reporters to pray for him if they ever prayed, and that he felt as if the moon, the stars, and all the planets had fallen on him. He was not being modest for the cameras. He had wanted, at most, the respect of a Senate that had once sneered at him. He inherited a war not finished, a relief state he had supported and not designed, an ally in Moscow that was about to become an enemy, and a weapon he had just been briefed on. What he brought from Missouri was not a program. It was the battery, the debts, and the refusal to hand a decision to someone else because the someone else would have been better born. The New Deal would continue because he was a Democrat who had voted for it. The rest he would have to decide as it arrived, which was the only method he had ever had.

In the office

Truman ended the war by authorizing the atomic bomb and then kept a large national-security state in place: the Marshall Plan, the North Atlantic alliance, and a limited war in Korea. The Fair Deal, his attempt to carry the New Deal further at home, was mostly blocked. The difference that became large was the Cold War government, which did not shrink to a prewar establishment when the Second World War was over.

The party

Truman kept the New Deal coalition and added the institutions of the Cold War. He also made a civil-rights break that southern Democrats would not swallow, and in 1948 a Dixiecrat ticket walked out of his convention. The party remained the party of national social insurance, labor, and the cities, and it became the party of containment and of an incomplete challenge to Jim Crow.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    Wartime tax rates were cut after 1945 but not toward the Coolidge schedule, and he vetoed the Revenue Act of 1948 only to see Congress override him. The Korean War brought new taxes, including an excess-profits tax, to pay for rearmament. He did not try to return the federal budget to its size before the depression and the war, and defense became a standing claim on a revenue still carrying the war debt.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    He continued the reciprocal-trade program and supported the General Agreement on Tariffs and Trade, concluded in 1947 as an executive agreement when the broader International Trade Organization failed in domestic politics. The Marshall Plan pushed Western Europe toward freer trade among the recipients as a condition of recovery. He was not a high-tariff president.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    The Employment Act of 1946 committed the federal government, in statutory language, to promote maximum employment, production, and purchasing power, and it created the Council of Economic Advisers, though the tools were weak relative to the declaration. Inflation followed the end of wartime controls, and during Korea he used the Defense Production Act to reimpose some of them. In March 1951 the Treasury-Federal Reserve Accord ended the Reserve's obligation to peg the price of government bonds, restoring a measure of monetary independence he had resisted.

  4. 4. Labor

    He vetoed the Taft-Hartley Act of 1947, which restricted the closed shop, permitted state right-to-work laws, required noncommunist affidavits from union officers, and authorized injunctions in national emergency strikes, and Congress overrode the veto. He later used those injunction provisions himself, and the minimum wage was raised in 1949 from forty cents to seventy-five cents. He did not repeal Taft-Hartley, and his attempt to seize the steel mills failed in court.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    The Displaced Persons Act of 1948 admitted a limited number of European refugees outside the ordinary quotas, and he signed it while objecting that its formulas favored some groups over Jewish and Catholic applicants, a bias a later amendment reduced. In 1952 he vetoed the McCarran-Walter Immigration and Nationality Act, which kept the national-origins quotas and added ideological grounds for exclusion. Congress passed it over the veto.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    In 1948, after his Committee on Civil Rights had reported, he asked Congress for an anti-lynching law, abolition of the poll tax, and a permanent fair-employment commission. Congress passed none of them, and the Dixiecrats bolted his convention. By executive order he directed equality of treatment and opportunity in the armed forces and in federal employment, a change the services carried through gradually under the pressure of the Korean War, while the Justice Department entered segregation cases and no comprehensive civil-rights statute was enacted.

  7. 7. Federalism

    The National Security Act of 1947 rebuilt the executive for permanent mobilization, with a secretary of defense, the National Security Council, and the Central Intelligence Agency, a large structural change at the center. His Fair Deal would have extended federal responsibility into medical insurance, education, and civil rights, and Congress stopped most of that domestic enlargement. Federalism at home changed less than the national-security state did.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    In March 1947 the Truman Doctrine asked aid for Greece and Turkey as a check on communist pressure, the Marshall Plan of 1948 followed with economic recovery for Western Europe, and the Berlin airlift of 1948 and 1949 supplied a blockaded city without a war. The North Atlantic Treaty of 1949 created a peacetime alliance. He recognized Israel, refused to recognize the People's Republic of China, and fought a limited war in Korea without carrying it into China, which was containment rather than rollback.

  9. 9. National defense

    He unified the armed services on paper under the National Security Act, kept the draft, and decided in January 1950 to build the hydrogen bomb after the Soviet atomic test of 1949. NSC-68 called for a sustained rearmament, Korea made the spending politically possible, and in 1951 he relieved MacArthur to keep the war limited and the command civilian. Defense did not return to the small establishment of the 1920s, and the difference in the permanent military was large.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    He made no change in the rule against an establishment of religion. He spoke of the Cold War in a plain Protestant moral vocabulary, which was rhetoric, not a church program. Recognition of Israel was a diplomatic decision, contested inside his own cabinet, and not an act of religious establishment.

  11. 11. Education

    He repeatedly asked for federal aid to elementary and secondary education and did not get it. Bills stalled on whether segregated schools and parochial schools could receive the money. The GI Bill continued for veterans of the Second World War, and there was no National Defense Education Act in his term.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    He did not undo the Tennessee Valley Authority, the grazing districts, or the soil-conservation apparatus he inherited. The Housing Act of 1949 put federal money behind local slum clearance, urban redevelopment, and a limited amount of public housing, which was a federal role in city land rather than in the public domain. He vetoed bills that would have given coastal states title to offshore oil lands, preferring a federal claim.

  13. 13. Energy

    The Atomic Energy Act of 1946 placed nuclear materials and development under a civilian Atomic Energy Commission, the government holding the fuel and the knowledge, with licensed commercial nuclear power still in the future. He defended federal title offshore against state claims to tidelands oil. There was no fuel crisis of the later kind, and the Tennessee Valley Authority remained a federal power producer.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    Street crime was not the center of his domestic policy, and he created no national program of aid to local police. In 1947 Executive Order 9835 established loyalty boards for federal employees and a list of organizations used in those inquiries, and he opposed Senator McCarthy's accusations while running that program. Espionage cases proceeded in the courts, and ordinary criminal law stayed with the states.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    He did not revive a Works Progress Administration. The Social Security Amendments of 1950 extended coverage and raised benefits, public assistance categories continued, and the Employment Act's language about maximum employment did not create a jobs program. Public housing under the 1949 act was built more slowly than he had asked, and poverty policy remained an extension of New Deal categories rather than a new relief state.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    Beginning in 1945 he asked Congress for national health insurance and repeated the request as part of the Fair Deal, and he did not get it. The bills died against the opposition of the American Medical Association, while the Hill-Burton Act of 1946 did pass and gave federal grants for hospital construction without insuring patients. Social Security remained a retirement and survivors program, disability insurance was not yet included, and Medicare was not his statute.

Of this presidency

  1. The atomic bomb and the end of the war

    In August 1945 he authorized the use of the atomic bombs against Hiroshima and Nagasaki. Japan surrendered within days, after the Soviet Union had also entered the war in Asia. The weapons had been built under Roosevelt. The decision to use them, without a prior demonstration and without a separate vote of Congress, was Truman's, inside a war Congress had already declared. He did not treat the bomb as a weapon that required a new declaration.

  2. The Marshall Plan

    Secretary of State George C. Marshall proposed European recovery in June 1947, and Truman signed the European Recovery Program in April 1948. It furnished large economic aid to Western European states that would coordinate their use of it. The Soviet Union kept its own sphere outside the offer. The plan was reconstruction, and it was also an instrument of containment. The sums and the administrative conditions were a peacetime commitment of a new size.

  3. The North Atlantic Treaty

    On April 4, 1949 the United States signed the North Atlantic Treaty. An armed attack on one ally in Europe or North America was to be considered an attack on all. The Senate ratified it. A standing peacetime military alliance, with a council and later an integrated command, broke the long American refusal of permanent entanglements. It was not a temporary wartime coalition of the kind Washington had warned against and Roosevelt had used.

  4. Korea and the dismissal of MacArthur

    When North Korea invaded the South on June 25, 1950, he committed air, sea, and ground forces under a United Nations resolution and without a declaration of war by Congress. Chinese intervention that autumn turned a march to the Yalu into a retreat, and the line stabilized near the thirty-eighth parallel. On April 11, 1951 he relieved General Douglas MacArthur for publicly opposing the decision not to widen the war into China. Civilian control of the military was the point he insisted on. The fighting had not ended when he left office.

  5. Recognition of Israel

    On May 14, 1948, minutes after Israel declared independence, he granted de facto recognition. Secretary of State Marshall and much of the department had wanted to wait on a United Nations arrangement and had warned him against acting at once. He acted anyway. De jure recognition followed in January 1949. The decision was diplomatic and political. It was not a religious establishment.

  6. Desegregation of the armed forces

    Executive Order 9981, issued on July 26, 1948, declared equality of treatment and opportunity in the armed forces without regard to race, color, religion, or national origin. The services did not integrate on the day it was signed. Resistance inside the military was worn down over the next several years, and the Korean War made segregated units increasingly impractical. A companion order addressed discrimination in federal employment. Congress still withheld the civil-rights statutes he had requested.

  7. The steel seizure

    On April 8, 1952, with a steel strike threatening production for the Korean War, he ordered the Secretary of Commerce to seize the mills, claiming inherent executive power when Congress had not authorized the seizure. The companies sued. In Youngstown Sheet and Tube Co. v. Sawyer the Supreme Court held, six to three, that he lacked the power. The mills went back to the owners. The loss was a limit on presidential emergency claims, written while he was still commander in chief of an unfinished war.

  8. The Fair Deal, mostly blocked

    In 1949 he gathered his domestic requests under the name Fair Deal: national health insurance, repeal of Taft-Hartley, federal aid to education, civil-rights legislation, and an expansion of Social Security and housing. Congress refused health insurance, repeal of Taft-Hartley, school aid, and the civil-rights bills. It did extend Social Security coverage and benefits in 1950, raise the minimum wage, and pass the Housing Act of 1949. The program was mostly blocked. It was not a second New Deal in statutes actually passed.

PreviousThe Court, the New Deal, and Korematsu NextTerm Limits and the Steel Seizure

1951–1952

Term Limits and the Steel Seizure

An amendment told a president he could not be elected more than twice. A decision told a president he could not seize a steel mill without a statute, even in a war he had sent troops to fight.

Amendment

The Twenty-second Amendment

Ratified February 27, 1951

How it was made

Congress proposed it in 1947, after Roosevelt’s four elections. No person may be elected president more than twice. A person who has succeeded to the office and served more than two years of another’s term may be elected only once. The amendment did not apply to the president then in office, which was Truman.

What it changed

The two-term habit Washington had started, and which had held until 1940, became a rule. A party can no longer renominate a popular president without end. The office turns over by the Constitution, not only by custom or by defeat. Truman was free of it and chose not to run in 1952. Every president since has been bound.

Decision

Youngstown Sheet & Tube Co. v. Sawyer

Decided June 2, 1952

How it was made

During the Korean War the steelworkers set a strike. Truman ordered the secretary of commerce to seize the mills and run them, so that production for the war would not stop. Congress had considered seizure in the Taft-Hartley Act and had not given him that power. The companies sued.

Who sat

Six to three. Black wrote the opinion of the Court. Jackson concurred, and his account of presidential power is the one later Courts have used. Two of Truman’s appointees, Burton and Clark, voted against the president who had named them. His chief justice, Vinson, dissented.

With the opinion

  • Hugo Black — F. Roosevelt
  • Felix Frankfurter — F. Roosevelt
  • William Douglas — F. Roosevelt
  • Robert Jackson — F. Roosevelt
  • Harold Burton — Truman
  • Tom Clark — Truman

In dissent

  • Fred Vinson — Truman
  • Stanley Reed — F. Roosevelt
  • Sherman Minton — Truman

What it changed

The seizures were unlawful. The president’s power as commander in chief did not include taking private industry inside the country when Congress had withheld that tool. Jackson sorted presidential acts into three kinds: those with Congress, those in Congress’s silence, and those against Congress’s will. This seizure was the third, and it was at its weakest. The war in Korea went on. The mills went back to the companies. A wartime president lost in his own Court, on appointments he had made.

PreviousHarry S. Truman NextDwight D. Eisenhower

1953–1961

Dwight D. Eisenhower

Republican

Before the office

He was born in 1890 in Denison, Texas, and Abilene, Kansas, is where the life actually was. His father, David, had failed at a store and worked as a mechanic. His mother, Ida, was a pacifist from a plain Protestant discipline, and she raised six sons on very little without calling it a hardship. Dwight was a fighter before he was a reader. The temper was real, and the long work of his life was not eliminating it but refusing to let a room see it. Abilene was a cattle town that had already gone quiet. It did not teach grandeur. It taught that a man who loses his temper in public has already lost the argument, and that a family gets through a year by not spending what it does not have.

West Point took him in the class of 1915, the class the stars later fell on. He was a football player until a knee ended that, and he graduated in the middle. The First World War ended before he could fight in it. He trained tank crews at Camp Colt, in Gettysburg, and he was good at the training, and the missing battle stayed with him as a debt he had not been allowed to pay. In 1916 he married Mamie Doud, who came from more money and more ease than Abilene and who spent the marriage in the rented houses the army assigned. Their first son died of scarlet fever in 1921. The second, John, lived. The death was not something he turned into a speech. It was something the household carried, and the army life went on around it because that was what an officer’s household did.

The education that mattered came after the armistice, from a general who made him read. Fox Conner, in Panama, put Clausewitz and the campaigns in front of him and treated staff work as a serious profession. At Leavenworth he finished first in his class. He served Pershing, and then he served Douglas MacArthur in Washington and in the Philippines. MacArthur was the lesson in negative. A brilliant man who needed an audience was a danger to the plan, and Eisenhower, who had a temper of his own, practiced the opposite: let the other man have the gesture, keep the order clear, do not make the alliance about yourself. The promotions were slow. The mind that would run a coalition was built in those years, not on a battlefield he had not yet been given.

Marshall gave him the battlefield after Pearl Harbor, and it was a headquarters. London, North Africa, the Mediterranean, then the supreme command for the invasion of France. The hardest part of the job was not the German army. It was Churchill, Montgomery, de Gaulle, and Patton, and the weather over the Channel. On the eve of the landing he wrote a note taking the blame if it failed, and dated it as if the failure had already happened. He did not have to issue it. In May 1945 the war in Europe ended with an alliance still intact, which was a political achievement wearing a uniform. The war also strained the marriage. His closeness to Kay Summersby, who drove for him and stayed near him, was close enough that the people around it did not mistake it for ordinary staff duty. He went home to Mamie. He did not talk about the other thing, which was his method with anything that could shake the public face of a command.

After the victory he was chief of staff, then president of Columbia, which he did not enjoy and was not built for, then supreme commander of the new Atlantic alliance. NATO was the same work in a colder form: hold the West together, spend enough to be believed, and do not start the battle if the alliance can prevent it. In 1952 both parties would have taken him. He chose the Republicans, and inside that party he chose against Robert Taft. Taft would have pulled the country back from the commitments Truman had made. Eisenhower would not. He also would not repeal the New Deal. Social Security stayed. The unions stayed, inside a frame he meant to police rather than smash. What he offered, against Stevenson and against the stalemate in Korea, was a soldier who had seen a real war and would not manage this one from a podium. In Detroit he said he would go to Korea. The sentence was the campaign.

He took the oath as the first Republican president in twenty years, and he was not a Republican of 1920. The government he found, the insurance state and the containment state, was the government he intended to run. He intended to end the shooting, slow the growth, balance what could be balanced, and keep the nuclear arsenal large enough that a president would not have to send another army across an ocean if he could help it. The men who later called the method a hidden hand were describing a habit he had learned at Leavenworth and proved in London. Let them argue. Write the order. Take the blame on paper before you have to take it in public. He had not come to undo Roosevelt and Truman. He had come to keep what they had built from being governed by the next excitement, and to stop a war that had stopped being a plan.

In the office

Eisenhower accepted the New Deal he inherited from Truman's Democrats and did not attempt a large rollback of it. He ratified Social Security, extended it, and left the legal place of unions standing. His own additions were a continental highway system and a peacetime military organized around nuclear weapons, not a repeal of the insurance and regulatory state.

The party

Eisenhower's Republicans accepted the New Deal's core and did not repeal it. Social Security stayed, and so did the legal place of unions. The party distinguished itself by promising to administer that settlement with more restraint, not by promising to take the government back to 1929.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    He preferred balanced budgets, ran surpluses in some years and deficits in recession years, and did not cut the top income-tax rate, which stood at 91 percent after the Internal Revenue Code of 1954. Defense remained the great expenditure, and the New Look leaned on nuclear weapons partly to limit the cost of conventional forces. The interstate highways were financed chiefly by a federal gasoline tax in a trust fund, a large public work that did not replace Social Security.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    He extended reciprocal-trade authority and did not restore a Smoot-Hawley tariff. Protection persisted at the edges, above all in oil, where voluntary limits gave way in 1959 to a mandatory import program meant to shelter domestic producers. The general direction was negotiated reduction of barriers, not a new protectionist system.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    He left the 1951 Accord in place, appointed William McChesney Martin to the Federal Reserve, and treated the Board's independence as a fact of the office. The dollar stayed convertible into gold for foreign governments under Bretton Woods, and he did not close the gold window.

  4. 4. Labor

    He accepted collective bargaining and did not ask to repeal the Wagner Act. Taft-Hartley remained, and he used its emergency injunction, notably against the steel strike of 1959. The Landrum-Griffin Act of 1959, signed after the McClellan hearings on union corruption, regulated union finances and internal government without restoring the labor injunction as ordinary law.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    He left the McCarran-Walter national-origins system in place and did not propose the repeal that came in 1965. The Refugee Relief Act of 1953, and parole for Hungarians after 1956, admitted people outside the quotas in limited numbers. In 1954 the Immigration and Naturalization Service carried out a large enforcement drive against Mexican nationals present unlawfully, officially named Operation Wetback.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    He signed the Civil Rights Acts of 1957 and 1960, limited statutes aimed chiefly at voting, and the Court under Chief Justice Earl Warren, whom he had appointed, decided Brown versus Board of Education in 1954. He did not ask Congress for a general desegregation of public life. When a governor used state troops to block a federal court order at Little Rock, he federalized the Arkansas National Guard and sent the 101st Airborne.

  7. 7. Federalism

    He preferred state and local action where he thought it would carry the load, and he still signed a continental federal highway program and kept federal social insurance. He opposed the strong forms of the Bricker Amendment, which would have cut down the treaty power and executive agreements, and the amendment failed. At Little Rock he held that a state could not nullify an order of a federal court.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    He ended the fighting in Korea and fought no second war on that scale. Covert operations he authorized helped remove Mohammad Mossadegh in Iran in 1953 and Jacobo Arbenz in Guatemala in 1954. He refused to back the British, French, and Israeli attack on Egypt in 1956, sent no troops into Hungary, landed Marines in Lebanon in 1958, and in 1960 took responsibility when a U-2 was shot down over the Soviet Union.

  9. 9. National defense

    The New Look organized strategy around nuclear deterrence, and conventional forces were reduced from the Korean peak without being abolished. After Sputnik he accelerated missile programs and signed the act creating the National Aeronautics and Space Administration. His farewell warning about a military-industrial complex concerned the permanent establishment he had maintained and tried to cap, not a force he intended to dismantle.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    He encouraged a public religious language of the Cold War and accepted Congress's addition of the words under God to the Pledge of Allegiance in 1954 and the statute of 1956 making In God We Trust the national motto. He proposed no establishment and no federal funding of churches. His own baptism as a Presbyterian, while in office, was a personal act and not a policy.

  11. 11. Education

    He was wary of general federal aid to schools, which raised both segregation and the fear of federal control, and no such general bill passed. After Sputnik, the National Defense Education Act of 1958 put federal money into science, mathematics, languages, and student loans, justified as defense. The Department of Health, Education, and Welfare, created in 1953, gathered existing work and was not a national school board.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    He did not abolish farm price supports, and the Soil Bank in the Agricultural Act of 1956 paid farmers to idle acreage, a subsidy of a kind Coolidge had vetoed. The Submerged Lands Act of 1953 gave coastal states title to offshore lands within their historic boundaries, reversing Truman's federal claim, while the outer continental shelf stayed federal. Park spending rose under Mission 66, and the Wilderness Act was not yet law.

  13. 13. Energy

    The Atomic Energy Act of 1954 opened civilian nuclear power to private firms under license from the Atomic Energy Commission, a shift from the government monopoly written in 1946. Mandatory oil-import limits protected domestic producers. He preferred private utilities to an expanded Tennessee Valley Authority, the Dixon-Yates contract designed to avoid that expansion collapsed, and the Authority itself was not sold.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    Ordinary criminal law stayed with the states, and he proposed no national crime code and no federal subsidy of local police. The federal controversies of the term were loyalty investigations, hearings on union corruption, and civil-rights enforcement, not a new list of street offenses.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    He ratified Social Security and extended it, adding disability benefits in 1956, and he created no works-relief agency and nothing like a later office of economic opportunity. Recessions were handled without a new federal relief corps, while aid to dependent children and distribution of surplus food continued. Poverty was not the organizing domestic subject of the presidency.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    The Department of Health, Education, and Welfare, created in 1953, housed programs already in being and was not itself a system of medical insurance. He opposed attaching hospital insurance for the aged to Social Security. The Kerr-Mills Act of 1960 offered federal matching money so that states could help some of the elderly poor pay medical bills, and it was not Medicare.

Of this presidency

  1. The Korean armistice

    The armistice of July 27, 1953 stopped the fighting in Korea along a line near the thirty-eighth parallel. There was no peace treaty and no unification of the peninsula. He had let it be known that he might widen the war if the talks failed, and the cease-fire came without an invasion of China and without a victory beyond the line already being held when Truman left. American forces remained. The war ended as a fight, not as a political settlement.

  2. The interstate highways

    On June 29, 1956 he signed the Federal-Aid Highway Act, authorizing the National System of Interstate and Defense Highways. A federal tax on gasoline, held in a Highway Trust Fund, was the main means of payment. He argued for the roads as commerce and as defense, including the movement of military traffic. It was the largest federal public-works commitment since the Second World War, and it was his statute.

  3. McCarthy at arm's length

    He distrusted Senator Joseph McCarthy and would not grant him a public combat with the presidency. He worked to limit McCarthy's access to the executive branch and stayed off the stage of the Army-McCarthy hearings in 1954. The Senate censured McCarthy on December 2, 1954. Eisenhower accepted that result and had not led it. Distance, rather than a presidential campaign against the senator, was the policy.

  4. Little Rock

    In September 1957 Governor Orval Faubus used the Arkansas National Guard to keep nine Black students out of Central High School in Little Rock, in defiance of a federal court. Eisenhower federalized the Guard and sent units of the 101st Airborne Division to enforce the order. The students entered under that force. He had already signed the Civil Rights Act of 1957, a narrow voting measure, and he signed a second limited act in 1960. The troops were the unmistakable act. He still did not ask Congress for a comprehensive desegregation of American life.

  5. The U-2

    On May 1, 1960 the Soviet Union shot down an American U-2 reconnaissance plane and captured the pilot, Francis Gary Powers. The administration first put out a cover story of a weather flight gone astray. Eisenhower then acknowledged that he had authorized the overflights. A summit with Khrushchev in Paris collapsed. The program was his, and so was the admission.

  6. The farewell address

    On January 17, 1961 he warned, in the farewell address, against the acquisition of unwarranted influence by the military-industrial complex, whether that influence was sought or not. He said in the same speech that a hostile ideology still had to be met with adequate force. The warning came from the president who had kept a large peacetime defense establishment and had tried to hold down its cost. It was not a proposal to dismantle that establishment.

  7. NASA

    The Soviet satellite Sputnik, orbited on October 4, 1957, was taken in Washington as a blow to national security as well as to prestige. He signed the National Aeronautics and Space Act on July 29, 1958, creating a civilian National Aeronautics and Space Administration, and the National Defense Education Act followed in the same season. Space and scientific training became federal commitments because of the strategic shock, not because either had been part of the New Deal he had ratified.

  8. No rollback of the New Deal

    Social Security was not repealed. Coverage was widened, and the amendments of 1956 added disability insurance, so that he ratified the system and enlarged it. The Wagner Act was not repealed. Landrum-Griffin policed union corruption and internal practice. It did not make collective bargaining unlawful. The Department of Health, Education, and Welfare gathered federal health and education programs in the cabinet. The Republican administration of the 1950s left the New Deal's core in place.

PreviousTerm Limits and the Steel Seizure NextBrown and the Twenty-third Amendment

1954–1961

Brown and the Twenty-third Amendment

The Court unanimously withdrew the constitutional permission Plessy had given to segregated schools. A later amendment gave the District of Columbia a voice in presidential elections, which the capital had never had.

Decision

Brown v. Board of Education

Decided May 17, 1954

How it was made

Parents in four states and the District of Columbia challenged laws that assigned children to schools by race. The cases were argued twice. After Vinson’s death, Eisenhower appointed Earl Warren chief justice, and the second argument was held so that a full Court could decide.

Who sat

Unanimous. Warren wrote. Five of the nine had been appointed by Roosevelt, three by Truman, one by Eisenhower. Reed had been expected by some to dissent. He did not.

With the opinion

  • Earl Warren — Eisenhower
  • Hugo Black — F. Roosevelt
  • Stanley Reed — F. Roosevelt
  • Felix Frankfurter — F. Roosevelt
  • William Douglas — F. Roosevelt
  • Robert Jackson — F. Roosevelt
  • Harold Burton — Truman
  • Tom Clark — Truman
  • Sherman Minton — Truman

What it changed

Separate schools are inherently unequal, and they deny the equal protection of the laws. Plessy’s formula, as applied to public education, was abandoned. The decision did not desegregate the schools that term. A year later the Court sent the cases back to local courts to proceed with all deliberate speed. Implementation was slow, and in places it was resisted by state power. The constitutional rule had nonetheless changed. A state could no longer point to Plessy.

Decision

Cooper v. Aaron

Decided September 12, 1958

How it was made

After Little Rock, the Arkansas legislature and governor claimed that the state was not bound by Brown. The school board asked for a delay. The Court, in a special term, refused.

Who sat

Unanimous, and each justice signed the opinion, which the Court does not ordinarily do. Four of the nine were now Eisenhower’s.

With the opinion

  • Earl Warren — Eisenhower
  • Hugo Black — F. Roosevelt
  • Felix Frankfurter — F. Roosevelt
  • William Douglas — F. Roosevelt
  • Harold Burton — Truman
  • Tom Clark — Truman
  • John Marshall Harlan — Eisenhower
  • William Brennan — Eisenhower
  • Charles Whittaker — Eisenhower

What it changed

A governor may not nullify a constitutional decision by announcing that the state disagrees. The Court said its exposition of the Constitution is the supreme law, in the sense that an official who has sworn to support the Constitution is bound by that exposition. Whether or not that sentence claimed more than Marbury, the practical holding was plain: Little Rock could not wait out Brown. Eisenhower had already sent the 101st Airborne. The decision said why a state officer could not treat that as optional.

Amendment

The Twenty-third Amendment

Ratified March 29, 1961

How it was made

Congress proposed it in June 1960. The District of Columbia, which is not a state and had no presidential electors, received the number it would have if it were a state, but no more than the least populous state. The states finished ratification six weeks after Kennedy’s inauguration. The proposal was Eisenhower’s Congress.

What it changed

Residents of the capital could vote for president. They still had no voting members of the House or Senate. The amendment changed the electoral college without making the District a state, and without giving it a voice in the body that proposes amendments.

PreviousDwight D. Eisenhower NextJohn F. Kennedy

1961–1963

John F. Kennedy

Democrat

Before the office

He was born in 1917 in Brookline, Massachusetts, the second son of Joseph P. Kennedy and Rose Fitzgerald. The father made a fortune and then a public career, including the embassy in London, and he intended the oldest boy, Joe Junior, for the office he never reached himself. The mother was the daughter of a Boston mayor, devout, and less tender than the family photographs implied. The house ran on competition and on the knowledge that Boston’s older Protestant world still did not quite admit them. Jack was the sickly one. Scarlet fever nearly killed him as a child. The back was bad early. The illnesses that doctors would later call Addison’s disease were real, and the family treated them as a secret rather than as a fact the public had a right to. The tan came later. The frailty came first.

He went to Choate and to Harvard, class of 1940, and he spent a year of it in London watching his father explain appeasement. His senior thesis, published as Why England Slept, was a young man’s case that democracies arm too late. It separated him, without a scene, from the ambassador. In the Navy he commanded a patrol torpedo boat in the Solomons. In August 1943 a Japanese destroyer cut PT-109 in two. He got his crew onto an island and towed a burned man through the water by a strap held in his teeth. The heroism was not a later invention. In 1944 his older brother died on a volunteer bombing run over Europe. The father’s plan did not die with Joe Junior. It moved one place down the table. Jack, who had thought he might write, became the political son because the first one was gone, and because he was too competitive to refuse the opening.

Massachusetts sent him to the House in 1946 and to the Senate in 1952, where he beat Henry Cabot Lodge and did not become an important legislator. He became a campaigner. In 1953 he married Jacqueline Bouvier. The wedding was the glamorous fact. The marriage, as Dallek records it, included his repeated and careless infidelities, the habit of a man who had been raised to win and to treat being caught as the actual sin. Profiles in Courage, in 1956, won the Pulitzer Prize and was written in substantial part by Theodore Sorensen. The back was often so bad he could not pull a sock on. The Addison’s was managed with steroids and denied. The pictures of touch football on the lawn were not false as pictures. They were incomplete as medicine. He was rehearsing a presidency in a body that required a staff to get him through a day, and he did not intend the country to read the chart.

In 1956 he came close to the vice-presidential nomination and was fortunate to miss it, because the ticket lost. In 1960 he beat Hubert Humphrey, and the West Virginia primary, poor and Protestant, was the test that answered the Catholic question for his own party. He took Lyndon Johnson for Texas and the South, and he beat Richard Nixon by a margin thin enough to be argued over. Television did some of the work. His brother Robert did more of the organizing, and his father’s money was not irrelevant. The argument against Eisenhower was not that the New Deal should be repealed or that containment was wrong. It was that the country had gone quiet. A missile gap, overstated, a Cuba, a promise to get moving again. He was a Cold War liberal of the generation that had come home from the Pacific and expected the federal government he had grown up under to be used, not dismantled.

What “moving” meant was still, on the day he took the oath, more a tempo than a statute book. A tax cut was in his mind. Hospital insurance for the aged was a bill. Civil rights he believed in more cautiously than the movement standing outside the gates, and he had spent the Senate protecting the Southern Democrats whose votes a Massachusetts Catholic could not yet do without. The Peace Corps fit the sentence he was about to say. The house he grew up in had asked its sons what they would win. He turned the question outward, toward a country that already had the insurance state, the unions, and the warfare budget, and that he thought had forgotten to treat them as a summons.

He was forty-three, the youngest man ever elected to the office, and sicker than the office was allowed to know. Dallek’s unfinished life is the medical record and the women and the ambition, held in one account so that the smile is not the whole story and the sickness is not the refutation of it. He asked Americans not to ask what their country could do for them. He had not come to undo what Roosevelt and Truman had built, or what Eisenhower had agreed to keep. He had come to run it faster, and to face the Soviet Union as if the previous calm had been a pause. The bills that would have extended the domestic state were still in front of Congress. The life that would not finish them was already the life: public, brief, and harder underneath than the pictures.

In the office

Kennedy's presidency was a Cold War presidency, marked by the failed landing at the Bay of Pigs, the missile crisis in Cuba, and a larger American advisory force in Vietnam. The civil-rights bill, the income-tax cut, and hospital insurance for the aged were before Congress and not yet law when he was assassinated. The domestic scale of the federal government did not change under him as it had in the New Deal or as it would in the Great Society.

The party

Kennedy's Democrats were Cold War liberals. They held the New Deal coalition, proposed to extend it into civil rights and medical care for the aged, and treated the contest with the Soviet Union as the organizing fact of national policy. The statutes that would have completed that domestic extension were not signed by him.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    He recommended a general reduction of income-tax rates and did not live to sign it. The reduction became the Revenue Act of 1964 under Johnson, lowering the top rate from 91 percent to 70 percent, while the piece enacted in his own term, in 1962, was an investment tax credit and faster depreciation. He ran deficits, Congress blocked much of the domestic spending he asked for, and in April 1962 he pressed the major steel companies into rescinding a price increase.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    The Trade Expansion Act of 1962, which he signed, enlarged the president's authority to cut tariffs by negotiation and offered adjustment assistance to firms and workers injured by imports. The Kennedy Round under the General Agreement on Tariffs and Trade was largely finished after his death. The direction was further liberalization, not a return to the tariffs of the 1920s.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    He did not close the gold window or rewrite the Federal Reserve. Gold outflows and the balance of payments worried him, and the Treasury and the Federal Reserve attempted, under the name Operation Twist, to hold short-term rates up while easing long-term rates. He proposed an interest-equalization tax on foreign borrowing in the American market, and that tax was enacted in 1964, after he had died.

  4. 4. Labor

    He accepted the Wagner Act and Taft-Hartley as the settlement in place, raised and somewhat extended the minimum wage in 1961, and by Executive Order 10988 in 1962 gave federal employees a limited right to organize, not a right to strike. He did not try to repeal Taft-Hartley. The steel episode of 1962 was a fight over a price increase, not a new labor code.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    He asked Congress to end the national-origins quota system and did not get the statute, which was the Immigration Act of 1965 under Johnson. Cubans leaving Castro's government were admitted by parole and through a refugee program. That was administration within the old law, not a replacement of it.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    For most of two years he moved by executive order, requiring federal contractors to take affirmative steps against discrimination and, late and narrowly, limiting discrimination in some federally supported housing. In 1962 federal marshals and the federalized National Guard enforced James Meredith's admission to the University of Mississippi, and on June 11, 1963, after federalizing the Alabama Guard to integrate the state university, he asked Congress for a broad civil-rights bill. The bill was unpassed when he was killed, and the Civil Rights Act of 1964 is not his statute.

  7. 7. Federalism

    He used federal force to carry out court orders in Mississippi and Alabama, and he asked for domestic programs, including a department of urban affairs, that Congress refused. He did not redraw the constitutional division between the nation and the states. The large statutory shift of responsibilities came in laws passed after his death.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    The Bay of Pigs, the hard meeting with Khrushchev in Vienna, the Berlin Wall, which he reinforced West Berlin rather than tried to tear down, and the missile crisis were the crises of the first half of the term. He raised the American military presence in Vietnam from under a thousand advisers to about sixteen thousand. In November 1963 his administration knew South Vietnamese generals were preparing to overthrow Ngo Dinh Diem and did not prevent the coup in which Diem was killed.

  9. 9. National defense

    Flexible response was the stated doctrine: more conventional forces and a larger missile force, so that nuclear retaliation would not be the only choice, and the defense budget rose. The Limited Test Ban of 1963 prohibited tests in the atmosphere, in outer space, and under water, and it did not dismantle either arsenal or ban underground tests. He did not commit an American ground army in Vietnam on the scale of 1965, and the draft continued.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    In September 1960, still a candidate, he told Protestant ministers in Houston that he held the separation of church and state to be absolute and that he would decide public questions by his own judgment, not by instruction from his church. He was the first Catholic elected president. In office he proposed no religious test and no aid to churches as churches, and a general bill for school aid failed partly because Congress could not settle the treatment of children in parochial schools.

  11. 11. Education

    His general bill for federal aid to elementary and secondary education failed in 1961, caught in part on the church-school question. The Manpower Development and Training Act of 1962 paid for training of unemployed workers and was not a national school system. The elementary and secondary aid statute, and the main higher-education act, were signed by his successor.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    With Stewart Udall at Interior he gave conservation more attention than a domestic program Congress would pass, and the Cape Cod National Seashore was authorized in 1961. A wilderness bill he supported did not become law until the next year, under Johnson. He did not replace the farm price-support system or open a new disposition of the public domain.

  13. 13. Energy

    He left private oil, gas, and coal, and the federal hydropower projects already built, essentially as he found them. There was no national energy program and no embargo. The nuclear test ban was an arms-control treaty, not a plan for civilian reactors, and he founded no new regional power authority on the scale of the Tennessee Valley.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    His attorney general, Robert Kennedy, pursued organized crime and corruption in the Teamsters with the statutes already available, more a change in priority than a new criminal code. The Juvenile Delinquency and Youth Offenses Control Act of 1961 was a small set of demonstration grants. Street crime remained, in law, a matter for the states.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    The Area Redevelopment Act of 1961 sent aid to depressed localities, Social Security amendments that year let men claim reduced benefits at sixty-two, and he widened pilot food-stamp projects by executive action. A larger antipoverty program was being drafted in his last months and was not enacted. The permanent food-stamp statute and the Office of Economic Opportunity are Johnson's.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    He asked Congress for hospital insurance for the aged under Social Security, the King-Anderson bills, and did not get it. Medicare is a law of 1965. In October 1963 he signed a statute funding construction of research centers for mental retardation and of community mental health centers, a limited building program, not a national system of medical insurance.

Of this presidency

  1. The Bay of Pigs

    In April 1961 he approved a landing of Cuban exiles at the Bay of Pigs, an operation the CIA had planned under Eisenhower, and he withheld American air cover. The landing force was destroyed within days. He said in public that the failure was his. It was the first crisis of the term, and it did not remove Fidel Castro.

  2. The Cuban missile crisis

    In October 1962, presented with Soviet nuclear missiles being placed in Cuba, he rejected an immediate air strike and imposed a naval quarantine. The Soviet Union agreed to withdraw the missiles. The United States pledged not to invade Cuba, and it was understood that American Jupiter missiles in Turkey would be removed, an understanding kept quiet at the time. The crisis did not become a war. It was the most dangerous passage of his Cold War presidency, and of the nuclear age to that date.

  3. The Limited Test Ban

    He signed the Limited Nuclear Test Ban Treaty in August 1963, and the Senate ratified it in September. Tests in the atmosphere, in outer space, and under water were forbidden. Underground tests were not. The parties were the United States, Britain, and the Soviet Union, and other states could accede. The treaty limited where bombs could be exploded. It did not require either side to give up the weapons it had.

  4. The Peace Corps

    He created the Peace Corps in 1961, first by executive order on March 1 and then by statute. Volunteers were sent abroad to teach and to work on development projects at the request of the host governments. It was an instrument of the Cold War that was not a military deployment and not an alliance. It did not replace the advisory buildup in Vietnam or the nuclear deterrent.

  5. The moon commitment

    On May 25, 1961 he asked a joint session of Congress to commit the nation to landing a man on the moon and returning him safely before the decade was out. The goal made the civilian space program Eisenhower had established into a very large federal project, justified as competition with the Soviet Union. He repeated the aim in public afterward. The landing itself took place in 1969, under Richard Nixon.

  6. The civil-rights bill, proposed and not passed

    On June 11, 1963, the day he federalized the Alabama National Guard so that Black students could enter the University of Alabama, he addressed the country and asked Congress for a broad civil-rights bill. Birmingham, the resistance at Ole Miss, and the movement in the streets had pushed him to that request. The March on Washington followed in August. When he was assassinated in November the bill had not been enacted. Credit for passing the Civil Rights Act does not belong to him.

  7. The assassination

    Lee Harvey Oswald shot him in Dallas on November 22, 1963, as the motorcade passed through the city. He died that day. Lyndon B. Johnson took the oath of office within hours. The tax cut, the civil-rights bill, and hospital insurance for the aged were left as proposals. The presidency that completed or abandoned them was Johnson's, not a second Kennedy term.

PreviousBrown and the Twenty-third Amendment NextBaker v. Carr and Gideon

1962–1963

Baker v. Carr and Gideon

In Kennedy’s years the Court entered a field it had long called political, the drawing of legislative districts, and it required the states to supply a lawyer to a poor defendant in a serious criminal case.

Decision

Baker v. Carr

Decided March 26, 1962

How it was made

Tennessee had not redrawn its legislative districts since 1901. Urban counties were badly underrepresented. Voters sued. Earlier Courts had refused such suits as political questions committed to the states and to Congress.

Who sat

Six to two. Brennan wrote. Whittaker left the Court before the decision and took no part. Frankfurter’s dissent said the Court was going into a political thicket it would not be able to leave.

With the opinion

  • William Brennan — Eisenhower
  • Earl Warren — Eisenhower
  • Hugo Black — F. Roosevelt
  • William Douglas — F. Roosevelt
  • Tom Clark — Truman
  • Potter Stewart — Eisenhower

In dissent

  • Felix Frankfurter — F. Roosevelt
  • John Marshall Harlan — Eisenhower

Not sitting

  • Charles Whittaker — Eisenhower

What it changed

Federal courts could hear a claim that a state’s legislative apportionment denied equal protection. The decision did not yet say what an equal district was. It said the question was no longer outside the Court’s business. District lines, which legislatures had drawn to keep themselves in office, became subject to a constitutional suit. Frankfurter retired soon after. Arthur Goldberg, Kennedy’s appointee, took his seat.

Decision

Gideon v. Wainwright

Decided March 18, 1963

How it was made

Clarence Earl Gideon was charged with a felony in a Florida court and asked for a lawyer. The state refused, because the charge was not a capital one. He defended himself, was convicted, and wrote the Supreme Court from prison.

Who sat

Unanimous. Black wrote. Kennedy’s two appointees, White and Goldberg, joined. The Court overruled its own 1942 decision, Betts v. Brady, which had left counsel to the circumstances of each case.

With the opinion

  • Hugo Black — F. Roosevelt
  • Earl Warren — Eisenhower
  • William Douglas — F. Roosevelt
  • Tom Clark — Truman
  • John Marshall Harlan — Eisenhower
  • William Brennan — Eisenhower
  • Potter Stewart — Eisenhower
  • Byron White — Kennedy
  • Arthur Goldberg — Kennedy

What it changed

A state must provide a lawyer to a poor defendant in a felony case. The right, which the Sixth Amendment states for federal trials, was held to bind the states through the Fourteenth Amendment. The change was in the criminal process of every state, not in a federal code. It cost the states money and it changed outcomes. It was a judicial requirement, not a statute Kennedy signed.

PreviousJohn F. Kennedy NextLyndon B. Johnson

1963–1969

Lyndon B. Johnson

Democrat

Before the office

He was born in 1908 in the Hill Country west of Austin, in a house that did not have electricity and in a family that had once had land and standing and had watched both thin out. His father, Sam Johnson, sat in the Texas legislature, talked like a Populist, and could not keep money. His mother, Rebekah Baines, had been to college, thought the Hill Country was a comedown, and put the disappointment into the oldest boy. Lyndon learned early that being poor was a shame and that being somebody was the cure. He also learned, because the farms around him were dark after sunset, what a government could do if it decided a wire should reach a kitchen. The two lessons never separated. He wanted power because he had been small. He had seen, in the dark, what power was for.

He worked his way through the teachers’ college at San Marcos, and in the winter of 1928 he taught Mexican-American children in Cotulla, a town poorer than the one he had left. Caro makes that year do a great deal of work, and it will bear it. He was kind to the children in the way of a young man who intended to be remembered as kind, and he was already the person who had to dominate a room. He graduated, taught briefly in Houston, and then went to Washington as secretary to a Texas congressman who was happy to let the secretary run the office. He learned the House as a staff man learns it: who returned a call, which committee actually moved, how a favor was remembered. He took over the organization of congressional aides and ran it. The ambition was not secret. The method was already his. Get close. Learn the count. Do not wait to be invited.

In 1934 he met Claudia Taylor, called Lady Bird, and married her ten weeks later. She had land and income his family had never had, and she had a steadiness he used for the rest of his life without ever quite matching it. He was unfaithful, and he could be cruel to the people who worked for him, and he could not function when she was away. The marriage was not a romance the public was shown. It was the base. Roosevelt’s National Youth Administration made him the Texas director at twenty-six, and he turned a relief program into a network of young men who owed him a job. In 1937 he won a special election to the House by declaring himself one hundred percent for Roosevelt, and he spent the next years dragging electric lines into the Hill Country he had left. That was real. The lights came on. A brief wartime tour in the Navy, and a decoration he was not shy about, did not turn him into a soldier. He was a congressman who had seen what a New Deal statute could do for a county that had been voting Democratic since the defeat of the Confederacy and had still been living in the dark.

He wanted the Senate and he did not get it cleanly. In 1941 he lost a narrow race to W. Lee O’Daniel. In 1948 he beat Coke Stevenson by eighty-seven votes. The margin came in late from Box 13 in Jim Wells County, and Stevenson spent the rest of his life saying the votes had been added after the count. Caro’s account agrees with Stevenson. Johnson took the seat, took the nickname Landslide Lyndon as a joke that dared anyone to reopen the box, and became, within a few years, the most effective leader the Senate had ever had. Whip, then leader. He learned every man’s price and every man’s shame, and he used both, the arm around the shoulder and the threat that was not quite spoken. A heart attack in 1955 nearly killed him and did not slow the method. The Civil Rights Act of 1957 was the first since Reconstruction, and it was weaker than liberals wanted, and it passed because he knew how much the South would swallow and how little the liberals could get without him. He believed a law on the books was worth more than a speech about the law he had not passed.

In 1960 he wanted the presidency and Kennedy took it from him, and then offered the vice presidency, which he hated and accepted. Power, he had told himself, is where power goes. It did not go to the man who presided over the Senate and waited in the hallway while the president’s men decided. Robert Kennedy despised him. He despised Robert Kennedy. For three years he was the most experienced legislator in the government and one of the loneliest men in it, drinking, brooding, and watching a younger president get credit for bills that were not yet law. On November 22, 1963, in Dallas, the president was shot. Johnson was sworn in on the airplane, with the widow standing beside him, and the office he had wanted since the Hill Country arrived in a form he would not have chosen and was not going to refuse.

Five days later he told Congress they had talked long enough about equal rights. A hundred years was enough. It was time to write the next chapter in the books of law. That was the difference from the man he replaced. Kennedy had the language and a bill. Johnson had the count, the stolen election behind him, the lights in the Hill Country behind him, and the conviction that a right not written down was a right the South could wait out. He meant to pass Kennedy’s civil rights bill, and he meant to pass more than Kennedy had been ready to spend his coalition on. The war that would wreck the intention was not yet the subject. The subject was a man who had never believed talk was the work. The work was the vote, and he had spent thirty years learning how a vote is got.

In the office

The Great Society and the war in Vietnam were large expansions of what the federal government undertook, and Johnson pursued them together. Statutes he signed made civil rights, medical care for the aged and the poor, and federal aid to elementary and secondary schools into national commitments. He also raised the American force in Vietnam above half a million and, in March 1968, took himself out of the presidential race with the war unfinished.

The party

Johnson added the Great Society and secured the Black vote's realignment into the Democratic Party, including southern Black voters the Voting Rights Act put on the rolls. The Civil Rights Act and the Voting Rights Act cost the party the white South, which had been Democratic since Reconstruction. A national party of civil rights, Medicare, and federal aid to education was a different coalition from the one Roosevelt had held together.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    In 1964 he signed the tax cut Kennedy had recommended, and the top individual rate fell from 91 percent to 70 percent. Spending for the domestic programs and for Vietnam then rose together, and he delayed asking for a tax to pay for the war. In 1968 he accepted a temporary ten percent surcharge on the income tax, once the combined cost had produced a deficit he would no longer leave alone.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    He carried through the Kennedy Round of negotiations under the General Agreement on Tariffs and Trade, concluded in 1967, which reduced industrial tariffs among the countries that signed. He was not a protectionist on the model of 1930, although restraints on textiles and a few other goods continued. Trade policy stayed on the path of negotiated reduction opened in 1934.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    He kept up the voluntary wage and price guideposts inherited from the previous term and argued publicly against increases that broke them. He did not impose a statutory freeze of the kind Nixon later ordered. After 1965, war spending and domestic expansion ran together, prices rose faster than in the early part of the decade, and the arrangement supporting the Bretton Woods gold price came under strain in 1968, while formal convertibility for foreign governments lasted until 1971.

  4. 4. Labor

    He was allied with the unions, did not try to repeal Taft-Hartley, and failed when he tried to repeal that act's permission for state right-to-work laws. Coverage of the minimum wage was extended. The statutes backed by labor that actually changed the reach of the federal government were Medicare and the education acts, not a replacement of the Wagner Act.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    On October 3, 1965 he signed the Immigration and Nationality Act, known as Hart-Celler, at the Statue of Liberty. It abolished the national-origins quotas of 1924 and based admission mainly on family ties and on skills, within ceilings by hemisphere. He presented the change as the rejection of a discriminatory rule rather than as a large new stream of immigration, and the sources of immigration later shifted more than that description allowed.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    The Civil Rights Act of 1964 and the Voting Rights Act of 1965 made a large change in federal law, barring discrimination in public accommodations and employment and suspending the tests that had kept Black citizens off the rolls in the South. He signed a fair-housing act in April 1968, a week after the murder of Martin Luther King, and in 1967 he appointed Thurgood Marshall to the Supreme Court, the first Black justice. Riots in Los Angeles, Newark, Detroit, and the cities after King's death accompanied those statutes, and he kept his distance from the breadth of the Kerner Commission's report on the disorders.

  7. 7. Federalism

    The Great Society worked through federal money with conditions, through categorical grants, and through rights enforceable against state practices and against private businesses covered by the civil-rights acts. Medicare was a national program, while Medicaid and aid to schools ran through states and districts under federal rules. He did not abolish the states, and he did make national standards and national appropriations a much larger part of education, medical care, and civil rights.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    Vietnam was the central foreign fact, and the other combat deployment was the Dominican Republic in April 1965, where he sent troops citing danger to American lives and the chance of a communist takeover. He met Premier Kosygin at Glassboro in 1967 with little result, signed the Nuclear Non-Proliferation Treaty in 1968, and condemned the Soviet occupation of Czechoslovakia that summer without intervening. He did not open diplomatic relations with the People's Republic of China.

  9. 9. National defense

    In 1965 he sent American ground-combat units to Vietnam and began a sustained bombing campaign against the North, and the force there grew to more than half a million. He did not ask Congress for a declaration of war. He treated the Gulf of Tonkin Resolution as his authority and filled the ranks by the draft, which became more bitterly contested as the casualties mounted, while the nuclear arsenal remained outside the Non-Proliferation Treaty's limits on spread.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    He proposed no establishment of religion and sponsored no constitutional amendment to restore school prayer. The elementary-education act was drawn so that children in private and parochial schools could receive some federally supported services through public bodies, avoiding direct grants to churches. The moral vocabulary he used for the domestic programs was not a religious establishment.

  11. 11. Education

    He signed the Elementary and Secondary Education Act on April 11, 1965, directing federal money to school districts that served poor children and thereby breaking the long failure of general federal aid. The Higher Education Act of the same year created grants and loans for college students, and Head Start, inside the poverty program, reached children before they entered school. The federal role in paying for education became large, as it had not been under any earlier peacetime administration.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    He signed the Wilderness Act and the Land and Water Conservation Fund Act in 1964, and in 1968 the acts creating a national system of wild and scenic rivers and a national trails system. Model Cities and the continuation of urban renewal were the federal programs aimed at city land. The conservation statutes enlarged protected federal land and federal help for local parks while the war was absorbing the budget.

  13. 13. Energy

    He did not face an oil embargo and did not create a regional power authority comparable to the Tennessee Valley. He signed the Water Quality Act of 1965 and the Air Quality Act of 1967, real but narrower statutes than the clean-air and clean-water laws of the next presidency. Oil and coal stayed private industries, and federal regulation of interstate natural-gas prices continued as he had inherited it.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    Riots and public alarm about street crime made criminal justice a national political question in a way it had not been under Eisenhower. He signed the Omnibus Crime Control and Safe Streets Act of 1968, which created the Law Enforcement Assistance Administration and put federal money into local policing, despite wiretap provisions and a dilution of gun controls he had wanted to be stronger. The Gun Control Act of 1968, after the murders of King and of Robert Kennedy, restricted interstate sales and some classes of buyers without prohibiting private ownership.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    He called the effort a war on poverty, and the Economic Opportunity Act of 1964 created the Office of Economic Opportunity to administer community action, the Job Corps, VISTA, Head Start, and legal services. The office was a real federal undertaking, and its appropriations stayed far smaller than the cost of Vietnam and far smaller than the entitlements. Medicaid, food stamps made permanent in 1964, and higher Social Security benefits were the large and durable commitments, and measured poverty declined while those transfers and a tight labor market operated together.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    Medicare and Medicaid, which he signed on July 30, 1965, were the medical-insurance programs not enacted under Roosevelt, Truman, Eisenhower, or Kennedy. Medicare, tied to Social Security, paid for hospital care and, through a voluntary part, for physicians' services for the aged, and Medicaid paid for defined categories of the poor through a federal-state match, so that the federal government became a payer of personal medical bills on a large scale. The Social Security Act of 1935 had been pensions and categorical relief, not medical insurance.

Of this presidency

  1. The Civil Rights Act of 1964

    He signed the Civil Rights Act on July 2, 1964. It prohibited racial discrimination in public accommodations and in employment, authorized the cutoff of federal funds to programs that discriminated, and gave the attorney general new authority to sue. He used the opening created by the assassination, and a successful cloture vote against the Senate filibuster, to pass a bill Kennedy had proposed and had not passed. The statute changed what hotels, employers, and state officials could lawfully do. It was the largest civil-rights enactment since Reconstruction.

  2. The Voting Rights Act

    On August 6, 1965, after the marches at Selma, he signed the Voting Rights Act. It suspended literacy tests and similar devices in jurisdictions with a record of exclusion and provided for federal examiners to register voters. Black registration in the Deep South, long blocked by local officials, rose under that federal machinery. The act, more than any speech, is what made the southern Black electorate a fact in national politics. It is also what began the departure of the white South from the Democratic Party.

  3. Medicare and Medicaid

    The Social Security Amendments of 1965 created Medicare and Medicaid, and he signed them on July 30. Medicare gave the aged hospital insurance under Social Security and a voluntary plan for doctors' bills. Medicaid was a joint federal-state program of medical care for categories of the poor, including many of the aged poor the Kerr-Mills program had only begun to reach. This was health insurance, national in the case of Medicare. It was not the old-age pension of 1935, and it was the request Truman had made for the whole population, here limited to the aged and to defined groups of the poor.

  4. The Office of Economic Opportunity

    The Economic Opportunity Act of 1964, signed on August 20, established the Office of Economic Opportunity and the programs gathered under the name he gave the effort, a war on poverty. Community action agencies, the Job Corps, VISTA, and Head Start were the conspicuous pieces, and they gave the federal government a direct role in local projects rather than only a check to the states. Appropriations never approached the scale of the war or of Social Security and Medicare. The agency was nonetheless a new federal instrument, and it was contested from the start.

  5. The Immigration Act of 1965

    Hart-Celler, signed at the Statue of Liberty on October 3, 1965, repealed the national-origins quota system that Coolidge had signed in 1924 and that every president since had administered. Family relationship became the principal claim to a visa, with occupational skill as a lesser category, and ceilings were set by hemisphere rather than by a ranking of European nations. Johnson and the bill's sponsors described it as a moral correction, not as a decision to change the composition of the country. The second description is the one the later numbers failed to bear out.

  6. The Gulf of Tonkin Resolution

    In August 1964, after reports that North Vietnamese forces had attacked American destroyers in the Gulf of Tonkin, he asked Congress for authority to use armed force in Southeast Asia. The resolution passed on August 7, with two senators opposed and the House unanimous. The second reported attack was later placed in serious doubt. He treated the resolution, which was not a declaration of war, as legal warrant for the escalation that followed.

  7. Escalation in Vietnam

    American involvement did not begin with him. Eisenhower had supplied aid, and Kennedy had raised the advisory presence to about sixteen thousand. In 1965 Johnson sent ground-combat troops and opened a sustained bombing campaign against the North, and he kept raising the number until more than half a million Americans were in the country. That decision, to fight an American ground war, was his. It was a large commitment of men, money, and the draft, undertaken without a declaration of war, and it did not produce a settlement he was willing to accept before he left office.

  8. The decision not to run

    On March 31, 1968, after the Tet offensive and a weaker than expected result in the New Hampshire primary, he told the country he would not seek and would not accept the Democratic nomination. He coupled the withdrawal with a partial halt in the bombing of North Vietnam. The war did not stop because he left the race. He served out the term, and the election of 1968 was fought by other men over the war and the domestic order he was leaving behind.

PreviousBaker v. Carr and Gideon NextOne Person, One Vote, and Two Amendments

1964–1967

One Person, One Vote, and Two Amendments
