Johnson’s years finished what Baker had opened, applied the commerce power to racial exclusion in public places, and added two amendments: one against the poll tax in federal elections, and one that wrote down what happens when a president dies or cannot serve.

Decision

Reynolds v. Sims

Decided June 15, 1964

How it was made

Alabama’s legislative districts gave some voters many times the weight of others. Baker had opened the courthouse door. This case said what equal protection requires once the Court is inside.

Who sat

Eight to one. Warren wrote. Harlan dissented. Stewart and Clark accepted the result on narrower grounds in some of the companion cases. The controlling rule was Warren’s.

With the opinion

  • Earl Warren — Eisenhower
  • Hugo Black — F. Roosevelt
  • William Douglas — F. Roosevelt
  • Tom Clark — Truman
  • William Brennan — Eisenhower
  • Potter Stewart — Eisenhower
  • Byron White — Kennedy
  • Arthur Goldberg — Kennedy

In dissent

  • John Marshall Harlan — Eisenhower

What it changed

Seats in both houses of a state legislature must be apportioned by population, so that one person’s vote is worth roughly another’s. A state could not model its senate on the federal Senate, with equal representation of counties. Legislatures across the country were redrawn by court order. The shift of power was from rural minorities to the places where the people lived. No statute of Congress did it. The equal protection clause, as eight justices read it, did.

Decision

Heart of Atlanta Motel v. United States

Decided December 14, 1964

How it was made

The Civil Rights Act of 1964 forbade racial discrimination in public accommodations. A motel in Atlanta that served interstate travelers sued, claiming Congress had no power to regulate its choice of guests. The government defended the act as a regulation of commerce among the states, not only as an enforcement of the Fourteenth Amendment.

Who sat

Unanimous. Clark wrote. Douglas and Goldberg concurred and would also have rested on the Fourteenth Amendment. The opinion of the Court rested on commerce.

With the opinion

  • Tom Clark — Truman
  • Earl Warren — Eisenhower
  • Hugo Black — F. Roosevelt
  • William Douglas — F. Roosevelt
  • John Marshall Harlan — Eisenhower
  • William Brennan — Eisenhower
  • Potter Stewart — Eisenhower
  • Byron White — Kennedy
  • Arthur Goldberg — Kennedy

What it changed

Congress may forbid a motel, and by the companion case a restaurant, to exclude customers by race, because that exclusion burdens interstate commerce. The Civil Rights Cases of 1883 had closed the Fourteenth Amendment as a basis for reaching private owners. This decision did not overrule that holding in terms. It upheld the same kind of statute on a different power, the one Gibbons had described and the New Deal cases had widened. The act was Congress’s. The decision is what made it stick.

Amendment

The Twenty-fourth Amendment

Ratified January 23, 1964

How it was made

Congress proposed it in 1962. The right to vote in a federal election shall not be denied for failure to pay a poll tax or any other tax. It does not, by its words, reach state elections. A later decision, Harper v. Virginia Board of Elections in 1966, struck poll taxes in state elections under the equal protection clause.

What it changed

A money qualification for voting in federal elections became unconstitutional. States that had used the poll tax to thin the electorate, including black voters, lost that device in presidential and congressional contests. The amendment is narrow on purpose. The broader result came from the Court two years later, and from the Voting Rights Act of 1965.

Amendment

The Twenty-fifth Amendment

Ratified February 10, 1967

How it was made

Kennedy’s assassination had exposed gaps. The original Constitution did not clearly let a vice president be replaced, and it did not say who decides that a living president is unable to serve. Congress proposed the article in 1965. The vice president becomes president if the president dies, resigns, or is removed. A vacancy in the vice presidency is filled by presidential nomination and a majority of both houses. The president, or the vice president with a majority of the cabinet, may declare the president unable. Congress decides if the president contests the declaration.

What it changed

The line of succession stopped depending on a statute alone, and the country stopped leaving the vice presidency empty for the rest of a term. Nixon would use the appointment provision twice, after Agnew’s resignation and after his own. The disability provisions have been used for short medical procedures. The amendment changed the continuity of the office. It did not change the powers of the person who holds it.

Decision

Miranda v. Arizona

Decided June 13, 1966

How it was made

Ernesto Miranda had confessed to rape and kidnapping after police questioning, without being told he could keep silent or have a lawyer. The conviction rested on the confession. The Court heard it with three other cases.

Who sat

Five to four. Warren wrote. Fortas, Johnson’s first appointee, was the fifth vote. Goldberg’s seat, which Fortas filled, had been in the majority’s kind of case before. Clark, who had written Gideon’s era from the other side of unanimity, dissented here.

With the opinion

  • Earl Warren — Eisenhower
  • Hugo Black — F. Roosevelt
  • William Douglas — F. Roosevelt
  • William Brennan — Eisenhower
  • Abe Fortas — L. Johnson

In dissent

  • Tom Clark — Truman
  • John Marshall Harlan — Eisenhower
  • Potter Stewart — Eisenhower
  • Byron White — Kennedy

What it changed

Before a person in custody is questioned, the police must tell him he may keep silent, that what he says may be used against him, and that he may have a lawyer, appointed if he is poor. A confession taken without those warnings is not admissible. The rule is a condition on state police, made by the Court from the Fifth Amendment’s privilege against self-incrimination. Congress and later Courts have narrowed its edges. The warnings themselves became the ordinary practice of an arrest.

PreviousLyndon B. Johnson NextRichard Nixon

1969–1974

Richard Nixon

Republican

Before the office

He was born in 1913 in Yorba Linda, California, in a house his father had built beside a lemon grove that failed. Frank Nixon was loud, unlucky, and sure the big interests were against a small man. Hannah Nixon was a Quaker, quiet, and the parent the boy tried to satisfy. Two of his brothers died of tuberculosis, Harold after a long illness that took whatever money the grocery in Whittier had managed to save. Richard was the one who worked the store, who did not complain in his mother’s hearing, and who noticed who looked down on a family behind a counter. The Quaker meeting taught him self-control. The counter taught him that self-control was not the same thing as being respected. He spent the rest of his life trying to close that gap, and he never believed the people on the other side of it would close it for him.

He stayed in Whittier for college because the family could not spare him, and he knew what he was missing. The boys who went east did not know his name. He debated, he studied, and he was not invited into the ease he could see. Duke gave him a law degree and a high rank in the class and no offer from a firm that mattered. He went home and practiced. He met Thelma Ryan, called Pat, a teacher who had already made her own way, and he pursued her until she married him in 1940. She was steadier than he was and less interested in the fight. The war took him to the South Pacific as a supply officer, not as a commander of anything that would become a legend. He played poker and came home with savings. The service was real. It was not Kennedy’s service, and he knew the comparison was waiting before anyone of Kennedy’s sort had been set beside him.

In 1946 he ran for Congress against a New Deal Democrat and won by making the New Deal sound like a softness toward communism. In 1948, on the House un-American activities committee, he pursued Alger Hiss. Hiss was polished, well defended, and lying. Nixon was right about him, and a large part of the respectable press spent years refusing the conclusion. The case made Nixon a national Republican and made him, to the people who had believed Hiss, a man who had broken a gentleman for sport. In 1950 he took a Senate seat from Helen Gahagan Douglas with a campaign that called her pink down to the paper it was printed on. Tricky Dick was the name his opponents gave him. He treated the name as proof that they could not answer the substance. The substance and the method had already become the same thing. He found the weakness, he used it, and he remembered who had called him a liar for doing it.

Eisenhower put him on the ticket in 1952 and then nearly threw him off over a private political fund. Nixon answered on television, with a list of his debts, his wife’s cloth coat, and a dog named Checkers that he said the family was keeping. The speech was sentimental, precise, and a triumph. It saved him, and it taught him that he could go over the heads of the men who wanted him destroyed and speak to a public that recognized a striver. Eisenhower kept him and never warmed to him. For eight years he was vice president of an administration that used him for the hard trips and the hard partisanship and did not intend to hand him the presidency as a reward. In 1960 he lost to Kennedy by a margin so thin that a fight over the count was possible. He did not make it. In 1962 he lost the governorship of California and told the press they would not have him to kick around anymore. He meant it when he said it. He did not mean it for long.

The years in New York were the remaking. He practiced law, he traveled, he campaigned for other Republicans, and he watched Goldwater’s defeat teach the party that a frontal assault on the New Deal would lose. He would not repeal Social Security. He would not run as a man who hated the government Eisenhower had agreed to keep. He would run against the disorder of the late 1960s, against a war the Democrats could not end, and for what he would later call a silent majority that still believed in the country the newspapers seemed to despise. In 1968 he took the nomination with the South in mind and without saying he would undo the civil rights statutes. He promised peace with honor. In the last weeks of the campaign, as Farrell’s account establishes, his people urged the government in Saigon to wait, and not to join the peace talks Johnson was trying to open before election day. He wanted the war as an issue, not as a headline that saved Humphrey. He won a close race against Humphrey, with Wallace taking the rest of the old Democratic South.

He took the oath calling the title of peacemaker the greatest honor history can bestow. He meant Vietnam, and he meant a relationship with the great communist powers that containment alone had not produced. He did not mean he had become a different man from the one who had counted Hiss’s lies and saved himself with a dog. The government he inherited was the Great Society plus the Cold War state, and he intended to use both, not to dismantle them. Wage and price controls, an environmental agency, an opening to China, a limit on arms: those were available to a Republican who no longer thought the election was a referendum on 1933. What he brought from Whittier was the other half. He did not trust the institutions that had laughed at him, and he did not think a secret was a sin if it protected the work. The peacemaker and the man who had told Saigon to wait were the same person. He had been that person since the grocery.

In the office

Nixon added a further layer of federal regulation, including national environmental standards and peacetime controls on wages and prices, so the administrative reach of the national government became larger still. He withdrew American ground troops from Vietnam, opened a relationship with China, and negotiated arms limits with the Soviet Union, and he also bombed Cambodia. He resigned in 1974 rather than face removal over Watergate.

The party

Nixon's Republicans began the long movement toward the South and toward what he called the silent majority. The presidential program was not the same as that electoral rhetoric. He governed with wage and price controls, created the Environmental Protection Agency, and pursued detente with the Soviet Union together with an opening to China.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    Domestic spending rose, and he did not shrink the federal government to its size before the New Deal. Revenue sharing in 1972 sent federal checks to states and localities with fewer strings than Johnson's categorical grants, and the Social Security amendments of that year indexed benefits to prices and created Supplemental Security Income. His Family Assistance Plan, a guaranteed income in place of Aid to Families with Dependent Children, passed the House in 1970 and died in the Senate, and the Congressional Budget and Impoundment Control Act of 1974 curbed his refusal to spend money Congress had appropriated.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    On August 15, 1971 he imposed a temporary surcharge of ten percent on imports, a bargaining move tied to the currency decision rather than a new Smoot-Hawley, and the surcharge came off after exchange rates were realigned. Textile restraints continued, and in 1973 he briefly limited soybean exports. The lasting trade fact of the term was the end of the fixed gold-dollar rate, which let currencies move.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    On August 15, 1971 he closed the gold window, ending the convertibility of the dollar into gold for foreign governments and with it the Bretton Woods link. The same order froze wages and prices for ninety days, later phases of control were relaxed, and by 1973 the major currencies were floating. He pressed the Federal Reserve, under Arthur Burns, toward an easier policy before the 1972 election, and rising prices and weak employment came together, sharply so after the oil shock.

  4. 4. Labor

    The wage controls bound union settlements as well as prices, a peacetime limit on bargaining the Wagner Act had not imposed. He signed the Occupational Safety and Health Act at the end of 1970, giving federal inspectors a direct role in workplace hazards. The Philadelphia Plan required minority hiring goals on federal construction and was opposed by the building-trades unions, and he repealed neither the Wagner Act nor Taft-Hartley.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    The Immigration Act of 1965 remained the law, and he proposed no substitute for its system of family preferences and hemispheric ceilings. Enforcement against unlawful presence was not the domestic centerpiece that crime and drugs were. No new quota statute marks the term.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    The campaign appeal to the white South, and the nominations of Clement Haynsworth and Harrold Carswell to the Supreme Court, both rejected by the Senate, were not the same thing as a repeal of civil-rights law. His Labor Department's Philadelphia Plan imposed goals and timetables for minority craft hiring on federal construction, and he signed a 1970 extension of the Voting Rights Act. Southern dual school systems were largely dismantled under court orders during his first term, even while he opposed busing for racial balance and asked Congress to restrain it.

  7. 7. Federalism

    New Federalism was his name for general revenue sharing and for an attempt to replace some categorical grants with broader ones, federal money with fewer instructions attached. In the same years he created the Environmental Protection Agency, signed the Clean Air Act and the occupational-safety law, and impounded funds to control spending from the White House. Decentralizing language and a larger regulatory apparatus in Washington arrived together.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    He cut the American ground role in Vietnam while extending the air war into Cambodia, opened relations with the People's Republic of China, and signed limited nuclear agreements with the Soviet Union. In the October 1973 war he resupplied Israel and raised the alert of American nuclear forces when the Soviet Union hinted it might intervene. He authorized covert pressure against Salvador Allende in Chile and recognized the military government that took power there in 1973.

  9. 9. National defense

    Vietnamization reduced American ground forces while the air war went on, including secret bombing of Cambodia from 1969 and a ground incursion in 1970, until the Paris Peace Accords of January 1973. Draft inductions ended in 1973, and the armed forces were put on the road to an all-volunteer service. The strategic-arms agreements capped certain launchers and anti-ballistic-missile sites and did not disarm the nuclear force.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    He made no change in the constitutional rule against an establishment of religion. In 1973, during his second term, the Supreme Court decided Roe versus Wade, holding that the Constitution restricted the power of the states to prohibit abortion. He had not asked Congress for a national abortion statute, and the decision was the Court's rather than an act of his administration.

  11. 11. Education

    He did not repeal the Elementary and Secondary Education Act. The Education Amendments of 1972, which he signed, included Title IX, forbidding sex discrimination in education programs that receive federal funds, and created the basic educational opportunity grant later called the Pell Grant. He vetoed some education appropriations as inflationary and impounded money, without taking Johnson's federal role in schooling off the books.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    The National Environmental Policy Act, signed on January 1, 1970, required environmental impact statements for major federal actions and thereby changed decisions about public land, roads, and dams. He signed the Coastal Zone Management Act in 1972 and, after the embargo, the statute authorizing the Trans-Alaska pipeline. Procedure and pollution rules, more than a new disposition of the public domain, were the land policy of the term.

  13. 13. Energy

    The Arab oil embargo that began in October 1973 brought shortages and a sharp increase in price. He answered with allocation and price controls under the Emergency Petroleum Allocation Act, with a stated goal of independence from foreign oil, and with approval of the Alaska pipeline. He created no Department of Energy, and the controls were an emergency regime, not an end to the dependence the embargo had made obvious.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    He made crime and narcotics subjects of presidential policy and drove the District of Columbia crime act of 1970, which allowed preventive detention and no-knock entry in the federal city. The same year he signed the Organized Crime Control Act, including the racketeering statute known as RICO, and the drug act that created the schedules of controlled substances still in use, and in 1973 the Drug Enforcement Administration was established. Federal drug and racketeering law grew sharply, while ordinary policing outside the District stayed local.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    The Family Assistance Plan would have put a federal income floor in place of Aid to Families with Dependent Children, and the Senate did not pass it. The Social Security Amendments of 1972 did pass, creating Supplemental Security Income for the aged, the blind, and the disabled poor and providing automatic cost-of-living increases in Social Security. He moved to break up the Office of Economic Opportunity, and the entitlement programs grew while the community-action model of the Johnson poverty agency was cut back.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    He did not repeal Medicare or Medicaid. In 1971 he proposed a wider scheme of insurance, through employer plans and a federal program for the poor, and Congress did not enact it. He signed the National Cancer Act of 1971 and the Health Maintenance Organization Act of 1973, which enlarged research funding and encouraged prepaid group practice, and neither statute was a new national insurance system.

Of this presidency

  1. Vietnam, Cambodia, and the Paris accords

    He reduced American ground troops by Vietnamization and on January 27, 1973 signed the Paris Peace Accords, under which the remaining American combat forces left. The same presidency widened the war on the map. Bombing of Cambodia began in secret in March 1969, and on April 30, 1970 he announced a ground incursion into that country. Protests followed, including the demonstration at Kent State University, where National Guardsmen killed four students. American withdrawal and a wider war in Cambodia and Laos were both his policy. The accords did not end the war between the Vietnamese parties.

  2. The opening to China

    After a secret trip by Henry Kissinger in July 1971, Nixon went to the People's Republic of China in February 1972, the first visit by an American president since the communist government had taken power in 1949. The Shanghai Communique acknowledged that there was one China and left Taiwan's status to be settled by the Chinese themselves, in the careful language of that document, without establishing full diplomatic relations. Recognition of Beijing as the government of China came later, under another president. The isolation Truman, Eisenhower, Kennedy, and Johnson had maintained was nonetheless broken by him.

  3. SALT and detente

    In Moscow in May 1972 he signed an interim agreement limiting strategic offensive missiles and the Anti-Ballistic Missile Treaty. The ceilings were on classes of launchers and on missile-defense sites, not a dismantling of either nuclear force. Detente was this bargaining, plus the opening to China and a measure of trade, including grain sales. It was not an alliance, and it was not a claim that the rivalry with the Soviet Union had ended. He remained a Cold War president who had chosen negotiation on a few subjects where his predecessors had not.

  4. The gold window and wage and price controls

    On August 15, 1971 he closed the gold window, so that foreign governments could no longer convert dollars into gold at the fixed Bretton Woods price, and he imposed a ninety-day freeze on wages and prices along with a temporary import surcharge. Peacetime controls of that breadth were a change in kind, adopted by a Republican president who had opposed them. Later phases loosened the controls. Prices rose again, especially when the oil embargo hit in 1973, and the major currencies moved to a float. The official gold convertibility of the dollar, fixed by Roosevelt's devaluation and kept by Truman and Eisenhower, was ended.

  5. The Environmental Protection Agency and the Clean Air Act

    By a reorganization plan effective in December 1970 he created the Environmental Protection Agency, gathering federal pollution programs into one regulatory body. He signed the National Environmental Policy Act at the start of that year and, on December 31, 1970, the Clean Air Act, which set national air-quality standards and regulated emissions from automobiles. He vetoed the Federal Water Pollution Control Act Amendments of 1972 as too costly, and Congress overrode the veto within a day. The regulatory addition was large. Not every major environmental statute of the term was one he was willing to sign, and the ones that became law changed what industry and cities had to do.

  6. The Philadelphia Plan

    In 1969 the Department of Labor under his administration put the revised Philadelphia Plan into effect, requiring federal construction contractors in that city, and then more widely, to commit to goals and timetables for hiring minority workers in the skilled trades. He defended the plan when Congress tried to block it by appropriation rider, and the rider failed. The requirement applied to federal contracting, not to every private employer in the country by a new civil-rights statute. It was nonetheless a hiring rule with numbers attached, issued by a Republican Labor Department, and it divided him from the building-trades unions that had been part of the Democratic coalition.

  7. Watergate and the resignation

    On June 17, 1972 burglars employed by his reelection campaign were arrested in the offices of the Democratic National Committee at the Watergate complex. The cover-up that followed reached him, as the tape recordings of his own conversations eventually showed. In October 1973 he dismissed the special prosecutor in the sequence called the Saturday Night Massacre, after the attorney general and the deputy attorney general refused the order and resigned. The Supreme Court in United States v. Nixon ordered the tapes produced, and the House Judiciary Committee voted articles of impeachment. He resigned on August 9, 1974, the first president to resign the office, before the Senate could try him.

PreviousOne Person, One Vote, and Two Amendments NextThe Vote at Eighteen, Roe, and the Tapes

1971–1974

The Vote at Eighteen, Roe, and the Tapes

An amendment lowered the voting age for every election. The Court declared a constitutional right to end a pregnancy, and then, in the same presidency, ordered the president who had appointed four of its members to surrender recordings of his own conversations.

Amendment

The Twenty-sixth Amendment

Ratified July 1, 1971

How it was made

Congress had tried, in the Voting Rights Act of 1970, to set eighteen as the voting age in state as well as federal elections. The Court held that Congress could do it for federal elections and not for state ones. The amendment removed the distinction. Citizens who are eighteen or older shall not be denied the vote on account of age. It was ratified faster than any other amendment.

What it changed

The electorate grew by the youngest adults, in state races as well as federal. The Vietnam draft had made the old line, twenty-one, look indefensible to the Congress that proposed the article. The change is in who chooses the government. It did not change what the government is allowed to do.

Decision

Roe v. Wade

Decided January 22, 1973

How it was made

A Texas statute made it a crime to procure an abortion except to save the mother’s life. Jane Roe, a pregnant woman, sued the district attorney of Dallas County. The Court had already found, in Griswold v. Connecticut in 1965, a constitutional privacy that covered a married couple’s use of contraceptives. Roe asked whether that privacy covered the decision to end a pregnancy.

Who sat

Seven to two. Blackmun wrote. Three of Nixon’s four appointees were in the majority. The fourth, Rehnquist, dissented with Kennedy’s White. Douglas was the last of Roosevelt’s appointees still sitting.

With the opinion

  • Harry Blackmun — Nixon
  • Warren Burger — Nixon
  • William Douglas — F. Roosevelt
  • William Brennan — Eisenhower
  • Potter Stewart — Eisenhower
  • Thurgood Marshall — L. Johnson
  • Lewis Powell — Nixon

In dissent

  • Byron White — Kennedy
  • William Rehnquist — Nixon

What it changed

A state could not forbid abortion in the first trimester, and it could regulate later stages only within limits the Court described. The decision took a subject that had been state criminal law and made it a constitutional right, variable by the stage of pregnancy. It did not appropriate money or build clinics. It forbade a kind of statute. The controversy did not end. A later Court, in 1992, restated the right and loosened the test. Another, after the period this volume covers, overruled it. Within this volume, Roe is the law from 1973 on, and it was made by justices, three of them named by the president then in office.

Decision

United States v. Nixon

Decided July 24, 1974

How it was made

A grand jury had indicted presidential aides in the Watergate matter. The special prosecutor subpoenaed tape recordings of conversations in the president’s offices. Nixon claimed an absolute executive privilege and refused. The case reached the Court on an expedited hearing.

Who sat

Eight to none. Burger wrote. Rehnquist did not sit, because he had served in Nixon’s Justice Department. Three Nixon appointees remained, and all three joined the order against him.

With the opinion

  • Warren Burger — Nixon
  • William Douglas — F. Roosevelt
  • William Brennan — Eisenhower
  • Potter Stewart — Eisenhower
  • Byron White — Kennedy
  • Thurgood Marshall — L. Johnson
  • Harry Blackmun — Nixon
  • Lewis Powell — Nixon

Not sitting

  • William Rehnquist — Nixon

What it changed

Executive privilege exists, and it is not absolute. A general claim of confidentiality yields to a specific need for evidence in a criminal trial. The tapes were surrendered. They recorded the president’s part in the obstruction. He resigned sixteen days later. The Court did not remove him. The Constitution’s impeachment process, and his own choice to leave before a vote, did that. The decision removed the legal ground on which he had withheld the evidence. His appointees supplied the opinion and part of the majority.

PreviousRichard Nixon NextGerald Ford

1974–1977

Gerald Ford

Republican

Before the office

He was born in 1913 in Omaha, and the name he was born with was not Ford. Leslie Lynch King Jr. was the son of a man who hit his wife. Dorothy Gardner left within days, went home to Grand Rapids, and married Gerald Rudolf Ford, a paint salesman, who raised the boy and gave him the name the country later used. The legal change came when he was twenty-two. He did not know about the first father until that man turned up, years on, at a restaurant where the boy was working. Smith’s account makes the unknown father matter, because the public Ford was so completely the product of the second household: a decent stepfather, a Midwestern city, no myth and no fortune. He played football because he was big and because Grand Rapids noticed a center. The noticing was the whole of his early ambition. He wanted to be useful in the place he was from.

Michigan made him a star, the most valuable player of the 1934 team, and the professional clubs offered him money. He went to Yale instead, coached football and boxing to pay for the law school, and finished in 1941. He had thought of a judgeship in Grand Rapids more readily than of a life in Washington. The war came first. He served in the Navy on the carrier Monterey in the Pacific, and in a typhoon in December 1944 he was nearly washed off the deck. He came home a lieutenant commander with a war behind him and no interest in making the war his credential. In 1948 he married Elizabeth Bloomer, called Betty, who had danced and who had been married before. He was in the middle of his first campaign for Congress. She was livelier than the life he was asking her to join, and she joined it. The marriage lasted. It was not as simple as the Christmas cards from Grand Rapids made it look.

The Fifth District sent him to the House in 1948 and kept him there for a quarter of a century. He was a Republican of the Eisenhower kind: internationalist, a man of the Appropriations Committee, able to work with Democrats because he did not think the other party was a conspiracy. After the Goldwater defeat the Republicans made him minority leader. He wanted, with a steadiness that amused people who did not know him, to be Speaker. The majority never arrived. He sat on the Warren Commission and did the work. He defended Richard Nixon longer than was comfortable, because loyalty was his method and because he did not begin by assuming a president was lying. When the tapes made the assumption impossible, he did not invent a theory that would save the man. He was, by then, the obvious Republican who had not been ruined, which was a distinction he had earned by not being brilliant and not being crooked.

Spiro Agnew resigned in October 1973. Nixon, using the Twenty-fifth Amendment for the first time to fill a vacancy, sent Ford’s name to Congress. Both houses confirmed him. He was vice president for eight months, a decent presence in a building that had stopped believing its president, and he still thought he might go home. On August 9, 1974, Nixon resigned. Ford took the oath and told the country, almost at once, that he knew he had not been elected. He asked to be confirmed by prayers instead of ballots. Then he said the long national nightmare was over, and that the Constitution worked, and that the Republic was a government of laws and not of men. The sentences were not poetry. They were the point of putting an ordinary House member in the office. The mechanism had been used. A president had left. Another had taken the oath. No one had been shot and no one had called out a guard.

What he brought was Grand Rapids, not a program. He did not intend to repeal the New Deal or the Great Society, any more than Eisenhower or Nixon had intended to. He did not intend a new majority. Nelson Rockefeller, whom he would ask to serve as vice president, was the old Eastern party. The conservatives who would challenge him in 1976 were already restless, and they were not yet the party. He was the Republican who had spent twenty-five years trying to be Speaker and had instead become president because two men ahead of him had left in disgrace. The pardon, a month later, was the act the country would not forgive. It was also the act of a man whose whole method was to close a fight rather than to win it in public. He thought a trial of a former president would keep the nightmare open. A great many Americans thought the closing was the new offense.

Smith’s title is an ordinary man, and the surprise is that ordinariness was enough to get the country from a resignation to an election. Ford was not naive about Nixon. He had been loyal, and then he had stopped, and then he had inherited the mess the loyalty had sat inside. He was not a movement. He was a representative who could count, who could lose without a scene, and who believed a constitution that had just been used was more important than a speech about how it might be improved. The nightmare line was the whole of his claim. He had not come to change the government he found. He had come to prove it still worked, and to take the anger for doing the proving in the way a Grand Rapids lawyer thought a quarrel ought to end.

In the office

Ford served out what remained of Nixon's second term without claiming a new constitutional theory or a new economic order. He kept the Atlantic alliance, the election calendar, and private property, and he met a Democratic Congress with vetoes more often than with programs. The term is fixed by the pardon of his predecessor, the fall of Saigon, the Helsinki Final Act, and a public campaign against inflation that exhortation could not win.

The party

Ford led Richard Nixon's Republican Party without Nixon, and he did not give it a new program. It was still a broad coalition: Nelson Rockefeller as vice president, a surviving moderate wing, and a conservative challenge, from Ronald Reagan in 1976, that did not yet control the nomination. The pardon of Nixon in September 1974 was an act inside that party rather than a break with it, and Ford paid for the pardon with a loss of public standing he never fully recovered. What changed was the president, not the coalition's premises about markets, alliances, or the New Deal programs Republicans had stopped promising to repeal.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    He faced inflation and recession together. His first instinct was restraint, including a proposed temporary surcharge on upper incomes and corporations, and in 1975 he accepted a tax cut; he then vetoed appropriations he regarded as excessive, repeatedly. The deficit stayed large by the standards of the early postwar years, and the voluntary campaign against inflation added no fiscal power at all.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    He signed the Trade Act of 1974, which armed presidents with negotiating authority and expedited congressional procedures for trade agreements, and which opened a legal path for relief against import injury. The Tokyo Round followed from that authority. He did not restore a high statutory tariff as the ordinary instrument of policy.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    Arthur Burns remained chairman of the Federal Reserve, and the dollar stayed in the floating regime Nixon had opened. Ford's own anti-inflation instrument was exhortation, not a new banking statute and not a new definition of the dollar. Inflation eased in the recession of 1975 and was not defeated.

  4. 4. Labor

    Unemployment rose sharply in that recession, and he answered with the tax cut and with vetoes rather than with a new labor-relations act. The full-employment bill favored by liberals and organized labor did not pass while he was president. He did not seek to repeal the existing framework of collective bargaining.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    There was no comprehensive immigration statute. After the fall of Saigon he supported admission and resettlement of Indochinese refugees, the start of a large and lasting migration, under a specific assistance act rather than a new permanent system. The framework enacted in 1965 stayed.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    He signed a 1975 extension of the Voting Rights Act that added protections for language minorities. He was skeptical of court-ordered busing and preferred other remedies, and he neither repealed the civil-rights statutes nor ended the busing orders. Enforcement of the 1960s acts continued without a new statute of that magnitude.

  7. 7. Federalism

    The New York City fiscal crisis was his lesson in what Washington would not automatically underwrite. He refused a general bailout, was caricatured for a sentence he did not speak, and then signed a program of seasonal loans that had to be repaid and that left the city's long austerity to New York's own control board. Revenue sharing and the categorical-grant state continued; he did not return major domestic functions to the states.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    The fall of Saigon in April 1975 ended the American war in Vietnam after Congress refused the aid he had requested for the South. At Helsinki that summer he signed the European security accords, which recorded the postwar borders and, with them, human-rights principles that dissidents in the East later invoked; conservatives in his own party, Reagan among them, attacked the document as a ratification of the Soviet sphere. He kept Henry Kissinger as secretary of state, continued the Sinai diplomacy, and did not leave NATO.

  9. 9. National defense

    The draft was already gone, and he did not restore it. Congress trimmed the defense increases he asked for in the aftermath of Vietnam. In May 1975 he ordered a brief rescue after Cambodia seized the merchant ship Mayaguez, a use of force, not a return to the war just lost.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    He was a conventional Episcopalian and did not organize a religious bloc as an instrument of the presidency. His wife's openly moderate views on abortion and sexual mores drew protest from conservatives who were beginning to mobilize. That mobilization was not yet the core of the Republican coalition, and he proposed no establishment of religion.

  11. 11. Education

    He signed the Education for All Handicapped Children Act in 1975, which conditioned federal aid on a state's duty to educate children with disabilities in the public system. That was a real extension of federal influence over local schools, aimed at a population many districts had excluded. He did not create a department of education, and curriculum remained outside any national ministry.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    The Federal Land Policy and Management Act of 1976 ended the presumption that the public lands were held chiefly for disposal and charged the Bureau of Land Management with multiple-use management. He did not settle Alaska; that statute belonged to his successor. The public domain stayed in federal ownership.

  13. 13. Energy

    The Energy Policy and Conservation Act of 1975 created the Strategic Petroleum Reserve, set the first federal standards for automobile efficiency, and continued oil-price controls with a path toward decontrol. He preferred a faster decontrol than Congress would give him. The controls, the reserve, and the efficiency rules were the energy policy, not a government oil company.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    The distinctive act was the pardon, which closed federal prosecution of Nixon for offenses during his presidency without an indictment or a trial. Ford also offered a limited and conditional clemency to Vietnam-era draft offenders, short of the broader pardon his successor issued. There was no omnibus crime statute; crime was already high, and ordinary policing remained local.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    The recession filled the existing rolls of unemployment insurance, food stamps, and cash welfare, and he neither replaced those programs nor repealed them. He signed the earned-income tax credit in 1975, a wage supplement for low-paid workers that later presidents of both parties enlarged. He preferred that kind of targeted device, and vetoes, to a new entitlement.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    National health insurance did not pass. In 1976 he launched a national immunization campaign against a feared swine influenza, then suspended it after the pandemic failed to arrive and after cases of Guillain-Barré syndrome were associated with the vaccine. Medicare and Medicaid continued in their basic design.

Of this presidency

  1. The Nixon pardon

    On September 8, 1974, a month after taking office, Ford granted Richard Nixon a full pardon for federal offenses committed or possibly committed during the presidency, from the inauguration in 1969 through the resignation. No indictment had been returned. Ford's stated purpose was to end the country's absorption in Watergate. The pardon could not reach state charges, and it did not include a confession. It made a trial impossible, it damaged Ford immediately in the country and inside his own party, and it remained the decision by which the short presidency was judged.

  2. The fall of Saigon

    In the spring of 1975 the North Vietnamese offensive overran the South. Ford asked Congress for emergency aid and did not receive what he asked. The evacuation of Americans and of Vietnamese linked to the American presence was improvised by air and by sea, and on April 30 Saigon fell. He did not reopen the war. The end of it was administered, not chosen, and it closed a commitment the previous administrations had already been unable to sustain.

  3. The Helsinki Final Act

    On August 1, 1975, Ford signed the Final Act of the Conference on Security and Cooperation in Europe. It was a political accord, not a treaty submitted to the Senate. It registered the borders produced by the Second World War and by the division of Europe, and it included commitments on human rights and on the freer movement of people and ideas. Reagan and others on the right treated the signature as an American seal on Soviet domination. Ford's answer, and the later use dissidents made of the human-rights articles, was that the document gave the East a standard its governments had publicly accepted.

  4. Whip Inflation Now

    In October 1974 Ford asked the public to spend less and save more, and the White House distributed buttons under the name Whip Inflation Now. The campaign created no legal authority, set no price, and appropriated no money. The recession did more to slow prices than the buttons did, and only for a time. Inflation returned before the decade was out, and the episode remained a lesson in the difference between exhortation and a policy.

  5. New York City

    New York City, after years of short-term borrowing against operating expenses, could not roll its notes in 1975. Ford refused a general federal assumption of the debt. A New York tabloid reduced the refusal to a death sentence he had not uttered. By the end of the year he signed a statute of seasonal federal loans, to be repaid, with conditions, after New York State had already imposed a control board and a rescue corporation of its own. The city avoided formal bankruptcy. The federal role was a loan, not a pension, and the austerity was local.

PreviousThe Vote at Eighteen, Roe, and the Tapes NextJimmy Carter

1977–1981

Jimmy Carter

Democrat

Before the office

He was born in 1924 in Plains, Georgia, the oldest son of Earl Carter, who owned the peanut warehouse and the land around it and did not think the racial order of Sumter County was a question. His mother, Lillian, was a nurse, and she talked to the Black families on the place in a way Earl did not. Jimmy read more than the town required and intended to leave it. He also intended, without saying so, to be the best at whatever he was assigned. The Baptist church gave him a language for that intention. The farm gave him the other fact he would spend years learning how to say out loud: he had grown up inside a system that was wrong, and he had not, as a boy, refused it.

Annapolis took him, class of 1946, just late enough to miss the fighting. He married Rosalynn Smith that summer. She was from Plains, younger, and she became the partner who remembered the politics when he was busy being right. The Navy put him in submarines and then under Hyman Rickover, who was building the nuclear fleet and who did not compliment people. Rickover asked him once whether he had always done his best. Carter said no. The question became the title of the book he later wrote and the standard he imposed on everyone who worked for him, including himself. When Earl died in 1953, he left the Navy, against Rosalynn’s wish, and went home to keep the warehouse from failing. It nearly failed. Then it paid. The return looked, to the officers he had left, like a retreat. It was the decision that put him back in a county where a man could still run for something.

He started with the school board and the library and the ordinary offices of a town that knew his father’s name. In 1962 he ran for the state senate, and the courthouse crowd in Quitman County stuffed the ballot box against him. He went to court, proved the count was dirty, and took the seat. The fight mattered more than the office. He was not a marcher in the movement that was transforming the South. He was a businessman who thought a stuffed ballot was a sin and a humiliation, and who did not want to be Lester Maddox. In 1966 he ran for governor and lost. In 1970 he ran again, and the second campaign was colder. He courted the voters who had liked Wallace, and he beat a more liberal opponent by letting those voters think he was safe. Then, in January 1971, he told Georgia that the time for racial discrimination was over. The people who had voted for the safety felt cheated. The rest of the country heard a New South governor it could use.

He spent four years reorganizing the state government as if it were a ship Rickover might inspect. He cut the number of agencies. He read the budgets. He did not drink with the legislators or flatter them, and they noticed. He taught Sunday school. He called himself born again and meant the theology, not the mood. Alter’s title, his very best, is the Rickover sentence turned into a life: the intensity, the obligation, the impatience with anyone who would settle. It was a strength in Atlanta, where the job was a reorganization. It was going to be a problem in a city that runs on the very favors he thought were a form of lying. He left the governorship unknown to most of the country except as the man who had said the sentence about discrimination, and as a Democrat the machines did not own.

Watergate and the pardon made the outsider plausible. In 1976 he said he would never lie to the American people, and he walked through the primaries as a man who had not been in Washington for the nightmare. He was a Southern evangelical in a party whose northern half was less and less religious, a fiscal conservative beside a liberal Congress, a friend of civil rights in a white South that was already leaving the Democrats. Labor did not love him. The cities did not know him. Black voters and the remnant of the old coalition were enough, with the disgust at Nixon, to beat Ford. He had not promised a new New Deal. He had promised competence and a government that would stop pretending it could do everything. The coalition that elected him was already the split the term would govern inside.

He took the oath and thanked Ford for healing the land. Then he said the country had learned that more is not necessarily better, that even a great nation has limits, and that it cannot answer every question or solve every problem. The line was the branch. He did not come to repeal the insurance state or the alliances, and he did not come to extend them on Johnson’s scale. He came to make them honest, to spend less political capital on pretension, and to put human rights into a foreign policy that still had to face the Soviet Union. The hostages and the inflation were not yet the story. The story was a Plains engineer who thought the highest political act was to do his best and say so, and who had not yet sat in a capital that does not grade on that scale.

In the office

Carter entered as a post-Watergate reformer and governed as a Democrat who deregulated more than he nationalized, appointed a central banker willing to break inflation with very high interest rates, and left NATO, elections, and private property in place. The changes of the term were real and only partly of his choosing. Egypt and Israel made peace, the canal treaties passed, a program of conservation and synthetic fuels was begun, and then the embassy in Tehran was seized and the Soviet Union invaded Afghanistan.

The party

Carter's Democrats were the party that won after Vietnam and Watergate, not the New Deal party in its old unity. He was a Southern moderate elected alongside a liberal Congress, and the two never settled into one program: energy, hospital costs, and the budget all showed the split. Labor, the cities, Black voters, and what remained of the white South were still in the coalition, but the South was already leaving, and Edward Kennedy's challenge in 1980 came from the liberal side. The party could deregulate and create departments more easily than it could agree on the size of the welfare state.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    He came in proposing stimulus and moved toward restraint as inflation worsened, and the late budgets disappointed liberals in his own party. The notable rescue of a firm was the Chrysler loan guarantee of 1980, a guarantee rather than a purchase of the company. Deficits continued, he did not balance the budget, and he left with the recession of 1980 underway.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    The Tokyo Round was concluded on his watch and implemented in the Trade Agreements Act of 1979. He used administrative devices, including trigger prices on steel, when a particular industry was injured. The direction remained multilateral reduction of barriers, not a new protective system.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    He appointed Paul Volcker chairman of the Federal Reserve in 1979, and that October the Fed let short-term interest rates rise toward 20 percent in order to break inflation, accepting the recession that followed. A support package in 1978 had already been thrown under a falling dollar. The banking act of 1980 began phasing out ceilings on deposit interest and extended reserve requirements; it was not a return to gold.

  4. 4. Labor

    A labor-law reform died in a Senate filibuster in 1978, and his relations with the unions were poor for a Democratic president. He invoked Taft-Hartley during the long coal strike of 1977 and 1978. Deregulation of airlines and trucking, which he signed, weakened the regulatory shelters in which those unions had bargained.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    The Refugee Act of 1980 gave refugee admissions a regular statutory process and adopted the international definition of a refugee. The same year the Mariel boatlift brought well over a hundred thousand Cubans to Florida in a few months, a crisis that statute had not been built to manage. Employer sanctions and a legalization for people already here waited until 1986.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    He appointed more Black federal judges and officials than his predecessors and did not enact a civil-rights statute on the scale of 1964 or 1965. The Bakke decision, during his term, rejected a fixed racial quota in medical-school admissions and allowed race as one factor among others. A number of Black elected leaders who had helped elect him came to judge the administration inattentive to unemployment in the cities.

  7. 7. Federalism

    The Departments of Energy and Education gathered existing and new federal functions into the cabinet; they did not transfer the running of power plants or of schools to Washington. Revenue sharing, a device from the Nixon years, continued. He talked of reorganization more than of a constitutional reallocation, and the states remained the operators of most domestic government.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    He began by pressing human rights on allies and adversaries alike, completed the Camp David accords and the Egypt-Israel peace treaty, and won Senate consent for canal treaties that scheduled the transfer of the Panama Canal at the end of 1999. He normalized relations with Beijing. The seizure of the embassy in Tehran and the Soviet invasion of Afghanistan then dominated the last year: fifty-two hostages, a failed rescue, a grain embargo, an Olympic boycott, an unratified arms treaty, and a declaration that the Persian Gulf was a vital interest.

  9. 9. National defense

    His early budgets continued the post-Vietnam restraint and canceled the B-1 bomber. After the Iranian revolution and the invasion of Afghanistan he turned toward sustained increases that his successor would enlarge. The second strategic-arms treaty was signed in 1979 and never ratified, and he neither left NATO nor restored the draft.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    He spoke as a Southern Baptist and governed without any project of religious establishment. Personally opposed to abortion, he accepted the Hyde Amendment's limits on federal funding and did not seek a constitutional ban. By 1980 a large share of the white evangelical voters who had supported him in 1976 had moved toward a Republican Party ready to make their moral program its own.

  11. 11. Education

    He won a Department of Education in 1979, a cabinet department long sought by the National Education Association, consolidating federal education programs without taking curriculum or the operation of schools from states and districts. Federal student aid continued. His successors complained of the department and did not abolish it.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    The Alaska lands statute of 1980 protected more than a hundred million acres of parks, refuges, and wilderness, the largest single conservation act in the nation's history. In the same lame-duck weeks he signed the Superfund law, a federal liability and cleanup regime for abandoned hazardous-waste sites. He had already used the Antiquities Act in Alaska to force Congress to choose.

  13. 13. Energy

    The National Energy Act of 1978, a diminished version of what he had asked, reworked natural-gas pricing, utility rules, and conservation, and the Energy Security Act of 1980 created a Synthetic Fuels Corporation with authority to subsidize substitutes for imported oil. In 1979 he announced a phased end to crude-oil price controls, coupled with a windfall-profits tax Congress enacted the next year. A partial meltdown at Three Mile Island in March 1979 stopped the political prospects of a rapid nuclear build-out; it did not produce a documented public death toll from radiation.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    On his first full day in office he pardoned most Vietnam-era violators of the Selective Service Act, and not, in general, military deserters. The Foreign Intelligence Surveillance Act of 1978 put national-security wiretaps under a special court, a post-Watergate limit on the executive. Street crime stayed high, and he passed no crime bill comparable to those of the decade that followed.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    Stagflation, and then the recession at the end of the term, increased hardship while inflation eroded real wages. His proposals to recast welfare around work and a broader income floor did not pass. Food stamps, Supplemental Security Income, and Aid to Families with Dependent Children continued as he had found them.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    Hospital cost containment failed, and so did national health insurance; he and Edward Kennedy could not agree on a bill Congress would pass. Medicare and Medicaid remained the federal system. Costs kept rising inside it.

Of this presidency

  1. Camp David

    In September 1978 Carter kept Menachem Begin and Anwar Sadat at Camp David until they accepted a framework for peace between Egypt and Israel and a second, much vaguer framework for the Palestinians. The Egypt-Israel peace treaty followed in March 1979, the first between Israel and an Arab state. Israel withdrew from the Sinai. The Palestinian framework did not become a treaty. The Egyptian peace held, and it was the strategic gain of the term.

  2. The Panama Canal treaties

    Two treaties, signed in 1977 and consented to by the Senate in 1978, transferred the canal to Panama at the end of 1999 and declared the waterway neutral and open. The fight in the Senate and in the country was bitter, and the votes were close to the two-thirds line. Carter treated the canal as an asset that had become a liability in Latin America, not as territory to be given away in silence. The transfer occurred on the schedule the treaties set, under a later president.

  3. The crisis of confidence

    On July 15, 1979, after days of meetings at Camp David, Carter spoke to the country about energy and about what he called a crisis of confidence. He did not use the word malaise; others attached it afterward. The policy content was conservation, import reduction, and a heavier public commitment to alternative fuels, on top of the decontrol of oil prices. The political sequel was a request for cabinet resignations and the departure of several secretaries. The speech did not restore confidence, and the energy statutes that passed were narrower than the crisis he had described.

  4. Paul Volcker

    In August 1979 Carter named Paul Volcker chairman of the Federal Reserve in place of G. William Miller, whom he moved to the Treasury. Volcker was known to favor a harder policy. In October the Federal Reserve changed its operating method and allowed interest rates to rise to levels without modern precedent, aiming at bank reserves rather than at a gentle path for the funds rate. Carter appointed him and then had to live with the disinflation. The worst of the resulting recession fell under the next president; the decision to break inflation by tight money was Carter's appointment.

  5. Deregulation

    The Airline Deregulation Act of 1978 put the Civil Aeronautics Board on a path to its own extinction and opened routes and fares to competition. The Motor Carrier Act and the Staggers Rail Act, both in 1980, did the related work for trucking and railroads. These were deregulations of price and entry inside industries the federal government had cartelized, not a repeal of the labor law or of antitrust. Reagan would extend the same tendency. It began, as statute, under Carter.

  6. The hostages in Tehran

    On November 4, 1979, militants seized the American embassy in Tehran. Fifty-two Americans were still held when Carter left office, 444 days after the taking. A military rescue in April 1980 was aborted in the Iranian desert after equipment failed, and eight Americans were killed in the accident that ended it. Negotiations through Algeria produced an agreement that returned the hostages on January 20, 1981, as the next president was inaugurated. The crisis, more than any domestic reform, defined the last year.

  7. Afghanistan

    The Soviet invasion of Afghanistan in December 1979 ended the arms-control and grain relationship Carter had still been pursuing. He embargoed additional grain sales to the Soviet Union, led a boycott of the Moscow Olympics that the United States Olympic Committee joined, withdrew the signed strategic-arms treaty from Senate consideration, and in January 1980 declared that an attempt by an outside power to control the Persian Gulf would be repelled by force if necessary. Covert aid to Afghan resistance began. None of this reversed the invasion during his term. It did mark the turn from detente toward the buildup his successor would claim as his own.

PreviousGerald Ford NextRonald Reagan

1981–1989

Ronald Reagan

Republican

Before the office

He was born in 1911 in Tampico, Illinois, and Dixon is the town he claimed. His father, Jack, sold shoes and drank. His mother, Nelle, belonged to the Disciples of Christ and believed a life could be told as a story with a decent ending. They moved when the work moved. There was no money that lasted. The story he told later, of finding his father passed out in the snow and getting him inside, is the childhood in one scene: he would do what had to be done, and he would not stand in the yard discussing it. The cheerfulness was not ignorance of the mess. It was the method Nelle had taught for living with one. He kept the method long after he had the money to replace it with something more complicated, and he distrusted people who thought complication was a virtue.

Eureka College, a small Disciples school, graduated him into the Depression with a gift for speaking and no profession. He became a radio announcer, first in Iowa, and he learned to describe baseball games he was not watching, from a telegraph wire, so that a listener believed he was in the park. The skill was not a fraud in the way a later age uses the word. It was the job. It was also a training. He could make an event vivid without having been at the center of it. Hollywood hired him in 1937. He was the Gipper, and he was the young man whose legs were cut off in King’s Row, and he was never quite a star of the first rank. He was good enough to be known, and he liked being known. In 1940 he married Jane Wyman. They lost a daughter in infancy. The marriage ended after the war. His eyes kept him out of combat. He spent the service in a film unit in California, making pictures for the Army Air Forces. The service was real. It was not overseas. Some of the stories he told later wandered toward a war he had not fought, which Boot’s account does not tidy up. The habit of the wire report was still there. He improved the scene.

What moved him out of Roosevelt’s party was not a seminar. He had been a New Deal Democrat, and he stayed one through the war. As president of the Screen Actors Guild he decided the communists in Hollywood were not a rumor, and he cooperated with the people who were driving them out of the business. The high wartime tax rates on an actor’s income did the rest of the personal instruction. He felt the government taking the money he had just begun to earn, and he did not experience it as social justice. General Electric gave him a television show and a speech. He toured the plants for years, telling the same story: the country was freer and richer when the government did less. In October 1964 he gave the speech for Barry Goldwater, who was about to lose, and the speech outlived the candidate. He had registered as a Republican two years earlier. The party he joined was still Eisenhower’s party in its offices. The speech was Goldwater’s argument in a voice that did not sound angry.

Nancy Davis, an actress and the daughter of a conservative Chicago surgeon, married him in 1952. This marriage held. She watched the room, remembered who had been loyal, and was harder about enemies than he liked to appear. The children of both marriages were not the center of the life the public saw, and several of them were unhappy about the distance. He was capable of great warmth at a podium and of not noticing, at the dinner table, that a child had been trying to speak. The gap is part of the record. It did not make the political gift false. It meant the gift was a public one. He was most himself when the audience was large enough that no single person had to be known all the way through.

California elected him governor in 1966, over Pat Brown, in the middle of the campus unrest and the tax revolt that did not yet have that name. In Sacramento he was not the speech. He raised taxes when the budget was short. He negotiated a welfare reform with a Democratic speaker. He denounced the disorder and then made a deal. The people who had loved the Goldwater speech were sometimes disappointed, and the people who had feared it were sometimes surprised. He lost the 1976 nomination to Ford and learned the map. In 1980 the map had changed. White Southerners who had left the Democrats, Northern ethnics who had stayed with Roosevelt and then with Nixon, and religious conservatives who wanted a language Ford would not give them were available at the same time. He promised to cut tax rates, rebuild the military, and stop apologizing. He did not promise to repeal Social Security. Carter had said the nation had limits. Reagan ran against the sentence.

He took the oath and said that in the present crisis government was not the solution to the problem; government was the problem. The hostages in Iran were released that day, which gave the sentence a stage it had not earned by itself. He had been a Democrat who voted for Roosevelt. He had been a governor who signed a tax increase. The line was a direction, not a claim that 1935 would be taken off the books. He meant the growth, the apology, and the idea that Washington was the author of prosperity. He did not mean the insurance state his own voters received, or the alliance he was about to rearm. Boot’s life is the legend and the man who helped build it, held apart often enough that the reader can see the work. What he brought into the office was the wire announcer’s confidence, the plant-gate speech, and a pragmatism he did not put in the speech. The country had asked for the confidence. He was ready to perform it, and, when the count required it, to sign something quieter than the performance.

In the office

Reagan cut marginal tax rates, rebuilt the armed forces, and broke a federal strike, and he did not repeal Social Security, leave NATO, or replace the private economy with something else. The late diplomatic achievement was a treaty that abolished a class of nuclear missiles by agreement with the Soviet state he had spent the first term confronting. The turn was large, and so was the breach of control inside his own administration when officials sold arms to Iran and diverted the proceeds to rebels in Nicaragua.

The party

Under Reagan the Republicans became the party of tax-rate cuts, of a harder line against the Soviet Union, and of a religious conservatism that had been only a rising faction under Ford and Nixon. The coalition absorbed former Democrats, particularly white Southerners and Northern ethnic voters who had stayed with the New Deal party into the 1970s, and the old liberal wing of the party shrank. He still signed later revenue increases, a Social Security rescue, and, at the end, an arms treaty. The commitments the party now led with were nonetheless the ones he had put at the center.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    The 1981 tax act cut individual rates on a schedule and brought the top statutory rate from 70 percent, where it had applied to income from capital, down to 50 percent; acts in 1982 and 1984 took back part of the revenue. The 1986 reform pushed the top statutory rate to 28 percent, broadened the base, and cut the corporate rate, and the 1983 Social Security amendments raised payroll taxes and scheduled a later increase in the retirement age. Deficits persisted, and the federal debt rose sharply.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    His language was free trade, and the record was mixed. He accepted voluntary restraints on Japanese automobile exports, temporary protection for a motorcycle company, and pressure on semiconductors, even as he opened the Uruguay Round and signed a free-trade agreement with Canada that took effect in January 1989. He did not install a general high tariff.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    He kept Paul Volcker, Carter's appointment, through the disinflation, and he named Alan Greenspan chairman of the Federal Reserve in 1987. The Plaza agreement of 1985 managed a decline in the dollar, and the Louvre agreement of 1987 tried to steady it. The thrift statute of 1982 loosened the savings-and-loan industry; the bill for the resulting failures arrived mainly under his successor.

  4. 4. Labor

    In August 1981, when the air-traffic controllers' union struck in violation of federal law, he fired those who would not return, and the union was decertified. Private-sector union density continued a decline already underway. He did not seek a new statutory structure for collective bargaining; the signal was the firing.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    The Immigration Reform and Control Act of 1986 paired penalties for employers who knowingly hired unauthorized workers with a legalization for people who had lived in the country continuously since before 1982. Nearly three million people eventually gained legal status under it. The sanctions proved hard to enforce, and illegal immigration did not stop.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    He signed the bill making King's birthday a federal holiday and a twenty-five-year extension of the Voting Rights Act, the latter with an effects test he had resisted. His Justice Department sought to narrow busing and some affirmative-action remedies, and his relations with civil-rights organizations were hostile. He did not repeal the Civil Rights Act, the Voting Rights Act, or the fair-housing laws.

  7. 7. Federalism

    The New Federalism proposal of 1982, a swap in which Washington would have taken Medicaid and the states would have taken income support and food stamps, did not pass. What passed were consolidations of some categorical grants into block grants, a real but limited shift. Regulatory power, the civil-rights statutes, and the major entitlements stayed national.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    The early policy armed anti-Soviet forces from Afghanistan to Central America, sent troops into Grenada in 1983, bombed Libya in 1986, and left Lebanon after a barracks bombing that killed more than two hundred American service members. Iran-Contra was an arms sale to Iran, under embargo and at war with Iraq, in an attempt to free hostages in Lebanon, with a diversion of proceeds to the Nicaraguan contras while statute barred military aid to them; he denied authorizing the diversion. Once Gorbachev was in power the later policy produced the treaty on intermediate-range nuclear forces, and it did not include an American exit from NATO.

  9. 9. National defense

    He presided over a large real increase in defense spending, revived the B-1 bomber Carter had canceled, announced the Strategic Defense Initiative in 1983, and set a goal of a six-hundred-ship navy. The stated aim was to raise the cost of Soviet competition and to negotiate from strength. He did not restore the draft, and the intermediate-range treaty later removed an entire class of nuclear delivery systems.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    The religious right moved into the Republican core: opposition to abortion, support for a school-prayer amendment, and a demand for judges of a stated philosophy became ordinary coalition business. The prayer amendment failed in the Senate, and the abortion decisions stood. He did not establish a national church or obtain a constitutional text on religion or abortion; the coalition changed the party, not the First Amendment.

  11. 11. Education

    A commission report in 1983, A Nation at Risk, treated weak schooling as an economic and strategic danger and pushed states toward standards and testing. He had proposed to abolish the Department of Education and did not. Federal aid continued, and the operation of schools remained with states and local boards.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    James Watt's Interior Department favored leasing and development and became a political liability; Watt resigned in 1983. The wish, associated with the Sagebrush Rebellion, to transfer large federal holdings to the states did not become law. The environmental statutes stayed on the books and were enforced with less zeal, and the public domain remained federal.

  13. 13. Energy

    In his first weeks he removed the remaining controls on oil prices, completing a decontrol Carter had already set in motion, and he let the Synthetic Fuels Corporation wind down until it was abolished. The Strategic Petroleum Reserve stayed. The price collapse of the mid-1980s changed the politics of energy more than any new production statute, and nuclear construction did not recover from Three Mile Island.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    The crime statute of 1984 created the machinery for federal sentencing guidelines, tightened bail, and expanded forfeiture. The anti-drug laws of 1986 and 1988 added mandatory minimums and a one-hundred-to-one sentencing ratio between crack and powder cocaine. The federal criminal law grew, and state prison populations grew with it, while ordinary policing stayed local.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    The reconciliation act of 1981 tightened eligibility and benefits for Aid to Families with Dependent Children, food stamps, and a range of other means-tested programs. Poverty rose in the recession and receded with the expansion, and the largest supports — Social Security, Medicare, Medicaid, and the earned-income credit — remained. He did not replace welfare with a negative income tax or with a pure block grant.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    Medicare's payment to hospitals shifted in 1983 to a prospective rate per diagnosis; a catastrophic-coverage expansion enacted in 1988 was repealed in 1989, under his successor, after beneficiaries rejected the supplemental premiums; and employers were required to offer temporary continuation of group coverage after a job loss. He did not propose a national health service. The federal response to AIDS was slow in the early years and more direct later, including a Surgeon General's report that spoke plainly about how the disease spread.

Of this presidency

  1. The 1981 tax cut

    The Economic Recovery Tax Act cut individual income-tax rates by about a quarter over three years and lowered the top statutory rate from 70 percent to 50 percent. It indexed the brackets to inflation, so that a purely nominal raise would no longer push a taxpayer into a higher rate, with indexing to begin in 1985. Business received faster depreciation. The act was the fiscal signature of the first year. It did not abolish the income tax, the payroll tax, or the spending programs that, together with the recession and the defense buildup, produced the deficits of the decade.

  2. The 1986 tax reform

    The Tax Reform Act of 1986 was a different kind of statute from the 1981 cut: a bargain that lowered rates and widened the base. The top statutory individual rate went to 28 percent, though a phaseout of the benefit of the lower bracket left some upper incomes facing a higher marginal rate across a band. The corporate rate fell from 46 percent to 34 percent. Capital gains were taxed as ordinary income, the investment tax credit ended, and a large number of shelters closed. It was a reform inside the income tax, passed with bipartisan votes, not a repeal of it.

  3. Recession and expansion

    Volcker's tight money, which Reagan retained, produced a severe recession in 1981 and 1982. Unemployment reached 10.8 percent late in 1982, the highest since the Depression. Inflation, which had been running at double-digit rates when Reagan took office, fell into the low single digits and stayed there. A long expansion then began and ran through the rest of his presidency. The sequence was not a gentle one, and he did not dismiss the chairman who had administered the painful half of it.

  4. The controllers' strike

    The Professional Air Traffic Controllers Organization called a strike in August 1981. Federal law forbade the strike. Reagan gave the controllers a short time to return and fired more than eleven thousand who did not. The union was decertified. Supervisors and replacements kept the system operating. Private-sector employers were not given a new statute, but the public signal was that a strike against the federal government would be treated as a firing offense rather than as a negotiation.

  5. The defense buildup

    Carter had already turned defense spending upward after Afghanistan. Reagan enlarged that turn and sustained it: the B-1, the MX missile, ships, readiness, and research on a strategic defense against ballistic missiles announced in 1983. The justification was that the Soviet economy could be strained and that arms agreements should be signed from a stronger position. The federal debt rose sharply while he did it. He did not restore conscription, and he did not take the country out of the alliance the forces were built to serve.

  6. The INF Treaty

    In December 1987 Reagan and Mikhail Gorbachev signed the treaty on intermediate-range nuclear forces, eliminating ground-launched missiles of that class, American and Soviet, with inspection to verify the destruction. The Senate consented. It was the first agreement that abolished an entire category of nuclear delivery systems rather than capping it. The president who had called the Soviet Union an evil empire in 1983 signed it without leaving NATO and without claiming that the rivalry was over. The Soviet state outlived the signing by only a few years.

  7. Iran-Contra

    In 1985 and 1986 the administration sold arms to Iran, then in a war with Iraq and under an American embargo, seeking the release of American hostages held in Lebanon. A portion of the proceeds was diverted to the Nicaraguan contras at a time when statute prohibited the use of appropriated funds for military support of those rebels. Reagan acknowledged the outline of the arms sales and said he had not known of the diversion. A presidential commission, a congressional investigation, and a special prosecutor found a serious breakdown in the management of covert action. Several officials were prosecuted; some convictions were later overturned on appeal; in December 1992 his successor pardoned six former officials.
