He did not abandon the contra policy or the hostility to the Iranian regime. The affair was a failure of control inside his own government.

  8. Sandra Day O'Connor

    Fulfilling a campaign statement that he would name a woman, Reagan nominated Sandra Day O'Connor in 1981, and the Senate confirmed her without a dissenting vote. She was the first woman to sit on the Supreme Court, a conservative formed by a state legislature and a state appellate court, and not the first choice of the social movements that had joined his coalition. He later elevated William Rehnquist to chief justice and appointed Antonin Scalia. The Senate rejected Robert Bork in 1987 and confirmed Anthony Kennedy instead. The Court moved, and it did not become an arm of the religious right. O'Connor's appointment was the one that changed who could sit there.

PreviousJimmy Carter NextChevron and the Independent Counsel

1984–1988

Chevron and the Independent Counsel

Two decisions of Reagan’s years fixed, for a time, how far a court would defer to an agency and whether Congress could create a prosecutor the president does not fully control.

Decision

Chevron U.S.A. v. Natural Resources Defense Council

Decided June 25, 1984

How it was made

The Clean Air Act required permits for new or modified major sources of pollution. The Environmental Protection Agency, under Reagan, allowed a plant to treat all its stacks as one source, a “bubble,” so that an increase at one stack could be offset by a decrease at another. Environmental groups said the statute did not allow it. The question became what a court does when the statute is silent and the agency has chosen.

Who sat

Six to none among those who sat. Stevens wrote. Marshall, Rehnquist, and O’Connor took no part. No dissent was filed.

With the opinion

  • John Paul Stevens — Ford
  • Warren Burger — Nixon
  • William Brennan — Eisenhower
  • Byron White — Kennedy
  • Harry Blackmun — Nixon
  • Lewis Powell — Nixon

Not sitting

  • Thurgood Marshall — L. Johnson
  • William Rehnquist — Nixon; chief justice by Reagan
  • Sandra Day O’Connor — Reagan

What it changed

If Congress has spoken clearly, the agency must obey. If Congress has not, a court accepts the agency’s reading so long as it is reasonable. Administration of statutes passed in outline moved, in practice, to the executive departments, and a change of president could change the reading without a change of law. The decision was unanimous and little noticed. It became the ordinary rule of judicial review of regulations. A later Court, after this volume ends, abandoned it.

Decision

Morrison v. Olson

Decided June 29, 1988

How it was made

After Watergate, Congress provided for an independent counsel, appointed by a special court on the attorney general’s request, to investigate high executive officers. The counsel could be removed by the attorney general only for good cause. Theodore Olson, a former assistant attorney general, argued that the arrangement violated the separation of powers: prosecution is an executive function, and the president must be able to fire those who wield it.

Who sat

Seven to one. Rehnquist wrote. Kennedy took no part. Scalia dissented alone. Reagan’s appointees split: his chief justice and O’Connor in the majority, Scalia against.

With the opinion

  • William Rehnquist — Nixon; chief justice by Reagan
  • William Brennan — Eisenhower
  • Byron White — Kennedy
  • Thurgood Marshall — L. Johnson
  • Harry Blackmun — Nixon
  • John Paul Stevens — Ford
  • Sandra Day O’Connor — Reagan

In dissent

  • Antonin Scalia — Reagan

Not sitting

  • Anthony Kennedy — Reagan

What it changed

Congress may limit the president’s power to remove an inferior prosecutor, if the limitation does not impede his duty to see that the laws are faithfully executed. The independent counsel stood. Investigations of presidents and their cabinets in the following decade, including the one that led to Clinton’s impeachment, rested on this statute and this decision. The statute was later allowed to expire. Scalia’s dissent, that prosecution is executive power and cannot be partly withdrawn, became the position of a later Court on related removal cases. In 1988 it was one vote.

PreviousRonald Reagan NextGeorge H. W. Bush

1989–1993

George H. W. Bush

Republican

Before the office

He was born in 1924 in Milton, Massachusetts, and raised in Greenwich, the son of Prescott Bush, a Wall Street banker who would become a senator, and of Dorothy Walker, who competed at games and did not allow her children to linger on the word I. There was money, a house at Kennebunkport, and Andover. The privilege was not hidden and it was not discussed. The code was duty, a letter written on time, and no scene. He grew up inside an establishment that still believed it would be asked to serve and that the service would not require an explanation of one’s feelings. The code made him trusted. It also left him, later, without a language for a party that wanted its leaders to explain their feelings in public and at length.

On his eighteenth birthday he enlisted in the Navy. He became one of the youngest aviators in the service and flew torpedo bombers off a carrier. In September 1944 he was shot down near Chichi Jima. He was rescued by a submarine. The two men in the plane with him were not. He was twenty. He did not make the loss into a story he told for credit, and he did not forget it. In January 1945 he married Barbara Pierce. After the war he finished Yale in two and a half years, captained the baseball team, and then did the thing his father’s world did not require: he left for West Texas. Midland, the oil business, a company of his own. He wanted a life that was not only an inheritance. In 1953 their daughter Robin died of leukemia. She was three. Barbara’s hair went white. He was often away, selling, drilling, running. The grief stayed in the house. The family’s method was to bear it and continue, which is a form of strength and also a way of not letting anyone, including the children, all the way in.

Texas Republicans were a minority, and he became one of their organizers. He lost a Senate race in the Goldwater year. Houston sent him to the House in 1966. He voted for the fair-housing law and took the anger of a district that had not sent him there to do that. He lost a second Senate race, to Lloyd Bentsen, in 1970. What followed was the career of a man other men trust with an errand: ambassador to the United Nations, chairman of the Republican National Committee while Nixon came apart, the unofficial envoy to China, director of Central Intelligence. At the committee he reached the conclusion the tapes required and told Nixon it was time to resign. He was good at all of it. None of it was a constituency. Meacham’s odyssey is a man who kept being given the hard job because he would do it without a manifesto, and who kept not having a public of his own when the job ended.

In 1980 he ran for president and called the tax-cutting faith voodoo economics. The phrase was his father’s arithmetic. The party was no longer his father’s party. Reagan beat him, and he took the vice presidency and served eight years without an independent note. He went to the funerals. He did not correct the president. The loyalty was real, and it was also the price of inheritance. He had described the program as voodoo and then became the program’s most faithful subordinate. When his own turn came he could not run as the man who had said it. He had to run as the man who had sat beside it.

The 1988 campaign was harder than he was. His managers used a furlough and a fight about the pledge of allegiance, and he let them. In New Orleans he gave the coalition the sentence it wanted. Read my lips: no new taxes. Four words, and they were not the words of a Greenwich banker who thought a budget had to close. They were the words of a party that had made the tax pledge a test of belonging. He won. He did not become a movement conservative by winning. He remained what the résumé had made: a foreign-policy hand, a manager, a man who would sign a disability law and a clean-air law with Democrats and who thought the Cold War, if it ended, should end without a boast that broke the alliances. The sentence from New Orleans was already the part that did not fit.

He took the oath as the last president formed entirely by the old establishment’s idea of the office: a war, a fortune made somewhere else, a series of jobs done quietly, and a suspicion of the personal pronoun. He had Reagan’s party and not Reagan’s gift. He had a promise on taxes that his own judgment did not believe. The judgment would reassert itself in the 1990 budget, and the promise would be what the coalition remembered. The life up to the oath is the explanation of both facts. He had been trained to correct a mistake without a speech. He had just been elected by people who thought the speech was the contract.

In the office

Bush managed the end of the Cold War without leaving NATO, and without any project to abolish private property, cancel elections, or establish a national church. The changes of degree were nonetheless concrete: a war that restored Kuwait and stopped there, the Americans with Disabilities Act, stronger clean-air rules, and a 1990 budget that raised taxes after a pledge of no new taxes.

The party

Bush inherited Reagan's party and conducted it in a managerial key: foreign-policy experience, bipartisan statutes on disability and clean air, and no desire to reopen the basic economic settlement. The religious right and the tax revolt remained inside the coalition rather than being his personal causes. He broke with the coalition's tax commitment when the 1990 budget agreement raised rates after he had pledged, in the 1988 campaign, that there would be no new taxes. Conservative opposition to that bargain, not a conversion to a Democratic program, split his party and weakened him for 1992.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    The budget reconciliation of 1990, negotiated with a Democratic Congress, raised the top income-tax rate from 28 percent to 31 percent, raised other taxes, and set caps on discretionary spending and a pay-as-you-go rule for new entitlements and tax cuts. A large bloc of Republicans in the House voted against their own president. The deficit stayed heavy through the recession; the caps shaped the next decade more quietly than the broken pledge, which was what damaged him in his party.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    He was a free trader of the postwar Republican kind. He carried the Uruguay Round forward and negotiated the North American Free Trade Agreement, which he signed in December 1992 and left for the next Congress to implement. A general tariff increase was not his answer to the recession.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    Alan Greenspan remained at the Federal Reserve. The recession of 1990 and 1991 was relatively short, and employment recovered slowly enough that the lag dominated the election of 1992. The thrift-reform statute of 1989 created the Resolution Trust Corporation and rewrote savings-and-loan supervision, which was the concrete banking legacy of the term.

  4. 4. Labor

    No labor-relations statute on the scale of the Wagner Act or Taft-Hartley was passed. Unemployment rose in the recession and stayed politically salient after output began to grow again. He vetoed one minimum-wage bill and signed another in 1989, the first statutory increase since the schedule enacted in the late 1970s had run out.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    The Immigration Act of 1990 raised the ceilings on legal immigration, reweighted the preference system toward skills as well as family, and created a diversity category. It was an expansion of lawful admission, not an enforcement statute, and it did not revisit the legalization of 1986. Refugee admissions continued under the 1980 framework.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    He vetoed a civil-rights bill in 1990 as a quota measure and signed the Civil Rights Act of 1991, which restored and clarified disparate-impact suits after a run of Supreme Court decisions. Clarence Thomas succeeded Thurgood Marshall after hearings dominated by Anita Hill's accusation of sexual harassment, which Thomas denied. The larger civil-rights statute of the term was about disability, not race, and it was his.

  7. 7. Federalism

    The Americans with Disabilities Act was a national rule for private employers and public accommodations, in the line of the Civil Rights Act of 1964, not a return of authority to the states. Complaints about unfunded mandates grew and were not answered with a constitutional amendment. He administered the federal system he had inherited rather than offering a theory of a smaller one.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    The Berlin Wall fell in his first year and Germany reunified inside NATO; he condemned the killing of protesters in Beijing in June 1989 without severing relations; and the Soviet Union dissolved in December 1991, which he handled chiefly as a question of nuclear custody and stable borders. Iraq's seizure of Kuwait produced a coalition war, authorized by the Security Council and by Congress, that freed Kuwait early in 1991 and did not go on to occupy Iraq or remove its government. He sent forces into Panama in December 1989 to seize Manuel Noriega, and he kept NATO after the rival it had been built to deter had ceased to exist.

  9. 9. National defense

    Defense budgets turned down as the Soviet threat receded, and base-closing rounds began to shrink the Cold War map of installations. The Gulf War was fought by the volunteer force, with a large coalition and a short ground campaign. In 1991 he announced unilateral withdrawals of many tactical nuclear weapons and signed a strategic-arms reduction treaty as the Soviet state approached its end.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    He courted the religious right without belonging to it, and the 1992 convention showed how firmly that wing now claimed a share of the party. He maintained the existing restrictions on foreign-aid funds connected to abortion and accepted platform language favoring a constitutional amendment. No such amendment passed, and he founded no religious establishment.

  11. 11. Education

    He called himself an education president and in 1989 brought the governors together at Charlottesville to set national goals. The goals were aspirations, not a new federal power to operate schools. The Department of Education stayed.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    The Oil Pollution Act of 1990 followed the Exxon Valdez spill and tightened liability and prevention for tanker oil. On the public lands he was less confrontational than Reagan's first interior secretary, and he opened no era of disposal. The fight in the Northwest over old-growth timber and the spotted owl was the period's species-and-land conflict, and it was not resolved by giving the forests to the states.

  13. 13. Energy

    The Energy Policy Act of 1992 encouraged efficiency, alternative fuels, and a limited opening for independent wholesale power, and it did not revive a synthetic-fuels corporation. He went to the Rio conference and signed the framework climate convention while resisting binding targets and timetables for emissions. The clean-air amendments, not a production program, were the environmental statute that changed behavior.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    He asked for a crime bill with a broader federal death penalty and tighter limits on habeas corpus, and he did not get it in the form he wanted. The statute that eventually did much of that work was signed by his successor. He continued the drug-enforcement apparatus he had inherited, including the White House drug office created at the end of the Reagan years, and ordinary criminal justice stayed local.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    The recession increased poverty and joblessness well into a slow recovery, and he signed extensions of unemployment benefits. He did not rewrite Aid to Families with Dependent Children. He preferred voluntary service as a complement to the federal safety net, which he left standing.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    He signed the Ryan White CARE Act in 1990, the first large federal statute aimed at care for people with HIV, and he signed the repeal of the Medicare catastrophic benefit Reagan had enacted. There was no national health-insurance plan. Medicare and Medicaid continued.

Of this presidency

  1. No new taxes

    In accepting the Republican nomination in 1988, Bush pledged that there would be no new taxes. The 1990 budget agreement broke the pledge. Negotiated with Democratic leaders of Congress, it raised the top marginal income-tax rate, the gasoline tax, and other levies, and it installed discretionary caps and a pay-as-you-go rule. Newt Gingrich led House Republicans against the bargain; a first version failed, and a revised bill passed with more Democratic than Republican votes in the House. Bush signed it. The rule-making outlived the argument. The argument ended his chance of holding the Reagan coalition together without a grievance at its center.

  2. The Gulf War

    Iraq invaded and annexed Kuwait in August 1990. Bush assembled a coalition, obtained a Security Council authorization to use force, and in January 1991 obtained a congressional authorization. It was not a declaration of war. The air campaign and a ground campaign of about a hundred hours liberated Kuwait in February. The coalition did not march on Baghdad and did not make the removal of Saddam Hussein a war aim. Uprisings inside Iraq that followed the cease-fire were not given American ground armies; constraints on the regime, including limits on its flights, came afterward and lasted into the next two presidencies. The war was fought for a restitution, and the restitution was the end of it.

  3. The end of the Soviet Union

    Bush's public response to the fall of the Berlin Wall in November 1989 was deliberately untriumphal. He worked to keep a reunified Germany inside NATO, negotiated with Gorbachev, and treated the failed coup of August 1991 as a reason to deal with the republics that were separating rather than as a spectacle. The Soviet Union dissolved in December 1991. He focused on the custody of nuclear weapons and on recognition that would not invite chaos. A strategic-arms treaty had been signed that July. He did not dismantle NATO because its original enemy had disappeared, and he did not claim a mandate to reorganize the domestic settlement in the name of a peace dividend.

  4. The Americans with Disabilities Act

    Signed in July 1990, the Americans with Disabilities Act forbade discrimination against qualified people with disabilities in employment, public services, and public accommodations, and it required reasonable accommodation and access. It was built on the model of the earlier civil-rights statutes: a national rule reaching private conduct, not a means-tested benefit and not a program run by the states at their option. Support in Congress was broad. It was the domestic statute of the term most clearly in the line of 1964, and he treated it as such.

  5. The Clean Air Act amendments

    The Clean Air Act amendments of 1990 strengthened controls on urban smog, toxic air pollutants, and the chemicals depleting stratospheric ozone. The acid-rain title capped sulfur-dioxide emissions from power plants and allowed trading in allowances, a market inside a regulatory limit. Emissions covered by the cap fell. The statute did not nationalize the utilities. It is the clearest case, in this presidency, of a large environmental rule adopted by bargain rather than by crisis rhetoric alone, and Bush had said he wanted it.

PreviousChevron and the Independent Counsel NextCasey and the Twenty-seventh Amendment

1992

Casey and the Twenty-seventh Amendment

In one year the Court declined to overrule Roe and restated it, and a proposal James Madison had sent to the states in 1789 became the last amendment in this volume.

Decision

Planned Parenthood v. Casey

Decided June 29, 1992

How it was made

Pennsylvania required a waiting period, parental consent for a minor, and spousal notice before an abortion. Clinics sued. The United States, under the Bush administration, asked the Court to overrule Roe. The Court reheard the shape of the right rather than the facts of one clinic.

Who sat

Five to four against overruling Roe. O’Connor, Kennedy, and Souter wrote jointly and controlled the result. Blackmun and Stevens joined them on that point and would have kept more of Roe. Rehnquist, White, Scalia, and Thomas would have overruled it. Two Reagan appointees and Bush’s Souter supplied the votes that kept the right. Bush’s Thomas and Reagan’s Scalia were on the other side.

With the opinion

  • Sandra Day O’Connor — Reagan
  • Anthony Kennedy — Reagan
  • David Souter — G. H. W. Bush
  • Harry Blackmun — Nixon
  • John Paul Stevens — Ford

In dissent

  • William Rehnquist — Nixon; chief justice by Reagan
  • Byron White — Kennedy
  • Antonin Scalia — Reagan
  • Clarence Thomas — G. H. W. Bush

What it changed

The right to end a pregnancy before viability survived. The trimester rules of Roe did not. A state regulation stands unless it places an undue burden on the woman. Pennsylvania’s waiting period and parental consent stood. Spousal notice fell. The Court said it was preserving Roe because the country had ordered itself on the decision, and because the Court should not discard such a holding under political pressure. Four justices answered that a wrong constitutional decision should be overruled. The law of abortion from 1992 until after this volume is Casey’s undue-burden test, not Blackmun’s original opinion. Justices appointed to move the Court did not all move it.

Amendment

The Twenty-seventh Amendment

Ratified May 7, 1992

How it was made

Madison’s compensation article, proposed with the Bill of Rights on September 25, 1789, says that no law varying the pay of senators and representatives shall take effect until an election of representatives has intervened. It sat without a deadline. A ratification campaign in the states, long after anyone who had voted for it was dead, reached three-fourths in 1992. The archivist certified it. Congress then recognized it.

What it changed

A sitting Congress cannot raise its own pay and collect the raise. The next Congress can. The governmental change is small beside the Sixteenth Amendment or the Fourteenth. The constitutional fact is not small: an article proposed in the first Congress became law in the 102nd, with no new proposal. Article V does not, by its words, require that ratification be prompt. This amendment is the proof.

PreviousGeorge H. W. Bush NextBill Clinton

1993–2001

Bill Clinton

Democrat

Before the office

He was born in 1946 in Hope, Arkansas, and the name on the certificate was William Jefferson Blythe III. His father had died in a car wreck three months earlier. His mother, Virginia, was a nurse, vivid, and not interested in being defeated by a town or a marriage. He lived for a time with his grandparents, then in Hot Springs after Virginia married Roger Clinton, a car dealer who drank and who hit her. Bill took the Clinton name. As a teenager he was big enough to make Roger stop. The household was charm and wreckage in the same rooms. He became the child who could talk a stranger into liking him, because liking was a kind of safety, and he never afterward trusted a room he had not won.

Hot Springs noticed the saxophonist and the boy who remembered every name. In 1963 Boys Nation sent him to Washington, and he shook John Kennedy’s hand in the Rose Garden. The photograph became the story he told about his own future, and it was not a false photograph. Georgetown made him class president. He worked for Senator Fulbright. Oxford took him as a Rhodes Scholar in the year the draft was the question for every young man who wanted a public life and did not want Vietnam. He managed a deferment. He wrote a letter to the colonel in charge of his draft board that opposed the war, thanked him, and kept the door from closing. He did not serve. The letter is the early Clinton on paper: ambitious, eloquent about his own motives, and confident the record could be arranged. It could, until someone else found the letter.

At Yale he met Hillary Rodham, who was more disciplined than he was and at least as ambitious. They married in 1975. She had the credentials to stay in Washington or New York. She went to Arkansas, which was the concession on which the partnership was built. He taught law, became attorney general, and in 1978, at thirty-two, was elected governor. The first term was what happens to a man who has not yet lost. A tax on car licenses, Cuban refugees the Carter administration sent to Fort Chaffee, a manner the state read as conceit. Frank White beat him in 1980. He apologized, which he could do with more skill than almost any politician of his generation, and the state took him back in 1982. The comeback was the political education. With Hillary he pushed an education reform through a legislature that did not like being improved. He learned to sound like a Democrat who would require something of people, not only provide it. He did not learn to be faithful. The marriage was a working alliance of two political minds, and he broke the private half of it more than once. She knew enough, and she stayed. The staying was a decision.

The national party, after Mondale and Dukakis, was looking for a Southerner who would not run as the heir of 1972. The Democratic Leadership Council found him, or he found it. He argued that the party had lost because it sounded as if it would tax, excuse, and explain, and that voters who worked and paid the bills had left for Reagan for a reason. Welfare should have a limit. Crime should be punished. Trade should be opened. The deficit should come down. The people who, in his phrase, worked hard and played by the rules should be the ones the program was for. He was not proposing to repeal the civil-rights laws or Medicare. He was proposing that his own party stop defending the pieces of the liberal program, especially an open-ended welfare check, that those voters had already rejected. A large part of the party still wanted to defend them. He intended to be nominated anyway.

In 1992 the campaign nearly ended before it began. A singer said she had been his lover, and the draft letter surfaced, and for a week he was finished. Then he talked his way back onto the stage, and Bush’s recession and Ross Perot’s protest did the rest. The sign in the headquarters said it was the economy. He promised to end welfare as the country knew it, to cut the deficit, and to insure people who worked. He won with forty-three percent of the vote and treated the plurality as permission to move the center. Maney’s new Gilded Age president and Harris’s survivor are the same character, seen from the years that followed: the most gifted politician of his cohort, and a man whose carelessness was not an accident of office. The carelessness was Hope, the letter to the colonel, and the apologies that had always worked in Arkansas.

He took the oath saying there was nothing wrong with America that could not be cured by what was right with America. The cheer was sincere, and it was also the instrument. Under it sat the bargain he had tried out for a decade in Little Rock. More opportunity, more responsibility. A tax increase at the top and a claim that the budget could be brought back to balance. A trade agreement the unions would hate. A welfare bill that would end the entitlement his own party had written. He had not come to build a third Great Society, and he had not come to dismantle the one he inherited. He had come to drag the Democrats off the positions that had lost three elections out of four. The impeachment was not yet a thought in the Capitol. The man who would have to survive it was already complete, including the part of him that would make the survival necessary.

In the office

Clinton widened trade and used NATO in combat rather than walking away from either the commercial order or the alliance, and he did not nationalize industry or suspend elections. The domestic changes of degree were a crime statute of wide reach, a welfare reform that ended the cash entitlement, and a health-care reconstruction that failed, followed late by federal budgets in surplus. The House impeached him and the Senate acquitted him, and he finished the term.

The party

Clinton pulled the Democrats toward welfare time limits, a crime bill, the North American trade agreement, and budget balance, the package associated with the New Democrats, and away from the unified opposition many in the party still preferred. Labor and much of the House caucus resisted the trade turn and lost. He kept the civil-rights and abortion-rights coalition, and after the Republican sweep of 1994 he spent more of his effort defending Medicare, Medicaid, and education spending than extending the tax increase of 1993. The party changed its program on welfare, crime, and trade without abandoning the electorate the rights revolutions had given it.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    His 1993 budget, passed with Democratic votes only and the vice president's tie-breaking vote in the Senate, raised the top individual rate to 39.6 percent and expanded the earned-income credit. After 1994 he fought and then bargained with a Republican Congress, including through two shutdowns of the federal government in 1995 and 1996; the 1997 balanced-budget agreement cut the capital-gains rate and created a child credit while trimming projected Medicare growth. The unified budget moved into surplus in the last fiscal years of the term, the first surpluses since 1969, and a statutory line-item veto he had been given was struck down by the Supreme Court as unconstitutional.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    The North American Free Trade Agreement took effect on the first day of 1994, after he added side agreements on labor and the environment and assembled a majority that depended on Republicans against most Democrats and against organized labor. The World Trade Organization replaced the old trade framework in 1995 with his support. In 2000 he won permanent normal trade relations with China, the step that accompanied China's entry into the world trade body.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    Alan Greenspan remained at the Federal Reserve, and Clinton reappointed him. When Congress would not appropriate a Mexican rescue in 1995, the Treasury used the Exchange Stabilization Fund to backstop the peso. He signed the 1999 act that repealed the barriers between commercial and investment banking dating from the 1930s, and a 2000 statute that left most over-the-counter derivatives outside the commodity-exchange rules.

  4. 4. Labor

    The Family and Medical Leave Act of 1993 required unpaid leave for specified family and medical reasons at larger employers, the labor statute of the term that passed. A ban on the permanent replacement of strikers failed. The trade agreements with Mexico, Canada, and China went through over the opposition of industrial unions, and the share of private workers represented by unions kept falling.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    The illegal-immigration statute of 1996 broadened removal, created expedited procedures at the border, and restricted judicial review and some benefits for noncitizens. It contained no broad legalization. The legal-immigration levels set in 1990 largely continued, and the argument over enforcement was not settled.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    He defended affirmative action in a narrowed form after the Court tightened the standard, and he appointed a cabinet and a judiciary more diverse than those of his predecessors. The crime bill and the welfare law were not written as race statutes; both operated on problems that were racially concentrated, and he defended the signatures on the merits he claimed for them. He offered no new voting-rights act of the 1965 kind.

  7. 7. Federalism

    Welfare reform was the real devolution: a block grant in place of an individual entitlement, with states designing programs inside federal time limits and work rules. The crime bill ran the other direction, adding federal offenses and federal money for local police. He signed a procedural reform of unfunded mandates, and the Court, not the White House, supplied the period's modest constitutional limits on the commerce power.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    He inherited a mission in Somalia and withdrew American forces after the fighting in Mogadishu in October 1993. He used the threat of invasion to restore Haiti's elected president, and after hesitation he backed NATO bombing in Bosnia that led to the Dayton accords and an American share of the ensuing force. In 1999 the alliance admitted Poland, Hungary, and the Czech Republic and fought an air war over Kosovo without a Security Council mandate or an American ground invasion; attacks on embassies in East Africa and on a warship in Yemen were answered with limited strikes, not with an occupation.

  9. 9. National defense

    The post-Cold War drawdown in forces and budgets continued and then leveled, and he used air power more readily than ground armies. Congress wrote into law an exclusion of openly homosexual service, and the administration's accompanying policy, called don't ask, don't tell, instructed the services not to ask recruits about orientation and not to hunt cases without evidence. NATO's campaign in Kosovo was the alliance's first sustained combat operation, and it did not become a permanent American occupation.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    He reversed executive limits on abortion counseling in federally funded clinics and vetoed federal bans on a late-term procedure, and he signed both a religious-freedom statute in 1993 and the Defense of Marriage Act in 1996. The welfare law's charitable-choice provision let faith-based organizations compete to deliver services, a modest opening his successor widened. He proposed no religious establishment, and the religious right was part of the opposition, not of his coalition.

  11. 11. Education

    A 1994 goals statute and the reauthorization of the main school-aid law put federal money behind state standards. The 1997 budget agreement created tax credits for college tuition. He encouraged charter schools and did not nationalize the curriculum or obtain the national examination he would have preferred, and the Department of Education he inherited gained programs and stayed.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    He used the Antiquities Act to designate large national monuments, among them Grand Staircase-Escalante in Utah, over the opposition of that state's political leadership. A rule restricting roads in national forests was issued as he left office and was contested afterward. He did not sell the public domain.

  13. 13. Energy

    There was no return to a synthetic-fuels program or to an energy statute on the scale of the late 1970s. Gasoline was relatively cheap for much of the decade, and electricity restructuring was left mostly to the states, with California's crisis arriving at the end of the term. He signed the Kyoto protocol on climate in 1998 and never submitted it to the Senate, which in 1997 had voted 95 to 0 against binding targets that spared developing countries and imposed serious costs at home.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    The crime bill of 1994 funded local police hiring and state prison construction, extended the federal death penalty, carried the Violence Against Women Act and a ten-year ban on defined semiautomatic weapons, and added a federal three-strikes rule. He also signed a 1993 law requiring background checks for firearm purchases from licensed dealers and a 1996 limit on successive petitions for habeas corpus. Violent crime fell through the decade; the statutes were part of the period and not a demonstrated sole cause.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    The welfare statute of 1996 ended Aid to Families with Dependent Children as an open entitlement and substituted a block grant, time limits, and work requirements; he had vetoed two earlier Republican versions and signed the third. Benefits for many legal immigrants were cut and later partly restored, and able-bodied adults without dependents faced new time limits in the food-stamp program. Caseloads fell sharply in a strong labor market, the expanded earned-income credit became a major support for low-wage workers, and Medicaid survived.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    The health plan of 1993 and 1994, developed under a task force led by Hillary Clinton, proposed universal coverage through regional purchasing alliances and an employer mandate, and it never reached a floor vote. What passed afterward was incremental: a 1996 statute on portability and preexisting conditions in group coverage, a children's insurance program in 1997, and private-plan options inside Medicare. Medicare still had no general outpatient drug benefit when he left.

Of this presidency

  1. NAFTA

    George Bush had signed the North American Free Trade Agreement in December 1992. Clinton negotiated side agreements on labor and the environment and sent the package to Congress, where most Democrats voted no and a Republican majority of the yes votes carried it. It took effect on January 1, 1994. Tariffs among the United States, Canada, and Mexico were phased out inside rules of origin, dispute settlement, and investment protection. Organized labor treated the vote as a break with the party. The agreement did not create a common government, a common currency, or free movement of people. It was a commercial enlargement of the order he had inherited.

  2. The crime bill

    The Violent Crime Control and Law Enforcement Act of 1994 was a compendium: grants meant to support on the order of one hundred thousand local police officers, incentives for states that adopted truth-in-sentencing rules and built prisons, the Violence Against Women Act, a ten-year ban on specified assault weapons and high-capacity magazines, a federal three-strikes provision, and a long list of new or expanded federal capital offenses. Republicans supplied votes, and so did many Democrats. Crime rates, already near a peak, fell for the rest of the decade. He claimed the bill as part of that fall. The fall had more causes than one statute, and the statute remained the federal crime policy of his presidency.

  3. Welfare reform

    The Personal Responsibility and Work Opportunity Reconciliation Act of 1996 replaced Aid to Families with Dependent Children with Temporary Assistance for Needy Families, a fixed block grant to the states. The general rule was work, and a general limit of five years of federally funded cash aid over a lifetime, with states free to be stricter. Clinton vetoed two earlier bills and signed this one, saying the system had failed and that the changes would not abandon children. Caseloads dropped sharply in the boom that followed. Medicaid was not block-granted. The entitlement to cash welfare, as it had existed since the 1930s, ended.

  4. The health-care plan

    In 1993 the administration proposed universal coverage built on regional health alliances, a standard benefit, an employer mandate, and limits on the growth of premiums. Hillary Clinton chaired the task force that produced it. Insurers, small businesses, and members of both parties attacked the complexity and the mandate. Democratic leaders never brought a bill to the floor, and alternative bills died as well. The failure taught the next Democratic president to pass a narrower reconstruction through the existing insurance market rather than through a single new structure. In Clinton's own term the substitutes were portability rules and coverage for children, not universality.

  5. Balanced budgets

    The 1993 increase in the top rates and the 1997 agreement with the Republican Congress, together with a long expansion and rising capital-gains revenue, produced unified-budget surpluses in the closing fiscal years of the administration, the first since 1969. He opposed a constitutional amendment that would have required balance. A statutory line-item veto Congress had given him was held unconstitutional in 1998. The surpluses were real in the accounts of those years, and they were not a permanent change in the structure of entitlements. Defense, Social Security, and Medicare were all still there when the accounts went into the black.

  6. Impeachment and acquittal

    The House impeached Clinton in December 1998 on two articles, perjury and obstruction of justice. The charges arose from false testimony about a sexual relationship with a White House intern, given in a deposition in a civil case and before a grand jury. Two other articles failed. In February 1999 the Senate acquitted him; neither article received even a majority, far short of the two-thirds required to remove a president. He remained in office and finished the term. The episode punished him and did not rearrange the powers of the office.

  7. Kosovo

    In March 1999 NATO began bombing the Federal Republic of Yugoslavia over Kosovo. There was no Security Council authorization, Russia and China being opposed, and no congressional authorization comparable to the Gulf War resolution. There was also no commitment of an American ground army to the fighting. After eleven weeks Serbian forces withdrew, and an international presence, including American troops, entered. It was the first time NATO had gone to war, and it was a war of air power for a limited political end. He did not treat it as a reason to rewrite the alliance or to leave it.

  8. Don't ask, don't tell

    Clinton had said homosexuals should be allowed to serve openly in the armed forces. The Joint Chiefs and a Democratic Congress refused that result. The compromise enacted in the 1994 defense authorization kept a statutory bar: service members were to be separated for homosexual acts or for a statement of homosexual orientation. The administration's policy, known as don't ask, don't tell, told the services not to ask and not to open an investigation without cause. It was a truce, not an end to the exclusion. Open service remained forbidden until a repeal signed in a later presidency.

PreviousCasey and the Twenty-seventh Amendment NextBush v. Gore

2000

Bush v. Gore

The election of 2000 did not produce a president on election night. Five weeks later the Court stopped a recount in Florida. The decision sits between the two presidencies it chose between.

Decision

Bush v. Gore

Decided December 12, 2000

How it was made

The Florida Supreme Court had ordered a recount of undervotes, ballots a machine had not read as a vote, under a standard of the voter’s intent. George W. Bush, ahead by a few hundred votes, asked the Supreme Court to stop it. The case came up on an emergency application and was decided the night after argument.

Who sat

Five to four on the result that ended the recount. The per curiam opinion held that the recount lacked a uniform standard and so denied equal protection, and that no constitutional recount could be finished by the date Florida law set. Rehnquist, Scalia, and Thomas also concurred on a separate ground, that the Florida court had rewritten state election law. Souter and Breyer agreed there was an equal-protection problem and dissented from the refusal to let a recount continue. Stevens and Ginsburg dissented more broadly. Bush’s father had appointed Thomas, who was in the majority, and Souter, who dissented.

With the opinion

  • William Rehnquist — Nixon; chief justice by Reagan
  • Sandra Day O’Connor — Reagan
  • Antonin Scalia — Reagan
  • Anthony Kennedy — Reagan
  • Clarence Thomas — G. H. W. Bush

In dissent

  • John Paul Stevens — Ford
  • David Souter — G. H. W. Bush
  • Ruth Bader Ginsburg — Clinton
  • Stephen Breyer — Clinton

What it changed

The recount stopped. Florida’s electoral votes went to George W. Bush, and he became president. The Court said its consideration was limited to the circumstances before it. No statute was struck in a way that rewrote election law for the next cycle. What changed was the occupant of the office, chosen in a lawsuit rather than by a completed count. Five justices, all appointed by Republican presidents, formed the majority. Four, including one appointed by the candidate’s father, dissented. The appointments were the Court. The Court was, for one night, the returning board.

PreviousBill Clinton NextGeorge W. Bush

2001–2009

George W. Bush

Republican

Before the office

He was born in 1946 in New Haven, while his father was finishing Yale, and he grew up in Midland and then Houston, the eldest son of a man who had been shot down in the Pacific and did not talk about it. The household rule was still Dorothy Bush’s: compete, do not brag, do not say I. Andover took him and he became the boy who was funny, a decent athlete, a cheerleader, not the scholar the name seemed to require. Yale, class of 1968, put him in Skull and Bones as it had put his father, and in a fraternity he enjoyed more. His grades were ordinary. He drank. The family had a way of producing sons who looked like a résumé. He looked, for a long time, like the one who would not write one.

He joined the Texas Air National Guard and learned to fly. He did not go to Vietnam. Whether he completed every obligation of that service was a fight for later; what was clear at the time was that he was not his father’s aviator and he was not a man who had refused to serve at all. Harvard gave him a business degree, the first president who would have one. He went back to Midland to look for oil. The companies, Arbusto and the ones that followed, did not make him. Family friends and his father’s name kept them from being simple failures. In 1977 he married Laura Welch, a librarian, quiet where he was noisy, and the marriage was the steady fact of the rest of his life. Twin daughters were born in 1981. The drinking did not get better. It got worse, into the years when a man is supposed to have become what he is going to be.

He dated the change to a weekend with Billy Graham at his parents’ house in Maine, and to his fortieth birthday, in 1986, when he woke up from a celebration and quit. He stopped. He became a regular at Bible study, a Methodist who would talk about faith in a personal way his father never managed and never wanted to manage. The jokes remained. The discipline was the new thing, and it was what he trusted: he had been the family disappointment, and he had decided not to be. People who had known the drinking were more impressed than people who met him later and thought the certainty was arrogance. He was not a theologian. He thought a changed life was a kind of evidence, and he was willing to offer the evidence in public.

Baseball gave him a success that was not his father’s. He helped put together the group that bought the Texas Rangers, became the partner the city saw, and the new ballpark and the later sale made him rich in his own right and known in Texas as something other than Junior. In 1994 he ran for governor against Ann Richards, who treated him as the son with the silver spoon. He beat her. She had been popular. He was sunny, repetitive, and specific about schools, juvenile crime, and lawsuits. In Austin he worked with Democrats, because he had to and because he liked the work better than his father’s party war. The education statutes, built on testing and accountability, were what he claimed. He cut taxes. He talked about churches and charities doing what bureaucracies did badly. Compassionate conservatism was the name for a Republicanism that would not frighten the suburbs and would not repeat 1990. He had watched the right leave his father over a tax increase. He was not going to hand them the same reason.

The nomination in 2000 required a harder fight, against John McCain, than Texas had required. He won it, and he chose Dick Cheney, who had run the Pentagon for his father, to carry the experience he did not have. Baker’s book is the history of that partnership after it had power. The choice itself was made before the oath, by a candidate who knew the résumé was thin and who wanted the weight in the next office rather than in his own past. The election against Gore was a tie in everything but the lawsuits. He lost the popular vote. Florida was a recount and then a Supreme Court that stopped it. He became president with half the country convinced the office had been awarded. He had spent the campaign calling himself a uniter. The count made the word sound like a taunt.

He took the oath and said that compassion is the work of a nation, not just a government. He did not deny the government its jobs in schools, safety, and civil rights. He said the deeper hurts answer to a mentor or a pastor, and that churches and charities would have a place in the plans. That was Austin, carried up. Reagan’s party on taxes and judges, a religious voice his father had never been able to use, and a promise, unspoken but understood by anyone who remembered 1990, that this Bush would not raise taxes in order to be responsible. He had not come to repeal the insurance state or to manage it in Greenwich’s quiet. He had come to cut the rates, test the schools, and prove the son was not the heir who would break a pledge. September was eight months away. Nothing in Midland had been a preparation for it, except the habit, practiced since the birthday he quit, of deciding and not looking back.

In the office

Bush did not discard elections, private ownership, or NATO. After the attacks of September 11, 2001, he built a homeland-security state the country had not had and invaded Iraq on a public case about weapons that later inspection did not bear out, while the Afghan war, begun when NATO invoked collective defense, outlasted his time in office. At home he cut tax rates and added a drug benefit to Medicare, and in 2008 he asked Congress for emergency authority to stabilize the credit system.

The party

Bush's Republicans kept the Reagan commitments on taxes, judges, and abortion, and they added three things the party of the 1980s had not made central: a Medicare drug benefit, a large federal role in school testing under the name of compassionate conservatism, and, after September 11, a war on terror that reorganized domestic security as well as foreign policy. Spending grew. The coalition still rested on white evangelicals, Southern and rural voters, and business, and it accepted a new entitlement when the benefit was drugs delivered through private plans. The financial rescue of 2008 was an emergency rather than a conversion, and it showed how far a Republican administration would go to keep the credit system operating.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    Tax cuts in 2001 and 2003 lowered income-tax rates, created a 10 percent bracket, reduced the tax on dividends and capital gains, and phased down the estate tax, with expiration dates written in because the bills moved under reconciliation rules. The surpluses of the late 1990s disappeared into deficits as those cuts, the recession of 2001, and war and security spending compounded. In October 2008 he signed an emergency stabilization act authorizing up to seven hundred billion dollars, used largely to inject capital into banks, much of it later repaid, and also to assist an insurer and the automobile companies.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    He regained trade-promotion authority and completed agreements with Singapore, Chile, Australia, and the countries of Central America and the Dominican Republic, among others. A safeguard tariff on steel, imposed in 2002, was withdrawn in 2003 after adverse rulings and the threat of retaliation. He did not leave the North American agreement or the World Trade Organization, and the Doha round of global talks did not conclude.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    Alan Greenspan and then Ben Bernanke cut short-term rates after the 2001 recession and raised them during a housing boom fed by mortgage credit and securitization. In 2008 Bear Stearns was sold with Federal Reserve assistance, Lehman Brothers failed, the insurer AIG was rescued, and money-market funds were given a temporary guarantee. He did not amend the Federal Reserve Act; the response used existing emergency powers and the new appropriation from Congress.

  4. 4. Labor

    He invoked Taft-Hartley in 2002 to reopen West Coast ports during a lockout. He opposed a bill that would have let unions win recognition by card check, and it did not pass. Private-sector union density continued to fall, and the heavy job losses of the financial crisis arrived at the end of the term and ran into the next.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    He wanted a comprehensive bill pairing enforcement with a guest-worker program and a path to legal status for many people already present. It failed in the Senate in 2007, with a large part of the opposition inside his own party. He signed a fence statute and increased removals, and he left the underlying immigration law unreconciled.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    He made no attempt to repeal the civil-rights statutes. Colin Powell and then Condoleezza Rice served as secretary of state, and Alberto Gonzales as attorney general, and the school law required districts to publish achievement by subgroup, including race. The failure of evacuation and relief after Hurricane Katrina in 2005 fell hardest on poor and Black residents of New Orleans and became a judgment on federal, state, and local competence together.

  7. 7. Federalism

    The school-testing law and the homeland-security reorganization pulled more conditions and more agencies toward Washington, even while policing and the daily operation of schools stayed local. He signed a statute setting federal standards for state driver's licenses, and he supported a constitutional amendment to define marriage nationally, which failed. In the Schiavo case he signed a one-time act putting a single state guardianship dispute into the federal courts.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    The September 11 attacks were followed by an invasion of Afghanistan, the only occasion on which NATO has invoked the collective-defense article, and by an invasion of Iraq in March 2003. The public case for Iraq rested in significant part on active stockpiles of chemical and biological weapons and a reconstituting nuclear program, and on ties to terrorism; inspectors had not established the stockpiles before the invasion, and the Iraq Survey Group did not find them afterward. He did not leave NATO, and the Iraq occupation, including a troop increase he ordered in 2007, was unfinished when he left office.

  9. 9. National defense

    Defense spending rose with the two wars, and a new Northern Command was given responsibility for the defense of the continental United States. Detention at Guantanamo and trial by military commission were revised after the Supreme Court insisted on a statutory footing, and coercive interrogation, including waterboarding of a small number of detainees in secret custody, was authorized early and was not in use at the end of the term. He did not restore a draft, and the intelligence reorganization that followed the commission on the September 11 attacks created a director of national intelligence over the existing agencies.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    White evangelical Protestants were a foundation of his electorate, and an office for faith-based initiatives invited religious charities into competition for social-service funds without creating a church. He limited federal funding of embryonic stem-cell research to cell lines already in existence, and he signed a statutory ban on a late-term abortion procedure that the Supreme Court upheld in 2007. He did not obtain a constitutional amendment on marriage, school prayer, or abortion.

  11. 11. Education

    No Child Left Behind, signed in January 2002, required annual tests in reading and mathematics, publication of results by subgroup, and a sequence of sanctions for schools that missed proficiency targets, as a condition of federal aid. Edward Kennedy was a principal Democratic sponsor, and the statute did not nationalize the schools. Curriculum and hiring stayed with states and districts, inside tighter federal conditions than the 1994 law had set, and the Department of Education created under Carter administered the regime.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    He was readier than his predecessor to lease and to thin forests under a 2003 statute aimed at fire and disease, and he did not match the previous pace of new land monuments in the continental states. He did designate very large marine national monuments in the Pacific, in 2006 and again at the end of the term, the biggest conservation withdrawals of his presidency. Congress did not open the Arctic National Wildlife Refuge to the drilling his party sought.

  13. 13. Energy

    The Energy Policy Act of 2005 offered tax incentives, loan guarantees, and a biofuel mandate, and it did not open the Arctic refuge. He rejected the Kyoto protocol's binding targets. After the Supreme Court held in 2007 that greenhouse gases fit the Clean Air Act's definition of an air pollutant, no comprehensive regulatory system for those gases was in force when he left.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    The PATRIOT Act and later amendments built a security law beside the ordinary criminal code: roving wiretaps, broader demands for records, and sharing between intelligence officers and prosecutors. Detentions at Guantanamo and military commissions, rewritten after the Supreme Court intervened, held people outside the ordinary criminal courts. He created no national police, and for most of the term the national rate of violent crime stayed on the downward path begun in the 1990s.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    Temporary Assistance for Needy Families, the structure signed in 1996, stayed in place. His tax bills raised the child credit and, at the margin, its refundability to families with earnings, and he did not repeal food stamps or Medicaid. Poverty declined in the expansion of the middle of the decade and rose again in the crisis of 2008.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    The Medicare drug law of 2003 added a voluntary outpatient benefit delivered by private plans, with a gap in coverage between an initial limit and catastrophic protection, and with a bar on the secretary's negotiating prices directly with manufacturers. It was the largest new Medicare benefit since the program was created. He vetoed broader children's-health bills in 2007, and in 2003 he also signed a large program of HIV treatment for poor countries.

Of this presidency

  1. September 11 and the security state

    On September 11, 2001, hijacked airliners destroyed the World Trade Center towers, damaged the Pentagon, and crashed in Pennsylvania. Congress authorized the use of force against those responsible and passed the PATRIOT Act with large majorities of both parties, widening surveillance, information-sharing, and terrorism offenses. In 2002 it created the Department of Homeland Security, combining border, immigration enforcement, customs, emergency management, and other functions in the largest departmental reorganization since the creation of the Defense Department. A later statute created a director of national intelligence. None of this was a suspension of the Constitution or of elections. It was a permanent enlargement of the domestic security apparatus, built in the emergency and left standing.

  2. Afghanistan

    In October 2001 the United States and allies attacked the Taliban government, which had harbored the leadership of the September 11 plot and had refused to give that leadership up. NATO treated the attack on the United States as an attack on all of its members, the only time the alliance has done so. Kabul fell quickly. Osama bin Laden was not captured and remained at large until 2011. A new Afghan government was installed, and an American and allied force stayed. Bush left office with the war open, the Taliban out of the capital and not destroyed, and no theory on offer for how a temporary intervention had become a standing commitment.

  3. Iraq

    The administration's public case for war held that Iraq possessed active stockpiles of chemical and biological weapons, was reconstituting a nuclear program, and had dangerous links to terrorist groups. United Nations inspectors had not confirmed an active stockpile before the invasion of March 2003. Congress had authorized force in October 2002; it had not declared war, and there was no new and explicit Security Council authorization of the kind Bush's father had obtained in 1991. Baghdad fell quickly. The occupation then faced an insurgency. The Iraq Survey Group reported afterward that the active stockpiles at the center of the public case were not there. In 2007 Bush ordered additional American brigades into the fighting, and violence declined from its worst levels. He did not withdraw, and he did not claim that the original factual case had been vindicated.

  4. The tax cuts

    The 2001 act cut income-tax rates, added a 10 percent bracket, raised the child credit, and began phasing down the estate tax. The 2003 act accelerated those rate cuts and lowered the tax on dividends and long-term capital gains. Because the bills were passed under budget-reconciliation limits, most of the relief was written to expire at the end of 2010. The immediate fiscal effect, with the recession and with war spending, was the return of deficits after the surpluses of the previous administration. He did not pair the cuts with a reduction of Medicare or Social Security. The tax code was lowered inside the same structure.

  5. No Child Left Behind

    Signed in January 2002, the statute reauthorized federal aid to elementary and secondary schools on new conditions: annual testing in reading and math, disaggregation of results so that the scores of poor, minority, and disabled students could not be hidden in an average, and a ladder of interventions for schools that missed targets. It was negotiated with Democratic senators, Kennedy foremost among them. It did not prescribe a national curriculum or seize the schools from local boards. It did make federal money more conditional than it had been, and it made the Department of Education a supervisor of state accountability systems rather than only a dispenser of grants.

  6. Medicare Part D

    The Medicare Modernization Act of 2003 created a voluntary prescription-drug benefit, Part D, delivered through competing private plans rather than through a single federal formulary. Coverage included a gap, soon called the doughnut hole, between an initial benefit and protection against very high spending. The statute barred the health secretary from interfering in price negotiations between manufacturers and the private plans. It also expanded the role of private health plans inside Medicare. Passed on largely partisan votes after a prolonged House roll call, it was a Republican expansion of a Great Society entitlement, and it was the domestic spending choice that most clearly separated this presidency from a politics of simple retrenchment.

  7. The panic of 2008

    House prices fell, mortgage securities became unpriceable, and in 2008 the credit system seized. The Federal Reserve facilitated the sale of Bear Stearns in March. In September Lehman Brothers filed for bankruptcy, the Federal Reserve rescued AIG, and a money-market fund broke the buck. Bush asked Congress for authority to buy or guarantee troubled assets, up to seven hundred billion dollars. The first House vote failed; a revised bill passed. In practice the Treasury used much of the authority to buy equity in banks, most of which was later repaid, and in December to bridge the automobile companies toward a restructuring the next president would complete. He had not campaigned for public capital in private banks. He took it when the alternative looked like a collapse of lending.

  8. PEPFAR

    In 2003 Bush signed the President's Emergency Plan for AIDS Relief, a multiyear authorization of treatment, prevention, and care aimed chiefly at countries in Africa and the Caribbean. It was bilateral foreign aid on a scale American HIV policy had not had, and it bought antiretroviral drugs for large numbers of people who would not otherwise have received them. Religious constituencies that formed part of his coalition argued over the prevention provisions and largely accepted the spending. It belonged to the same presidency as the tax cuts and the wars, and it was not a war measure. It was a decision to use federal money, at a distance, on a disease.

PreviousBush v. Gore NextDistrict of Columbia v. Heller

2008

District of Columbia v. Heller

The Court held, for the first time, that the Second Amendment protects a person’s right to keep a firearm for self-defense in the home, and that a federal enclave’s ban on handguns violated it.

Decision

District of Columbia v. Heller

Decided June 26, 2008

How it was made

The District of Columbia forbade possession of a handgun and required that lawful long guns in the home be kept unloaded and disassembled or locked. Dick Heller, a special police officer who wanted a handgun at home, sued. The Amendment speaks of a well regulated militia and of the right of the people to keep and bear arms. The case asked which clause controls.

Who sat

Five to four. Scalia wrote. Roberts and Alito, the two appointees of the president then in office, were both in the majority. Souter, appointed by the first President Bush, dissented. Stevens wrote the principal dissent and read the amendment as a right tied to militia service.

With the opinion

  • Antonin Scalia — Reagan
  • John Roberts — G. W. Bush
  • Anthony Kennedy — Reagan
  • Clarence Thomas — G. H. W. Bush
  • Samuel Alito — G. W. Bush

In dissent

  • John Paul Stevens — Ford
  • David Souter — G. H. W. Bush
  • Ruth Bader Ginsburg — Clinton
  • Stephen Breyer — Clinton

What it changed

A competent adult may keep a handgun at home for self-defense, and the federal government may not ban that class of arm. The decision left in place, by its own words, longstanding limits: possession by felons and the mentally ill, carrying in sensitive places, conditions on commercial sale, and weapons not in common use. It bound the District of Columbia, which is federal. It did not yet bind the states. That step was McDonald v. Chicago in 2010, under the next president, by the same division of the Court with Sotomayor, Obama’s appointee, in dissent in place of Souter. After Heller, the Second Amendment is an individual right a court will enforce, which is not what the law of the lower courts had been for most of the twentieth century.

PreviousGeorge W. Bush NextBarack Obama

2009–2017

Barack Obama

Democrat

Obama took office in the worst recession since the 1930s and did not use it to nationalize the ordinary economy, leave NATO, or put aside elections. The change of degree that defined the domestic term was the Affordable Care Act, a reconstruction of the individual insurance market and an expansion of Medicaid, passed with a stimulus and a new banking statute. Abroad he reduced and then, against a new enemy, partly restored an American military role in Iraq, surged and then drew down in Afghanistan without ending that war, and concluded a nuclear agreement with Iran that was never a treaty ratified by the Senate.

The party

Obama's Democrats used their first two years to pass a stimulus and the Affordable Care Act, and those statutes became the party's program in a way the failed health plan of 1993 never had. The coalition was more uniformly liberal than Clinton's, because the white South had largely finished leaving and no longer had to be bargained with inside the caucus. After 2010 he defended the health law and the new financial statute against a Republican House, and he used executive action on immigration and on power-plant carbon rules where Congress would not legislate. The shift was in the party's composition and in the health entitlement, not in any decision to abandon private markets or the alliance system.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    The Recovery Act of February 2009 was a stimulus on the order of eight hundred billion dollars, mixing tax relief, aid to states, infrastructure, and safety-net spending, passed while the recession was still deepening. Deficits were very large and then narrowed with the recovery without disappearing, and the debt-ceiling crisis of 2011 produced statutory caps whose sequester cut defense and domestic discretionary spending with a bluntness neither party had intended as policy. At the start of 2013 a bargain with Congress restored the top income-tax rate to 39.6 percent and left the lower rates of the previous administration in place.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    He criticized the North American agreement in the campaign and did not withdraw from it or from the World Trade Organization. Congress, at his urging, approved trade agreements with South Korea, Colombia, and Panama that his predecessor had negotiated. The Trans-Pacific Partnership, the negotiation that was his own, was signed near the end of the term and had not been ratified when he left.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    He used what remained of the crisis authorities for bank stress tests and the automobile restructurings, and he signed a banking statute that added a council to watch systemic risk, a liquidation procedure for failing financial firms, restrictions on proprietary trading by banks, and a bureau for consumer finance. He did not rewrite the Federal Reserve Act. The Federal Reserve, first under Bernanke, whom he renominated, and then under Yellen, whom he nominated, kept short-term rates near zero for years and bought large quantities of assets.

  4. 4. Labor

    The first statute he signed extended the time in which a worker could sue over discriminatory pay. A bill to allow union recognition by signed cards rather than by election did not pass. The automobile bankruptcies reduced labor costs, with the unions at the table, in order to keep the firms alive, and they did not reverse the long decline in private-sector union membership.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    A bill to legalize people brought to the country as children failed in the Senate in 2010. In 2012 his administration granted deferred action by memorandum, a case-by-case postponement of removal and a route to work authorization, not citizenship and not a statute, and a later attempt to extend similar protection to parents was blocked in court and never took effect. A comprehensive bill passed the Senate in 2013 and died in the House, while formal removals ran high in the first term and lower in the second.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    He was the first Black president, which changed the visible meaning of the office and enacted no statute by itself. A 2010 sentencing law reduced the disparity between crack and powder cocaine from one hundred to one down to eighteen to one, and near the end of the term he commuted far more sentences than his recent predecessors, most of them drug sentences. After the Supreme Court in 2013 disabled the coverage formula of the Voting Rights Act, he asked Congress for a replacement formula, and Congress did not enact one.

  7. 7. Federalism

    The health law's Medicaid expansion became optional for each state when the Supreme Court, in 2012, forbade using a state's existing Medicaid funds as the penalty for refusal, and a substantial number of states declined for a time. Marriage changed by judicial decision after his administration stopped defending the federal definition; he signed no statute repealing the Defense of Marriage Act. Enforcement against marijuana businesses that complied with state law was narrowed by Justice Department guidance, not by an amendment of the Controlled Substances Act.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    He ended the Iraq combat mission and withdrew American forces in December 2011 after no agreement was reached on a residual force, then sent a limited contingent back in 2014 against the Islamic State; in Afghanistan he ordered a surge of about thirty thousand troops and afterward a drawdown that left the war unfinished. Osama bin Laden was killed in a special-operations raid in Pakistan in May 2011, conducted without Pakistani consent. A NATO air campaign in Libya, conducted under a Security Council resolution but without a specific authorization from Congress, helped topple that government and did not leave a stable successor; he did not carry out a threatened strike after a chemical attack in Syria, accepting a Russian-brokered removal of declared chemical stocks, and later bombed Islamic State positions there; sanctions followed Russia's seizure of Crimea, and the Iran nuclear arrangement of 2015 was not submitted as a treaty.

  9. 9. National defense

    The statute excluding openly homosexual military service was repealed in his first term, and the repeal took effect in 2011. A strategic-arms reduction treaty with Russia received the Senate's consent in 2010, and discretionary defense spending was then squeezed by the caps and the sequester of the 2011 budget law. Drone strikes and special operations continued the counterterrorism war, including the killing in Yemen in 2011 of Anwar al-Awlaki, an American citizen, by a targeted strike rather than by a criminal trial.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    The health law's requirement that insurance cover contraception produced the religious-liberty conflict of the term, and the Supreme Court held that closely held corporations could invoke a religious-freedom statute against it. He supported legal abortion and left the annual appropriations rider limiting federal funding of abortion in place. He proposed no national church; his coalition ran from religious Black Protestants to a growing share of religiously unaffiliated voters, while white evangelicals remained overwhelmingly with the other party.

  11. 11. Education

    A competitive grant program inside the stimulus, Race to the Top, pushed states toward shared academic standards and new systems for evaluating teachers. Waivers from the previous administration's school-testing law relaxed its proficiency deadline in exchange for conditions of that kind. The successor statute of 2015 kept annual testing, returned more of the design of accountability to the states, and left the Department of Education in place.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    He designated a large number of national monuments, among them Bears Ears in Utah at the end of 2016, and he withdrew additional offshore areas from new leasing. He denied a presidential permit for the Keystone XL pipeline. The Dakota Access pipeline was not killed by any statute of his; in the last weeks the Army's engineers withheld an easement pending further review, a pause rather than a final legislative decision.

  13. 13. Energy

    Domestic oil and gas production rose through the shale boom, on private leases and under state rules more than under any production statute he signed. A cap-and-trade bill passed the House in 2009 and died in the Senate; the Clean Power Plan that followed was a regulation under the existing Clean Air Act, aimed at carbon dioxide from existing power plants, and the Supreme Court stayed it in 2016 before it could take stable effect. He joined the Paris climate agreement by executive action and did not submit it as a treaty.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    The sentencing law of 2010 cut the crack-to-powder cocaine ratio from one hundred to one to eighteen to one and was not made fully retroactive. Prosecutors were instructed to avoid some mandatory-minimum charges in lower-level drug cases, and he used commutation heavily, late, on drug sentences. There was no crime bill on the scale of 1994, and violence nationally stayed far below the peak of the early 1990s even as homicides rose in a number of large cities in 2015 and 2016.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    The Recovery Act temporarily enlarged food stamps, unemployment insurance, and refundable tax credits. He did not restore the cash-welfare entitlement ended in 1996. Where states accepted it, the Medicaid expansion was the lasting addition to support for low-income adults, and a large statutory increase in the minimum wage did not pass.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    The Affordable Care Act barred insurers from denying coverage for preexisting conditions, subsidized purchases on new exchanges, let young adults remain on a parent's policy until age twenty-six, imposed a penalty for going without insurance, and expanded Medicaid, which the Supreme Court then made optional for the states. It was not a single-payer takeover of hospitals or physicians, and the public option had been dropped before passage; private insurance remained the main coverage for people under sixty-five. The share of the population without insurance fell substantially once the major provisions were in effect.

Of this presidency

  1. The Recovery Act

    Signed in February 2009, the American Recovery and Reinvestment Act was the fiscal answer to a recession already the worst since the 1930s. The sum was on the order of eight hundred billion dollars over several years: tax relief for workers and for business investment, aid so that states could pay teachers and Medicaid bills, extended unemployment insurance, a larger food-stamp benefit, infrastructure, and energy projects. It was not a permanent change in the size of government, and it was not a public-works program alone. Passed with almost no Republican support, it slowed the fall and did not produce a quick return to the employment of 2007. The argument over its size began immediately and outlasted the spending.

  2. The Affordable Care Act

    The health statute signed in March 2010 was the largest federal coverage law since Medicare and Medicaid in 1965, and it was not those programs rewritten for the whole population. Insurers in the individual and small-group markets had to take the sick, could not set premiums by health status, and had to cover a defined set of benefits; subsidies reduced premiums on new exchanges; young adults could stay on a parent's policy until age twenty-six; larger employers faced a mandate; and Medicaid was to expand to adults without children up to a higher income line. An individual penalty for remaining uninsured was included, and the Supreme Court upheld it as a tax while making the Medicaid expansion optional, so that a state could refuse it without losing the older program. A later decision upheld subsidies on exchanges run by the federal government as well as by the states. There was no public option in the final Senate design, and not a single Republican voted for passage. The uninsured rate fell substantially. Private insurance, Medicare, and Medicaid all remained.

  3. Dodd-Frank
