    The Dodd-Frank Act of 2010 was the statutory answer to the panic of 2008. It created a council of regulators to designate systemic firms, an orderly-liquidation authority meant to avoid a naked bankruptcy of the Lehman kind, a requirement that more derivatives be cleared, a restriction — the Volcker Rule — on banks trading for their own account, and a Consumer Financial Protection Bureau housed at the Federal Reserve but with its own mandate. It did not break up the largest banks, restore the 1933 separation of commercial and investment banking in full, or change the dollar. What it changed was the regulatory perimeter around institutions whose failure had just been treated as a public problem.

  4. The automobile bankruptcies

    Bush had used crisis funds in December 2008 to keep General Motors and Chrysler from collapsing in the transition. Obama continued that rescue into structured bankruptcies in 2009. Chrysler emerged with an Italian firm as the industrial partner; General Motors emerged with the Treasury as a majority owner for a time. Labor contracts were rewritten in the process, and the unions' retiree health funds received equity. The government later sold its stakes. The cash put into the automobile companies was not fully recovered. The industry was not nationalized as a going policy: the firms returned to private ownership, smaller, and still private.

  5. Wars and the Abbottabad raid

    Obama had campaigned on ending the Iraq war. Combat forces left, and in December 2011 the troop presence ended after Baghdad and Washington could not agree on legal protections for a residual force. The Islamic State later seized much of the Sunni north; from 2014 he sent air power and a limited number of troops back, a different mission from the occupation and not an end to American force in Iraq. In Afghanistan he announced in 2009 a surge of about thirty thousand troops and a date to begin drawing down, and he left office with American forces still there and the Taliban undefeated. In May 2011 a special-operations team killed Osama bin Laden in Abbottabad, Pakistan, without telling the Pakistani government in advance. The raid closed the search that had begun in 2001. It did not close either war.

  6. The Iran nuclear agreement

    In July 2015 Iran and six powers, the United States among them, agreed to limit Iran's centrifuges, enrichment level, and uranium stockpile, to redesign a reactor, and to accept more intrusive inspection, in exchange for relief from nuclear-related sanctions. Some of the limits were written to expire. Obama did not submit the arrangement as a treaty. Congress had passed a review statute that gave it a window to disapprove; a resolution of disapproval did not survive. International inspectors reported Iranian compliance with the nuclear limits through the remainder of his term. His successor withdrew. While Obama was president the agreement was an executive bargain inside the sanctions system, not a statute and not an alliance.

  7. Marriage and the Defense of Marriage Act

    Clinton had signed the Defense of Marriage Act, which defined marriage for federal purposes as a union of one man and one woman and allowed states to refuse recognition of other states' marriages. Obama's Justice Department initially defended the federal definition, then stopped and argued that it was unconstitutional. The Supreme Court in 2013 struck that federal definition; it did not, in that case, require the states to license such marriages. In 2015 the Court held that the Constitution required states both to license marriages between two people of the same sex and to recognize those licensed elsewhere. Obama by then supported that result. He signed no statute repealing the 1996 act. The change in the law of marriage was the Court's, after a shift in the executive's litigation position, not a bill he passed.

  8. Deferred action

    The DREAM Act, which would have created a path to legal status for people brought to the country as children, failed a Senate vote in December 2010. In June 2012 the homeland-security secretary issued a memorandum offering deferred action to those who had arrived as children, had lived here continuously, were in school or had finished school or served in the military, and lacked a significant criminal record. Deferred action was an exercise of enforcement discretion: a postponement of removal and, under existing regulations, eligibility for work authorization. It was not a green card and not citizenship, and a later president could try to take it back. A 2014 attempt to extend a similar deferral to parents of citizens and permanent residents was enjoined and never operated. Congress did not ratify any of it by statute during his term.

  9. The Garland nomination

    Antonin Scalia died in February 2016. In March Obama nominated Merrick Garland, the chief judge of the court of appeals in Washington, to the seat. The Republican Senate majority refused to hold a hearing, on the stated ground that a Supreme Court vacancy in a presidential-election year should be filled by the next president. No vote was taken. The seat was empty when Obama left office. The episode used a power the Constitution gives the Senate, the power to withhold consent, and it fixed the conditions under which his successor would appoint the next justice.

PreviousDistrict of Columbia v. Heller NextThe Court and the Statutes of a Generation

2010–2015

The Court and the Statutes of a Generation

In Obama’s years the Court opened election spending by corporations and unions, upheld the health law on a ground Congress had not led with, disabled the formula that chose which places needed federal approval to change their voting rules, and held that states must license and recognize the marriages of same-sex couples.

Decision

Citizens United v. Federal Election Commission

Decided January 21, 2010

How it was made

A nonprofit corporation had made a film critical of Hillary Clinton and wanted to advertise it during the 2008 primaries. Federal law barred corporations and unions from using general treasury funds for electioneering communications near a primary or a general election. The Court ordered reargument on whether those bans should be overruled.

Who sat

Five to four. Kennedy wrote. Stevens read his dissent from the bench. Obama’s first appointee, Sotomayor, was in dissent. His second, Kagan, was not yet on the Court; as solicitor general she had argued the government’s side.

With the opinion

  • Anthony Kennedy — Reagan
  • John Roberts — G. W. Bush
  • Antonin Scalia — Reagan
  • Clarence Thomas — G. H. W. Bush
  • Samuel Alito — G. W. Bush

In dissent

  • John Paul Stevens — Ford
  • Ruth Bader Ginsburg — Clinton
  • Stephen Breyer — Clinton
  • Sonia Sotomayor — Obama

What it changed

Corporations and unions may spend their own money on independent electioneering. The ban on contributions directly to candidates was not the question and was not struck. The decision treated the expenditure as speech, and the corporate identity of the speaker as irrelevant to the First Amendment. Campaigns thereafter drew money from entities that do not appear on a ballot. Congress did not pass this regime. Five justices did, all of them appointed before the president then in office.

Decision

National Federation of Independent Business v. Sebelius

Decided June 28, 2012

How it was made

Twenty-six states and others challenged the Affordable Care Act. Two provisions mattered to the structure of the government: the requirement that individuals obtain health insurance or pay the government, and the expansion of Medicaid, which a state would lose all of its existing Medicaid funds by refusing.

Who sat

The lineup is not one vote. Roberts, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, and Alito agreed that Congress could not impose the insurance requirement under the commerce power. Roberts then joined Ginsburg, Breyer, Sotomayor, and Kagan to sustain the requirement as a tax. On Medicaid, Roberts, Breyer, and Kagan joined Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, and Alito to hold that threatening all existing Medicaid funds was coercive. Ginsburg and Sotomayor dissented from that limit. The chief justice, appointed by the previous president, was the only justice in both sustaining majorities.

With the opinion

  • John Roberts — G. W. Bush
  • Ruth Bader Ginsburg — Clinton
  • Stephen Breyer — Clinton
  • Sonia Sotomayor — Obama
  • Elena Kagan — Obama

In dissent

  • Antonin Scalia — Reagan
  • Anthony Kennedy — Reagan
  • Clarence Thomas — G. H. W. Bush
  • Samuel Alito — G. W. Bush

What it changed

The insurance requirement survived, but as an exercise of the taxing power, not of the commerce power. Congress may not, on this reading, compel a person to buy a product by calling the compulsion commerce. It may tax him for not buying it. The Medicaid expansion survived only as an option: a state that refuses it may not be stripped of the older program. The health law, which was a statute, remained. The decision redrew two boundaries Congress had crossed, one back toward the states and one into the tax power. Roberts wrote both.

Decision

Shelby County v. Holder

Decided June 25, 2013

How it was made

The Voting Rights Act of 1965 required certain states and counties, chosen by a coverage formula last updated in the 1970s, to obtain federal approval before changing voting rules. Shelby County, Alabama, a covered place, sued.

Who sat

Five to four. Roberts wrote. The four dissenting justices included both of Obama’s appointees. Thomas joined the majority and would have gone further against the preclearance section itself.

With the opinion

  • John Roberts — G. W. Bush
  • Antonin Scalia — Reagan
  • Anthony Kennedy — Reagan
  • Clarence Thomas — G. H. W. Bush
  • Samuel Alito — G. W. Bush

In dissent

  • Ruth Bader Ginsburg — Clinton
  • Stephen Breyer — Clinton
  • Sonia Sotomayor — Obama
  • Elena Kagan — Obama

What it changed

The coverage formula was unconstitutional. Preclearance, the requirement of federal permission before a covered place changes its voting rules, was left in the statute and made inoperative, because Congress had not written a current formula. Places that had needed approval no longer did, unless Congress acted again. It did not, in the years this volume covers. A statute of 1965 lost its geographic reach by a decision, not by repeal.

Decision

Obergefell v. Hodges

Decided June 26, 2015

How it was made

Voters and legislatures in many states had defined marriage as a union of a man and a woman. Couples in Ohio, Michigan, Kentucky, and Tennessee challenged those definitions. The questions were whether a state must license a marriage of two people of the same sex, and whether it must recognize such a marriage performed elsewhere.

Who sat

Five to four. Kennedy wrote. Reagan’s appointees split: Kennedy in the majority, Scalia in dissent. Both of Obama’s appointees were in the majority. Roberts, the chief justice, dissented.

With the opinion

  • Anthony Kennedy — Reagan
  • Ruth Bader Ginsburg — Clinton
  • Stephen Breyer — Clinton
  • Sonia Sotomayor — Obama
  • Elena Kagan — Obama

In dissent

  • John Roberts — G. W. Bush
  • Antonin Scalia — Reagan
  • Clarence Thomas — G. H. W. Bush
  • Samuel Alito — G. W. Bush

What it changed

A state may not refuse to license a marriage of two people of the same sex, and it may not refuse to recognize one licensed by another state. Marriage licensing, which had been state law in its details since the founding, became subject to a constitutional rule of inclusion. The decision did not pass a statute and did not amend the Constitution. It held that the Fourteenth Amendment already required the result. Four justices, including the chief justice, said that question belonged to the voters of the states. The licenses changed because five justices read the amendment as they did. Kennedy, appointed thirty-one years earlier by Reagan, was the fifth.

PreviousBarack Obama NextDonald Trump

2017–2021

Donald Trump

Republican

In his first term Trump did not take the United States out of NATO, abolish private property, or cancel the constitutional calendar of elections, and he did not establish a national church. The changes of degree were in the instruments he reached for: tariffs, especially against China; immigration enforcement, including a travel ban rewritten and then sustained by the Supreme Court; and a tax cut weighted toward the corporate rate. He failed to repeal the health law, and he left office in January 2021 after the House had impeached him a second time, with the Senate trial of that article falling only after the term was over.

The party

Trump's Republicans moved restriction on trade and on immigration, and a skepticism of alliances as they had been managed since 1945, into the center of the administration, while still cutting taxes and confirming conservative judges, the older commitments that could pass a Republican Congress. The voters he led with were working-class, above all white voters in the industrial states, a group the party had courted since Nixon and Reagan without previously offering a program whose economic core was tariffs and immigration enforcement. He did not take the party out of NATO or out of electoral competition. He changed what it was willing to treat as open questions: who may enter, whether a trade deficit is a failure of policy, and whether the postwar alliance system is a finished settlement or a contract to be repriced.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    The tax act of 2017 cut the corporate rate from 35 percent to 21 percent, lowered individual rates, capped the deduction for state and local taxes at ten thousand dollars, and created a deduction for pass-through business income; the individual provisions were largely written to expire after 2025, and the corporate cut was not. Deficits widened during an expansion, the reverse of the late-1990s pattern. In 2020 he signed a series of very large relief acts, among them one known as the CARES Act, combining direct rebates, expanded unemployment insurance, and support for firms and hospitals.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    He withdrew from the Trans-Pacific Partnership, which had been signed and not ratified, imposed tariffs of 25 percent on steel and 10 percent on aluminum on national-security grounds, and laid successive tariffs on a large share of Chinese goods after an investigation into forced technology transfer. A revised North American agreement, with stricter rules for automobile content and a mechanism to enforce labor standards, passed with votes from both parties and replaced the agreement of 1994; it was a renegotiation, not an exit from continental trade. A January 2020 bargain with China left most of the new China tariffs in place and included purchase commitments that were already lagging when the term ended.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    He appointed Jerome Powell chairman of the Federal Reserve and then criticized the Fed in public for raising rates, breaking the recent habit of presidential silence on those decisions, without removing Powell or amending the Federal Reserve Act. In 2018 he signed a partial rollback of the post-crisis banking statute that raised, from fifty billion dollars in assets to two hundred fifty billion, the threshold at which the strictest prudential standards automatically applied. When the pandemic shut activity down, the Fed returned rates to the floor and reopened emergency lending.

  4. 4. Labor

    The expansion he inherited brought unemployment to low levels by 2019, and the shutdowns of 2020 then pushed the measured rate above 14 percent before a partial recovery by the time he left. The labor board, through appointments and decisions, moved in a direction more favorable to employers. He did not obtain a rewriting of the statutes that govern collective bargaining.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    Early versions of a travel ban aimed at several majority-Muslim countries were blocked by courts; a revised proclamation was upheld by the Supreme Court in 2018. Refugee ceilings were cut sharply; a policy of prosecuting illegal entry separated children from parents until he curtailed the systematic practice under a court order and public pressure; and a national-emergency declaration moved money toward a border wall Congress had not agreed to fund in full. His rescission of deferred action for people brought here as children was set aside in 2020 as arbitrary under the administrative-procedure statute, not as a ruling that the program was lawful, and Congress enacted neither a legalization nor a comprehensive replacement.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    He signed a sentencing statute that shortened federal drug terms falling heavily on Black prisoners, and he did not seek repeal of the Civil Rights Act or the Voting Rights Act. After a white-nationalist rally in Charlottesville in August 2017 ended in a death, leading figures in his own party treated his public response as inadequate. The protests that followed the killing of George Floyd in Minneapolis in 2020 produced no federal statute on policing.

  7. 7. Federalism

    Orders closing businesses and schools in 2020 were mainly orders of the states; his administration controlled restrictions on international travel, the purchase and regulation of vaccines and other countermeasures, and the relief payments, and he pressed for reopening without a national closure law or a national reopening law. Attempts to strip unrelated funds from cities and states that limited cooperation with immigration officers were largely blocked in court. He declared a national emergency at the southern border in order to spend money Congress had refused to appropriate in the amount he wanted, and the courts upheld some of the transfers and blocked others.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    He did not withdraw the United States from NATO, though he pressed allies in public to spend more and treated the alliance as a bargain open to renegotiation rather than as a finished settlement. He left the Iran nuclear agreement, and he completed an American withdrawal from the treaty on intermediate-range nuclear forces on the stated ground that Russia had deployed a prohibited missile. He recognized Jerusalem as Israel's capital and moved the embassy there, mediated normalization between Israel and four Arab states, met the North Korean leader three times and brought back no denuclearization agreement, ordered the strike that killed Qasem Soleimani — Iranian missiles then hit an American base in Iraq, and the exchange did not become a general war — and signed with the Taliban a timetable for leaving Afghanistan that had not been completed when his term ended in January 2021.

  9. 9. National defense

    Congress raised defense appropriations with support in both parties, and the authorization he signed in December 2019 created the Space Force as a separate armed service inside the Department of the Air Force. The territory the Islamic State still held in Iraq and Syria was retaken during the term by local forces with American air power and special operations, a campaign he continued rather than a new occupation he began. He did not restore conscription, and he did not renounce the mutual-defense treaties.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    White evangelical Protestants remained the most reliable bloc in his coalition, and the administration's offering to them was judges and regulatory exemptions, not a national church. Rules under the health law were widened so that employers and others could claim religious or moral objections to covering contraception, and restrictions on foreign-aid funds connected to abortion were restored and extended. The religion clauses were not amended.

  11. 11. Education

    The elementary and secondary statute enacted in 2015 stayed in place, and a federal tax credit for scholarships to private schools, which his education secretary sought, did not pass. In 2020 the department, by regulation, required more formal hearings, including cross-examination, in campus cases under the sex-discrimination law. The Department of Education remained.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    He sharply reduced the Bears Ears and Grand Staircase-Escalante national monuments in Utah, and those reductions were challenged in court. The 2017 tax law opened the coastal plain of the Arctic National Wildlife Refuge to oil and gas leasing, and a sale held at the very end of the term drew almost no bids from private companies. The public lands as a whole were not put up for sale, and the wall on the southern border was the land project he treated as the urgent one.

  13. 13. Energy

    He announced in 2017 that the United States would leave the Paris climate agreement; under the agreement's own waiting rules the withdrawal took effect only in November 2020. A narrower rule meant to replace the previous administration's regulation of carbon from power plants was vacated by the court of appeals in Washington in the closing days of the term, so neither version was a settled program when he left. Production of oil and gas rose to record levels before demand collapsed in 2020, and coal did not return to its old share of generation, because gas was cheaper.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    The First Step Act, which he signed in 2018, made the earlier cut in the crack-cocaine sentencing ratio more retroactive and eased some federal mandatory minimums and the rules for earning time off a sentence. Robert Mueller's investigation reported in 2019 that it had not established a conspiracy between the 2016 campaign and the Russian government; on obstruction of justice the report did not exonerate him, and no obstruction charge was brought. The department resumed federal executions in 2020 after a hiatus of nearly two decades, and homicides rose sharply that year in many cities.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    Poverty fell during the late expansion, and the cash-welfare block grant enacted in 1996 was not replaced. The tax law's opportunity zones gave a capital-gains preference for investment in designated poor census tracts, a place-based tax expenditure rather than a cash benefit. The relief acts of 2020 temporarily increased unemployment payments, sent rebates, and, through a public-health order whose statutory basis was disputed, paused certain evictions.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    He urged repeal of the Affordable Care Act, and in July 2017 the Senate defeated a stripped-down repeal by 49 to 51. The market rules, the subsidies, and the Medicaid expansion survived; the tax act only set the penalty for going uninsured to zero, and a suit claiming that the zeroed penalty invalidated the whole statute was still undecided when he left office. By the day he departed, on the order of four hundred thousand Americans had died in the coronavirus pandemic, and in December two vaccines had received emergency authorization after a federal effort of funding, guaranteed purchase, and accelerated trials.

Of this presidency

  1. The 2017 tax cut

    The tax statute signed in December 2017 cut the corporate income-tax rate from 35 percent to 21 percent and made that cut permanent. Individual rates were lowered, the standard deduction was increased, the deduction for state and local taxes was capped at ten thousand dollars, pass-through businesses received a new deduction, and the estate-tax exemption was roughly doubled. Most of the individual provisions were written to expire after 2025, a consequence of passing the bill under reconciliation rules that limit deficits outside a ten-year window. The same act reduced the health law's individual-mandate penalty to zero. Deficits rose even as the economy expanded. The income tax and the corporate tax remained; the rates and the base changed.

  2. Tariffs and the USMCA

    Trump withdrew the United States from the Trans-Pacific Partnership in his first week, before that agreement had been ratified. He then imposed tariffs of 25 percent on steel and 10 percent on aluminum, citing national security, and, after an investigation under the trade statute into Chinese technology practices, tariffs on successive lists of Chinese imports. Retaliation followed. With Mexico and Canada he renegotiated the North American agreement. The replacement, approved by Congress in a vote that included both parties and signed in January 2020, tightened the regional-content rules for automobiles, added digital-trade provisions, and created a labor-enforcement tool aimed especially at Mexico. It kept tariff-free trade in North America inside new conditions. The China tariffs, unlike the North American renegotiation, were still largely in force at the end of the term, alongside a 2020 agreement whose purchase promises China was not meeting on the expected pace.

  3. Agreements left

    Three departures defined the break with the diplomacy he had inherited. The Pacific trade agreement, signed under Obama and not ratified, was abandoned immediately. The Paris climate agreement, which Obama had joined without submitting it as a treaty, was denounced in 2017, and the withdrawal could take legal effect only in November 2020 because of the agreement's waiting period. The Iran nuclear arrangement was left in May 2018, nuclear-related sanctions were reimposed, and Iran afterward exceeded the limits on enrichment and stockpiles that the arrangement had set. He did not replace the Iran bargain with another nuclear agreement during the term. He also did not withdraw from NATO, which was the alliance, as opposed to the agreements, that his criticism most often named.

  4. The travel ban

    In January 2017 an executive order restricted entry from several majority-Muslim countries and immediately produced detention and confusion at airports. Courts blocked it. A second order was also blocked in important respects. A third proclamation, narrower, with a process for waivers and with North Korea and certain Venezuelan officials added to a revised list of countries, was the version the Supreme Court upheld in 2018. The Court treated the proclamation as within the president's statutory power over entry and rejected the constitutional attack brought before it. The ban that operated was not the first text he had signed. It was an immigration restriction rewritten to survive review, and it did.

  5. The First Step Act

    In December 2018 Trump signed the First Step Act, a bipartisan criminal-justice statute. It made the 2010 reduction in the crack-and-powder sentencing disparity retroactive, so that people still serving under the old ratio could seek the new one. It widened a safety valve around some mandatory minimums, limited the stacking of certain gun counts for first-time offenders going forward, and expanded programs through which federal prisoners could earn time in less restrictive confinement. It did not repeal mandatory minimums as a class, legalize any drug, or federalize local policing. It was the main criminal-law reform of the term, and it passed because majorities of both parties, and a president who had campaigned on toughness about crime, were willing to shorten some federal drug sentences.

  6. The Abraham Accords

    In 2020 the United Arab Emirates and Bahrain agreed to normalize relations with Israel, and Sudan and Morocco entered agreements of their own, with the United States as mediator. The Emirati bargain included an Israeli suspension of plans to annex parts of the West Bank. Morocco's was tied to American recognition of Moroccan sovereignty over Western Sahara. None of the agreements was a peace treaty with the Palestinians or the creation of a Palestinian state. They were the largest addition to Israel's set of Arab recognitions since the treaty with Jordan in 1994. They belonged to a foreign policy that was transactional and, in this case, achieved a result previous administrations had not.

  7. The health law survives

    Repeal of the Affordable Care Act was a stated priority of the first year. The House passed a replacement bill. The Senate did not. On July 28, 2017, a stripped-down repeal failed by 49 to 51, with John McCain, Susan Collins, and Lisa Murkowski voting no among the Republicans. No later Congress of his term sent him a repeal. The tax act zeroed the penalty attached to the individual mandate and left the insurance rules, the subsidies, and the Medicaid expansion standing. Litigation arguing that a mandate without a penalty made the entire statute unconstitutional was argued before the Supreme Court in November 2020 and was still pending on the day he left office. He had not repealed the law he had campaigned against.

  8. Two impeachments

    The House impeached him in December 2019 for abuse of power and obstruction of Congress, on the charge that he had paused security aid to Ukraine while seeking the announcement of investigations into Joseph Biden, a political rival. The Senate acquitted him in February 2020, well short of two-thirds. He lost the 2020 election and did not accept the certified result; lawsuits did not overturn any state's certification. On January 6, 2021, during the electoral count, a crowd from a rally he had addressed forced its way into the Capitol and stopped the proceedings, and one person in the crowd was shot and killed by Capitol police. He had told the rally to go to the Capitol and to do so peacefully, after weeks of pressing officials to reverse the outcome. The House impeached him again on January 13 for incitement of insurrection. The Senate tried that article the next month, after he had left office, and a majority, including several Republicans, voted to convict, short of the two-thirds required. He is the only president to have been impeached twice, and the office passed to his successor on the appointed day.

  9. The Space Force

    The defense authorization signed in December 2019 established the United States Space Force as a separate armed service, placed inside the Department of the Air Force, the first new service since the Air Force itself was separated in 1947. The change reorganized space missions the department already had more than it fielded a new fleet in the year that remained. He had asked for the service. Congress, by then with a House of the other party, agreed and wrote it into the annual military statute. It was an institutional addition inside the existing defense establishment, not a new kind of war and not a withdrawal from the older services.

  10. Three justices

    Neil Gorsuch was confirmed in 2017 to the seat of Antonin Scalia, which had been vacant since 2016 because the Senate majority had refused to consider Merrick Garland. Brett Kavanaugh was confirmed in 2018 after a hearing dominated by an allegation of sexual assault, which he denied. Amy Coney Barrett was confirmed in late October 2020, after Ruth Bader Ginsburg's death in September and days before the presidential election. Three appointments in one term changed the balance of the Supreme Court. The Constitution's method was followed: nomination, Senate consent, commission. What the new justices would decide in later years was not yet the record of this term.

  11. The pandemic

    The coronavirus was in the country by early 2020. Restrictions on foreign travelers who had been in China came at the end of January; they did not stop Americans from returning, and diagnostic testing was slow to become available at scale. By spring he had signed the CARES Act, the largest of the relief statutes, while urging governors to reopen. On the order of four hundred thousand Americans had died of the disease by January 20, 2021. In December two vaccines received emergency-use authorization, the product of a federal program that paid for development, promised to buy successful doses, and compressed the trial calendar. The public-health orders that closed much of ordinary life were, in the main, state orders. The term ended with the pandemic still the central fact of the economy and of daily government, and with vaccination only begun.

PreviousThe Court and the Statutes of a Generation NextTrump v. Hawaii

2018

Trump v. Hawaii

The Court upheld a presidential proclamation restricting entry from several countries, most of them majority-Muslim. One of the five votes was the first justice appointed by the president whose proclamation it was. The decisions a later majority of his appointees would join came in the next term, and they are taken up after that president.

Decision

Trump v. Hawaii

Decided June 26, 2018

How it was made

The president’s first orders suspending entry from a list of countries were blocked by lower courts. A third proclamation, written after an interagency review, restricted entry from eight countries, with exceptions and waivers. Hawaii and others said the proclamation exceeded the immigration statutes and violated the First Amendment’s bar on an establishment of religion, because it fulfilled a campaign promise aimed at Muslims.

Who sat

Five to four. Roberts wrote. Gorsuch, Trump’s first appointee, joined the majority. Kennedy, who retired a day later, concurred and reminded the government that its acts remain subject to the Constitution abroad as at home. Sotomayor’s dissent compared the opinion to Korematsu and said the Court was repeating the error. Roberts’s majority answered that Korematsu was gravely wrong and had no place in the law.

With the opinion

  • John Roberts — G. W. Bush
  • Anthony Kennedy — Reagan
  • Clarence Thomas — G. H. W. Bush
  • Samuel Alito — G. W. Bush
  • Neil Gorsuch — Trump

In dissent

  • Ruth Bader Ginsburg — Clinton
  • Stephen Breyer — Clinton
  • Sonia Sotomayor — Obama
  • Elena Kagan — Obama

What it changed

A president may, under the immigration statutes, suspend entry of classes of aliens on a finding that it is detrimental to the interests of the United States, and a proclamation that is facially neutral as to religion does not fall because of statements made in a campaign. The Court also said, in answering the dissent, that Korematsu was overruled in the court of history and now expressly repudiated. The travel restriction stood. The appointment that made the fifth vote was Trump’s. He would name two more justices before the term covered here ended, Brett Kavanaugh and Amy Coney Barrett. What those three later did together, on abortion, on the administrative state, and on other subjects, was decided after he had left office the first time. This volume stops at the proclamation the Court upheld, and at the bench that upheld it.

PreviousDonald Trump NextJoseph Biden

2021–2025

Joseph Biden

Democrat

Biden did not nationalize industry, leave NATO, or suspend elections, and he left office on the appointed day after his party had replaced him on the ticket. The changes of degree were fiscal and military in the limited sense: a relief act near two trillion dollars, an infrastructure statute, a climate-and-health tax act, subsidies for domestic semiconductors, and a completed withdrawal from Afghanistan that ended in a collapse. Inflation, which had been quiet for a generation, became the domestic fact of the first two years. The border became the fact that Congress would not legislate. The Supreme Court, remade before he arrived, struck the broad student-debt cancellation, returned abortion to the states, and ended the use of race in university admissions.

The party

Biden's Democrats were the party Obama had left, after four years out of the White House and after a nominating fight in which a democratic socialist came second. Biden won that fight by refusing the socialist program — no single-payer health law, no Green New Deal as a statute — and then carried pieces of its spending into the bills he could pass. The congressional party was narrower than Obama's in 2009: a 50–50 Senate in the first two years, then a one-seat majority, then a House of the other party after 2022. What passed was larger than anything after Obama's first two years, and what the party's public language asked for was larger still. The shift from Obama was not a new constitution. It was industrial subsidy, a bigger relief state, and a coalition that had lost the nomination's left wing without expelling it.

Sixteen points of comparison

  1. 1. Fiscal policy and the size of government

    The American Rescue Plan, signed in March 2021, was a relief act on the order of $1.9 trillion: direct payments, extended unemployment, aid to states and schools, and, for one year, a fully refundable child tax credit paid monthly. A bipartisan infrastructure law followed in November, mixing a reauthorization of highway spending with new money for roads, bridges, broadband, and water. The Inflation Reduction Act of August 2022 was not a broad tax increase; it imposed a 15 percent minimum tax on the book income of large corporations, a tax on stock buybacks, and a larger budget for the revenue service, and it spent on climate subsidies and health. Deficits were very large in 2021 and then narrowed without disappearing. A debt-ceiling bargain in 2023 set caps. Prices, not the size of the later bills, were the argument: consumer prices rose 9.1 percent in the year ending June 2022, the fastest in four decades, and the administration treated the spending as relief of a pandemic rather than as a cause.

  2. 2. Trade and tariffs

    He did not rejoin the Trans-Pacific agreement his party had already walked away from, and he left most of the China tariffs of the previous administration in place. The substitute for a trade treaty was a subsidy. The CHIPS Act of 2022 appropriated money to build semiconductor factories in the United States and to research, and the administration then restricted the export of advanced chips and the tools that make them to China. An Indo-Pacific economic framework was negotiated without opening the American market. No major free-trade agreement was sent to Congress. The commercial republic remained; the method shifted from reciprocal tariff cuts toward paying firms to produce particular goods at home.

  3. 3. Monetary policy and banking

    He renominated Jerome Powell as chairman of the Federal Reserve. The Fed, which had held rates near zero through the pandemic, raised them sharply in 2022 and 2023, to above 5 percent, and he did not try to remove Powell or to amend the Federal Reserve Act. When Silicon Valley Bank and Signature Bank failed in March 2023, the authorities invoked a systemic-risk exception, guaranteed uninsured depositors at those banks, and opened emergency lending. The banks were not nationalized, and the separation of commerce and banking was not rewritten. He left the monetary decision where the statute had put it, including the decision that tightened credit while his own earlier relief act was still being spent.

  4. 4. Labor

    Unemployment, which the pandemic had driven above 14 percent, fell to 3.4 percent in January 2023, a low not seen since 1969, and the expansion in payrolls continued through most of the term. A bill to ease union organizing, including recognition by signed cards, did not pass. The labor board, by appointment and by decision, moved toward unions. In September 2023 he joined a United Auto Workers picket line in Michigan, which no sitting president had done. He did not obtain a revision of the bargaining statutes. The private-sector union share did not recover to its old level.

  5. 5. Immigration and citizenship

    Encounters at the southern border, already high when he took office, ran in the millions in successive fiscal years. Early on, his administration narrowed the categories of people it treated as priorities for removal. The pandemic expulsion order known as Title 42 ended in May 2023. Parole programs admitted nationals of several countries by air if they had a sponsor, and an application was required of many who presented themselves between the ports. A bipartisan enforcement bill negotiated in the Senate in early 2024 failed after the Republican nominee opposed it and the House would not take it up. In June 2024 he proclaimed a restriction on asylum when daily encounters were high, using a power he had criticized. Congress enacted neither a legalization nor a durable enforcement statute. The statutes on the books were the ones he had inherited.

  6. 6. Race and civil rights

    He nominated Ketanji Brown Jackson to the Supreme Court, the first Black woman confirmed to it, and Kamala Harris, already elected with him, was the first Black woman and the first person of South Asian descent to be vice president. A policing bill written after the killing of George Floyd did not pass, nor did bills to restore the voting-rights coverage formula or to nationalize parts of election administration. On his first day he ordered the agencies to pursue racial equity in their programs. The Court, not Congress, then held in 2023 that race could not be a factor in university admissions. His administration disagreed with the decision and told schools what it believed they might still do. The Civil Rights Act stayed. The use of race as a plus factor in admissions did not.

  7. 7. Federalism

    An emergency rule requiring vaccination or testing at large private employers was blocked by the Supreme Court in January 2022; a vaccination requirement for workers at facilities paid by Medicare and Medicaid was upheld. A further extension of the eviction pause was struck down as beyond the health agency's statute. The broad cancellation of student debt was struck down in 2023 as beyond the education statutes. Abortion, after the Court overruled Roe, was again a question for state legislatures except where Congress might act, and Congress did not enact a national rule. He pardoned federal convictions for simple possession of marijuana and did not repeal the Controlled Substances Act. The pattern of the term was an administration reaching for statutes written for other emergencies, and a Court sending the reach back.

  8. 8. Foreign policy and military intervention

    The withdrawal from Afghanistan, which the previous administration had scheduled and he delayed into the summer, was completed in August 2021. The Afghan government collapsed, the evacuation ran through a single airfield, and thirteen American service members were killed by a bomb at the Abbey Gate. He did not send the force back. When Russia invaded Ukraine in February 2022 he refused a no-fly zone and refused American troops in the fight, and he sent weapons, money, and intelligence on a scale that made the United States the largest supplier of the defense. After the attack on Israel in October 2023 he backed Israel's campaign with munitions and diplomatic cover, paused one shipment of heavy bombs, and did not put American combat units into Gaza. He rejoined the Paris climate agreement by executive action. He did not re-enter the Iran nuclear arrangement. He did not leave NATO; he enlarged the practical American commitment in Europe.

  9. 9. National defense

    Defense appropriations rose with votes from both parties, driven by China and then by the war in Ukraine. Supplemental aid to Ukraine became, by 2024, a fight inside the House, and shipments paused while the statute was delayed. He kept the Space Force. An agreement with Australia and the United Kingdom opened a path for Australia to acquire nuclear-powered submarines, a transfer of a technology the United States had shared only with Britain. The all-volunteer force remained. The ground war that had begun in 2001 ended in the form of an American combat presence; the obligations he added were supplies and a submarine partnership, not a new occupation.

  10. 10. Religion and public morality

    He reversed the restrictions on foreign-aid funds connected to abortion and supported a statute to codify the abortion right the Court had just withdrawn; the statute did not pass. The vaccination rules produced religious-liberty suits, and the Court blocked the large-employer mandate on statutory grounds rather than by rewriting the religion clauses. He proposed no national church. His coalition ran from religious Black Protestants to the religiously unaffiliated, while white evangelicals remained with the other party, as they had since the 1980s. The moral argument of the term was abortion after Dobbs, and it was settled in the states and in the Court, not by a law he signed.

  11. 11. Education

    The elementary and secondary statute stayed in place, and a large share of the Rescue Plan went to school districts for reopening and for recovery. A promise of free community college did not pass. The distinctive education act of the term was an attempt to cancel, by the education secretary, several hundred billion dollars of federal student debt under a statute written for wartime and national emergencies. The Supreme Court held in June 2023 that the statute did not authorize a cancellation of that breadth. He then used income-driven repayment and smaller authorities; courts blocked the broadest of the replacement plans. The Department of Education remained, and the debt, for most borrowers, remained with it.

  12. 12. Land and natural resources

    He restored the boundaries of Bears Ears and Grand Staircase-Escalante, which his predecessor had reduced. He revoked the permit for the Keystone XL pipeline on his first day. A pause on new oil and gas leases on public land did not last: courts pushed back, and the climate statute that he later signed paired new renewable leasing with oil and gas leasing. In 2023 his administration approved the Willow oil project on Alaska's North Slope. A conservation goal of protecting 30 percent of the nation's lands and waters by 2030 was announced as policy and was not enacted as a statute that transferred title. The public domain was not sold.

  13. 13. Energy

    He rejoined the Paris agreement and signed, in the Inflation Reduction Act, the largest federal subsidy for lower-carbon energy the government had enacted: credits for electricity, vehicles, hydrogen, nuclear, and manufacturing, structured as the tax code rather than as a cap on carbon. A cap-and-trade bill did not pass, as it had not passed under Obama. Domestic oil production rose to a record during the term, and in 2022 he released on the order of 180 million barrels from the Strategic Petroleum Reserve after the invasion of Ukraine. He did not ban hydraulic fracturing. Coal continued to lose share to gas and to subsidized renewables. The energy state got larger as a payer of subsidies. It did not become the owner of the wells.

  14. 14. Crime and justice

    He said, against a slogan in his own coalition, that police should be funded, and the Rescue Plan allowed cities to spend its money on public safety. The policing statute did not pass. Homicides, which had jumped in 2020, fell in many cities in the later years and did not return everywhere to the level of 2019. A special counsel's report in 2024 found that he had willfully retained classified documents after leaving the vice presidency and declined to prosecute, describing his memory in terms his lawyers disputed. Another special counsel brought federal charges against his predecessor; those cases were not tried to a verdict during the term. He signed no crime bill on the scale of 1994. In December 2024 he pardoned his son Hunter, whom he had said he would not pardon, and he commuted most of the remaining federal death sentences.

  15. 15. Relief of poverty

    The child tax credit in the Rescue Plan, fully refundable and paid monthly for 2021, was the poverty measure of the term. By the Census Bureau's supplemental measure, child poverty fell sharply that year and rose when the expansion expired. The extension he wanted was in a larger bill that failed when a Democratic senator, Joe Manchin, would not supply the vote. The cash-welfare block grant of 1996 was not repealed. Food aid and rental assistance were increased in the emergency and then receded. The experiment was real, it was for one year, and the party could not make it the permanent law.

  16. 16. Healthcare

    He did not pass a public option or a single-payer plan. The Rescue Plan and then the Inflation Reduction Act extended the larger Affordable Care Act subsidies. The same 2022 act authorized Medicare to negotiate the prices of a list of drugs, capped insulin at thirty-five dollars a month for Medicare beneficiaries, and capped out-of-pocket drug spending in the Medicare drug benefit. The public-health emergency for the coronavirus ended in May 2023. Vaccination requirements split: the Court allowed the one tied to federal health-care payments and blocked the one laid on large private employers. Abortion drugs remained available under Food and Drug Administration rules; a 2024 decision turned the challengers away on standing and did not reach the merits. The health settlement he left was the one Obama had signed, with a drug-price amendment and a longer subsidy.

Of this presidency

  1. The Rescue Plan

    Signed on March 11, 2021, the American Rescue Plan was the first major statute of the term and the one that did not need a Republican vote. It sent payments to individuals, extended the pandemic unemployment supplements, funded schools and state governments, and for 2021 converted the child tax credit into a monthly benefit that reached families with no income-tax liability. The Congressional Budget Office put the cost near $1.9 trillion. Economists who had served Democratic administrations, among them Lawrence Summers, warned before passage that the sum was large relative to the remaining gap in the economy and would add to inflation. Inflation arrived. How much of it the bill caused, and how much came from supply breaks, energy, and the war, was disputed and was not settled by any later statute. What is not disputed is that the child credit expired on the schedule written into the law, and that the Senate would not renew it.

  2. Afghanistan

    Trump had signed an agreement with the Taliban for a withdrawal by May 2021. Biden extended the date into the summer and carried the withdrawal out. In August the Afghan army and the government in Kabul collapsed faster than the administration had prepared for. Evacuation was concentrated at the airport. On August 26 a suicide bomb at the Abbey Gate killed thirteen American service members and many Afghans. A drone strike days later, aimed at a further attack, killed ten civilians, including children. He defended the decision to leave and acknowledged the execution. The United States had no embassy in Kabul at the end, and it had no army there. The war that had begun in 2001 was over as an American combat presence, and it ended in a defeat of the government Washington had spent twenty years supporting.

  3. Chips, climate, and the tax code

    Two 2022 statutes were the industrial policy of the term. The CHIPS and Science Act put federal money into semiconductor fabrication and research inside the United States, a subsidy Congress passed with votes from both parties after the pandemic had exposed the thinness of the supply. The Inflation Reduction Act, passed with Democratic votes alone under reconciliation, was a climate and health law under a name taken from the inflation of that summer. Its climate provisions were tax credits for clean electricity, vehicles, manufacturing, hydrogen, and nuclear, not a cap on carbon and not public ownership of the energy industry. Its revenue provisions were a minimum tax on the book income of very large corporations and a tax on buybacks. It did not repeal the 2017 corporate rate cut. It spent through the tax code, which is a way of enlarging the government's role without creating a new department to run the plants.

  4. The border without a statute

    No Congress of his term sent him an immigration law. Encounters on the southern border rose into the millions a year. Title 42, the public-health expulsion, ended in May 2023. The administration built parole programs and an appointment system and still faced record numbers. In early 2024 Senate negotiators from both parties produced an enforcement bill; it died when the Republican presidential nominee opposed it and Republican votes in the Senate peeled away. On June 4, 2024, Biden himself proclaimed a bar on asylum when crossings were high. The proclamation was an executive act of the kind he had run against, and it was not a substitute for a statute. The legal immigration system, the asylum rules, and the status of people brought here as children remained what courts and old laws had left of them.

  5. Debt that the Court would not cancel

    In August 2022 the education secretary announced a cancellation of ten or twenty thousand dollars in federal student debt for borrowers under an income line, citing a 2003 statute that allows the secretary to waive student-aid rules in a national emergency. The Court decided Biden v. Nebraska on June 30, 2023. The chief justice, writing for six, held that the statute did not authorize a mass cancellation of that size; the three dissenters, including Jackson, his own appointee, would have allowed it. He then tried narrower routes, including a generous income-driven repayment plan. Courts enjoined the broadest of them. The debt was not abolished by Congress, and it was not abolished by the department. The attempt, and the decision, belong to the term: an executive reading of an emergency power, and a Court that would not extend it.

  6. Ukraine, without troops

    Russia's full invasion began on February 24, 2022. Biden ruled out American combat forces and a no-fly zone enforced by American pilots, and he assembled sanctions and a flow of weapons that grew from anti-tank missiles to artillery, air defense, and, later and after argument, longer-range systems and tanks. The policy was the opposite of a formal declaration of war and the opposite of neutrality. By 2024 the aid depended on supplemental appropriations that a Republican House delayed. The delay did not change the premise. The United States was the arsenal of a war it was not fighting with its own soldiers. That is an old American method. The scale, and the identity of the adversary, were new for the period after the Cold War.

  7. He left the ticket

    On June 27, 2024, he debated the Republican nominee and was unable to sustain the argument. Members of his party, after a delay, asked him to withdraw. On July 21 he ended his candidacy and endorsed the vice president. She became the nominee without a primary. She lost the November election. He remained president until January 20, 2025, and the constitutional term finished on that day. No president in this volume had been replaced on the ticket by his own party after the primaries and then served out the office. The party concluded that he could not win, and it did not conclude that he could not finish the term. Both judgments stood.

  8. The pardon of his son

    Hunter Biden was convicted in June 2024 on federal gun charges and pleaded guilty in September to tax charges. The president had said he would not pardon him. On December 1, 2024, he issued a full and unconditional pardon covering federal offenses his son had committed or may have committed over more than ten years, not only the charges on which he had been convicted. Later that month he commuted the sentences of most federal prisoners on death row. On the last morning of the term he issued preemptive pardons to members of his family and to officials and legislators he said were threatened with prosecution, including Anthony Fauci and a former chairman of the joint chiefs. The pardon power is plenary. He used it, at the end, more broadly than he had told the country he would, and more broadly than the offenses already tried.

PreviousTrump v. Hawaii NextThe Court He Did Not Appoint

2022–2024

The Court He Did Not Appoint

Biden named one justice, Ketanji Brown Jackson, who replaced Stephen Breyer in June 2022. The majority that decided the structural cases of his term was the other eight, and especially the three justices confirmed in the previous administration. Those cases returned abortion to the legislatures, ended race as a factor in university admissions, and told the executive that old statutes did not silently authorize a mass cancellation of debt or a judicial deference that left the reading of the law to the agencies.

Decision

Dobbs v. Jackson Women's Health Organization

Decided June 24, 2022

How it was made

Mississippi had forbidden abortion after fifteen weeks. The Court could have upheld that line and left Roe and Casey standing. It asked the parties to brief whether those cases should be overruled. Jackson had been confirmed and was not yet seated; Breyer was still on the Court when the case was argued and when it was decided.

Who sat

Alito wrote for five. Those five overruled Roe and Casey. Roberts concurred only in the judgment upholding the Mississippi statute; he would not have overturned the earlier cases. Six votes upheld the state law. Five withdrew the constitutional rule that had constrained the states since 1973. Biden's appointee took no part. Three of the five were Trump's.

With the opinion

  • Samuel Alito — G. W. Bush
  • Clarence Thomas — G. H. W. Bush
  • Neil Gorsuch — Trump
  • Brett Kavanaugh — Trump
  • Amy Coney Barrett — Trump

Concurring

  • John Roberts — G. W. Bush

In dissent

  • Stephen Breyer — Clinton
  • Sonia Sotomayor — Obama
  • Elena Kagan — Obama

What it changed

The Constitution, on this reading, does not confer a right to abortion. Regulation returned to the states and to Congress. Some states tightened their laws to the edge of a total ban. Others wrote the previous right into their own statutes or constitutions. Congress, with Biden's support, did not pass a national rule in either direction. The Court did not ban abortion. It held that the question was not its own, and that the justices who had made it so in 1973 were not owed obedience. The change in what government could do was a change in which government: state legislatures, not the federal courts.

Decision

Students for Fair Admissions v. Harvard

Decided June 29, 2023

How it was made

The Court heard, together, challenges to the admissions programs of Harvard and of the University of North Carolina. Both considered the race of applicants. Harvard is private and was sued under the statute that reaches institutions taking federal money. North Carolina is public and was sued under the equal-protection clause as well. Jackson, who had served on a Harvard governing board, recused from the Harvard case and sat on the North Carolina case.

Who sat

Roberts wrote. The majority was six in both cases. The dissent was two in the Harvard case, because Jackson took no part, and three in the North Carolina case, where she wrote as well as joined. Every justice in the majority was appointed by a Republican president. The one justice Biden had appointed was in dissent.

With the opinion

  • John Roberts — G. W. Bush
  • Clarence Thomas — G. H. W. Bush
  • Samuel Alito — G. W. Bush
  • Neil Gorsuch — Trump
  • Brett Kavanaugh — Trump
  • Amy Coney Barrett — Trump

In dissent

  • Sonia Sotomayor — Obama
  • Elena Kagan — Obama
  • Ketanji Brown Jackson — Biden

What it changed

Admissions programs that use race as a factor violate the equal-protection clause and the corresponding statute. The Court did not forbid a university to consider an applicant's account of how race had shaped a particular life. It forbade the racial classification itself. Grutter, which had allowed a limited use of race, was no longer the rule. No statute of Biden's had required the universities to stop. The decision did, and it bound public universities directly and private ones through the spending statute. An administrative practice of half a century, which presidents of both parties had defended, ended because six justices read the Fourteenth Amendment to forbid it.

Decision

Biden v. Nebraska

Decided June 30, 2023

How it was made

The secretary of education announced that he would cancel ten thousand dollars of federal student debt for a wide class of borrowers, and twenty thousand for those who had received a Pell grant, if their income was under a stated line. The authority he cited was a 2003 statute allowing him to waive or modify student-aid provisions in a national emergency. Several states sued. The cancellations would have run to several hundred billion dollars.

Who sat

Six to three. Roberts wrote. Kagan wrote the dissent. Jackson, the only justice appointed by the president whose program it was, was with the dissent. Barrett filed a concurrence and stayed in the majority.

With the opinion

  • John Roberts — G. W. Bush
  • Clarence Thomas — G. H. W. Bush
  • Samuel Alito — G. W. Bush
  • Neil Gorsuch — Trump
  • Brett Kavanaugh — Trump
  • Amy Coney Barrett — Trump

In dissent

  • Elena Kagan — Obama
  • Sonia Sotomayor — Obama
  • Ketanji Brown Jackson — Biden

What it changed

The cancellation did not take effect. The Court held that 'waive or modify' does not authorize the secretary to rewrite the loan program into a grant. A decision of that economic magnitude, it said, required a clear statement from Congress. The debt remained unless Congress canceled it, and Congress did not. The executive had treated an emergency statute as a power of the purse. The Court returned the purse to the branch the Constitution names. Biden's appointee would have let the cancellation stand.

Decision

Loper Bright Enterprises v. Raimondo

Decided June 28, 2024

How it was made

A herring fishery challenged a rule that required the industry to pay for monitors on its boats. The case became the vehicle for a larger question: whether courts must defer to an agency's reasonable reading of an ambiguous statute, the rule of Chevron, decided in 1984. The Court took a companion case, Relentless, so that Jackson, who recused from Loper Bright, could sit.

Who sat

Six to three in the companion case, where the full Court sat. Roberts wrote. Kagan dissented. Jackson took no part in Loper Bright itself and joined the dissent in Relentless. The majority again included all three of Trump's appointees and none of the Democratic appointees then sitting.

With the opinion

  • John Roberts — G. W. Bush
  • Clarence Thomas — G. H. W. Bush
  • Samuel Alito — G. W. Bush
  • Neil Gorsuch — Trump
  • Brett Kavanaugh — Trump
  • Amy Coney Barrett — Trump

In dissent

  • Elena Kagan — Obama
  • Sonia Sotomayor — Obama
  • Ketanji Brown Jackson — Biden

What it changed

Chevron is overruled. A court interpreting a statute the government administers is to decide the best reading, not to accept a permissible one because the agency prefers it. Congress may still delegate. An ambiguity is no longer, by itself, a delegation. Regulations already upheld were not automatically reopened. What changed is the next case: the agency's view is evidence, not a command. The administrative state did not disappear. The habit by which judges had let it choose the meaning of its own statutes did. That habit was a judicial doctrine, not a law Biden signed, and the Court that ended it was not a Court he had made.

PreviousJoseph Biden NextThe Party That Kept the Name

Afterward

The Party That Kept the Name

The continuity this book has traced is a continuity of the office. Through Obama, the men who held it still worked inside a commercial republic, a written constitution, elections, private property, and a national government whose energy was for defense, credit, and commerce rather than for the ordinary life of the citizen. That claim cannot be stretched to cover the Democratic Party as it speaks now. The party that carries the old name does not overlap, in program or in theory, with the Democratic Party of the nineteenth century, or with the party of Roosevelt and Truman, or with the party Bill Clinton led a couple of decades ago. The break became visible while Obama was president. It was not his statute book. It was the splitting of his coalition into directions no earlier American governing party had been willing to call its own.

Clinton’s Democrats were still inside the line, and the distance is short enough to measure. He raised the top income-tax rate and defended Medicare. He also signed a crime bill, a statute that ended the federal entitlement to cash welfare and put a work rule and a time limit in its place, and trade agreements that most of his own party opposed. He told Congress that the era of big government was over, and for a few years the budget was in surplus. He did not nationalize industry, did not propose that medicine or tuition be a federal grant, and did not treat private employment as a problem for the state to absorb. Labor hated the trade turn and lost. The left of the party hated the welfare law and lost. A Democrat of 1996 could sit in the same argument as the presidents in this book. A Democrat who now apologizes for that presidency cannot.

Obama, in the office, did not leave the framework. The health law subsidized private insurance and expanded Medicaid. It did not abolish the insurance industry. The banks were regulated and left in private hands. The trade agreements of his predecessor were passed, and a further Pacific agreement was signed. Removals at the border ran high. He said, more than once, that he was not a socialist, and the statutes match the sentence. What changed under him was the party around the office. The white South had finished leaving. The remaining coalition no longer contained a conservative wing that had to be bargained with. In that space the party came apart into several directions at once. They were not the same direction, and none of them was Clinton’s.

One of them was socialism, and it used the word. Bernie Sanders, an independent who had not been a Democrat, sought the party’s nomination in 2016 and again in 2020 as a democratic socialist. Members of the House took the same name. Their program was not the New Deal. Roosevelt’s social insurance assumed a private economy and a worker who paid in. Theirs was public payment for medicine as a right, public college, public employment in the name of the climate, and taxation whose purpose was to reduce fortunes rather than merely to support the government. Democratic socialism keeps elections, and it does not propose the confiscation of every shop. It is still outside the Democratic Party as that party existed under any president in this volume. Jefferson and Jackson were not socialists. Cleveland vetoed a federal dole for farmers in a drought. Truman ran against the left of his own coalition. Kennedy did not propose public ownership. Clinton had defeated this tendency in his own primaries and then governed against it. By Obama’s second term it was no longer outside the party. It was a lane in the next nominating contest.

Beyond that lane there was a further talk, and it should be called what it is. Communism is not a hard word for a large welfare state. It is a program in which the state owns production, competing parties are not permitted to displace the ruling party, and the market ceases to be the way goods are made and sold. The Democratic Party did not enact that program. It did not write it into a platform. It did not govern by it. What it did, beginning under Obama and openly afterward, was to stop treating the premises of that program as disqualifying. Public ownership of energy, the replacement rather than the regulation of private medicine, the direction of housing and work by political authority, and the use of schools and employers as instruments of a political doctrine all moved from the fringe of the left into the party’s ordinary argument. Activists said the quiet part without euphemism. Candidates learned to accommodate them. That is not a communist government. It is a party that has let a communist destination into its argument, which no Democratic president from Jefferson through Clinton, and not Obama in his statutes, was willing to do. No governing party in American history had done it.

A third direction was not a theory of ownership. It was a theory of dependence. The older parties, including the Democratic parties that built the insurance programs of 1935 and 1965, still assumed a citizen who worked, owned, and was not the client of an administration. Relief of poverty was local, or it was temporary, or it was a contribution paid back as a pension. Clinton’s welfare law was the last national restatement of that assumption. The direction that opened beside it treats income, rent, tuition, medicine, and the raising of children as federal goods, owed without a return of work and limited only by the size of the appropriation. Emergency payments and the suspension of rents and student debts were argued, almost at once, into the ordinary program. The person in this picture does not support the government. The government supports the person. That is a different relation from the one Washington, Jefferson, Lincoln, Roosevelt, or Clinton described, and it is a different relation from the one the Federalists and the Whigs described on the other side. A republic of dependents is not a variation on a republic of citizens. It is the end of the arrangement this book has been about.

The last direction was moral rather than fiscal, and it cut against the party’s own civil-rights settlement. The mid-century party, and Clinton’s after it, had argued for a common citizenship: the removal of legal bars of race, and then opportunity inside one set of rules. The later argument organized rights, speech, hiring, and schooling by identity, and it treated dissent from that organization as a harm the institution should punish. It was not a statute of Obama’s. It was the culture of the coalition that formed under him, carried by universities, foundations, and the party’s public language more than by any single law. A party that no longer believes in a common citizen cannot claim the Civil Rights Act, or Lincoln, or the Declaration, as the same party merely updated. The category has changed.

These directions disagree with one another. A socialist who wants workers’ control is not the same as an administrator who wants a population of clients, and neither is the same as a politics of identity that sorts those clients by group. What they share is the refusal of the older settlement. Private property is conditional. The constitutional division of power is an obstacle. Work is optional. The nation is a collection of groups with claims, not a people with a government. No party that has held this office, Democratic or Republican, Federalist or Whig, was aligned with that refusal. The Republican Party has altered its coalition and its manners, and the chapters on the later Republican presidents say where. It did not adopt this theory of the citizen. The Democratic Party did, not as one vote on one night, but as the permitted range of what a Democrat may now believe.

The name on the ballot is the name Jackson used, and the name Roosevelt used, and the name Clinton used. The overlap with those parties is the name, a few inherited programs, and the cities that still return the ticket. It is not the view of property, or of work, or of the Union, or of what a government is for. The presidency through Obama remained inside the government the earlier chapters describe. The party that now seeks the presidency does not.

PreviousThe Court He Did Not Appoint NextThe Democratic Socialist Party of America

Afterward II

The Democratic Socialist Party of America

The chapter before this one recorded a split. Under Obama the Democratic coalition came apart into socialism, a tolerance for arguments that used to be communist, a theory of dependence, and a politics of identity. Those were described as directions. They are better described as one program, already named, already organized, and already inside the party. The Democratic Party has become the Democratic Socialist Party of America. It has not changed the name on the ballot. It has changed the doctrine a Democrat is permitted to hold, the program a nomination now requires, and the members it will no longer throw out.

The organization that uses nearly that name has existed since 1982. The Democratic Socialists of America was formed by the merger of Michael Harrington’s Democratic Socialist Organizing Committee and the New American Movement. Harrington’s decision, and the organization’s after him, was not to found a third party that would lose. It was to enter the Democratic Party and replace its doctrine from the inside. For thirty years the attempt was small. A few thousand members, a magazine, a presence at the edge of a few city elections. The Democratic presidents of those years — Carter, Clinton, Obama in his statutes — governed against it. Clinton said the era of big government was over. Obama said he was not a socialist, and the health law left the insurance companies in place. The organization did not accept the refusal as the end of the strategy. It waited for a nominating contest in which the refusal would be optional.

That contest came in 2016, and again in 2020. Bernie Sanders was not a Democrat. He was an independent who called himself a democratic socialist, and he sought the Democratic nomination twice. The second time he won the early contests. The party’s officeholders stopped him by consolidating behind Joseph Biden, not by declaring his program outside the party. They were no longer able to do that. In the same years the organization grew from a few thousand members to several tens of thousands, and members of it took Democratic nominations for the House and kept them. Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez and Rashida Tlaib sat in the Democratic caucus under that description. Others took the same name. None was expelled. None was stripped of a committee for the belief. A party that will not remove a member for holding a doctrine has adopted the doctrine. The ballot line did the rest. A democratic socialist who wins a Democratic primary is a Democratic nominee. After enough of those wins, the line is the organization.

The program is not a mood, and it is not the New Deal. Roosevelt’s insurance assumed a worker who paid in and a private economy that remained private. Truman ran against the left of his own coalition. Kennedy did not propose public ownership. Clinton ended the cash entitlement and signed the trade agreements his left opposed. The program that is now ordinary in the party is the other one. Medicine is to be a public payment, not a regulated private market. College is to be a public grant. Energy and a large share of employment are to be directed by a Green New Deal, a resolution introduced in 2019 by a democratic socialist in the House and a senator of the party’s mainstream, which is itself the evidence. Fortunes are to be taxed not only to support the government but to reduce them. Immigration enforcement is treated as a wrong to be abolished rather than an obligation of a sovereign country. Policing is treated as a structure to be reduced. Work is no longer the condition of support. Housing, rent, tuition, and the raising of children are argued as federal goods. That list is the list the Democratic Socialists of America has published, and it is the list Democratic candidates learned to endorse, or to decline only in part and only with an apology.

Biden’s presidency is not a rebuttal of the case. It is the case in office. He won the nomination by refusing the socialist name, and by refusing single-payer medicine and the Green New Deal as a single statute. He then carried the spending. A relief act near two trillion dollars, a public child credit, climate subsidies on the scale of an industrial policy, an attempted cancellation of student debt, and a party that described each of these as a down payment rather than as a limit. The refusal was how he got the votes of people who still remembered Clinton. The statutes, and the demands his party made of him after the statutes, were the program he had refused. A nominee who must apologize for the refusal, and who cannot read the socialists out of the convention, is not the leader of the older party. He is the acceptable face of the newer one.

What the earlier chapter called dependence and identity sits inside the same program rather than beside it. A democratic socialist party that does not trust a private wage to support a citizen will offer the wage’s substitutes, and it will distribute them by the groups it has decided are owed. Public payment, public direction of work, and a moral doctrine enforced through schools, employers, and speech are not three parties. They are the one party the organization has been describing for forty years. Communism, in the strict sense, remains a further shore: one party, state title to production, no election that can remove it. The Democratic Party has not crossed to that shore. Democratic socialism is the shore it has settled. It keeps elections. It keeps a private sector it means to direct, tax, and, where it can, replace. It calls the arrangement democracy. The arrangement is the public ownership of the goods a citizen used to provide, and the use of the old party’s name to do it.

A party is not its letterhead. It is the doctrine it rewards, the doctrine it refuses to punish, and the program its candidates must approach. By those tests the name Jackson used, and Roosevelt used, and Clinton used, is no longer the name of their party. The Democratic Socialists of America did not have to win a resolution changing the ballot. Harrington’s strategy was to make the change unnecessary. The strategy has succeeded. The Democratic Party is now the Democratic Socialist Party of America, wearing the name that still wins the cities, and carrying a program no Democratic president in this book, including the last one, was willing to call by its right name.

Sources

The chapters from Washington through George W. Bush were written with these lives in hand. Each of those chapters begins from the life that formed the man, and Cleveland’s return begins from the four years out of office. Barack Obama, Donald Trump, and Joseph Biden are not on this list. The public record of those three presidencies is already full enough that no single life is the source. Caro’s life of Johnson is four published volumes; the fifth is not yet out.
